John — Saints Holy Bible cross-references
867 completed source verses; 878 retained pairs; 878 source units read; 11 deferred units.
First unfinished: John 2:2. Next new source review in this book: none. All original source units in this book have been read. Overall next new source: Acts 15:1.
Completed means an initial curated cross-reference pass: the complete supplied English source unit and each retained target passage were read, and each pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean exhaustive coverage or independent specialist approval.
Original Saints numbering is retained.

John 1:1
In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God.
  Genesis 1:1–1:3 — allusion: John's beginning, divine Word, creation context and light recall Genesis' beginning with God making heaven and earth, speaking, and bringing light. This is a creation-pattern comparison, not a claim that Genesis explicitly states John's full account of the Word being with God and being God.
    Genesis 1:1 In the beginning God made heaven and earth.
    Genesis 1:2 Now the earth was unseen and unformed, and darkness was over the deep; and the Spirit of God was moving over the water.
    Genesis 1:3 And God said, “Let there be light.” And there was light.

John 1:2
He was in the beginning with God.
  1 John 1:1–1:2 — teaching parallel: The Word's being with God in the beginning is compared with the eternal life that was with the Father and was manifested to the witnesses. Both passages join prior divine fellowship with subsequent revelation, while Word, life, God and Father retain the actual expressions used in their respective contexts.
    1 John 1:1 What was from the beginning, what we have heard, what we have seen with our eyes, what we looked upon and our hands touched, concerning the word of life—
    1 John 1:2 and the life was manifested, and we have seen and bear witness and announce to you the eternal life that was with the Father and was manifested to us—

John 1:3
All things came into being through Him, and without Him not one thing came into being. What has come into being
  Colossians 1:16–1:17 — teaching parallel: John says all things came into being through Him and excludes any exception; Colossians says all things, heavenly and earthly, were created through and for Him and that He is before all things. The comparison concerns Christ's comprehensive relation to creation, without changing John's sentence division where the final clause continues into 1:4.
    Colossians 1:16 because in Him all things were created, in the heavens and upon the earth, visible and invisible, whether thrones or dominions or rulers or authorities. All things have been created through Him and for Him.
    Colossians 1:17 And He is before all things, and in Him all things hold together.

John 1:4
in Him was life, and the life was the light of humankind.
  John 8:12 — teaching parallel: The prologue joins life in Him with humanity's light; Jesus later identifies Himself as the world's Light and promises the follower the light of life. This is an explicit recurring life-and-light connection, while the earlier syntax remains continuous with the end of 1:3.
    John 8:12 Then Jesus spoke to them again, saying, “I am the Light of the world. The one who follows Me will certainly not walk in the darkness, but will have the light of life.”

John 1:5
And the light shines in the darkness, and the darkness did not overcome it.
  John 12:35–12:36 — teaching parallel: The light shines in darkness that did not overcome it; the later saying urges people to walk and believe while the Light is present so darkness may not overtake them. The contrast preserves unconquered light versus a warning about people being overtaken, rather than transferring the first statement into an unconditional guarantee about every person.
    John 12:35 Then Jesus said to them, “For a little time longer the Light is among you. Walk while you have the Light, so that darkness may not overtake you. The one walking in the darkness does not know where he is going.
    John 12:36 While you have the Light, believe in the Light, so that you may become sons of light.” Jesus spoke these things, and went away and hid Himself from them.

John 1:6
There came a man sent from God; his name was John.
  Luke 3:2–3:3 — narrative parallel: John is introduced as a man sent from God; Luke names John son of Zechariah as the recipient of God's word in the wilderness and describes his ensuing baptismal proclamation. Both establish the Baptist's divine commission, with Luke supplying its particular setting and activity rather than a duplicate of John's prologue.
    Luke 3:2 during the high priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas, God’s word came to John, Zechariah’s son, in the wilderness.
    Luke 3:3 He went into all the region around the Jordan, proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins,

John 1:7
He came as a witness, to bear witness concerning the Light, so that all might believe through him.
  John 5:33–5:35 — teaching parallel: The prologue says John's witness serves others' belief; Jesus later recalls John's testimony to truth and explains that He mentions it so His hearers may be saved. Both passages describe John's testimony as directed toward others' response, and the later passage calls him a shining lamp rather than making him the ultimate Light.
    John 5:33 You have sent to John, and he has testified to the truth.
    John 5:34 But I do not receive testimony from a person; rather, I say these things so that you may be saved.
    John 5:35 He was the lamp that burned and shone, and you were willing to rejoice for an hour in his light.

John 1:8
He was not the Light, but came to bear witness concerning the Light.
  John 5:35–5:36 — teaching parallel: John is distinguished from the Light to whom he bears witness; Jesus later calls John a burning, shining lamp but describes a greater testimony in His own Father-given works. The distinction permits the Baptist's figurative light as a lamp without identifying him as the true Light of the prologue.
    John 5:35 He was the lamp that burned and shone, and you were willing to rejoice for an hour in his light.
    John 5:36 But I have testimony greater than John’s: for the works the Father has given Me to complete, the very works I do, bear witness concerning Me that the Father has sent Me.

John 1:9
The true Light, which gives light to every person, was coming into the world.
  John 8:12 — teaching parallel: The prologue announces the true Light coming into the world and illuminating people; Jesus later calls Himself the Light of the world and connects following Him with life rather than darkness. The shared worldwide light language does not silently equate being illuminated with every person's believing response.
    John 8:12 Then Jesus spoke to them again, saying, “I am the Light of the world. The one who follows Me will certainly not walk in the darkness, but will have the light of life.”

John 1:10
He was in the world, and the world came into being through Him, and the world did not know Him.
  John 3:19 — teaching parallel: The world fails to recognize the One present in it; the later discourse explains judgment in terms of the Light coming into the world while people prefer darkness because their deeds are evil. This compares failed recognition with the later account of adverse response to the same light motif; the creation clause remains additional in the prologue.
    John 3:19 And this is the judgment: the Light has come into the world, and people loved the darkness more than the Light, for their deeds were evil.

John 1:11
He came to what was His own, and His own did not receive Him.
  Luke 4:24–4:30 — narrative parallel: John speaks of failure to receive Him among His own; Luke narrates Jesus' saying about a prophet's unacceptability at home and the resulting hostile hometown response. Luke provides a concrete rejection scene, while John's broader expression His own is not reduced exclusively to Nazareth or made a claim that every member of a people rejects Him.
    Luke 4:24 He said, “Amen, I tell you, no prophet is acceptable in his homeland.
    Luke 4:25 But in truth I tell you, there were many widows in Israel in Elijah’s days, when heaven was shut for three years and six months and a great famine came over the whole land.
    Luke 4:26 Yet Elijah was sent to none of them except to a widow at Zarephath in Sidon’s territory.
    Luke 4:27 And there were many leprous people in Israel in the time of Elisha the prophet, but none of them was cleansed except Naaman the Syrian.”
    Luke 4:28 Everyone in the synagogue was filled with anger when they heard these things.
    Luke 4:29 They rose, threw Him out of the city, and led Him to the brow of the hill on which their city was built, intending to hurl Him down.
    Luke 4:30 But He passed through their midst and went on His way.

John 1:12
But as many as received Him, He gave them authority to become children of God—to those who believe in His name,
  Galatians 3:26 — teaching parallel: John connects receiving Christ and believing in His name with becoming God's children; Galatians calls its hearers God's sons through faith in Christ Jesus. Both relate membership in God's family to faith in Christ, using authority to become children and being sons as their respective expressions.
    Galatians 3:26 For you are all sons of God through faith in Christ Jesus.

John 1:13
who were born not from bloods, nor from the will of flesh, nor from the will of a man, but from God.
  1 Peter 1:23 — teaching parallel: John contrasts divine birth with bloods and human or fleshly will; Peter describes birth anew from imperishable rather than perishable seed through God's living, enduring word. Both distinguish a God-given birth from ordinary mortal derivation, without replacing John's stated negatives or turning the different birth images into identical wording.
    1 Peter 1:23 having been born anew, not from perishable seed but from imperishable, through the living and enduring word of God.

John 1:14
And the Word became flesh and dwelt among us, and we beheld His glory, glory as of the Only-begotten from the Father, full of grace and truth.
  Philippians 2:6–2:8 — teaching parallel: John says the Word became flesh and dwelt among witnesses who saw His glory; Philippians describes Christ in God's form taking a slave's form and human likeness, then humbling Himself to death. The comparison concerns divine Christ entering human existence, with John's glory-and-revelation emphasis and Philippians' humility-and-obedience emphasis preserved.
    Philippians 2:6 who, existing in the form of God, did not regard being equal with God as something to exploit,
    Philippians 2:7 but emptied Himself, taking the form of a slave, coming to be in the likeness of human beings; and being found in appearance as a human being,
    Philippians 2:8 He humbled Himself, becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross.

John 1:15
John bears witness concerning Him and has cried out, saying, “This was He of whom I said, ‘The One coming after me has come before me, because He was before me.’”
  John 1:30 — narrative parallel: The Baptist's testimony about the One coming after him who has come before him because He was before him is explicitly repeated when he identifies Jesus. This is an internal recurrence of the priority saying, not another incident independently establishing a different birth order.
    John 1:30 This is He of whom I said, ‘After me comes a man who has come before me, because He was before me.’

John 1:16
For from His fullness we all received, and grace in place of grace.
  Ephesians 4:7 — teaching parallel: John's community receives from Christ's fullness and grace in place of grace; Ephesians says grace was given to each according to Christ's gift. Both describe received grace originating in Christ, while Ephesians' distribution language does not resolve or replace John's particular grace-in-place-of-grace expression.
    Ephesians 4:7 But grace was given to each one of us according to the measure of Christ’s gift.

John 1:17
For the Law was given through Moses; grace and truth came through Jesus Christ.
  Exodus 24:12 — thematic parallel: John identifies Moses as the intermediary of the Law; Exodus records God's promise to give Moses the written stone tablets, law and commandments. This supplies a concrete scriptural basis for Moses' law-receiving role, while the grace-and-truth statement about Jesus is additional in John.
    Exodus 24:12 And the Lord said to Moses, “Come up to Me into the mountain, and remain there; and I will give you the tablets of stone, the law and the commandments which I have written to give them as law.”
  Romans 5:20–5:21 — teaching parallel: John places the Law given through Moses beside grace and truth coming through Jesus Christ; Romans places the law's arrival alongside abounding grace reigning toward eternal life through Jesus Christ. This is a specific law-and-grace comparison, without making the two authors' explanations or the phrase grace and truth identical.
    Romans 5:20 But law came in alongside, so that the trespass might increase; but where sin increased, grace abounded beyond measure,
    Romans 5:21 so that, just as sin reigned in death, so also grace might reign through righteousness for eternal life through Jesus Christ our Lord.

John 1:18
No one has ever seen God; the only-begotten God, who is in the bosom of the Father, He has made Him known.
  Matthew 11:27 — teaching parallel: John presents the only-begotten God in the Father's bosom as the One who makes God known; Matthew presents the Son's unique knowledge of the Father and His ability to reveal Him. Both give the Son a distinctive revelatory relation to the Father. John's supplied wording only-begotten God is preserved rather than replaced with another reading.
    Matthew 11:27 All things have been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.

John 1:19
And this is the testimony of John, when the Jews sent priests and Levites from Jerusalem to him, to ask him, “Who are you?”
  John 5:33 — narrative parallel: The priests-and-Levites delegation asks John for his identity; Jesus later explicitly recalls people having sent to John and his testimony to truth. The later retrospective notice is compared with this narrated inquiry, without adding names of envoys or claiming that every detail of the delegation is repeated.
    John 5:33 You have sent to John, and he has testified to the truth.

John 1:20
And he confessed and did not deny, and confessed, “I am not the Christ.”
  John 3:28 — narrative parallel: John's denial I am not the Christ is explicitly recalled later when he asks his hearers to witness that he had said this and had been sent before Him. This is a deliberate internal repetition of the Baptist's stated role, rather than an independent competing claim to the title.
    John 3:28 You yourselves bear witness for me that I said, ‘I am not the Christ, but I have been sent before Him.’

John 1:21
They asked him, “What, then? Are you Elijah?” And he said, “I am not.” “Are you the Prophet?” And he answered, “No.”
  Luke 1:17 — thematic parallel: John denies being Elijah when questioned; Luke's angel describes his going before the Lord in Elijah's spirit and power. The comparison preserves both the denial and the stated likeness of mission, without rewriting either as a simple assertion that John is the same historical Elijah or denying John's prophetic activity.
    Luke 1:17 He himself will go before Him in Elijah’s spirit and power, to turn fathers’ hearts toward children and the disobedient to the understanding of the righteous, to prepare for the Lord a people made ready.”
  Deuteronomy 18:15–18:18 — allusion: The envoys' question about the Prophet is compared with Moses' promise that God will raise a prophet like him from the people and put His word in that prophet's mouth. This supplies scriptural background for the expected prophetic figure, not proof of every assumption behind the envoys' question or a denial that John functions as a prophet in other passages.
    Deuteronomy 18:15 The Lord your God shall raise up unto you a prophet from among your brethren, like unto me; you shall hearken unto him,
    Deuteronomy 18:16 according to all that you asked of the Lord your God in Horeb, in the day of the assembly, saying: We will no more hear the voice of the Lord our God, neither will we see this great fire any more, lest we die.
    Deuteronomy 18:17 And the Lord said unto me: All that they have spoken is right.
    Deuteronomy 18:18 I will raise up for them a prophet from among their brethren, like unto you, and will put My word in his mouth; and he shall speak unto them all that I command him.

John 1:22
So they said to him, “Who are you, so that we may give an answer to those who sent us? What do you say about yourself?”
  Luke 7:19–7:22 — narrative parallel: Both scenes send a delegation to ask about a minister's identity and obtain a report for the senders. The envoys questioning John the Baptist seek his own account after the proposed titles are denied; in Luke, John the Baptist sends disciples to Jesus and Jesus tells them what to report back. Questioned person, sender, answers and occasions differ, so the parallel is the concrete inquiry-and-report structure rather than one delegation.
    Luke 7:19 and sent them to the Lord, saying, “Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?”
    Luke 7:20 When the men came to Him, they said, “John the Baptist sent us to Thee, saying, ‘Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?’”
    Luke 7:21 At that hour He healed many of diseases, afflictions and evil spirits, and graciously gave sight to many blind people.
    Luke 7:22 He answered them, “Go and report to John what you have seen and heard: the blind recover sight, the lame walk, leprous people are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead are raised, and the poor receive good news.

John 1:23
He said, “I am a voice of one crying in the wilderness, ‘Make straight the way of the Lord,’ just as Isaiah the prophet said.”
  Isaiah 40:3 — quotation: John explicitly identifies himself with Isaiah's wilderness voice and the command to straighten the Lord's way. The actual Isaiah unit speaks of preparing the Lord's way and making straight the paths of our God. The named citation is retained with the differing wording of the supplied passages, rather than silently making their formulations identical.
    Isaiah 40:3 A voice of one crying in the wilderness: Prepare the way of the Lord; make straight the paths of our God.

John 1:24
Now those sent were from the Pharisees.
  Luke 7:29–7:30 — thematic parallel: The Pharisaic background of John's envoys is compared with Luke's explicit contrast between recipients of John's baptism and Pharisees and Law experts described as rejecting God's purpose and not being baptized by him. Both concern that religious group's relation to this particular baptismal ministry. Luke's stated response is not assigned to these individual envoys or made proof of their identities, motives or personal baptism history.
    Luke 7:29 When all the people and the tax collectors heard, they acknowledged God’s righteousness, having been baptized with John’s baptism.
    Luke 7:30 But the Pharisees and experts in the law rejected God’s purpose for themselves, not having been baptized by him.

John 1:25
And they questioned him and said to him, “Why, then, do you baptize if you are not the Christ, nor Elijah, nor the Prophet?”
  Mark 11:28–11:33 — narrative parallel: The envoys challenge John's authority to baptize in the absence of the titles they propose; Mark's authority dispute asks whether John's baptism came from heaven or humans. Both concern the authorization of John's baptism, while Mark's later question is posed by Jesus to temple authorities and is not the same interrogation of John.
    Mark 11:28 and said to Him, “By what authority art Thou doing these things? Or who gave Thee this authority to do them?”
    Mark 11:29 Jesus said to them, “I will ask you one question. Answer Me, and I will tell you by what authority I do these things.
    Mark 11:30 Was John’s baptism from heaven or from humans? Answer Me.”
    Mark 11:31 They reasoned with one another, saying, “If we say, ‘From heaven,’ He will say, ‘Why then did you not believe him?’
    Mark 11:32 But should we say, ‘From humans’?” They feared the crowd, for everyone held that John truly was a prophet.
    Mark 11:33 They answered Jesus, “We do not know.” Jesus said to them, “Neither do I tell you by what authority I do these things.”

John 1:26
John answered them, saying, “I baptize in water. Among you stands One whom you do not know,
  Mark 1:8 — narrative parallel: John states that he baptizes in water and distinguishes the One standing among his questioners; Mark similarly contrasts his water baptism with the coming One's baptism in the Holy Spirit. John's notice that the hearers do not know that One remains particular to this exchange, with the Spirit identification developed later in 1:33.
    Mark 1:8 I baptized you with water, but He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit.”

John 1:27
the One coming after me, whose sandal strap I am not worthy to untie.”
  Mark 1:7 — narrative parallel: Both accounts give the Baptist's coming-after-me saying and his unworthiness to untie the stronger One's sandal strap. Mark adds stooping and explicitly says stronger; John places the saying within the delegation's inquiry. The close saying parallel does not combine their narrative order.
    Mark 1:7 He proclaimed, saying, “The One stronger than I is coming after me. I am not worthy to stoop down and untie the strap of His sandals.

John 1:28
These things happened in Bethany beyond the Jordan, where John was baptizing.
  John 10:40 — narrative parallel: The earlier scene is located beyond the Jordan where John baptizes; the later narrative explicitly returns beyond the Jordan to the place where John was first baptizing. This is a retrospective location link within John, not a settled modern site identification or an equation with the Bethany of Lazarus's household.
    John 10:40 And He went away again beyond the Jordan, to the place where John was first baptizing, and remained there.

John 1:29
The next day he saw Jesus coming toward him and said, “Behold, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!
  1 Peter 1:18–1:19 — thematic parallel: John identifies Jesus as God's Lamb taking away the world's sin; Peter describes ransom through Christ's precious blood as that of an unblemished, spotless lamb. Both use lamb imagery for Christ's saving work. Peter specifies blood and ransom, while John's world's-sin wording is not narrowed or replaced by those expressions.
    1 Peter 1:18 knowing that you were ransomed from the futile conduct handed down from your ancestors, not with perishable things, silver or gold,
    1 Peter 1:19 but with precious blood, as of a lamb without blemish and without spot—the blood of Christ,

John 1:30
This is He of whom I said, ‘After me comes a man who has come before me, because He was before me.’
  John 1:15 — narrative parallel: The Baptist's identification of Jesus repeats the earlier coming-after-yet-before-me testimony with the same because-He-was-before-me explanation. This is an explicit internal cross-reference to the witness already announced, not an independently dated second account of their births.
    John 1:15 John bears witness concerning Him and has cried out, saying, “This was He of whom I said, ‘The One coming after me has come before me, because He was before me.’”

John 1:31
And I did not know Him, but I came baptizing in water for this reason: so that He might be revealed to Israel.”
  Luke 1:76–1:77 — thematic parallel: John explains his water-baptizing mission as revealing Jesus to Israel; Zechariah's prophecy assigns his child the role of preceding the Lord and giving His people knowledge of salvation. Both give John's ministry a preparatory, revelatory purpose. Luke does not explain John's personal statement I did not know Him or settle the sequence of recognition in other accounts.
    Luke 1:76 And you also, child, will be called prophet of the Most High, for you will go before the Lord to prepare His ways,
    Luke 1:77 to give His people knowledge of salvation in the forgiveness of their sins,

John 1:32
And John testified, saying, “I have beheld the Spirit descending like a dove from heaven, and He remained upon Him.
  Mark 1:10 — narrative parallel: Both passages describe the Spirit's dove-like descent at Jesus: Mark narrates His seeing the heavens torn open and the Spirit descending into Him, while John reports the Baptist's testimony that the Spirit descended from heaven and remained upon Him. Observer, preposition and remaining-language retain their actual differences.
    Mark 1:10 Immediately as He came up out of the water, He saw the heavens being torn open and the Spirit descending like a dove into Him.

John 1:33
And I did not know Him, but the One who sent me to baptize in water said to me, ‘The One upon whom you see the Spirit descending and remaining—this is the One who baptizes in the Holy Spirit.’
  Matthew 3:11 — narrative parallel: John's commissioned sign identifies the One who baptizes in the Holy Spirit, distinct from his own water baptism; Matthew likewise contrasts John's water baptism with the stronger One's baptism in the Holy Spirit and fire. Matthew does not supply John's recognition-sign wording, and its fire clause is not inserted into John.
    Matthew 3:11 I baptize you in water for repentance, but the One coming after me is stronger than I, whose sandals I am not fit to carry. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.

John 1:34
And I have seen and have testified that this is the Son of God.”
  Matthew 3:17 — narrative parallel: The Baptist testifies that Jesus is God's Son; Matthew records the heavenly voice identifying Jesus as the beloved Son in whom God is pleased. Both concern Jesus' sonship at the baptismal setting, while a human witness and a voice from heaven remain distinct speakers and forms of testimony.
    Matthew 3:17 And behold, a voice from the heavens said, “This is My beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased.”

John 1:35
The next day John was standing there again, and two of his disciples,
  John 1:40 — narrative parallel: The later unit explicitly identifies Andrew as one of the two who heard the Baptist and followed Jesus, continuing the pair introduced standing with John here and acting in 1:36–39. This is a specific internal participant clarification. The second disciple remains unnamed, and the later verse does not independently restate the next-day timing or standing posture.
    John 1:40 Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, was one of the two who heard from John and followed Him.

John 1:36
and looking at Jesus as He walked, he said, “Behold, the Lamb of God!”
  John 1:29 — narrative parallel: The Baptist repeats the designation Lamb of God while looking at Jesus; the preceding day's fuller identification adds the taking away of the world's sin. This is a repeated identifying title within the narrative, with the extra explanatory words retained in 1:29 rather than added to the shorter saying.
    John 1:29 The next day he saw Jesus coming toward him and said, “Behold, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!

John 1:37
The two disciples heard him speaking and followed Jesus.
  John 1:40 — narrative parallel: The two hear the Baptist and follow Jesus; the later unit explicitly identifies Andrew as one of those two who heard from John and followed. This is an internal clarification of one participant in the narrated action. It does not name the second disciple or equate the pair with a differently named calling scene.
    John 1:40 Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, was one of the two who heard from John and followed Him.

John 1:38
Jesus turned and, seeing them following, said to them, “What do you seek?” They said to Him, “Rabbi”—which, translated, means Teacher—“where art Thou staying?”
  John 20:16 — thematic parallel: The disciples address Jesus as Rabbi, which the narrator translates Teacher; Mary later addresses the risen Jesus as Rabbouni, likewise glossed Teacher. The link concerns the expressly translated forms of address. Mary's resurrection encounter does not reproduce the earlier disciples' question about where Jesus is staying.
    John 20:16 Jesus says to her, “Mary.” Turning, she says to Him in Hebrew, “Rabbouni!”—which means Teacher.

John 1:39
He said to them, “Come, and you will see.” So they came and saw where He was staying, and stayed with Him that day. It was about the tenth hour.
  John 1:46 — narrative parallel: Jesus' invitation Come, and you will see is echoed by Philip's invitation Come and see to Nathanael. Both move an inquirer toward personal encounter with Jesus, with Jesus and Philip as different inviters. Philip's later words do not establish the location or tenth-hour timing of the first disciples' stay.
    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

John 1:40
Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, was one of the two who heard from John and followed Him.
  Matthew 4:18–4:20 — narrative parallel: Both passages identify Andrew as Simon Peter's brother and describe his following Jesus. John places Andrew among the Baptist's two hearers; Matthew places the brothers at their fishing work when Jesus calls them. These are related discipleship accounts with different settings and participants, not a merged chronology or an identification of John's unnamed second disciple.
    Matthew 4:18 As He walked beside the Sea of Galilee, He saw two brothers, Simon called Peter and Andrew his brother, casting a net into the sea, for they were fishermen.
    Matthew 4:19 He said to them, “Come after Me, and I will make you fishers of people.”
    Matthew 4:20 Immediately they left their nets and followed Him.

John 1:41
He first found his own brother Simon and said to him, “We have found the Messiah”—which, translated, is Christ.
  Matthew 16:16 — narrative parallel: Andrew announces to Simon that Jesus is the Messiah, explicitly translated Christ; Simon Peter later confesses Jesus as the Christ and Son of the living God. The matching Christ identification is compared across the brothers' distinct speeches, without adding Peter's fuller sonship language to Andrew's announcement.
    Matthew 16:16 Simon Peter answered, “Thou art the Christ, the Son of the living God.”

John 1:42
He brought him to Jesus. Jesus looked at him and said, “You are Simon, the son of John; you shall be called Cephas”—which is translated Peter.
  Matthew 16:17–16:18 — narrative parallel: Both scenes present Jesus addressing Simon in connection with the name Peter. John gives Cephas translated Peter and calls him son of John; Matthew addresses Simon Bar-Jonah and expands the rock-and-Church saying. The patronymic forms, wording and occasions remain distinct rather than being silently harmonized.
    Matthew 16:17 Jesus answered him, “Blessed are you, Simon Bar-Jonah, because flesh and blood did not reveal this to you, but My Father in the heavens.
    Matthew 16:18 And I tell you that you are Peter, and upon this rock I will build My Church, and the gates of Hades shall not prevail against it.

John 1:43
The next day He wanted to go out into Galilee. And He found Philip, and Jesus said to him, “Follow Me.”
  Mark 2:14 — narrative parallel: Jesus finds Philip and commands Follow Me; in Mark He addresses Levi at the tax station with the same command, and Levi follows. The concrete link is Jesus' individual summons to discipleship. Philip and Levi, their locations and the narrated responses are different and are not treated as one calling.
    Mark 2:14 As He passed by, He saw Levi son of Alphaeus sitting at the tax station. He said to him, “Follow Me.” He arose and followed Him.

John 1:44
Now Philip was from Bethsaida, the city of Andrew and Peter.
  John 12:21 — narrative parallel: John later again identifies Philip as being from Bethsaida of Galilee when Greek visitors approach him about seeing Jesus. That repeats this unit's particular hometown identification for Philip. It does not separately confirm this unit's additional statement about Andrew and Peter or name a modern site.
    John 12:21 These approached Philip, who was from Bethsaida of Galilee, and asked him, saying, “Sir, we want to see Jesus.”

John 1:45
Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”
  Luke 24:27 — teaching parallel: Philip says Moses and the prophets wrote of the One he identifies as Jesus; Luke describes the risen Jesus interpreting the things concerning Himself beginning from Moses and all the prophets. Both concern Jesus as the subject of Scripture. Philip's additional reported description son of Joseph from Nazareth remains his speech, not a new biological-paternity conclusion derived from Luke's passage.
    Luke 24:27 And beginning from Moses and from all the prophets, He interpreted to them in all the Scriptures the things concerning Himself.

John 1:46
Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”
  John 7:41–7:42 — narrative parallel: Nathanael questions whether anything good can come from Nazareth; the later crowd questions whether the Christ can come from Galilee and invokes Davidic Bethlehem. Both scenes contain objections based on perceived geographic origin. The characters' assumptions are reported rather than endorsed, and Nathanael is not identified with the later crowd.
    John 7:41 Others said, “This is the Christ.” But some said, “Surely the Christ does not come from Galilee?
    John 7:42 Has not the Scripture said that the Christ comes from the offspring of David and from Bethlehem, the village where David was?”

John 1:47
Jesus saw Nathanael coming toward Him and said concerning him, “Behold, truly an Israelite in whom there is no deceit!”
  Psalms 31:2 — thematic parallel: Jesus commends an Israelite in whom there is no deceit; the psalm blesses a man whose sin is not reckoned and in whose mouth there is no deceit. This compares the specific no-deceit commendation, keeping the psalm's mouth wording and forgiveness context. It does not make Nathanael's description a statement of absolute sinlessness.
    Psalms 31:2 Blessed is the man whose sin the Lord will certainly not reckon, and in whose mouth there is no deceit.

John 1:48
Nathanael said to Him, “How dost Thou know me?” Jesus answered him, “Before Philip called you, while you were under the fig tree, I saw you.”
  John 2:24–2:25 — narrative parallel: Jesus tells Nathanael He saw him under the fig tree before Philip called; the later narrator describes Jesus' knowledge of people without needing human testimony. This compares the reported prior recognition with the explicit knowledge theme, while the fig-tree location stays particular and no distance or mechanism of seeing is added.
    John 2:24 But Jesus Himself did not entrust Himself to them, because He knew everyone
    John 2:25 and because He had no need for anyone to testify concerning humanity, for He Himself knew what was within a person.

John 1:49
Nathanael answered Him, “Rabbi, Thou art the Son of God; Thou art the King of Israel.”
  Psalms 2:6–2:7 — thematic parallel: Nathanael joins the titles God's Son and Israel's King; the psalm joins the appointed king on Zion with the declaration You are My son. This is a concrete royal-sonship comparison, without asserting an explicit quotation or inserting the psalm's today-I-have-begotten-you wording into Nathanael's confession.
    Psalms 2:6 “But I was appointed king by Him on Zion, His holy mountain,
    Psalms 2:7 announcing the Lord’s decree: The Lord said to me, ‘You are My son; today I have begotten you.

John 1:50
Jesus answered him, “Because I told you that I saw you beneath the fig tree, do you believe? You will see greater things than these.”
  John 20:29 — teaching parallel: Jesus questions the ground of belief in both scenes: Nathanael responds after Jesus reports seeing him beneath the fig tree, while Thomas responds after seeing the risen Jesus. The earlier promise of greater sights and the later blessing of believers who have not seen are distinct responses, not the same evidential circumstance or future promise.
    John 20:29 Jesus says to him, “Because you have seen Me, have you believed? Blessed are those who have not seen and have believed.”

John 1:51
And He said to him, “Amen, amen, I tell you all: you will see heaven opened and the angels of God ascending and descending upon the Son of Man.”
  Genesis 28:12 — allusion: The promise uses Genesis' distinctive angels-of-God ascending-and-descending vision. Jacob sees their movement on a ladder reaching from earth to heaven, while Jesus' plural promise places their movement upon the Son of Man with heaven opened. The shared vision pattern does not turn the accounts into one incident or add a literal ladder to John's words.
    Genesis 28:12 And he dreamed, and behold, a ladder was set upon the earth, whose top reached to heaven; and the angels of God ascended and descended upon it.

John 2:1
And on the third day a wedding took place in Cana of Galilee, and the mother of Jesus was there.
  John 4:46 — narrative parallel: The wedding is located in Cana of Galilee; the later narrative expressly returns to Cana of Galilee and recalls the water becoming wine there. This is a direct internal location-and-sign reference, while the third-day date and Jesus' mother's presence are not separately restated in the later unit.
    John 4:46 So He came again to Cana of Galilee, where He made the water wine. And there was a certain royal official whose son was ill in Capernaum.

John 2:3
When the wine ran short, the mother of Jesus said to Him, “They have no wine.”
  John 11:3–11:4 — narrative parallel: Jesus' mother reports the lack of wine; Lazarus's sisters later send Him a report of their beloved brother's illness. Both stories present a need as a brief notification to Jesus, with His subsequent response and the wider sign narrative concerned with glory. The women, their relations to Jesus, needs and outcomes differ; the two Mary figures are not equated.
    John 11:3 So the sisters sent to Him, saying, “Lord, behold, the one Thou lovest is ill.”
    John 11:4 When Jesus heard, He said, “This illness is not for death, but for the glory of God, so that the Son of God may be glorified through it.”

John 2:4
And Jesus said to her, “What is this to Me and to you, woman? My hour has not yet come.”
  John 7:30 — teaching parallel: Jesus says His hour has not yet come; the later narrator explains that an attempt to seize Him fails because His hour has not yet come. This is a specific recurring not-yet-hour expression about Jesus' timing, not an assertion that the wedding request and attempted arrest are the same event or that His reply alone fixes a date.
    John 7:30 So they sought to seize Him, but no one laid a hand on Him, because His hour had not yet come.

John 2:5
His mother said to the servants, “Whatever He tells you, do.”
  Genesis 41:55 — thematic parallel: Mary tells the servants to do whatever Jesus tells them; Pharaoh tells the hungry Egyptians to go to Joseph and do whatever he says. Both direct people to follow another's instructions in a setting of needed provision. The speakers, recipients and kinds of provision differ, without identifying Mary with Pharaoh or making the stories one event.
    Genesis 41:55 And all the land of Egypt hungered, and the people cried to Pharaoh for bread. And Pharaoh said to all the Egyptians, “Go to Joseph; do whatever he says to you.”

John 2:6
Now six stone water jars were set there for the purification of the Jews, each holding two or three metretes.
  Mark 7:3–7:4 — thematic parallel: John explains that stone jars were present for Jewish purification; Mark explains handwashing and received washings of cups, jugs, copper vessels and couches. Both narrators explain water-related Jewish customs to readers. Mark does not attest these six stone jars, their capacity or their particular use at the wedding, and the practices are not made identical regulations.
    Mark 7:3 For the Pharisees and all the Jews do not eat unless they wash their hands with the fist, holding to the tradition of the elders.
    Mark 7:4 On returning from the marketplace, they do not eat unless they wash themselves. There are many other things they received to observe: washings of cups, jugs, copper vessels, and couches.

John 2:7
Jesus said to them, “Fill the jars with water.” And they filled them to the top.
  2 Kings 4:3–4:6 — narrative parallel: Jesus commands the jars to be filled with water and they are filled to the top; Elisha directs the widow to obtain vessels and pour into them until they are full. Both provision narratives give commands involving filled vessels. Elisha's oil continues pouring, while John's ensuing sign concerns water becoming wine; the substances and manner of provision remain distinct.
    2 Kings 4:3 He said to her, ‘Go, ask for vessels from outside, from all your neighbors—empty vessels, not a few.’
    2 Kings 4:4 ‘Then go inside and shut the door behind yourself and your sons. Pour into these vessels, and take away each full one.’
    2 Kings 4:5 She went away from him and did so. She shut the door behind herself and her sons. They kept bringing vessels to her, and she kept pouring,
    2 Kings 4:6 until the vessels were full. She said to her sons, ‘Bring me another vessel.’ They said to her, ‘There is no other vessel.’ Then the oil stopped.

John 2:9
When the master of the feast tasted the water that had become wine and did not know where it came from—though the servants who had drawn the water knew—the master of the feast called the bridegroom
  John 4:46 — narrative parallel: The feast-master tastes water that has become wine, while the servants know its source and he does not; the later Cana notice explicitly recalls that Jesus made the water wine there. It directly identifies the earlier transformation as Jesus' act, without repeating the feast-master's knowledge, the servants' role, or his conversation with the bridegroom.
    John 4:46 So He came again to Cana of Galilee, where He made the water wine. And there was a certain royal official whose son was ill in Capernaum.

John 2:10
and said to him, “Everyone sets out the good wine first, and when people have become drunk, the inferior wine. You have kept the good wine until now.”
  Luke 5:39 — thematic parallel: Both passages explicitly evaluate wine in relation to priority: the feast-master contrasts usual good-first/inferior-later service with good wine kept until now, while Luke's saying describes preference for old wine as good rather than new. The quality-and-sequence comparison preserves their different directions, speakers and contexts without assigning Old/New Covenant identities or treating either as an independently established feast custom.
    Luke 5:39 And no one who has drunk old wine desires new, for he says, ‘The old is good.’”

John 2:11
Jesus did this as the beginning of His signs in Cana of Galilee and manifested His glory, and His disciples believed in Him.
  John 4:53–4:54 — narrative parallel: The narrator calls Cana the beginning of Jesus' signs and records the disciples' belief; the later Galilean account records the official's household believing and labels the act the second sign after coming from Judea. These are explicit sign markers and responses, not a denial of the other Jerusalem signs already mentioned in John 2:23.
    John 4:53 So the father knew that it was in that hour when Jesus said to him, “Your son lives,” and he believed, he and his whole household.
    John 4:54 This again was the second sign Jesus did when He came from Judea into Galilee.

John 2:12
After this He went down to Capernaum, He and His mother and His brothers and His disciples, and they stayed there not many days.
  Matthew 4:13 — narrative parallel: Both narratives bring Jesus to Capernaum, but John records a short stay with mother, brothers and disciples, while Matthew describes settling there after leaving Nazareth and after the preceding arrest notice. This is a bounded Capernaum-ministry setting comparison, not the same journey, a shared date or independent attestation of John's family company and short duration.
    Matthew 4:13 Leaving Nazareth, He came and settled in Capernaum beside the sea, in the territories of Zebulun and Naphtali,

John 2:13
Now the Passover of the Jews was near, and Jesus went up to Jerusalem.
  Luke 2:41–2:42 — narrative parallel: Jesus goes up to Jerusalem for Passover; Luke describes His family's customary yearly Passover journey and His going at age twelve. Both portray Jesus in the Jerusalem Passover setting at different stages of life. The journeys and years are distinct, and the later temple incident is not inserted into the childhood visit.
    Luke 2:41 His parents went each year to Jerusalem for the Festival of Passover.
    Luke 2:42 When He became twelve years old, they went up according to the festival’s custom.

John 2:14
And He found in the temple those selling oxen and sheep and doves, and the money changers seated there.
  Mark 11:15 — narrative parallel: Both temple accounts identify commerce involving sellers, money changers and doves. John additionally names oxen and sheep and describes what Jesus finds before the expulsion; Mark describes the ensuing removal and overturned furnishings. Their different narrative placements and the lists' differences are preserved rather than merged into a single sequence.
    Mark 11:15 They came to Jerusalem. He entered the temple and began driving out those selling and those buying in the temple. He overturned the tables of the money changers and the seats of those selling doves,

John 2:15
Making a whip from cords, He drove them all out of the temple, both the sheep and the oxen, and poured out the money changers’ coins and overturned their tables.
  Matthew 21:12 — narrative parallel: Both passages narrate expulsion from the temple and overturning the money changers' tables. John additionally gives the cord whip, animals and scattered coins; Matthew names sellers, buyers and the dove sellers' seats. The shared actions do not transfer either account's additional details, timing or wording into the other.
    Matthew 21:12 Jesus entered the temple and cast out all those selling and buying in the temple. He overturned the tables of the money changers and the seats of those selling doves.

John 2:16
And He said to those selling the doves, “Take these things away from here. Do not make My Father’s house a house of trade.”
  Luke 19:45–19:46 — narrative parallel: Jesus rejects turning His Father's house into a house of trade; Luke's temple expulsion cites the house of prayer and reproaches a den of robbers. Both interpret the removal of commerce by reference to God's house. John's trade saying is not rewritten as Luke's scriptural quotation, and their narrative placements remain distinct.
    Luke 19:45 And He entered the temple and began to drive out those who were selling,
    Luke 19:46 saying to them, “It is written, ‘And My house shall be a house of prayer,’ but you have made it a den of robbers.”

John 2:17
His disciples remembered that it is written, “Zeal for Thy house will consume Me.”
  Psalms 68:10 — quotation: The disciples remember the written saying about zeal for God's house consuming the speaker; the actual psalm says zeal for Thy house devoured me and continues with reproaches falling on him. This is the named scriptural quotation with the supplied Greek-OT numbering and John's future versus the psalm's past wording kept visible.
    Psalms 68:10 for zeal for Thy house devoured me, and the reproaches of those reproaching Thee fell upon me.

John 2:18
So the Jews answered Him, “What sign dost Thou show us, since Thou doest these things?”
  Matthew 12:38–12:40 — narrative parallel: John's questioners demand a sign after Jesus' temple action; Matthew's scribes and Pharisees request a sign and receive the Jonah comparison involving the Son of Man and three days. Both sign requests introduce sayings associated with His death and return, while temple/body and Jonah/sea-creature images, occasions and stated durations are not conflated.
    Matthew 12:38 Then some of the scribes and Pharisees answered Him, saying, “Teacher, we wish to see a sign from Thee.”
    Matthew 12:39 He answered them, “An evil and adulterous generation seeks a sign, but no sign will be given it except the sign of Jonah the prophet.
    Matthew 12:40 For just as Jonah was in the sea creature’s belly three days and three nights, so the Son of Man will be in the heart of the earth three days and three nights.

John 2:19
Jesus answered them, “Destroy this sanctuary, and in three days I will raise it.”
  Mark 14:57–14:59 — narrative parallel: Both passages connect sanctuary destruction and three days, but John records Jesus' challenge Destroy this sanctuary and His promise to raise it, later explained as His body. Mark explicitly labels the witnesses false and reports their different claim that He would tear down a hand-made sanctuary and build another, with testimony that still disagrees. The accusation is not used to replace Jesus' actual words.
    Mark 14:57 Some stood up and bore false witness against Him, saying,
    Mark 14:58 “We heard Him say, ‘I will tear down this sanctuary made by hands, and in three days I will build another not made by hands.’”
    Mark 14:59 Yet even in this their testimony did not agree.

John 2:20
So the Jews said, “This sanctuary was built in forty-six years, and wilt Thou raise it in three days?”
  John 3:3–3:5 — narrative parallel: Both responses frame Jesus' language through an ordinary physical process: forty-six years of sanctuary construction versus a mother's womb and a second birth. John then explains the sanctuary as His body, while Jesus explains water-and-Spirit birth. The specific misunderstanding-and-clarification pattern does not verify the forty-six-year figure or make temple and birth the same image.
    John 3:3 Jesus answered and said to him, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from above, he cannot see the kingdom of God.”
    John 3:4 Nicodemus said to Him, “How can a person be born when he is old? Can he enter his mother’s womb a second time and be born?”
    John 3:5 Jesus answered, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from water and Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God.

John 2:21
But He was speaking about the sanctuary of His body.
  1 Corinthians 6:19–6:20 — thematic parallel: John explains that Jesus' sanctuary saying referred to His body; Paul calls the believers' body a temple of the Holy Spirit and grounds its use in belonging to God. Both employ bodily temple imagery, with Jesus' own body and the addressed believers as distinct referents and resurrection explanation versus ethical exhortation as different purposes.
    1 Corinthians 6:19 Or do you not know that your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit within you, whom you have from God? And you are not your own,
    1 Corinthians 6:20 for you were bought at a price. Therefore glorify God in your body.

John 2:22
When, therefore, He was raised from the dead, His disciples remembered that He had said this, and they believed the Scripture and the word Jesus had spoken.
  Luke 24:6–24:8 — narrative parallel: After Jesus is raised, John's disciples remember His earlier saying and believe the Scripture and His word; Luke's tomb messengers recall His Galilean prediction and the women remember His words. Both explicitly connect the resurrection with remembering earlier speech. The hearers, sayings and scene details differ rather than becoming one recollection.
    Luke 24:6 He is not here, but has been raised. Remember how He spoke to you while He was still in Galilee,
    Luke 24:7 saying that the Son of Man must be handed over into the hands of sinful people and be crucified and rise on the third day.”
    Luke 24:8 And they remembered His words,

John 2:23
While He was in Jerusalem at the Passover, during the feast, many believed in His name, seeing the signs He was doing.
  John 20:30–20:31 — teaching parallel: People believe in Jesus' name on seeing signs at the feast; the Gospel's closing purpose statement says written signs are meant to produce belief that Jesus is the Christ and God's Son, and life in His name. Seeing signs and receiving their written testimony are distinct settings. The link does not declare every sign-response identical or necessarily mature.
    John 20:30 Now Jesus also did many other signs before His disciples, which are not written in this book;
    John 20:31 but these have been written so that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and so that, believing, you may have life in His name.

John 2:24
But Jesus Himself did not entrust Himself to them, because He knew everyone
  John 6:64 — narrative parallel: Jesus withholds entrusting Himself because He knows people; the later narrator explicitly says He knows which followers do not believe and who will hand Him over. Both emphasize His knowledge beyond an outward appearance of following. John 6's stated unbelief is not automatically assigned to every person whom John 2:23 calls a believer.
    John 6:64 But there are some among you who do not believe.” For Jesus knew from the beginning who those were who did not believe and who it was who would hand Him over.

John 2:25
and because He had no need for anyone to testify concerning humanity, for He Himself knew what was within a person.
  Jeremiah 17:9–17:10 — thematic parallel: Jesus needs no human testimony to know what is within a person; Jeremiah asks who can know the deep heart and declares that the Lord examines hearts and kidneys. The comparison concerns knowledge of the inner person rather than ordinary reports. Jeremiah's supplied deep-heart wording and its judgment context remain distinct from John's description.
    Jeremiah 17:9 The heart is deep beyond all things, and so is a human; and who will know him?
    Jeremiah 17:10 I, the Lord, examine hearts and test kidneys, to give each according to his ways and the fruits of his practices.

John 3:1
Now there was a man from the Pharisees named Nicodemus, a ruler of the Jews.
  John 7:50–7:51 — narrative parallel: Nicodemus is introduced as a Pharisee and ruler; the later scene explicitly identifies Nicodemus as one of the group who had come to Jesus earlier, and records his question about hearing a person before judging. This is a recurring-person reference, not evidence that he speaks for every Pharisee or that his later stance describes his earlier understanding.
    John 7:50 Nicodemus, who had come to Him earlier and was one of them, said to them,
    John 7:51 “Does our Law judge a person unless it first hears from him and knows what he does?”

John 3:2
He came to Him at night and said to Him, “Rabbi, we know that Thou hast come from God as a teacher, for no one can do these signs that Thou doest unless God is with him.”
  John 19:39 — narrative parallel: The first encounter occurs at night; the burial narrative explicitly recalls Nicodemus as the one who first came to Jesus by night and describes his later bringing spices. This is a direct retrospective identification of the visitor. The later act is not silently inserted into his first conversation or used alone to determine the maturity of his initial belief.
    John 19:39 And Nicodemus also came, the one who had first come to Him by night, bringing a mixture of myrrh and aloes, about a hundred litrai.

John 3:3
Jesus answered and said to him, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from above, he cannot see the kingdom of God.”
  John 1:12–1:13 — teaching parallel: Jesus makes birth from above necessary for seeing God's kingdom; the prologue describes believers becoming God's children and being born from God rather than ordinary human derivation. The shared divine-birth teaching supplies context for from above without replacing it solely with Nicodemus's second-birth interpretation.
    John 1:12 But as many as received Him, He gave them authority to become children of God—to those who believe in His name,
    John 1:13 who were born not from bloods, nor from the will of flesh, nor from the will of a man, but from God.

John 3:4
Nicodemus said to Him, “How can a person be born when he is old? Can he enter his mother’s womb a second time and be born?”
  John 4:11–4:15 — narrative parallel: Nicodemus responds to birth from above by asking about entering a mother's womb again; the woman at the well initially asks about drawing living water and avoiding physical thirst. Both conversations contrast a questioner's ordinary bodily understanding with Jesus' further teaching. Birth and water are distinct images, and the questioners' circumstances and responses are not equated.
    John 4:11 The woman said to Him, “Sir, Thou hast no vessel for drawing water, and the well is deep. Where, then, dost Thou get this living water?
    John 4:12 Art Thou greater than our father Jacob, who gave us the well and drank from it himself, and his sons and his livestock?”
    John 4:13 Jesus answered her, “Everyone who drinks from this water will thirst again.
    John 4:14 But whoever drinks from the water that I will give him will certainly never thirst; rather, the water I will give him will become in him a spring of water leaping up into eternal life.”
    John 4:15 The woman said to Him, “Sir, give me this water, so that I may not thirst or come here to draw.”

John 3:5
Jesus answered, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from water and Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God.
  Ezekiel 36:25–36:27 — allusion: Jesus joins water and Spirit in the birth required for entrance into God's kingdom; Ezekiel promises cleansing water, a new heart and spirit, and God's Spirit within His people. These provide concrete scriptural renewal motifs as possible background. John does not explicitly quote Ezekiel here, and the comparison alone does not settle every interpretation of water or its ritual application.
    Ezekiel 36:25 I will sprinkle clean water upon you, and you will be cleansed from all your uncleannesses and all your idols. I will cleanse you.
    Ezekiel 36:26 And I will give you a new heart, and I will give a new spirit within you; and I will take the heart of stone out of your flesh, and give you a heart of flesh.
    Ezekiel 36:27 I will put My Spirit within you and cause you to walk in My ordinances, keep My judgments and do them.

John 3:6
What is born from the flesh is flesh, and what is born from the Spirit is spirit.
  Romans 8:5–8:9 — teaching parallel: Jesus distinguishes what originates from flesh and from Spirit; Romans contrasts flesh-directed and Spirit-directed life, with God's Spirit dwelling in believers. Both passages distinguish flesh and Spirit, but birth/origin and moral orientation are not identical explanations. John's statement is not expanded into a claim that physical embodiment itself is inherently evil.
    Romans 8:5 For those who are according to flesh set their minds on the things of the flesh, but those according to Spirit, on the things of the Spirit.
    Romans 8:6 For the mind of the flesh is death, but the mind of the Spirit is life and peace,
    Romans 8:7 because the mind of the flesh is hostility toward God; for it does not submit to God’s law, nor indeed can it.
    Romans 8:8 And those who are in flesh cannot please God.
    Romans 8:9 But you are not in flesh, but in Spirit, if indeed God’s Spirit dwells in you. But if anyone does not have Christ’s Spirit, that person does not belong to Him.

John 3:7
Do not marvel that I said to you, ‘You all must be born from above.’
  John 3:3–3:5 — teaching parallel: Jesus explicitly recalls His saying about being born from above after the earlier requirement, Nicodemus's objection and the water-and-Spirit explanation. This is a deliberate return to the same birth teaching. The source's you all is preserved rather than restricting the requirement to Nicodemus alone or treating it as a separately reported conversation.
    John 3:3 Jesus answered and said to him, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from above, he cannot see the kingdom of God.”
    John 3:4 Nicodemus said to Him, “How can a person be born when he is old? Can he enter his mother’s womb a second time and be born?”
    John 3:5 Jesus answered, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from water and Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God.

John 3:8
The wind blows where it wills, and you hear its sound, but you do not know where it comes from or where it goes; so it is with everyone born of the Spirit.”
  Ecclesiastes 11:5 — thematic parallel: Jesus compares Spirit-born people with the wind's audible but untraced movement; Ecclesiastes joins the unknown way of the spirit and bones in a pregnant womb with human inability to know God's works. Both connect untraceable divine activity with birth imagery. Ecclesiastes' supplied spirit wording is not silently changed to wind, nor are the two illustrations made one physical explanation.
    Ecclesiastes 11:5 among which no one knows the way of the spirit. As with bones in the womb of the pregnant woman, so you will not know God’s works, all that He will make.

John 3:9
Nicodemus answered Him, “How can these things happen?”
  Luke 1:34–1:35 — thematic parallel: Both how questions occur in birth conversations speaking of the Spirit, but Mary asks about conceiving the Son before the angel's explanation, whereas Nicodemus asks again after Jesus has taught birth from water and Spirit. This compares human questions amid divine birth agency while keeping virginal conception and the hearers' new birth distinct, along with speakers, order and responses.
    Luke 1:34 Mary said to the angel, “How will this be, since I do not know a man?”
    Luke 1:35 The angel answered her, “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; and so the holy One to be born will be called Son of God.

John 3:10
Jesus answered him, “Are you the teacher of Israel, and do you not understand these things?
  Ezekiel 36:25–36:27 — thematic parallel: Jesus challenges Israel's teacher about not understanding the preceding water-and-Spirit teaching; Ezekiel addresses Israel with promised cleansing water, a new heart and God's Spirit. The passage provides actual prophetic background relevant to that expectation of understanding. John does not name Ezekiel, so this is not a claim about Nicodemus's private syllabus or a proved direct quotation.
    Ezekiel 36:25 I will sprinkle clean water upon you, and you will be cleansed from all your uncleannesses and all your idols. I will cleanse you.
    Ezekiel 36:26 And I will give you a new heart, and I will give a new spirit within you; and I will take the heart of stone out of your flesh, and give you a heart of flesh.
    Ezekiel 36:27 I will put My Spirit within you and cause you to walk in My ordinances, keep My judgments and do them.

John 3:11
Amen, amen, I tell you: we speak what we know and bear witness to what we have seen, and you all do not receive our testimony.
  John 3:32 — teaching parallel: Both sayings describe testimony grounded in what is known, seen or heard and resistance to receiving it. The earlier uses we and you all, while the later speaks of the heavenly One and no one receiving His testimony. These differing pronouns and emphases remain, with 3:33 immediately acknowledging an actual receiver rather than making rejection an exceptionless count.
    John 3:32 What He has seen and heard, this He testifies, and no one receives His testimony.

John 3:12
If I told you earthly things and you do not believe, how will you believe if I tell you heavenly things?
  Wisdom of Solomon 9:16–9:17 — thematic parallel: Jesus contrasts difficulty believing earthly teaching with the prospect of heavenly teaching; Wisdom says earthly matters are inferred only with difficulty and asks who traced heavenly things without God giving wisdom and sending His Holy Spirit. Both make the earthly/heavenly contrast about human reception of divine knowledge, with belief and knowing as distinct expressions rather than a claim of verbal quotation.
    Wisdom of Solomon 9:16 With difficulty we infer the things on earth, and find what lies at hand only with labor; but who has traced out the things in heaven?
    Wisdom of Solomon 9:17 And who knew Thy purpose, unless Thou didst give wisdom and send Thy Holy Spirit from on high?

John 3:13
And no one has ascended into heaven except the One who descended from heaven, the Son of Man.
  Ephesians 4:9–4:10 — teaching parallel: John joins the Son of Man's heavenly descent with His unique ascent; Ephesians identifies the descending One as the same One who ascended above all heavens. The concrete connection is Christ's descent-and-ascent pattern. Ephesians' lower-parts-of-the-earth phrase and filling-all-things purpose are additional and are not assigned a new geographic interpretation by this comparison.
    Ephesians 4:9 Now what does “He ascended” mean, except that He also descended into the lower parts of the earth?
    Ephesians 4:10 The One who descended is also the One who ascended far above all the heavens, so that He might fill all things.

John 3:14
And just as Moses lifted up the snake in the wilderness, so the Son of Man must be lifted up,
  Numbers 21:8–21:9 — allusion: Jesus expressly compares the Son of Man's being lifted up with Moses' lifted snake. Numbers records the bronze serpent set on a standard and bitten people looking at it and living. This supplies the specific wilderness episode, while John's ensuing belief-and-eternal-life application remains distinct from the narrated physical healing through looking.
    Numbers 21:8 And the Lord said unto Moses: Make a serpent for yourself, and set it upon a standard; and it shall be, if a serpent bites a man, that everyone bitten, when he looks upon it, shall live.
    Numbers 21:9 And Moses made a bronze serpent and set it on a standard. Whenever a serpent bit a person, that person looked at the bronze serpent and lived.

John 3:15
so that everyone who believes may have eternal life in Him.
  John 6:40 — teaching parallel: Both passages connect belief with eternal life in relation to the Son: John 3 speaks of life in Him, and John 6 of beholding the Son and believing in Him as the Father's will. The later promise to raise the believer on the last day is additional and is not inserted into the shorter earlier statement.
    John 6:40 For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”

John 3:16
For God loved the world in this way: He gave the only-begotten Son, so that everyone who believes in Him might not perish but have eternal life.
  1 John 4:9–4:10 — teaching parallel: Both passages describe God's love expressed in giving or sending His Son into the world for people's life. The Gospel calls Him the only-begotten Son and describes belief versus perishing; the epistle calls Him His only Son and adds propitiation for sins. The matching divine-love-and-Son-gift pattern preserves those wording and explanatory differences.
    1 John 4:9 In this the love of God was manifested among us: God has sent His only Son into the world so that we might live through Him.
    1 John 4:10 In this is love: not that we have loved God, but that He loved us and sent His Son as propitiation for our sins.

John 3:17
For God did not send the Son into the world to judge the world, but so that the world might be saved through Him.
  John 12:47–12:48 — teaching parallel: Jesus' stated mission to save rather than judge the world recurs in the later discourse. John 12 also says His rejected word judges on the last day. The repeated saving-purpose statement is compared with its judgment context, without changing the earlier not-to-judge mission language into a claim that the Gospel contains no judgment.
    John 12:47 And if anyone hears My words and does not keep them, I do not judge him; for I did not come to judge the world, but to save the world.
    John 12:48 The one who rejects Me and does not receive My words has one who judges him: the word that I spoke will judge him on the last day.

John 3:18
The one who believes in Him is not judged; but the one who does not believe has already been judged, because he has not believed in the name of the only-begotten Son of God.
  John 5:24 — teaching parallel: John 3 contrasts belief in the Son without judgment with unbelief already judged; John 5 promises the hearer who believes the Sender eternal life and passage from death without coming into judgment. The shared faith-life-nonjudgment connection preserves belief in the Son versus in His Sender and present condemnation versus the later promise's formulation.
    John 5:24 Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who hears My word and believes the One who sent Me has eternal life, and does not come into judgment, but has passed from death into life.

John 3:19
And this is the judgment: the Light has come into the world, and people loved the darkness more than the Light, for their deeds were evil.
  John 1:9–1:11 — teaching parallel: The discourse explains judgment by the Light's coming into the world and people's preference for darkness; the prologue announces the Light coming and describes the world's nonrecognition and failure of reception. This is a recurring world-Light-response pattern. The discourse adds the evil-deeds explanation rather than making every clause a quotation of the prologue.
    John 1:9 The true Light, which gives light to every person, was coming into the world.
    John 1:10 He was in the world, and the world came into being through Him, and the world did not know Him.
    John 1:11 He came to what was His own, and His own did not receive Him.

John 3:20
For everyone who practices base things hates the light and does not come to the light, so that his deeds may not be exposed.
  Ephesians 5:11–5:13 — teaching parallel: Those doing base things avoid the light so their deeds are not exposed; Ephesians describes secret works of darkness and the exposing, making-visible action of light. Both use light as moral disclosure, while the exhortation to expose works in Ephesians and the explanation of avoidance in John have different roles.
    Ephesians 5:11 And do not share in the unfruitful works of darkness, but rather expose them,
    Ephesians 5:12 for it is shameful even to speak of the things done by them in secret.
    Ephesians 5:13 But all things, when exposed by the light, become visible,

John 3:21
But the one who does the truth comes to the light, so that his deeds may be manifested, because they have been done in God.”
  1 John 1:6–1:7 — teaching parallel: John joins doing truth with coming to the light and God-wrought deeds; the epistle contrasts walking in darkness without practicing truth with walking in the light and fellowship. The recurring truth-and-light ethical pattern is concrete, while the epistle's fellowship and cleansing-by-Jesus'-blood statements remain additional.
    1 John 1:6 If we say that we have fellowship with Him while walking in darkness, we lie and do not practice the truth.
    1 John 1:7 But if we walk in the light, as He is in the light, we have fellowship with one another, and the blood of Jesus His Son cleanses us from every sin.

John 3:22
After these things Jesus and His disciples came into the Judean countryside, and there He stayed with them and was baptizing.
  John 4:1–4:2 — narrative parallel: John describes Jesus staying with disciples and baptizing; the later narrator reports His growing baptismal ministry and expressly clarifies that His disciples performed the baptisms rather than Jesus Himself. This is an internal clarification of agency within the same ministry, preserving the earlier attribution rather than editing its wording.
    John 4:1 So when Jesus learned that the Pharisees had heard that Jesus was making and baptizing more disciples than John—
    John 4:2 although Jesus Himself was not baptizing, but His disciples—

John 3:23
John was also baptizing in Aenon near Salim, because there was much water there, and people came and were baptized.
  John 1:28 — narrative parallel: Both passages give a named setting for the same Baptist's activity: Aenon near Salim here and Bethany beyond the Jordan earlier. The comparison tracks the Gospel's baptismal-ministry locations, not their equivalence or modern coordinates. John 1 does not confirm Aenon's identity, its water supply or an unstated baptismal technique.
    John 1:28 These things happened in Bethany beyond the Jordan, where John was baptizing.

John 3:24
For John had not yet been thrown into prison.
  Mark 6:17 — narrative parallel: John places this baptismal scene before the Baptist's imprisonment; Mark explicitly describes Herod's seizing and binding John in prison. The later-life event provides the concrete temporal boundary John names. Mark's retrospective telling is not made the date of the arrest or used to move John's scene into another Gospel's sequence.
    Mark 6:17 For Herod himself had sent men to seize John and bind him in prison because of Herodias, the wife of his brother Philip, because he had married her.

John 3:25
Then a discussion arose between John’s disciples and a Jew concerning purification.
  John 2:6 — thematic parallel: John's disciples and a Jew dispute about purification amid the baptismal narrative; the earlier Cana description explicitly identifies water jars for Jewish purification. The shared purification subject gives a local contextual comparison, not a claim that the dispute concerned those jars or that its unstated arguments and particular rites can be reconstructed.
    John 2:6 Now six stone water jars were set there for the purification of the Jews, each holding two or three metretes.

John 3:26
And they came to John and said to him, “Rabbi, He who was with you beyond the Jordan, to whom you testified—behold, He is baptizing, and everyone is coming to Him.”
  John 4:1–4:2 — narrative parallel: John's disciples report that Jesus is baptizing and everyone is coming to Him; the later report heard by Pharisees says Jesus makes and baptizes more disciples than John and clarifies the disciples' agency. Both concern the comparative growth of the two ministries. Everyone in the earlier complaint is not converted into a literal count of all people everywhere.
    John 4:1 So when Jesus learned that the Pharisees had heard that Jesus was making and baptizing more disciples than John—
    John 4:2 although Jesus Himself was not baptizing, but His disciples—

John 3:27
John answered, “A person can receive not even one thing unless it has been given to him from heaven.
  1 Corinthians 4:7 — teaching parallel: John says a person can receive nothing unless it is given from heaven; Paul asks what anyone has that was not received and challenges boasting as if it were not a gift. Both oppose a self-originating claim to what is received, with John's ministry-response setting and Paul's factional boasting context kept distinct.
    1 Corinthians 4:7 For who makes you different? And what do you have that you did not receive? But if you did receive it, why do you boast as though you had not received it?

John 3:28
You yourselves bear witness for me that I said, ‘I am not the Christ, but I have been sent before Him.’
  John 1:20–1:23 — narrative parallel: John asks his hearers to witness his earlier denial of being the Christ and his preceding role; the earlier testimony explicitly denies the Christ title and identifies him as Isaiah's preparatory voice. This is a retrospective self-description, not a second independent commission or proof that every word of the later formulation appeared in the earlier quotation.
    John 1:20 And he confessed and did not deny, and confessed, “I am not the Christ.”
    John 1:21 They asked him, “What, then? Are you Elijah?” And he said, “I am not.” “Are you the Prophet?” And he answered, “No.”
    John 1:22 So they said to him, “Who are you, so that we may give an answer to those who sent us? What do you say about yourself?”
    John 1:23 He said, “I am a voice of one crying in the wilderness, ‘Make straight the way of the Lord,’ just as Isaiah the prophet said.”

John 3:29
The one who has the bride is the bridegroom. But the bridegroom’s friend, who stands and hears him, rejoices greatly because of the bridegroom’s voice. So this joy of mine has been fulfilled.
  Mark 2:19–2:20 — teaching parallel: John compares Jesus' role with a bridegroom and his own joy with that of the bridegroom's friend; Jesus' fasting answer uses the bridegroom's presence with his companions and later removal. Both use wedding fellowship to explain response to Jesus. The Baptist's fulfilled joy and the disciples' fasting circumstances differ, without assigning every wedding figure an unstated identity.
    Mark 2:19 Jesus said to them, “Can the bridegroom’s companions fast while the bridegroom is with them? As long as they have the bridegroom with them, they cannot fast.
    Mark 2:20 But days will come when the bridegroom is taken away from them, and then they will fast on that day.

John 3:30
He must increase, but I must decrease.”
  Mark 1:7 — teaching parallel: John's increase/decrease saying places Jesus' prominence above his own; Mark's Baptist announces the stronger One after him and his unworthiness to untie His sandal. Both express the Baptist's subordinate role in relation to Jesus, without reducing the must to a mere attendance statistic or equating different sayings word for word.
    Mark 1:7 He proclaimed, saying, “The One stronger than I is coming after me. I am not worthy to stoop down and untie the strap of His sandals.

John 3:31
The One coming from above is above all. The one who is from the earth is from the earth and speaks from the earth. The One coming from heaven is above all.
  John 8:23 — teaching parallel: The passage contrasts the One coming from heaven with the one from earth; Jesus later contrasts His own being from above with His hearers' being from below and from this world. The origin-and-preeminence theme is shared, while the later direct address is not assigned to an unstated speaker in John 3's unquoted reflection.
