    John 8:23 And He said to them, “You are from below; I am from above. You are from this world; I am not from this world.

John 3:32
What He has seen and heard, this He testifies, and no one receives His testimony.
  John 3:11 — teaching parallel: The testimony based on what the heavenly One has seen and heard repeats the earlier knowing/seeing/witnessing and nonreception pattern. The source's singular subject and no one wording differ from the earlier we and you all. The following 3:33 speaks of a receiver, so the rejection formula is not expanded into a denial of any believers.
    John 3:11 Amen, amen, I tell you: we speak what we know and bear witness to what we have seen, and you all do not receive our testimony.

John 3:33
The one who has received His testimony has set his seal that God is true.
  1 John 5:9–5:10 — teaching parallel: Receiving the testimony affirms that God is true; the epistle describes God's testimony concerning His Son and says refusing to believe it makes God a liar. These are corresponding positive and negative responses to divine testimony. The seal image in John is not treated as an added physical ritual or a different person's identification.
    1 John 5:9 If we receive human testimony, the testimony of God is greater, because this is God’s testimony: He has borne witness concerning His Son.
    1 John 5:10 The one who believes in the Son of God has the testimony within himself. The one who does not believe God has made Him a liar, because he has not believed in the testimony God has borne concerning His Son.

John 3:34
For the One whom God sent speaks the words of God, for He gives the Spirit without measure.
  John 12:49–12:50 — teaching parallel: The One sent by God speaks God's words; Jesus later explicitly says the Father who sent Him commanded His speech and that He speaks as the Father told Him. This is a close sending-and-speaking connection. It supports that clause without assigning an unstated recipient or a new quantitative meaning to the additional Spirit-without-measure statement.
    John 12:49 Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.
    John 12:50 And I know that His command is eternal life. So the things I speak, just as the Father has told Me, thus I speak.”

John 3:35
The Father loves the Son and has given all things into His hand.
  John 5:20 — teaching parallel: The Father loves the Son in both passages; John 5 explains that the Father shows Him all His own works and promises further works. This is an explicit recurring Father-Son love relation. The knowledge-and-works language there is not simply substituted for this unit's additional giving-all-things-into-His-hand clause.
    John 5:20 For the Father loves the Son and shows Him everything He Himself does, and He will show Him works greater than these, so that you may marvel.
  John 13:3 — teaching parallel: Both passages expressly state that the Father has given all things into Jesus' hand or hands. John 13 presents this as knowledge accompanying His later actions, while John 3 places it with the Father's love and the response to the Son. The matching grant language is preserved without claiming an identical discourse setting.
    John 13:3 knowing that the Father had given all things into His hands, and that He came out from God and was going to God,

John 3:36
The one who believes in the Son has eternal life; but the one who disobeys the Son will not see life, but the wrath of God remains upon him.
  1 John 5:11–5:12 — teaching parallel: The Son is the decisive relation to having life in both passages: John 3 contrasts believing and disobeying the Son, while the epistle contrasts having and not having the Son. The shared life-in-the-Son teaching keeps those actual verbs and leaves John 3's remaining-wrath statement as its additional explicit warning.
    1 John 5:11 And this is the testimony: God gave us eternal life, and this life is in His Son.
    1 John 5:12 The one who has the Son has life; the one who does not have the Son of God does not have life.

John 4:1
So when Jesus learned that the Pharisees had heard that Jesus was making and baptizing more disciples than John—
  John 3:22–3:26 — internal narrative reference: The earlier scene describes both baptizing ministries and John's followers reporting that everyone is coming to Jesus. John4:1 continues that growing-ministry setting with the Pharisees' hearing. The target does not itself narrate the Pharisees hearing or establish the numerical comparison as an independent census.
    John 3:22 After these things Jesus and His disciples came into the Judean countryside, and there He stayed with them and was baptizing.
    John 3:23 John was also baptizing in Aenon near Salim, because there was much water there, and people came and were baptized.
    John 3:24 For John had not yet been thrown into prison.
    John 3:25 Then a discussion arose between John’s disciples and a Jew concerning purification.
    John 3:26 And they came to John and said to him, “Rabbi, He who was with you beyond the Jordan, to whom you testified—behold, He is baptizing, and everyone is coming to Him.”

John 4:2
although Jesus Himself was not baptizing, but His disciples—
  John 3:22 — internal narrative reference: John3:22 attributes baptizing to Jesus while mentioning His disciples;4:2 expressly clarifies that the disciples, rather than Jesus personally, performed it. This is an internal agency clarification of the earlier narrative, not a second independent witness.
    John 3:22 After these things Jesus and His disciples came into the Judean countryside, and there He stayed with them and was baptizing.

John 4:3
He left Judea and went away again into Galilee.
  Matthew 4:12 — bounded narrative comparison: Both narrate Jesus withdrawing to Galilee. Matthew gives hearing of John's handover as the occasion; John places the departure after news of Jesus' baptizing ministry reaching the Pharisees. The different triggers and chronology are not merged into one asserted journey.
    Matthew 4:12 When He heard that John had been handed over, He withdrew to Galilee.

John 4:4
And He had to pass through Samaria.
  Luke 9:51–9:53 — narrative parallel: Both journeys bring Jesus into a Samaritan setting, but John moves from Judea toward Galilee and its wider chapter gives a welcoming city, whereas Luke moves toward Jerusalem and gives a village's refusal. The specific regional-and-reception contrast does not identify the communities or explain the force of John's had to as a proved geographic or divine necessity.
    Luke 9:51 As the days for His being taken up approached their fulfillment, He firmly set His face to go to Jerusalem.
    Luke 9:52 He sent messengers before His face. They went and entered a Samaritan village to prepare for Him.
    Luke 9:53 But they did not receive Him, because His face was set toward Jerusalem.

John 4:5
So He came to a city of Samaria called Sychar, near the plot of land Jacob gave to his son Joseph.
  Joshua 24:32 — scriptural background comparison: Both connect a field near Shechem/Sychar with Jacob's gift to Joseph. The actual Joshua unit says Jacob acquired the Shechem field and gave it to Joseph as a portion. John locates Sychar near the plot; the link does not equate the two city names or add identification of the well itself.
    Joshua 24:32 The sons of Israel brought Joseph’s bones up from Egypt and buried them at Shechem in the portion of the field that Jacob acquired from the Amorites living at Shechem for one hundred ewe lambs; he gave it to Joseph as a portion.

John 4:6
Jacob’s spring was there. Jesus, therefore, wearied from the journey, sat down as He was by the spring. It was about the sixth hour.
  John 19:28–19:30 — thematic parallel: John's well scene gives journey weariness followed by Jesus' request for a drink in 4:7; the passion scene gives His thirst and received sour wine. This is a concrete recurring bodily-need-and-drink motif. It does not establish Jacob's spring, the sixth-hour seating, an identical occasion or an unreported drink actually received at the well.
    John 19:28 After this, Jesus, knowing that all things had already been completed, says, so that the Scripture might be brought to completion, “I thirst.”
    John 19:29 A vessel full of sour wine was standing there. So, putting a sponge full of the sour wine on hyssop, they brought it to His mouth.
    John 19:30 When therefore Jesus had received the sour wine, He said, “It is finished.” And bowing His head, He gave up His spirit.

John 4:7
A woman from Samaria came to draw water. Jesus said to her, “Give Me a drink.”
  Genesis 24:17 — bounded narrative comparison: Both scenes have a request for drinking water addressed to a woman at a spring. Genesis' servant asks from Rebekah's jar; John has Jesus address the Samaritan woman. The concrete encounter pattern is compared without identifying the people or making the Samaritan encounter a marriage arrangement.
    Genesis 24:17 And the servant ran to meet her, and said, “Give me a little water to drink from your water jar.”

John 4:8
For His disciples had gone away into the city to buy food.
  John 4:27–4:31 — narrative parallel: The disciples' earlier departure to buy food frames their later return and subsequent Rabbi, eat appeal around Jesus' conversation with the woman. The same group and food concern give a delayed internal narrative resumption, rather than a generic buying comparison. The later scene is not made an explicit receipt or account of what they successfully purchased.
    John 4:27 At this His disciples came and marveled that He was speaking with a woman. Yet no one said, “What dost Thou seek?” or, “Why art Thou speaking with her?”
    John 4:28 So the woman left her water jar and went away into the city and said to the people,
    John 4:29 “Come, see a man who told me everything I did. Could this be the Christ?”
    John 4:30 They went out of the city and were coming to Him.
    John 4:31 Meanwhile the disciples urged Him, saying, “Rabbi, eat.”

John 4:9
So the Samaritan woman said to Him, “How dost Thou, being a Jew, ask a drink from me, a Samaritan woman?” For Jews do not associate with Samaritans.
  Luke 9:52–9:53 — bounded narrative comparison: Both depict a barrier between Jesus' Jewish/Jerusalem association and Samaritans. John records the woman's surprise at a Jew requesting her drink; Luke narrates another village refusing reception because of His Jerusalem destination. These are different communities and journeys; Luke does not prove a universal response by every Samaritan.
    Luke 9:52 He sent messengers before His face. They went and entered a Samaritan village to prepare for Him.
    Luke 9:53 But they did not receive Him, because His face was set toward Jerusalem.

John 4:10
Jesus answered her, “If you knew the gift of God and who it is who says to you, ‘Give Me a drink,’ you would have asked Him, and He would have given you living water.”
  John 7:37–7:39 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both place the offer of living water in Jesus' speech. John7 additionally connects its water saying with the Spirit and Jesus' glorification;4:10 speaks of God's gift and knowing the speaker. The later explanation is a related Gospel passage, without inserting its whole formulation into this unit.
    John 7:37 On the last day, the great day of the feast, Jesus stood and cried out, saying, “If anyone thirsts, let him come to Me and drink.
    John 7:38 The one who believes in Me—as the Scripture said, rivers of living water will flow from his belly.”
    John 7:39 He said this concerning the Spirit, whom those who believed in Him were about to receive; for the Spirit was not yet, because Jesus had not yet been glorified.

John 4:11
The woman said to Him, “Sir, Thou hast no vessel for drawing water, and the well is deep. Where, then, dost Thou get this living water?
  John 3:4 — teaching or imagery comparison: The woman asks how Jesus can provide the promised water without a drawing vessel; Nicodemus asks how an old person can enter a womb and be born. Both dialogues place a tangible-mechanism question beside Jesus' life-giving imagery. The water and birth images and their questioners remain different.
    John 3:4 Nicodemus said to Him, “How can a person be born when he is old? Can he enter his mother’s womb a second time and be born?”

John 4:12
Art Thou greater than our father Jacob, who gave us the well and drank from it himself, and his sons and his livestock?”
  John 8:53 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both interlocutors ask whether Jesus is greater than a named father: Jacob here and Abraham in8:53. John4 invokes the ancestral well, whereas John8 invokes death and Jesus' word. This is a repeated rhetorical challenge to Jesus' significance, not an identification of the patriarchs or settings.
    John 8:53 Art Thou greater than our father Abraham, who died? The prophets also died. Who dost Thou make Thyself out to be?”

John 4:13
Jesus answered her, “Everyone who drinks from this water will thirst again.
  John 6:49–6:50 — teaching or imagery comparison: John4 contrasts repeatedly needed well water with Jesus' enduring gift in the following verse. John6:49–50 contrasts manna eaten by ancestors who died with heaven's bread and life. The shared temporary/enduring provision contrast is retained; food, death, water and renewed thirst are not treated as identical assertions.
    John 6:49 Your fathers ate the manna in the wilderness and died.
    John 6:50 This is the bread that comes down from heaven, so that someone may eat from it and not die.

John 4:14
But whoever drinks from the water that I will give him will certainly never thirst; rather, the water I will give him will become in him a spring of water leaping up into eternal life.”
  John 6:35 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both promises explicitly say that the one receiving from Jesus will not thirst. John4 describes His water becoming a spring unto eternal life; John6 calls Him the bread of life and joins coming/believing with hunger/thirst. The different images remain visible.
    John 6:35 Jesus said to them, “I am the bread of life; the one who comes to Me will certainly never hunger, and the one who believes in Me will certainly never thirst.

John 4:15
The woman said to Him, “Sir, give me this water, so that I may not thirst or come here to draw.”
  John 6:34 — teaching or imagery comparison: The woman asks for the water Jesus has offered, just as John6's hearers ask Him always to give the offered bread. The water request adds avoiding thirst and the drawing trip; the bread request does not. This compares the dialogue responses, not identical physical provisions.
    John 6:34 So they said to Him, “Lord, always give us this bread.”

John 4:17
The woman answered Him, “I have no husband.” Jesus said to her, “You have spoken well in saying, ‘I have no husband,’
  John 4:18 — narrative parallel: Jesus' following for explanation explicitly supports His approval of the woman's no-husband statement: five earlier husbands are distinguished from the person she now has who is not her husband, ending again with speaking truly. This is a direct internal statement-and-explanation link, not identification of the men or approval of every feature of her unstated biography.
    John 4:18 for you have had five husbands, and the one you have now is not your husband. This you have spoken truly.”

John 4:18
for you have had five husbands, and the one you have now is not your husband. This you have spoken truly.”
  John 1:47–1:49 — narrative parallel: Both conversations disclose Jesus' prior personal knowledge and lead into a response about His identity: Nathanael asks how He knows him and hears about the fig tree; the woman hears her marital history and next calls Him a prophet. The knowledge-and-recognition pattern preserves different facts and confessions without identifying the five husbands or adding a mechanism of distant perception.
    John 1:47 Jesus saw Nathanael coming toward Him and said concerning him, “Behold, truly an Israelite in whom there is no deceit!”
    John 1:48 Nathanael said to Him, “How dost Thou know me?” Jesus answered him, “Before Philip called you, while you were under the fig tree, I saw you.”
    John 1:49 Nathanael answered Him, “Rabbi, Thou art the Son of God; Thou art the King of Israel.”

John 4:19
The woman said to Him, “Sir, I see that Thou art a prophet.
  John 9:17 — bounded narrative comparison: Both recipients of Jesus' disclosure or healing call Him a prophet. The Samaritan woman responds after His knowledge of her marital history; John9's healed man responds about the opening of his eyes. The two recognitions have different persons and evidential settings.
    John 9:17 So they said to the blind man again, “What do you say about Him, since He opened your eyes?” He said, “He is a prophet.”

John 4:20
Our fathers worshiped on this mountain, and you all say that in Jerusalem is the place where one must worship.”
  Psalms 121:1–121:4 — scriptural background comparison: The Greek-numbered Psalm121 explicitly describes going to the Lord's house in Jerusalem and the tribes going up to give thanks. It supplies a concrete Jerusalem-worship counterpart to the woman's stated contrast. It neither names her mountain nor settles her dispute by identifying its site.
    Psalms 121:1 A song of the ascents. I rejoiced over those who said to me, “We will go to the Lord’s house.”
    Psalms 121:2 Our feet were standing in your courts, Jerusalem.
    Psalms 121:3 Jerusalem, being built as a city whose common sharing is together,
    Psalms 121:4 for there the tribes went up, the tribes of the Lord—a testimony for Israel— to give thanks to the Lord’s name.

John 4:21
Jesus said to her, “Believe Me, woman, an hour is coming when neither on this mountain nor in Jerusalem will you worship the Father.
  Acts 7:48–7:50 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both passages challenge confinement of divine worship or dwelling to a constructed earthly site. Jesus forecasts worship of the Father beyond the two disputed locations; Stephen says the Most High does not dwell in handmade things and quotes heaven/earth imagery. Stephen does not reproduce Jesus' hour or name the Samaritan mountain.
    Acts 7:48 Yet the Most High does not dwell in things made by hands, as the prophet says:
    Acts 7:49 ‘Heaven is My throne, and the earth the footstool of My feet. What kind of house will you build for Me, says the Lord, or what place is there for My rest?
    Acts 7:50 Did not My hand make all these things?’

John 4:22
You worship what you do not know; we worship what we know, because salvation is from the Jews.
  Romans 9:4–9:5 — teaching or imagery comparison: Romans locates covenant privileges, worship, promises and Christ's fleshly descent among Israelites. This supports comparison with the specific claim that salvation is from the Jews. It does not independently repeat Jesus' judgment about what the Samaritan hearers know.
    Romans 9:4 who are Israelites. Theirs are the adoption as sons and the glory and the covenants and the giving of the law and the worship and the promises;
    Romans 9:5 theirs are the fathers, and from them comes the Christ according to the flesh, who is over all, God blessed throughout the ages. Amen.

John 4:23
But an hour is coming, and now is, when the true worshipers will worship the Father in spirit and truth; for the Father seeks such people to worship Him.
  Philippians 3:3 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both describe worship associated with God's Spirit. John adds the Father seeking true worshipers in spirit and truth; Philippians joins worship by the Spirit with boasting in Christ and rejecting confidence in flesh. The formulations are related without making all their qualifications identical.
    Philippians 3:3 For we are the circumcision, who worship by the Spirit of God and boast in Christ Jesus and put no confidence in the flesh,

John 4:24
God is spirit, and those who worship Him must worship in spirit and truth.”
  Philippians 3:3 — teaching or imagery comparison: The requirement for worship in spirit has a counterpart in Philippians' worship by the Spirit of God. John additionally says God is spirit and requires truth; the target does not repeat those whole claims. This is a bounded worship comparison, not a new definition of the relationship between divine and human spirit.
    Philippians 3:3 For we are the circumcision, who worship by the Spirit of God and boast in Christ Jesus and put no confidence in the flesh,

John 4:25
The woman said to Him, “I know that Messiah is coming, the One called Christ. When He comes, He will announce all things to us.”
  John 1:41 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both passages explicitly identify Messiah with the title Christ. The woman anticipates His coming and instruction, whereas Andrew announces that they have found Him. The target does not repeat the woman's prediction of announcing all things.
    John 1:41 He first found his own brother Simon and said to him, “We have found the Messiah”—which, translated, is Christ.

John 4:26
Jesus said to her, “I am He, the One speaking to you.”
  Mark 14:61–14:62 — teaching or imagery comparison: In both settings Jesus answers an explicitly messianic context with personal affirmation. John has the woman's Messiah expectation followed by His I-am-He reply; Mark has the high priest's Christ question and Jesus' I-am answer. The private conversation and trial, and Mark's added Son-of-Man saying, remain distinct.
    Mark 14:61 He remained silent and answered nothing. Again the high priest questioned Him and said to Him, “Art Thou the Christ, the Son of the Blessed One?”
    Mark 14:62 Jesus said, “I am. And you will see the Son of Man sitting at the right of the Power and coming with the clouds of heaven.”

John 4:27
At this His disciples came and marveled that He was speaking with a woman. Yet no one said, “What dost Thou seek?” or, “Why art Thou speaking with her?”
  Luke 7:39 — narrative parallel: Both scenes give an observer's unvoiced response to Jesus' interaction with a woman. John's disciples marvel at speech without asking their questions; Luke's host internally questions Jesus' prophetic knowledge in view of a woman's touch and her status as a sinner. The comparison concerns the observers' responses, not the women's identity or an assignment of Luke's sinner label and the host's motives to John's woman or disciples.
    Luke 7:39 When the Pharisee who had invited Him saw it, he said within himself, “If this man were a prophet, He would know who and what sort of woman is touching Him, that she is a sinner.”

John 4:28
So the woman left her water jar and went away into the city and said to the people,
  Genesis 24:28–24:30 — narrative parallel: Both well encounters lead the woman to leave the meeting place and report to her social circle, followed by listeners coming to the man. Rebekah reports to her mother's house and Laban comes; the Samaritan woman addresses the city. The concrete well-report-response pattern does not equate the women or men, prove a betrothal, or supply symbolism for John's left-behind water jar.
    Genesis 24:28 And the maiden ran and told her mother’s house of these things.
    Genesis 24:29 Now Rebekah had a brother, whose name was Laban; and Laban ran to the man outside, beside the spring.
    Genesis 24:30 And when he saw the earrings and the bracelets upon his sister’s hands, and when he heard the words of Rebekah his sister, saying, “This is what the man said to me,” he came to the man, who stood beside the camels at the spring,

John 4:29
“Come, see a man who told me everything I did. Could this be the Christ?”
  John 1:45–1:46 — bounded narrative comparison: Both invitations ask someone to come and encounter Jesus in a messianic context. Philip says come and see after identifying the One of whom Moses and the prophets wrote; the woman says come, see a man and asks whether He is Christ. Her knowledge-of-deeds testimony is not attributed to Philip.
    John 1:45 Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”
    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

John 4:30
They went out of the city and were coming to Him.
  Genesis 24:29–24:30 — narrative parallel: People leave the city toward Jesus after the woman's report; Laban hears Rebekah's report and runs to the man beside the spring. Both describe a listener's outward movement after a woman's well-encounter testimony. Group versus brother, city versus household and the persons' purposes remain distinct rather than a single journey or an identity claim.
    Genesis 24:29 Now Rebekah had a brother, whose name was Laban; and Laban ran to the man outside, beside the spring.
    Genesis 24:30 And when he saw the earrings and the bracelets upon his sister’s hands, and when he heard the words of Rebekah his sister, saying, “This is what the man said to me,” he came to the man, who stood beside the camels at the spring,

John 4:31
Meanwhile the disciples urged Him, saying, “Rabbi, eat.”
  John 21:12–21:13 — narrative parallel: The disciples urge Jesus to eat; the risen Jesus later invites the disciples to breakfast and distributes bread and fish. The specific meal invitation and Jesus-disciple roles recur in reversed directions. John 4's ensuing food-of-doing-the-Father's-will explanation is not made the same literal food or occasion as the shore breakfast.
    John 21:12 Jesus says to them, “Come, have breakfast.” But none of the disciples dared to question Him, “Who art Thou?” knowing that it was the Lord.
    John 21:13 Jesus comes and takes the bread and gives it to them, and the fish likewise.

John 4:32
But He said to them, “I have food to eat that you do not know.”
  Matthew 4:4 — teaching or imagery comparison: Jesus' unfamiliar food, explained as doing the Sender's will in4:34, is compared with His refusal to make bread alone the measure of life in Matthew's temptation scene. Matthew quotes life by God's word; it does not narrate the disciples' meal conversation or say Jesus never ate physical food.
    Matthew 4:4 But He answered, “It is written: ‘A human being shall not live on bread alone, but on every word proceeding through the mouth of God.’”

John 4:33
So the disciples said to one another, “Has someone brought Him something to eat?”
  John 4:11–4:15 — narrative parallel: The disciples frame Jesus' food saying as someone bringing ordinary food; the woman earlier frames living water through a drawing vessel, a deep well and avoiding physical thirst. Both exchanges contrast ordinary provision questions with further life-giving or mission teaching. The water and food images, hearers and explanations remain distinct rather than one misunderstanding or a claim that bodily needs are unreal.
    John 4:11 The woman said to Him, “Sir, Thou hast no vessel for drawing water, and the well is deep. Where, then, dost Thou get this living water?
    John 4:12 Art Thou greater than our father Jacob, who gave us the well and drank from it himself, and his sons and his livestock?”
    John 4:13 Jesus answered her, “Everyone who drinks from this water will thirst again.
    John 4:14 But whoever drinks from the water that I will give him will certainly never thirst; rather, the water I will give him will become in him a spring of water leaping up into eternal life.”
    John 4:15 The woman said to Him, “Sir, give me this water, so that I may not thirst or come here to draw.”

John 4:34
Jesus said to them, “My food is to do the will of the One who sent Me and to complete His work.
  John 6:38 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both identify doing the will of the One who sent Jesus as His mission. John4 calls that obedience His food and adds completing the work; John6 contrasts the Sender's will with His own and mentions descent from heaven. The shared obedience claim does not erase the different images.
    John 6:38 because I have come down from heaven not to do My own will, but the will of the One who sent Me.

John 4:35
Do you not say, ‘There are still four months, and the harvest comes’? Behold, I tell you, lift your eyes and look at the fields, for they are white for harvest. Already
  Matthew 9:37–9:38 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both address disciples with a harvest image for their mission. John speaks of fields ready for harvest in contrast to a four-month saying; Matthew calls the harvest plentiful and asks for workers. Matthew does not establish John's four-month detail or a literal agricultural timetable.
    Matthew 9:37 Then He says to His disciples, “The harvest indeed is plentiful, but the labourers are few.
    Matthew 9:38 Pray therefore to the Lord of the harvest, that He send out labourers into His harvest.”

John 4:36
the one who reaps receives wages and gathers fruit for eternal life, so that the one who sows and the one who reaps may rejoice together.
  1 Corinthians 3:6–3:9 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both spiritual-labor passages speak of workers and wages using cultivation imagery. John describes reaping, eternal-life fruit and shared joy; Paul distinguishes planting/watering, God's growth and wages according to labor. The roles and reward descriptions are compared without assigning Paul or Apollos to John's unnamed workers.
    1 Corinthians 3:6 I planted, Apollos watered, but God gave the growth.
    1 Corinthians 3:7 So neither the one who plants nor the one who waters is anything, but God who gives the growth.
    1 Corinthians 3:8 The one who plants and the one who waters are one, but each will receive his own wages according to his own labor.
    1 Corinthians 3:9 For we are God’s fellow workers; you are God’s cultivated field, God’s building.

John 4:37
For in this the saying is true: ‘One is the sower and another the reaper.’
  1 Corinthians 3:6–3:9 — teaching or imagery comparison: John distinguishes sower from reaper; Paul distinguishes his planting from Apollos' watering while crediting God with growth. Both recognize distinct contributions to one cultivation work. Watering is not silently changed into reaping, and the participants are not identified across the passages.
    1 Corinthians 3:6 I planted, Apollos watered, but God gave the growth.
    1 Corinthians 3:7 So neither the one who plants nor the one who waters is anything, but God who gives the growth.
    1 Corinthians 3:8 The one who plants and the one who waters are one, but each will receive his own wages according to his own labor.
    1 Corinthians 3:9 For we are God’s fellow workers; you are God’s cultivated field, God’s building.

John 4:38
I sent you to reap what you have not labored for. Others have labored, and you have entered into their labor.”
  1 Corinthians 3:10 — teaching or imagery comparison: Jesus describes entering work others have labored at; Paul describes laying a foundation on which another builds. The common feature is succeeding to another's prior ministry labor. The construction and harvest images differ, and Paul is not identified as one of the earlier workers in Jesus' saying.
    1 Corinthians 3:10 According to the grace of God given to me, I laid a foundation as a wise master builder, and another builds upon it. But let each one watch how he builds upon it.

John 4:39
Many of the Samaritans from that city believed in Him because of the word of the woman, who testified, “He told me everything I did.”
  John 4:29 — internal narrative reference: The narrator repeats the woman's everything-I-did testimony already voiced in4:29 and now reports its effect on Samaritan belief. The earlier verse is invitation and question, the later verse a reception notice. This is an explicit internal return to her words, not independent corroboration of the encounter.
    John 4:29 “Come, see a man who told me everything I did. Could this be the Christ?”

John 4:40
So when the Samaritans came to Him, they asked Him to stay with them, and He stayed there two days.
  Luke 9:52–9:53 — bounded narrative comparison: The Samaritan hosts ask Jesus to remain; Luke's different Samaritan village refuses Him because of His Jerusalem destination. The opposite receptions form a concrete narrative contrast involving Jesus and Samaritan communities. The two-day stay belongs only to John and no universal Samaritan attitude is inferred.
    Luke 9:52 He sent messengers before His face. They went and entered a Samaritan village to prepare for Him.
    Luke 9:53 But they did not receive Him, because His face was set toward Jerusalem.

John 4:41
And many more believed because of His word.
  Romans 10:17 — teaching or imagery comparison: The Samaritans' further belief because of Jesus' word illustrates the word/hearing/faith connection stated in Romans. Romans supplies a general account of hearing through Christ's word, not an independent account of this city or a count of its converts.
    Romans 10:17 So faith is from hearing, and hearing through the word of Christ.

John 4:42
They said to the woman, “We no longer believe because of your speech, for we ourselves have heard and know that this is truly the Savior of the world.”
  1 John 4:14 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both expressly call Jesus/the Son Savior of the world. John4 presents the Samaritans' firsthand-hearing confession;1John presents testimony that the Father sent the Son. Their witnesses and formulations differ without narrowing the shared world-wide title to one local community.
    1 John 4:14 And we have beheld and bear witness that the Father has sent the Son as Savior of the world.

John 4:43
After the two days He went out from there into Galilee.
  John 4:40 — internal narrative reference: The departure after two days closes the two-day stay explicitly reported in4:40. This internal chronological return links the end of the visit to its earlier duration statement;4:40 does not independently narrate the later departure into Galilee.
    John 4:40 So when the Samaritans came to Him, they asked Him to stay with them, and He stayed there two days.

John 4:44
For Jesus Himself had testified that a prophet has no honor in his own homeland.
  Mark 6:4 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both report Jesus' proverb about a prophet lacking honor at home. Mark adds relatives and household in a setting of offense; John places the testimony beside the Galilean welcome notice. Those contexts remain visible without forcing an identification of occasions or explaining away their differing reception.
    Mark 6:4 Jesus said to them, “A prophet is not without honor except in his hometown, among his relatives, and in his own house.”

John 4:45
So when He came into Galilee, the Galileans welcomed Him, having seen everything He did in Jerusalem at the feast, for they too had gone to the feast.
  John 2:23 — internal narrative reference: John2 records people in Jerusalem at Passover believing after seeing signs;4:45 recalls Galileans seeing Jesus' deeds in Jerusalem at the feast before welcoming Him. This links the narrative's feast-and-seen-deeds notices. It does not identify every member of the two groups or equate welcome with the full belief claim.
    John 2:23 While He was in Jerusalem at the Passover, during the feast, many believed in His name, seeing the signs He was doing.

John 4:46
So He came again to Cana of Galilee, where He made the water wine. And there was a certain royal official whose son was ill in Capernaum.
  John 2:9–2:11 — internal narrative reference: John4 expressly recalls Cana's water becoming wine. John2:9–11 narrates the water-wine discovery and locates the beginning of signs in Cana. That earlier sign is the clear internal counterpart; the royal official and ill son enter the later account rather than the wedding episode.
    John 2:9 When the master of the feast tasted the water that had become wine and did not know where it came from—though the servants who had drawn the water knew—the master of the feast called the bridegroom
    John 2:10 and said to him, “Everyone sets out the good wine first, and when people have become drunk, the inferior wine. You have kept the good wine until now.”
    John 2:11 Jesus did this as the beginning of His signs in Cana of Galilee and manifested His glory, and His disciples believed in Him.

John 4:47
When this man heard that Jesus had come from Judea into Galilee, he went to Him and asked Him to come down and heal his son, for he was about to die.
  Mark 5:22–5:23 — bounded narrative comparison: Both fathers seek Jesus' coming because a child is near death. John's man is a royal official with a son; Mark's named synagogue ruler Jairus has a daughter and asks for laying on hands. The common urgent plea is retained without merging the families or healing journeys.
    Mark 5:22 One of the synagogue rulers came, named Jairus. Seeing Him, he fell at His feet
    Mark 5:23 and begged Him earnestly, saying, “My little daughter is at the point of death. Please come and lay Thy hands on her, so that she may be saved and live.”

John 4:48
So Jesus said to him, “Unless you all see signs and wonders, you will certainly not believe.”
  John 6:30 — teaching or imagery comparison: John6's hearers explicitly ask for a sign so that they may see and believe, matching the condition criticized by Jesus' plural you-all in4:48. One is a rebuke and the other a demand; the groups and occasions are not equated.
    John 6:30 So they said to Him, “What sign, then, dost Thou perform, so that we may see and believe Thee? What work dost Thou do?

John 4:49
The royal official said to Him, “Sir, come down before my little child dies.”
  Mark 5:23 — bounded narrative comparison: Both pleas ask Jesus to come while a little child is dying or before death. The royal official's son and Jairus' daughter remain different children, and Mark additionally asks for hands to be laid on her. Neither account is used to identify the other parent.
    Mark 5:23 and begged Him earnestly, saying, “My little daughter is at the point of death. Please come and lay Thy hands on her, so that she may be saved and live.”

John 4:50
Jesus said to him, “Go; your son lives.” The man believed the word Jesus spoke to him and went on his way.
  Matthew 8:13 — bounded narrative comparison: Both join Jesus' go command, belief and a dependent's restoration without narrating Jesus arriving at the patient. John has an official's son and a word that he lives; Matthew has a centurion's servant healed at that hour. The officials, dependents and wording are kept distinct.
    Matthew 8:13 Jesus said to the centurion, “Go; as you have believed, let it happen for you.” And his servant was healed at that hour.

John 4:51
While he was already going down, his slaves met him, saying that his boy was alive.
  Luke 7:10 — bounded narrative comparison: Both report confirmation of a dependent's health through servants or messengers. John's slaves meet the returning father; Luke's sent people return to a house and find the slave well. The movement, patient and household relationships are different, not one harmonized scene.
    Luke 7:10 Those who had been sent returned to the house and found the slave in good health.

John 4:52
So he asked them the hour when he had begun to improve. They said to him, “Yesterday at the seventh hour the fever left him.”
  Matthew 8:13 — bounded narrative comparison: Both healing accounts explicitly notice the hour of restoration. John gives the servants' yesterday/seventh-hour fever report; Matthew says the centurion's servant was healed at that hour. Matthew does not independently establish John's clock time or child, and John's next verse separately relates the hour to Jesus' word.
    Matthew 8:13 Jesus said to the centurion, “Go; as you have believed, let it happen for you.” And his servant was healed at that hour.

John 4:53
So the father knew that it was in that hour when Jesus said to him, “Your son lives,” and he believed, he and his whole household.
  Acts 16:34 — bounded narrative comparison: Both narratives join a man's belief with his whole household. John ties the father's recognition to his son's recovery at Jesus' word; Acts describes the jailer's household rejoicing after belief and baptism. The target supports the household-faith motif, not the timing or identity of John's healing.
    Acts 16:34 He brought them up into his house, set food before them, and rejoiced with his whole household, having believed in God.

John 4:54
This again was the second sign Jesus did when He came from Judea into Galilee.
  John 2:11 — internal narrative reference: Both sign narratives contain an ordinal notice connected with Cana/Galilee: John2 names the beginning of signs, and4:54 calls this again the second sign on the return from Judea. This marks the Gospel's narrative sign notices, not an exhaustive assertion that Jesus had performed only two signs anywhere;2:23 already mentions Jerusalem signs.
    John 2:11 Jesus did this as the beginning of His signs in Cana of Galilee and manifested His glory, and His disciples believed in Him.

John 5:1
After these things there was a feast of the Jews, and Jesus went up to Jerusalem.
  John 2:13 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both narrate Jesus going up to Jerusalem in connection with a Jewish feast. John2 explicitly names Passover;5:1 does not name this feast. The recurring itinerary is compared without supplying a feast identity or treating the two visits as one.
    John 2:13 Now the Passover of the Jews was near, and Jesus went up to Jerusalem.

John 5:2
Now in Jerusalem by the Sheep Gate there is a pool called Bethzatha in Hebrew, having five porticoes.
  Nehemiah 3:1 — scriptural background comparison: Both name a Sheep Gate in the Jerusalem setting. Nehemiah records priests building and consecrating it; John locates the Bethzatha pool near it. The shared toponym does not prove unchanged masonry or supply John's pool name, five porticoes or precise archaeological identification.
    Nehemiah 3:1 Elisoub the high priest and his brothers the priests arose and built the Sheep Gate. They consecrated it and set up its doors, and consecrated as far as the Tower of the Hundred, as far as the Tower of Ananeel,

John 5:3
In these lay a multitude of the sick, the blind, the lame, and those with withered limbs.
  Matthew 15:30 — bounded narrative comparison: Both describe groups including blind and lame people in a healing context. Matthew's crowd brings sufferers to Jesus on the Galilean mountain; John's sufferers lie at a Jerusalem pool and the ensuing account concerns one man. Other conditions and the scope of healings are not made identical.
    Matthew 15:30 Great crowds came to Him, bringing with them lame, blind, maimed, mute, and many others. They laid them at His feet, and He healed them,

John 5:5
And a certain man was there who had been ill for thirty-eight years.
  Luke 13:10–13:13 — bounded narrative comparison: Both stories specify a multi-year condition before Jesus' restoration in a Sabbath setting. John's man has been ill thirty-eight years; Luke's bent woman has eighteen years of a spirit of weakness. The persons, durations, descriptions and causes remain different; no diagnosis is added to John's unit.
    Luke 13:10 Now He was teaching in one of the synagogues on the Sabbath.
    Luke 13:11 And behold, there was a woman who had had a spirit of weakness for eighteen years. She was bent over and could not straighten up completely.
    Luke 13:12 When Jesus saw her, He called to her and said, “Woman, you have been released from your weakness.”
    Luke 13:13 And He laid His hands on her, and at once she was made straight, and she glorified God.

John 5:6
Jesus, seeing him lying there and knowing that he had already been that way a long time, said to him, “Do you want to become well?”
  Matthew 20:32 — bounded narrative comparison: Both have Jesus address prospective recipients with a question about the help desired. John asks one long-ill man whether he wants to become well; Matthew asks two blind men what they wish Him to do. The wording, number and initiative differ without identifying the encounters.
    Matthew 20:32 Jesus stopped, called them, and said, “What do you wish Me to do for you?”

John 5:7
The sick man answered Him, “Sir, I have no one to put me into the pool when the water is stirred. While I am coming, someone else goes down before me.”
  Mark 2:3–2:4 — bounded narrative comparison: John's man lacks a helper to reach the pool; Mark's paralyzed man has four bearers who overcome a crowd and roof barrier. The concrete contrast concerns human assistance in access to healing. Mark supplies no pool mechanism, and the absent John5:4 angel account is not restored to explain the stirred water.
    Mark 2:3 They came, bringing to Him a paralyzed man carried by four men.
    Mark 2:4 Since they could not bring him to Jesus because of the crowd, they removed the roof above the place where He was. After digging through it, they lowered the mat on which the paralyzed man lay.

John 5:8
Jesus said to him, “Rise, take up your mat, and walk.”
  Mark 2:11 — bounded narrative comparison: Both commands tell a man to rise and take his mat. John adds walking; Mark tells the paralyzed man to go home. The Jerusalem-pool and Capernaum-house settings and patients remain different.
    Mark 2:11 “I say to you, arise, take your mat, and go to your house.”

John 5:9
And at once the man became well, took up his mat, and walked. Now it was a Sabbath on that day.
  Mark 2:12 — bounded narrative comparison: Both narrate immediate restoration followed by taking up the mat and moving. John explicitly dates the event to Sabbath; Mark's present passage does not. The parallel response does not identify the men or import John's date into Mark.
    Mark 2:12 He arose, immediately took up the mat, and went out before everyone. They were all amazed and glorified God, saying, “We have never seen anything like this!”

John 5:10
So the Jews said to the man who had been healed, “It is a Sabbath, and it is not lawful for you to take up the mat.”
  Jeremiah 17:21–17:22 — scriptural background comparison: Jeremiah explicitly forbids lifting or carrying burdens on Sabbath in the Jerusalem setting. It is a concrete legal-text background to the objection to carrying the mat. The target does not itself judge Jesus' healing or establish that His opponents' application to this case was correct.
    Jeremiah 17:21 Thus says the Lord: Guard your souls, and do not lift burdens on the Sabbath day or go out through Jerusalem’s gates,
    Jeremiah 17:22 and do not bring burdens out of your houses on the Sabbath day, and you must do no work; consecrate the Sabbath day, as I commanded your fathers. And they did not hear or incline their ear,

John 5:11
But he answered them, “The One who made me well said to me, ‘Take up your mat and walk.’”
  John 5:8 — internal narrative reference: The healed man reports the take-up-and-walk command actually spoken in5:8. This is an explicit internal quotation used in his response to the objection. It does not create a second independent witness or add a moral motive for his reply.
    John 5:8 Jesus said to him, “Rise, take up your mat, and walk.”

John 5:12
So they asked him, “Who is the man who told you, ‘Take it up and walk’?”
  John 9:19–9:21 — bounded narrative comparison: Both healing investigations pursue how restoration happened and who was responsible. John5's question asks who gave the walking command; John9's parents answer questions about their son and say they do not know who opened his eyes. Questioners, witnesses, conditions and the stated questions differ.
    John 9:19 and asked them, saying, “Is this your son, whom you say was born blind? How, then, does he now see?”
    John 9:20 His parents answered, “We know that this is our son and that he was born blind.
    John 9:21 But how he now sees we do not know, or who opened his eyes we do not know. Ask him; he is of age. He will speak for himself.”

John 5:13
But the man who had been healed did not know who He was, for Jesus had slipped away, since there was a crowd in the place.
  John 9:12 — bounded narrative comparison: Both healed men cannot provide requested information about an absent healer. John5's man does not know who He is; John9's man, who has already named Jesus, does not know where He is. The contrast preserves the different kinds of ignorance and does not claim the men had the same knowledge.
    John 9:12 And they said to him, “Where is He?” He said, “I do not know.”

John 5:14
After these things Jesus found him in the temple and said to him, “Behold, you have become well. Sin no more, so that something worse may not happen to you.”
  John 8:11 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both encounters include Jesus' instruction to sin no more. John5 addresses a restored man and adds a warning about something worse; the bracket-marked John8 passage concerns a woman accused of adultery and a non-condemnation reply. The link does not establish a cause for the man's original illness or a new critical-text verdict.
    John 8:11 She said, “No one, Lord.” Jesus said, “Neither do I condemn you. Go, and from now on sin no more.”⟧

John 5:15
The man went away and reported to the Jews that it was Jesus who made him well.
  John 9:11 — bounded narrative comparison: Both healed men identify Jesus as responsible for their restoration when reporting to other people. John5 follows Jesus finding the man again; John9 recounts mud, washing and restored sight. The methods and patients differ, and no betrayal motive is assigned to John5's report.
    John 9:11 He answered, “The man called Jesus made mud and spread it on my eyes and said to me, ‘Go to Siloam and wash.’ So I went away and washed, and received my sight.”

John 5:16
And for this reason the Jews persecuted Jesus, because He did these things on a Sabbath.
  Luke 13:14 — bounded narrative comparison: Both accounts narrate opposition to Jesus because He healed on Sabbath. John records persecution by the Jews in this scene; Luke records the synagogue ruler's indignation and crowd instruction. These are particular narrative participants and different healings, not a claim about every Jewish person.
    Luke 13:14 But the synagogue ruler, indignant because Jesus had healed on the Sabbath, said to the crowd, “There are six days on which work ought to be done. So come and be healed on those days, and not on the Sabbath day.”

John 5:17
But Jesus answered them, “My Father is working even now, and I am working.”
  John 9:4 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both relate Jesus' work to the divine Sender. John5 speaks of the Father working even now and Jesus working; John9 says we must work the Sender's works while it is day. The plural workers and day/night image in John9 are not made identical to John5's Sabbath defense.
    John 9:4 We must work the works of the One who sent Me while it is day. Night is coming, when no one can work.

John 5:18
For this reason, therefore, the Jews sought all the more to kill Him, because He not only broke the Sabbath, but also called God His own Father, making Himself equal to God.
  John 10:31–10:33 — comparison of controversy narratives: Both connect lethal hostility with Jesus' relation to God. John5:18 gives the narrator's explanation involving Sabbath breaking and calling God His own Father, making Himself equal to God; John10:33 quotes opponents accusing Him of blasphemy and making Himself God. The different formulations and narrative attribution remain distinct, without adding a separate doctrinal ruling.
    John 10:31 Then the Jews took up stones again to stone Him.
    John 10:32 Jesus answered them, “I showed you many good works from the Father. For which work among them do you stone Me?”
    John 10:33 The Jews answered Him, “We do not stone Thee for a good work, but for blasphemy, and because Thou, being a man, makest Thyself God.”

John 5:19
So Jesus answered them, “Amen, amen, I tell you: the Son can do nothing from Himself unless He sees the Father doing it. For whatever He does, the Son also does these things in the same way.
  John 12:49 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both sayings deny autonomous action or speech from Jesus Himself and refer it to the Father. John5 concerns the Son doing what He sees the Father do; John12 concerns speaking what the sending Father commanded. Doing and speaking are related but not interchangeable formulations.
    John 12:49 Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.

John 5:20
For the Father loves the Son and shows Him everything He Himself does, and He will show Him works greater than these, so that you may marvel.
  John 3:35 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both explicitly state that the Father loves the Son. John5 describes showing works and future greater works; John3 describes giving all things into His hand. The common love statement is direct, while the different descriptions of showing and entrusting remain intact.
    John 3:35 The Father loves the Son and has given all things into His hand.

John 5:21
For just as the Father raises the dead and gives life, so also the Son gives life to whom He wills.
  John 11:25 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both attribute life-giving power to Jesus/the Son. John5 compares the Son's giving life with the Father raising the dead; John11 names Jesus resurrection and life and promises life to the believer despite death. Their qualifications are retained rather than replaced by a universal timetable.
    John 11:25 Jesus said to her, “I am the resurrection and the life; the one who believes in Me will live, even if he dies,

John 5:22
For the Father judges no one, but has given all judgment to the Son,
  2 Timothy 4:1 — teaching or imagery comparison: John says the Father has given all judgment to the Son;2Timothy names Christ Jesus as the coming judge of living and dead. The latter gives a direct counterpart for Christ's judging role but does not restate the entire Father-to-Son grant.
    2 Timothy 4:1 I charge you before God and Christ Jesus, who is to judge the living and the dead, and by His appearing and His kingdom:

John 5:23
so that all may honor the Son just as they honor the Father. The one who does not honor the Son does not honor the Father who sent Him.
  Philippians 2:9–2:11 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both intertwine honor directed to Jesus with God the Father. John requires honoring the Son as the Father; Philippians describes God's exaltation, knees bending and tongues confessing Jesus as Lord to the Father's glory. The particular just-as requirement and the exaltation narrative are not collapsed into one wording.
    Philippians 2:9 Therefore God also highly exalted Him and graciously gave Him the name above every name,
    Philippians 2:10 so that at the name of Jesus every knee should bend, of those in heaven and on earth and under the earth,
    Philippians 2:11 and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father.

John 5:24
Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who hears My word and believes the One who sent Me has eternal life, and does not come into judgment, but has passed from death into life.
  1 John 3:14 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both expressly describe passing from death into life. John5 associates this with hearing Jesus and believing His Sender;1John gives love of the brothers as the sign by which we know the transition. The passages' different conditions or evidential emphasis remain visible.
    1 John 3:14 We know that we have passed from death into life, because we love the brothers. The one who does not love abides in death.

John 5:25
Amen, amen, I tell you: an hour is coming, and now is, when the dead will hear the voice of the Son of God, and those who hear will live.
  John 11:43–11:44 — teaching or imagery comparison: John11 narrates Jesus' loud command followed by the dead Lazarus coming out, a concrete voice-and-life counterpart. John5 speaks more broadly of the dead hearing the Son and living. One bodily restoration does not by itself settle the full meaning, scope or coming/now-is timing of that saying.
    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”
    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

John 5:26
For just as the Father has life in Himself, so He also gave the Son to have life in Himself.
  John 1:4 — teaching or imagery comparison: The prologue locates life in the Word, while John5 speaks of Father and Son having life in themselves and the Father giving this to the Son. The shared life-in-the-divine-person theme is compared without silently adding John5's gave formulation to the prologue.
    John 1:4 in Him was life, and the life was the light of humankind.

John 5:27
And He gave Him authority to execute judgment, because He is the Son of Man.
  Matthew 25:31–25:32 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both associate the Son of Man with judgment. John states the granting of authority; Matthew portrays the Son of Man enthroned and separating the gathered nations. The enacted judgment scene is a counterpart for the role, not a repetition of the grant's wording.
    Matthew 25:31 When the Son of Man comes in His glory, and all the angels with Him, then He will sit upon the throne of His glory.
    Matthew 25:32 All the nations will be gathered before Him, and He will separate them from one another as the shepherd separates the sheep from the goats.

John 5:28
Do not marvel at this, because an hour is coming in which all who are in the tombs will hear His voice
  John 11:43–11:44 — teaching or imagery comparison: The command at Lazarus' tomb and the dead man's emergence provide a concrete comparison for voice reaching those in tombs. John5 predicts all in the tombs hearing His voice; John11 narrates one man, not completion of the universal future event.
    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”
    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

John 5:29
and come out: those who did good things to a resurrection of life, but those who practiced base things to a resurrection of judgment.
  Daniel 12:2 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both passages distinguish outcomes among those arising from death. John names resurrection of life and judgment by good/base deeds; the supplied Greek-based Daniel names everlasting life, reproach, dispersion and everlasting shame among those arising. Daniel's actual wording and many are retained rather than replaced with another edition's two-part formula.
    Daniel 12:2 Many of those sleeping in the breadth of the earth shall arise: some unto everlasting life, others unto reproach, and others unto dispersion and everlasting shame.

John 5:30
I can do nothing from Myself. As I hear, I judge, and My judgment is righteous, because I do not seek My own will, but the will of the One who sent Me.
  John 6:38 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both deny pursuing Jesus' own will in favor of the will of the One who sent Him. John5 applies this to hearing and righteous judgment; John6 frames His heavenly descent and mission. The shared will statement does not make their full subjects identical.
    John 6:38 because I have come down from heaven not to do My own will, but the will of the One who sent Me.

John 5:31
If I bear witness concerning Myself, My testimony is not true.
  John 8:13–8:18 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both passages explicitly discuss Jesus bearing witness concerning Himself. John5's conditional says His testimony is not true; John8 affirms its truth and adds the Father and the two-person testimony rule. The differing formulations are left visible rather than silently changing true into another term or forcing a complete harmonization.
    John 8:13 So the Pharisees said to Him, “Thou bearest witness concerning Thyself; Thy testimony is not true.”
    John 8:14 Jesus answered them, “Even if I bear witness concerning Myself, My testimony is true, because I know where I came from and where I am going. But you do not know where I come from or where I am going.
    John 8:15 You judge according to the flesh; I judge no one.
    John 8:16 But even if I judge, My judgment is true, because I am not alone, but I and the Father who sent Me.
    John 8:17 And in your own Law it is written that the testimony of two people is true.
    John 8:18 I am the One who bears witness concerning Myself, and the Father who sent Me bears witness concerning Me.”

John 5:32
There is Another who bears witness concerning Me, and I know that the testimony He bears concerning Me is true.
  John 8:18 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both introduce witness beyond Jesus' own testimony. John8 explicitly names the sending Father; the present John5 unit says Another without naming Him. The comparison marks the additional-witness pattern without establishing that identity from this isolated word alone;5:33 and5:37 supply further context.
    John 8:18 I am the One who bears witness concerning Myself, and the Father who sent Me bears witness concerning Me.”

John 5:33
You have sent to John, and he has testified to the truth.
  John 1:19–1:23 — internal narrative reference: John1 explicitly narrates people sent to John and his testimony in response. John5 recalls sending to John and his truthful testimony. This is a concrete earlier Gospel scene to compare, without assuming every individual listener in the later controversy was one of the earlier delegates.
    John 1:19 And this is the testimony of John, when the Jews sent priests and Levites from Jerusalem to him, to ask him, “Who are you?”
    John 1:20 And he confessed and did not deny, and confessed, “I am not the Christ.”
    John 1:21 They asked him, “What, then? Are you Elijah?” And he said, “I am not.” “Are you the Prophet?” And he answered, “No.”
    John 1:22 So they said to him, “Who are you, so that we may give an answer to those who sent us? What do you say about yourself?”
    John 1:23 He said, “I am a voice of one crying in the wilderness, ‘Make straight the way of the Lord,’ just as Isaiah the prophet said.”

John 5:34
But I do not receive testimony from a person; rather, I say these things so that you may be saved.
  John 20:31 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both explain communication as directed toward the hearers'/readers' salvation or life. Jesus says these things so they may be saved; the Gospel's ending says the written signs aim at belief and life in His name. The latter does not repeat the disclaimer about receiving human testimony.
    John 20:31 but these have been written so that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and so that, believing, you may have life in His name.

John 5:35
He was the lamp that burned and shone, and you were willing to rejoice for an hour in his light.
  John 1:6–1:8 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both use light imagery for John's witness role. John5 calls him a burning, shining lamp; the prologue expressly says he was not the Light but witnessed to it. Lamp and the true Light are not made identical, nor is John identified as Jesus.
    John 1:6 There came a man sent from God; his name was John.
    John 1:7 He came as a witness, to bear witness concerning the Light, so that all might believe through him.
    John 1:8 He was not the Light, but came to bear witness concerning the Light.

John 5:36
But I have testimony greater than John’s: for the works the Father has given Me to complete, the very works I do, bear witness concerning Me that the Father has sent Me.
  John 10:25 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both expressly say Jesus' works bear witness concerning Him in relation to the Father. John5 compares that testimony with John's and speaks of completing given works; John10 says the works done in His Father's name testify. The additional qualifications remain distinct.
    John 10:25 Jesus answered them, “I told you, and you do not believe. The works I do in My Father’s name bear witness concerning Me.

John 5:37
And the Father who sent Me has Himself testified concerning Me. You have never heard His voice or seen His form,
  John 8:18 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both expressly attribute testimony concerning Jesus to the Father who sent Him. John5 additionally says the hearers have not heard His voice or seen His form; John8 does not repeat that accusation. No particular heavenly-voice event is supplied as an unmentioned identity for the testimony.
    John 8:18 I am the One who bears witness concerning Myself, and the Father who sent Me bears witness concerning Me.”

John 5:38
and you do not have His word remaining in you, because you do not believe the One He sent.
  John 8:37 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both describe a divine/Jesus word failing to remain or find room in hostile hearers. John5 connects this with not believing the sent One; John8 connects it with seeking to kill Jesus. The different word formulations and responses are not erased.
    John 8:37 I know that you are Abraham’s offspring. Yet you seek to kill Me, because My word has no room in you.

John 5:39
You search the Scriptures because you think that in them you have eternal life; and it is they that bear witness concerning Me.
  Luke 24:27 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both explicitly present the Scriptures as concerning Jesus. John speaks of searching them and their witness; Luke narrates Jesus explaining Moses and the prophets concerning Himself. Luke's different audience and resurrection setting do not reproduce the accusation against John's hearers.
    Luke 24:27 And beginning from Moses and from all the prophets, He interpreted to them in all the Scriptures the things concerning Himself.

John 5:40
Yet you are unwilling to come to Me so that you may have life.
  John 6:35 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both connect coming to Jesus with the life He provides. John5 rebukes unwillingness to come for life; John6 calls Him the bread of life and promises lasting provision to the one who comes/believes. Rejection and invitation remain different rhetorical functions.
    John 6:35 Jesus said to them, “I am the bread of life; the one who comes to Me will certainly never hunger, and the one who believes in Me will certainly never thirst.

John 5:41
I do not receive glory from people,
  John 7:18 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both distinguish Jesus' mission from self-directed glory seeking. John5 denies receiving glory from people; John7 contrasts a self-speaker's own glory with seeking the Sender's glory. The link does not turn these sayings into a denial that Jesus may rightly be honored.
    John 7:18 The one who speaks from himself seeks his own glory. But the one who seeks the glory of the one who sent him is true, and there is no unrighteousness in him.

John 5:42
but I know you, that you do not have the love of God within yourselves.
  John 2:24–2:25 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both portray Jesus as knowing people's inner state. John5 specifies their lack of God's love; John2 says He knew what was within a person and did not entrust Himself to the sign-believing group. The target does not independently name or diagnose every John5 listener.
    John 2:24 But Jesus Himself did not entrust Himself to them, because He knew everyone
    John 2:25 and because He had no need for anyone to testify concerning humanity, for He Himself knew what was within a person.

John 5:43
I have come in My Father’s name, and you do not receive Me. If another comes in his own name, you will receive him.
  John 1:11–1:12 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both describe Jesus' coming and failure to receive Him. John1 contrasts His own not receiving with others receiving and becoming God's children; John5 contrasts His Father's-name coming with hypothetical reception of someone in his own name. The hypothetical other is not assigned an unverified personal identity.
    John 1:11 He came to what was His own, and His own did not receive Him.
    John 1:12 But as many as received Him, He gave them authority to become children of God—to those who believe in His name,

John 5:44
How can you believe, receiving glory from one another, and not seeking the glory that comes from the only God?
  John 12:42–12:43 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both connect preference for human glory over God's glory with impaired reception of Jesus. John5 asks how such hearers can believe; John12 explicitly says some rulers believed but withheld confession because of that preference and fear. The difference between the question and the narrated hidden belief is preserved.
    John 12:42 Nevertheless, many even from the rulers believed in Him, but because of the Pharisees they did not confess Him, so that they might not be put out of the synagogue,
    John 12:43 for they loved the glory of people more than the glory of God.

John 5:45
Do not think that I will accuse you before the Father. There is one who accuses you: Moses, in whom you have set your hope.
  Deuteronomy 31:26–31:27 — scriptural background comparison: Deuteronomy places Moses' law book as a witness against the people and recalls their rebellion. This is concrete Mosaic accusation background for John's naming Moses as accuser. It does not narrate Moses speaking before the Father or turn the two scenes into one event.
    Deuteronomy 31:26 Take this book of the law, and lay it beside the ark of the covenant of the Lord your God; and it shall be there for a witness against you.
    Deuteronomy 31:27 For I know your provocation and your stiff neck; for while I yet live with you this day, you have been provoking God: how much more after my death!

John 5:46
For if you believed Moses, you would believe Me, for he wrote concerning Me.
  Acts 3:20–3:23 — scriptural background comparison: Acts explicitly places Jesus the appointed Christ beside Moses' promised prophet and the command to hear him. It provides a specific Mosaic-prophecy comparison for Jesus' saying that Moses wrote concerning Him. John5 does not name this as the only Mosaic text intended.
    Acts 3:20 so that times of refreshing may come from the presence of the Lord, and He may send the Christ appointed for you, Jesus,
    Acts 3:21 whom heaven must receive until the times of restoration of all things of which God spoke through the mouth of His holy prophets from long ago.
    Acts 3:22 Moses said, ‘The Lord your God will raise up for you a prophet like me from among your brothers. You shall listen to him in everything he says to you.
    Acts 3:23 Every person who does not listen to that prophet will be destroyed from among the people.’

John 5:47
But if you do not believe his writings, how will you believe My words?”
  Luke 16:29–16:31 — teaching or imagery comparison: Both sayings connect refusal of Moses' witness with failure to receive a further disclosure. John5 contrasts Moses' writings and Jesus' words; Luke's Abraham contrasts hearing Moses/Prophets with persuasion by someone risen. The parable's speaker and resurrection scenario remain distinct.
    Luke 16:29 But Abraham said, ‘They have Moses and the Prophets; let them hear them.’
    Luke 16:30 But he said, ‘No, father Abraham; but if someone goes to them from the dead, they will repent.’
    Luke 16:31 And he said to him, ‘If they do not hear Moses and the Prophets, neither will they be persuaded if someone rises from the dead.’”

John 6:1
After these things Jesus went away across the Sea of Galilee, the Sea of Tiberias.
  Mark 6:32 — narrative parallel: Both feeding accounts begin with a water journey and withdrawal. Mark explicitly names a private boat trip to a deserted place; John names the Sea of Galilee/Tiberias. Their location descriptions remain distinct.
    Mark 6:32 They went away privately in the boat to a deserted place.

John 6:2
A great crowd followed Him, because they were seeing the signs He did upon the sick.
  Matthew 14:13–14:14 — narrative parallel: Both describe crowds following Jesus in a healing context. John attributes following to previously seen signs; Matthew describes following on foot and Jesus subsequently healing their sick.
    Matthew 14:13 When Jesus heard this, He withdrew from there by boat to a deserted place privately. Hearing of it, the crowds followed Him on foot from the cities.
    Matthew 14:14 Coming out, He saw a great crowd, was moved with compassion for them, and healed their sick.

John 6:3
Jesus went up the mountain and sat there with His disciples.
  John 6:15 — internal setting callback: The later again-to-the-mountain notice recalls this initial mountain setting. Jesus first sits with disciples; later He withdraws alone because of the crowd's royal intentions. The notices do not independently attest each other.
    John 6:15 So Jesus, knowing that they were about to come and seize Him to make Him king, withdrew again to the mountain, Himself alone.

John 6:4
Now the Passover, the feast of the Jews, was near.
  John 2:13 — same-book feast notice: Both explicitly mark the Jewish Passover's nearness. John 2 introduces a Jerusalem journey; John 6 places the notice in the Galilean feeding setting. No identical year or journey is inferred.
    John 2:13 Now the Passover of the Jews was near, and Jesus went up to Jerusalem.

John 6:5
So Jesus lifted His eyes and, seeing that a great crowd was coming toward Him, said to Philip, “Where are we to buy loaves so that these people may eat?”
  Mark 6:37 — narrative parallel: Both feeding scenes discuss buying bread for the crowd. John has Jesus question Philip; Mark has the disciples question buying after Jesus tells them to provide food. Speakers and sequence differ.
    Mark 6:37 He answered them, “You give them something to eat.” They said to Him, “Should we go and buy two hundred denarii worth of bread and give them something to eat?”

John 6:6
He said this to test him, for He Himself knew what He was about to do.
  Deuteronomy 8:2–8:3 — possible narrative allusion: Both provision settings include testing. Deuteronomy explains Israel's wilderness testing, hunger and manna; John explains Jesus' question testing Philip before the feeding. This compares the pattern, not identical tests or an independently attested motive.
    Deuteronomy 8:2 And you shall remember all the way wherein the Lord your God led you in the wilderness, to afflict you and to test you, that the things in your heart might be known, whether you would keep His commandments or not.
    Deuteronomy 8:3 And He afflicted you, and made you hunger, and fed you with manna, which your fathers did not know, that He might declare unto you that man shall not live by bread alone, but man shall live by every word that proceeds through the mouth of God.

John 6:7
Philip answered Him, “Two hundred denarii worth of loaves are not enough for them, so that each may receive even a little.”
  Mark 6:37 — narrative parallel: Both mention two hundred denarii for bread. Philip calls it insufficient for even small portions; Mark's disciples ask whether to buy that amount. Their questions and judgments differ.
    Mark 6:37 He answered them, “You give them something to eat.” They said to Him, “Should we go and buy two hundred denarii worth of bread and give them something to eat?”

John 6:8
One of His disciples, Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, said to Him,
  John 1:40 — same-book identity link: Both identify Andrew as Simon Peter's brother and Jesus' disciple. John 1 specifies his earlier hearing from the Baptist; John 6 introduces his contribution to the feeding discussion.
    John 1:40 Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, was one of the two who heard from John and followed Him.

John 6:9
“There is a boy here who has five barley loaves and two small fish. But what are these among so many?”
  Mark 6:38 — narrative parallel: Both feeding accounts specify five loaves and two fish. John additionally names a boy, barley and small fish and questions sufficiency; Mark reports the disciples' inventory after Jesus asks.
    Mark 6:38 He said to them, “How many loaves do you have? Go and see.” When they found out, they said, “Five, and two fish.”
  2 Kings 4:42–4:44 — narrative comparison: Both provision stories involve barley loaves, a question about feeding many, and ensuing abundance. Elisha's story uses twenty loaves for a hundred; John uses five with fish for thousands. The quantities, people and provisions differ.
    2 Kings 4:42 A man passed through from Baithsarisa and brought the man of God twenty barley loaves of firstfruits and cakes of dried fruit. He said, ‘Give them to the people, and let them eat.’
    2 Kings 4:43 His minister said, ‘How can I put this before a hundred men?’ He said, ‘Give it to the people, and let them eat, for thus says the Lord: They will eat and leave some over.’
    2 Kings 4:44 They ate and left some over, according to the word of the Lord.

John 6:10
Jesus said, “Have the people recline.” Now there was much grass in the place. So the men reclined, about five thousand in number.
  Luke 9:14–9:15 — narrative parallel: Both arrange about five thousand men to recline. Luke names groups of about fifty and narrated compliance; John names much grass. The organization details are not silently transferred.
    Luke 9:14 For there were about five thousand men. He said to His disciples, “Have them recline in groups of about fifty each.”
    Luke 9:15 They did so and had everyone recline.

John 6:11
Then Jesus took the loaves, gave thanks, and distributed them to those reclining, and likewise from the fish, as much as they wanted.
  Mark 6:41 — narrative parallel: Both describe taking loaves, blessing or thanksgiving, and distributing bread and fish. Mark specifies breaking and giving through disciples; John describes Jesus distributing as much as wanted, without that mediation clause here.
    Mark 6:41 He took the five loaves and the two fish, looked up to heaven, pronounced a blessing, and broke the loaves. He kept giving them to His disciples to set before the people, and He divided the two fish among them all.

John 6:12
When they were filled, He said to His disciples, “Gather the remaining fragments, so that nothing may be lost.”
  Mark 6:42–6:43 — narrative parallel: Both narrate satisfied eaters and gathered leftovers. John quotes Jesus' instruction that nothing be lost; Mark records the collection without independently stating that command.
    Mark 6:42 They all ate and were satisfied.
