    Mark 6:43 They took up broken pieces, twelve baskets full, and some of the fish.

John 6:13
So they gathered them and filled twelve baskets with fragments from the five barley loaves that remained over from those who had eaten.
  Mark 6:43 — narrative parallel: Both report twelve baskets of fragments. John identifies the five barley loaves; Mark additionally mentions fish among the leftovers. Their descriptions remain intact.
    Mark 6:43 They took up broken pieces, twelve baskets full, and some of the fish.

John 6:14
When the people saw the sign He did, they said, “This is truly the Prophet who is coming into the world.”
  Deuteronomy 18:15–18:18 — scriptural expectation background: The crowd's identification of the Prophet is compared with the promised prophet like Moses from Israel's brethren. Deuteronomy supplies expectation background, not a judgment on every political assumption of this crowd.
    Deuteronomy 18:15 The Lord your God shall raise up unto you a prophet from among your brethren, like unto me; you shall hearken unto him,
    Deuteronomy 18:16 according to all that you asked of the Lord your God in Horeb, in the day of the assembly, saying: We will no more hear the voice of the Lord our God, neither will we see this great fire any more, lest we die.
    Deuteronomy 18:17 And the Lord said unto me: All that they have spoken is right.
    Deuteronomy 18:18 I will raise up for them a prophet from among their brethren, like unto you, and will put My word in his mouth; and he shall speak unto them all that I command him.

John 6:15
So Jesus, knowing that they were about to come and seize Him to make Him king, withdrew again to the mountain, Himself alone.
  Matthew 14:22–14:23 — narrative parallel: Both place Jesus alone on a mountain after feeding. John specifies avoiding forcible kingship; Matthew describes dismissing the crowd and praying. No prayer or royal attempt is inserted into the other account.
    Matthew 14:22 Immediately He compelled the disciples to enter a boat and go ahead of Him to the other side, while He dismissed the crowds.
    Matthew 14:23 After dismissing the crowds, He went up the mountain privately to pray. When evening came, He was there alone.

John 6:16
When evening came, His disciples went down to the sea.
  Matthew 14:22 — narrative parallel: Both move the disciples toward their post-feeding water journey. John describes evening descent to the sea; Matthew describes Jesus sending them ahead in a boat. The initiative and timing notices differ.
    Matthew 14:22 Immediately He compelled the disciples to enter a boat and go ahead of Him to the other side, while He dismissed the crowds.

John 6:17
They entered a boat and were going across the sea toward Capernaum. Darkness had already come, and Jesus had not yet come to them,
  Mark 6:45–6:47 — narrative comparison: Both separate the disciples' boat journey from Jesus on land as evening comes. John names Capernaum and darkness; Mark names Bethsaida and Jesus' mountain prayer. No reconstructed route erases the destination difference.
    Mark 6:45 Immediately He compelled His disciples to board the boat and go ahead to the other side, toward Bethsaida, while He Himself dismissed the crowd.
    Mark 6:46 After saying farewell to them, He went away to the mountain to pray.
    Mark 6:47 When evening came, the boat was in the middle of the sea, and He was alone on the land.

John 6:18
and the sea was being stirred up by a strong wind blowing.
  Matthew 14:24 — narrative parallel: Both describe wind disturbing the disciples' boat journey. John says the strong wind stirs the sea; Matthew describes opposing wind and waves battering the boat.
    Matthew 14:24 The boat was already many stadia from land, battered by the waves, for the wind was against them.

John 6:19
So when they had rowed about twenty-five or thirty stadia, they saw Jesus walking upon the sea and drawing near the boat, and they were afraid.
  Mark 6:48–6:50 — narrative parallel: Both describe rowing difficulties, seeing Jesus walking on the sea and fear. John measures twenty-five or thirty stadia; Mark specifies the fourth watch and His intention to pass them. Details are not merged.
    Mark 6:48 Seeing them straining at the rowing, for the wind was against them, He came toward them about the fourth watch of the night, walking on the sea. He intended to pass by them.
    Mark 6:49 When they saw Him walking on the sea, they thought it was a phantom and cried out,
    Mark 6:50 for they all saw Him and were troubled. Immediately He spoke with them and said to them, “Take courage! It is I; do not fear.”

John 6:20
But He said to them, “It is I; do not fear.”
  Mark 6:50 — teaching parallel: Both give Jesus' It is I and do not fear reassurance during the sea encounter. Mark additionally says take courage.
    Mark 6:50 for they all saw Him and were troubled. Immediately He spoke with them and said to them, “Take courage! It is I; do not fear.”

John 6:21
Then they were willing to receive Him into the boat, and at once the boat was at the land to which they were going.
  Matthew 14:32–14:33 — narrative comparison: Both resolve the sea encounter. John records willingness to receive Jesus and immediate arrival; Matthew explicitly narrates boat entry, calming wind and worship. Those actions are not independently asserted by both units.
    Matthew 14:32 When they climbed into the boat, the wind ceased.
    Matthew 14:33 Those in the boat worshiped Him, saying, “Truly Thou art God’s Son.”

John 6:22
The next day, the crowd standing across the sea saw that there had been no other small boat there except one, and that Jesus had not entered the boat with His disciples, but His disciples had gone away alone.
  John 6:16–6:17 — narrative parallel: The earlier departure narrates the disciples entering a boat and setting out while Jesus had not yet come to them. The crowd's later observation looks back to that departure without Him. This internal back-reference preserves the crowd's additional single-boat observation and does not add an actual boarding statement to6:21's willingness to receive Jesus.
    John 6:16 When evening came, His disciples went down to the sea.
    John 6:17 They entered a boat and were going across the sea toward Capernaum. Darkness had already come, and Jesus had not yet come to them,

John 6:23
Other small boats came from Tiberias near the place where they ate the bread after the Lord had given thanks.
  John 6:11 — internal event reference: The place notice explicitly recalls eating bread after the Lord's thanksgiving, narrated earlier with distribution. It identifies that feeding, not independent evidence of the arriving Tiberias boats or every observer's knowledge.
    John 6:11 Then Jesus took the loaves, gave thanks, and distributed them to those reclining, and likewise from the fish, as much as they wanted.

John 6:24
So when the crowd saw that Jesus was not there, nor His disciples, they themselves entered the small boats and came to Capernaum, seeking Jesus.
  John 6:26 — narrative parallel: Jesus explicitly interprets this crowd's seeking as motivated by having eaten the loaves and been filled. This links the narrated search with His later assessment, rather than a generic travel comparison. The motive is attributed to Jesus' reported words and is not independently inferred for every person or for another crowd.
    John 6:26 Jesus answered them, “Amen, amen, I tell you: you seek Me not because you saw signs, but because you ate from the loaves and were filled.

John 6:25
Finding Him across the sea, they said to Him, “Rabbi, when didst Thou come here?”
  John 6:16–6:21 — narrative parallel: The crowd asks when Jesus arrived, whereas the reader has already received the evening departure, encounter upon the sea and the boat's immediate arrival narrative. This is a specific contrast between narrated travel knowledge and the crowd's question, not Jesus' spoken travel explanation or a newly calculated clock time.
    John 6:16 When evening came, His disciples went down to the sea.
    John 6:17 They entered a boat and were going across the sea toward Capernaum. Darkness had already come, and Jesus had not yet come to them,
    John 6:18 and the sea was being stirred up by a strong wind blowing.
    John 6:19 So when they had rowed about twenty-five or thirty stadia, they saw Jesus walking upon the sea and drawing near the boat, and they were afraid.
    John 6:20 But He said to them, “It is I; do not fear.”
    John 6:21 Then they were willing to receive Him into the boat, and at once the boat was at the land to which they were going.

John 6:26
Jesus answered them, “Amen, amen, I tell you: you seek Me not because you saw signs, but because you ate from the loaves and were filled.
  John 6:11–6:12 — internal event interpretation: Jesus refers to the crowd's eating and satisfaction, narrated in the feeding account. His present explanation of their motive is additional; the earlier narrative does not itself ascribe that motive.
    John 6:11 Then Jesus took the loaves, gave thanks, and distributed them to those reclining, and likewise from the fish, as much as they wanted.
    John 6:12 When they were filled, He said to His disciples, “Gather the remaining fragments, so that nothing may be lost.”

John 6:27
Do not work for the food that perishes, but for the food that remains for eternal life, which the Son of Man will give you; for upon Him the Father, God, has set His seal.”
  John 4:13–4:14 — same-book teaching comparison: Both contrast ordinary provision with Jesus' enduring life-giving gift. John 4 contrasts renewed thirst with eternal-life water; John 6 contrasts perishing food with eternal-life food. The Father's seal is additional here.
    John 4:13 Jesus answered her, “Everyone who drinks from this water will thirst again.
    John 4:14 But whoever drinks from the water that I will give him will certainly never thirst; rather, the water I will give him will become in him a spring of water leaping up into eternal life.”

John 6:28
So they said to Him, “What are we to do, so that we may work the works of God?”
  Acts 16:30–16:31 — teaching-question comparison: Both ask what response is required and receive a faith-centered answer in context. John asks about God's works; the jailer asks about salvation and is told to believe Jesus. The questions and household promise differ.
    Acts 16:30 He brought them outside and said, “Sirs, what must I do to be saved?”
    Acts 16:31 They said, “Believe in the Lord Jesus, and you will be saved, you and your household.”

John 6:29
Jesus answered them, “This is the work of God: that you believe in the One He sent.”
  Acts 16:31 — teaching parallel: Both direct belief toward Jesus. John calls believing the One God sent God's work; Acts names the Lord Jesus and promises salvation with the household. Their formulations and audiences differ.
    Acts 16:31 They said, “Believe in the Lord Jesus, and you will be saved, you and your household.”

John 6:30
So they said to Him, “What sign, then, dost Thou perform, so that we may see and believe Thee? What work dost Thou do?
  John 2:18 — same-book sign-request comparison: Both ask Jesus what sign He performs or shows. John 6 seeks a basis for belief after bread teaching; John 2 challenges His temple action. The requests do not imply identical occasions or motives.
    John 2:18 So the Jews answered Him, “What sign dost Thou show us, since Thou doest these things?”

John 6:31
Our fathers ate the manna in the wilderness, just as it is written, ‘He gave them bread from heaven to eat.’”
  Psalms 77:24 — quotation: The bread-from-heaven citation corresponds to the psalm's manna and heavenly bread given to the people. John introduces the ancestral wilderness meal; the psalm narrates God's provision in its wider history.
    Psalms 77:24 and rained manna for them to eat, and gave them heaven’s bread.

John 6:32
So Jesus said to them, “Amen, amen, I tell you: Moses has not given you the bread from heaven, but My Father gives you the true bread from heaven.
  Exodus 16:4 — allusion: Exodus identifies the Lord as promising bread from heaven while instructing Moses. John contrasts Moses with the Father's giving of true heavenly bread. Exodus does not itself identify that bread as Jesus.
    Exodus 16:4 The Lord said to Moses, “Behold, I am raining bread from heaven for you. The people shall go out and gather each day’s portion, so that I may test whether they will walk in My law or not.

John 6:33
For the bread of God is the One who comes down from heaven and gives life to the world.”
  John 3:13–3:17 — same-book teaching parallel: Both join heavenly descent with life and salvation for the world. John 3 names the Son of Man and God's given Son; John 6 calls the descending One God's bread. The images and surrounding comparisons differ.
    John 3:13 And no one has ascended into heaven except the One who descended from heaven, the Son of Man.
    John 3:14 And just as Moses lifted up the snake in the wilderness, so the Son of Man must be lifted up,
    John 3:15 so that everyone who believes may have eternal life in Him.
    John 3:16 For God loved the world in this way: He gave the only-begotten Son, so that everyone who believes in Him might not perish but have eternal life.
    John 3:17 For God did not send the Son into the world to judge the world, but so that the world might be saved through Him.

John 6:34
So they said to Him, “Lord, always give us this bread.”
  John 4:15 — same-book response comparison: Both hearers ask Jesus for His offered provision. The crowd asks for bread always; the woman asks for water to avoid thirst and drawing. The gifts, hearers and responses are distinct.
    John 4:15 The woman said to Him, “Sir, give me this water, so that I may not thirst or come here to draw.”

John 6:35
Jesus said to them, “I am the bread of life; the one who comes to Me will certainly never hunger, and the one who believes in Me will certainly never thirst.
  John 4:13–4:14 — same-book teaching parallel: Both promise life-giving provision that removes thirst. John 6 also joins bread, hunger, coming and belief; John 4 uses an eternal-life spring. Neither passage is rewritten as ordinary bodily immunity.
    John 4:13 Jesus answered her, “Everyone who drinks from this water will thirst again.
    John 4:14 But whoever drinks from the water that I will give him will certainly never thirst; rather, the water I will give him will become in him a spring of water leaping up into eternal life.”

John 6:36
But I told you that you have both seen Me and do not believe.
  John 12:37 — same-book response parallel: Both contrast exposure to Jesus or His signs with unbelief. Here Jesus addresses the bread-discourse hearers; John 12 gives a later narrative summary. The groups are not automatically identical.
    John 12:37 Though He had done so many signs before them, they did not believe in Him,

John 6:37
Everything the Father gives Me will come to Me, and the one who comes to Me I will certainly not cast out,
  John 10:27–10:29 — same-book teaching parallel: Both connect the Father's gift with Jesus' protection of His own. John 6 speaks of coming and not being cast out; John 10 uses sheep, eternal life and hands from which none snatches.
    John 10:27 My sheep hear My voice, and I know them, and they follow Me.
    John 10:28 And I give them eternal life, and they will certainly never perish, and no one will snatch them from My hand.
    John 10:29 What My Father has given Me is greater than all, and no one can snatch from the Father’s hand.

John 6:38
because I have come down from heaven not to do My own will, but the will of the One who sent Me.
  John 5:30 — same-book teaching parallel: Both subordinate Jesus' own will to the will of His Sender. John 6 adds heavenly descent; John 5 places the statement in His explanation of hearing and judging.
    John 5:30 I can do nothing from Myself. As I hear, I judge, and My judgment is righteous, because I do not seek My own will, but the will of the One who sent Me.

John 6:39
And this is the will of the One who sent Me: that of everything He has given Me I should lose nothing, but raise it up on the last day.
  John 17:12 — same-book teaching comparison: Both address preservation of Jesus' followers from loss. John 6 states the Father's will for those given and last-day raising; John 17 describes guarding them in the Father's given name and explicitly excepts the son of destruction. The exception is not erased or explained away.
    John 17:12 When I was with them, I kept them in Thy name, which Thou hast given Me, and guarded them; and not one of them perished except the son of destruction, so that the Scripture might be fulfilled.

John 6:40
For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”
  John 11:25–11:26 — same-book teaching parallel: Both connect believing Jesus with eternal life and resurrection. John 6 names the Father's will and last day; John 11 names Jesus as resurrection and life and addresses living despite death. Settings and formulations differ.
    John 11:25 Jesus said to her, “I am the resurrection and the life; the one who believes in Me will live, even if he dies,
    John 11:26 and everyone who lives and believes in Me will certainly never die. Do you believe this?”

John 6:41
So the Jews grumbled concerning Him, because He said, “I am the bread that came down from heaven.”
  Exodus 16:2–16:3 — possible narrative allusion: Both bread-related settings include grumbling. Exodus' people complain to Moses and Aaron over food; John's hearers grumble over Jesus' heavenly-bread claim. The motives, people and claims are not identical.
    Exodus 16:2 The whole congregation of the sons of Israel murmured against Moses and Aaron.
    Exodus 16:3 The sons of Israel said to them, “If only we had died, struck by the Lord in the land of Egypt, when we sat by the meat pots and ate our fill of bread! You have brought us into this wilderness to kill this whole congregation with hunger.”

John 6:42
And they said, “Is this not Jesus, the son of Joseph, whose father and mother we know? How does He now say, ‘I have come down from heaven’?”
  Luke 4:22 — narrative comparison: Both audiences invoke Jesus' being Joseph's son. Luke places it amid hometown wonder; John adds known parents and objection to heavenly descent. These are reported reactions, not independent biological-paternity conclusions.
    Luke 4:22 All bore witness to Him and wondered at the words of grace coming from His mouth. They said, “Is this not Joseph’s son?”

John 6:43
Jesus answered them, “Do not grumble among yourselves.
  Philippians 2:14 — teaching parallel: Both discourage grumbling. Jesus addresses this bread-discourse complaint; Paul instructs community conduct without grumbling or disputes. The audiences and immediate reasons differ.
    Philippians 2:14 Do all things without grumbling and disputing,

John 6:44
No one can come to Me unless the Father who sent Me draws him, and I will raise him up on the last day.
  John 6:65 — internal teaching recurrence: The later saying explicitly recalls the necessity of the Father's enabling approach to Jesus. Here the verb is draws and resurrection is added; later the expression is given from the Father.
    John 6:65 And He said, “For this reason I have told you that no one can come to Me unless it has been given to him from the Father.”

John 6:45
It is written in the Prophets, ‘And they shall all be taught by God.’ Everyone who has heard from the Father and learned comes to Me.
  Isaiah 54:13 — quotation: The God-taught saying corresponds to Isaiah's promise concerning taught children. John applies hearing and learning from the Father to coming to Jesus; Isaiah's addressed children and peace language remain its own wording.
    Isaiah 54:13 and all your sons taught by God, and your children in great peace.

John 6:46
Not that anyone has seen the Father except the One who is from God; He has seen the Father.
  John 1:18 — same-book teaching parallel: Both distinguish unique divine sight or revelation from general human sight. John 6 says the One from God has seen the Father; the prologue names the only-begotten God making Him known. Titles and expressions are preserved.
    John 1:18 No one has ever seen God; the only-begotten God, who is in the bosom of the Father, He has made Him known.

John 6:47
Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who believes has eternal life.
  John 3:36 — same-book teaching parallel: Both say belief is joined with eternal life. John 3 explicitly names the Son and adds disobedience and remaining wrath; the shorter John 6 assurance does not repeat those clauses.
    John 3:36 The one who believes in the Son has eternal life; but the one who disobeys the Son will not see life, but the wrath of God remains upon him.

John 6:48
I am the bread of life.
  John 6:35 — internal teaching recurrence: Jesus repeats the explicit I am the bread of life identification. The earlier saying continues with coming, believing and absence of hunger and thirst; those additions are not inserted into this shorter unit.
    John 6:35 Jesus said to them, “I am the bread of life; the one who comes to Me will certainly never hunger, and the one who believes in Me will certainly never thirst.

John 6:49
Your fathers ate the manna in the wilderness and died.
  John 6:58 — internal teaching recurrence: Both recall ancestors eating and nevertheless dying. The later conclusion repeats that contrast against the bread giving enduring life; this is a repeated teaching, not independent historical attestation.
    John 6:58 This is the bread that came down from heaven, not as the fathers ate and died. The one who eats this bread will live forever.”

John 6:50
This is the bread that comes down from heaven, so that someone may eat from it and not die.
  John 6:51 — internal teaching recurrence: Both describe descending heavenly bread, eating and life beyond death. The next unit identifies Jesus as the living bread and adds His flesh for the world. The added explanation is not silently inserted here.
    John 6:51 I am the living bread that came down from heaven. If anyone eats from this bread, he will live forever. And the bread that I will give is My flesh, for the life of the world.”

John 6:51
I am the living bread that came down from heaven. If anyone eats from this bread, he will live forever. And the bread that I will give is My flesh, for the life of the world.”
  Luke 22:19 — body-and-bread comparison: Both connect given bread with Jesus' body or flesh for others. Luke gives the supper's body-given-for-you and remembrance words; John gives flesh for the world's life. Distinct scenes and terms do not settle every ritual interpretation.
    Luke 22:19 And He took bread, and having given thanks, He broke it and gave it to them, saying, “This is My body, which is given for you; do this in remembrance of Me.”

John 6:52
So the Jews disputed with one another, saying, “How can this man give us His flesh to eat?”
  John 3:4 — same-book response comparison: Both questioners ask how Jesus' life-giving teaching can be physically understood. Nicodemus asks about returning to a womb; this group asks about flesh to eat. Their images, identities and subsequent explanations remain distinct.
    John 3:4 Nicodemus said to Him, “How can a person be born when he is old? Can he enter his mother’s womb a second time and be born?”

John 6:53
So Jesus said to them, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless you eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink His blood, you have no life in yourselves.
  Luke 22:19–22:20 — body-and-blood comparison: Both teachings connect Jesus' body or flesh and blood with consumed provision. Luke narrates supper bread and covenant cup; John states the need to eat and drink for life. The wording and setting are not made identical.
    Luke 22:19 And He took bread, and having given thanks, He broke it and gave it to them, saying, “This is My body, which is given for you; do this in remembrance of Me.”
    Luke 22:20 And likewise the cup after supper, saying, “This cup is the new covenant in My blood, poured out for you.

John 6:54
The one who eats My flesh and drinks My blood has eternal life, and I will raise him up on the last day.
  John 6:40 — internal teaching parallel: Both promise eternal life and raising on the last day. Here the terms are eating flesh and drinking blood; earlier they are beholding the Son and believing. The stated actions are preserved rather than automatically substituted.
    John 6:40 For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”

John 6:55
For My flesh is true food, and My blood is true drink.
  1 Corinthians 10:16 — body-and-blood comparison: Both relate Christ's body or flesh and blood to shared food and drink. Paul names bread and the blessing cup as participation; John calls flesh true food and blood true drink. Neither account supplies all the other's expressions.
    1 Corinthians 10:16 The cup of blessing that we bless—is it not participation in the blood of Christ? The bread that we break—is it not participation in the body of Christ?

John 6:56
The one who eats My flesh and drinks My blood remains in Me, and I in him.
  John 15:4–15:5 — same-book teaching parallel: Both describe mutual remaining in Jesus and His followers. John 6 connects it with flesh and blood; John 15 uses vine, branches and fruit. The images are not turned into identical physical descriptions.
    John 15:4 Remain in Me, and I in you. Just as the branch cannot bear fruit from itself unless it remains in the vine, neither can you unless you remain in Me.
    John 15:5 I am the vine; you are the branches. The one who remains in Me, and I in him, bears much fruit, because apart from Me you can do nothing.

John 6:57
Just as the living Father sent Me and I live because of the Father, so the one who eats Me will also live because of Me.
  John 5:26 — same-book teaching parallel: Both describe life in the Father-Son relation. John 5 names the Father's gift of life-in-Himself to the Son; John 6 names living because of the living Sender and adds the eater living because of Jesus.
    John 5:26 For just as the Father has life in Himself, so He also gave the Son to have life in Himself.

John 6:58
This is the bread that came down from heaven, not as the fathers ate and died. The one who eats this bread will live forever.”
  John 6:49–6:51 — internal teaching summary: This conclusion reprises the contrast between ancestors who ate and died and Jesus' heavenly bread giving enduring life. The earlier units contain that contrast and identify His flesh, without independently attesting another discourse.
    John 6:49 Your fathers ate the manna in the wilderness and died.
    John 6:50 This is the bread that comes down from heaven, so that someone may eat from it and not die.
    John 6:51 I am the living bread that came down from heaven. If anyone eats from this bread, he will live forever. And the bread that I will give is My flesh, for the life of the world.”

John 6:59
He said these things while teaching in a synagogue in Capernaum.
  Mark 1:21 — teaching-setting comparison: Both place Jesus teaching in a Capernaum synagogue. Mark explicitly names a Sabbath entry; John identifies the bread discourse's venue without that Sabbath notice. The occasions are not identified.
    Mark 1:21 They entered Capernaum, and immediately on the Sabbath He went into the synagogue and taught.

John 6:60
So many of His disciples, when they heard, said, “This saying is hard. Who can hear it?”
  John 8:43 — same-book hearing comparison: Both address difficulty hearing Jesus' teaching. Here disciples call the saying hard; later Jesus challenges opponents' inability to hear His word. Speakers, audiences and explanations are not made identical.
    John 8:43 Why do you not understand My speech? Because you cannot hear My word.

John 6:61
But Jesus, knowing within Himself that His disciples were grumbling about this, said to them, “Does this cause you to stumble?
  John 2:24–2:25 — same-book knowledge comparison: Both portray Jesus knowing people beyond a supplied human report. John 6 describes His inward awareness of disciples' grumbling; John 2 explains His knowledge of people. No mechanism or universal judgment on all followers is inferred.
    John 2:24 But Jesus Himself did not entrust Himself to them, because He knew everyone
    John 2:25 and because He had no need for anyone to testify concerning humanity, for He Himself knew what was within a person.

John 6:62
Then what if you see the Son of Man ascending where He was before?
  John 3:13 — same-book teaching parallel: Both connect the Son of Man with ascent and prior heavenly origin. John 3 explicitly names heaven and descent; John 6 asks about ascending where He was before. No extra elapsed timeline is supplied.
    John 3:13 And no one has ascended into heaven except the One who descended from heaven, the Son of Man.

John 6:63
The Spirit is the One who gives life; the flesh benefits nothing. The words I have spoken to you are spirit and are life.
  2 Corinthians 3:6 — teaching parallel: Both explicitly say the Spirit gives life. Paul contrasts Spirit with the killing letter; John contrasts Spirit with unprofitable flesh and calls Jesus' words spirit and life. Their distinct contrasts are preserved.
    2 Corinthians 3:6 who also made us sufficient as ministers of a new covenant, not of letter but of Spirit; for the letter kills, but the Spirit gives life.

John 6:64
But there are some among you who do not believe.” For Jesus knew from the beginning who those were who did not believe and who it was who would hand Him over.
  John 13:11 — same-book narrative parallel: Both narrators explicitly describe Jesus knowing His betrayer. John 6 additionally names prior knowledge of unbelievers; John 13 explains not all are clean in the footwashing scene. The descriptions are not assigned indiscriminately to every follower.
    John 13:11 For He knew the one who was handing Him over; for this reason He said, “Not all of you are clean.”

John 6:65
And He said, “For this reason I have told you that no one can come to Me unless it has been given to him from the Father.”
  John 6:44 — internal teaching callback: Jesus' for-this-reason explanation recalls the earlier no-one-can-come saying. Here coming must be given from the Father; earlier the Father draws and Jesus promises resurrection. The different verbs remain visible.
    John 6:44 No one can come to Me unless the Father who sent Me draws him, and I will raise him up on the last day.

John 6:66
From this, many of His disciples went back and no longer walked with Him.
  John 8:31 — same-book discipleship contrast: John 6 narrates disciples ceasing to walk with Jesus after the discourse; John 8 defines true discipleship through remaining in His word. The comparison does not supply an unrecorded verdict or motive for every departing person.
    John 8:31 So Jesus said to the Jews who had believed Him, “If you remain in My word, you are truly My disciples,

John 6:67
So Jesus said to the Twelve, “Do you also want to go away?”
  John 6:68–6:71 — narrative parallel: Peter's reply to the question about leaving names Jesus' words of eternal life and the group's confession; Jesus then qualifies the Twelve's position by speaking of one devil, identified as Judas. This explains the narrative response without asserting every individual had Peter's faith or predicting that no disciple would depart.
    John 6:68 Simon Peter answered Him, “Lord, to whom shall we go? Thou hast words of eternal life.
    John 6:69 And we have believed and have known that Thou art the Holy One of God.”
    John 6:70 Jesus answered them, “Did I not choose you, the Twelve? Yet one of you is a devil.”
    John 6:71 He was speaking of Judas, son of Simon Iscariot, for this man was about to hand Him over, one of the Twelve.

John 6:68
Simon Peter answered Him, “Lord, to whom shall we go? Thou hast words of eternal life.
  John 12:49–12:50 — same-book word-and-life comparison: Peter credits Jesus with words of eternal life; Jesus later explains His Father-given speech and calls the Father's command eternal life. The confessing disciple and Jesus' explanation are different voices.
    John 12:49 Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.
    John 12:50 And I know that His command is eternal life. So the things I speak, just as the Father has told Me, thus I speak.”

John 6:69
And we have believed and have known that Thou art the Holy One of God.”
  Mark 1:24 — identity-title comparison: Both identify Jesus as God's Holy One. Peter's collective confession of belief differs from the unclean spirit's recognition and confrontation. The shared title does not equate the speakers' faith or occasions.
    Mark 1:24 “What have we to do with Thee, Jesus of Nazareth? Hast Thou come to destroy us? I know who Thou art—the Holy One of God!”

John 6:70
Jesus answered them, “Did I not choose you, the Twelve? Yet one of you is a devil.”
  John 13:21 — same-book betrayal anticipation: Both identify danger from one within Jesus' group. Here one of the chosen Twelve is called a devil, with the next unit identifying Judas; later Jesus says one will betray Him. The sayings and occasions differ.
    John 13:21 Having said these things, Jesus was troubled in spirit and testified, saying, “Amen, amen, I tell you that one of you will hand Me over.”

John 6:71
He was speaking of Judas, son of Simon Iscariot, for this man was about to hand Him over, one of the Twelve.
  John 13:26–13:27 — same-book identity callback: Both identify Judas son of Simon Iscariot in the betrayal narrative. John 6 anticipates his handing Jesus over; John 13 narrates the morsel and Satan's entry. The later actions are not inserted into the earlier notice.
    John 13:26 Jesus answered, “It is the one for whom I will dip the morsel and give it to him.” So He dipped the morsel, took it, and gave it to Judas, son of Simon Iscariot.
    John 13:27 And after the morsel, then Satan entered him. So Jesus said to him, “What you do, do more quickly.”

John 7:1
After these things Jesus walked about in Galilee, for He did not want to walk about in Judea, because the Jews were seeking to kill Him.
  John 5:18 — same-book hostility link: Both narrate efforts to kill Jesus. John 5 gives the opponents' Sabbath and Father-language concerns; John 7 explains His avoiding Judea. The notices concern the narrated opponents, not every Jewish person.
    John 5:18 For this reason, therefore, the Jews sought all the more to kill Him, because He not only broke the Sabbath, but also called God His own Father, making Himself equal to God.

John 7:2
Now the feast of the Jews, the feast of Booths, was near.
  Leviticus 23:34–23:36 — festival background: John names the approaching Booths festival; Leviticus prescribes its days and holy gatherings. The law supplies festival background, not this visit's date or an unstated water ceremony.
    Leviticus 23:34 Speak to the sons of Israel, saying: On the fifteenth day of this seventh month is the Feast of Tents unto the Lord for seven days.
    Leviticus 23:35 And on the first day there shall be a holy convocation. You shall do no laborious work.
    Leviticus 23:36 For seven days you shall bring whole burnt offerings unto the Lord. The eighth day shall be a holy convocation for you, and you shall bring whole burnt offerings unto the Lord. It is a closing observance; you shall do no laborious work.

John 7:3
So His brothers said to Him, “Leave here and go into Judea, so that Thy disciples also may see the works Thou doest.
  John 11:7–11:16 — narrative parallel: Both episodes discuss going into Judea in relation to Jesus' work and the disciples. Here His brothers urge the trip so disciples see His works; later Jesus proposes going and speaks of the disciples' believing in the Lazarus setting. Speakers, purpose and danger differ; neither is made the same journey or a proof of the brothers' private motives.
    John 11:7 After this He said to the disciples, “Let us go into Judea again.”
    John 11:8 The disciples said to Him, “Rabbi, the Jews were just now seeking to stone Thee, and art Thou going there again?”
    John 11:9 Jesus answered, “Are there not twelve hours of the day? If anyone walks in the day, he does not stumble, because he sees the light of this world.
    John 11:10 But if anyone walks in the night, he stumbles, because the light is not in him.”
    John 11:11 He said these things, and after this He said to them, “Lazarus our friend has fallen asleep, but I am going to awaken him.”
    John 11:12 So the disciples said to Him, “Lord, if he has fallen asleep, he will be saved.”
    John 11:13 But Jesus had spoken of his death, while they thought He was speaking of resting in sleep.
    John 11:14 So then Jesus told them openly, “Lazarus died,
    John 11:15 and for your sake I rejoice that I was not there, so that you may believe. But let us go to him.”
    John 11:16 Then Thomas, who was called Didymus, said to his fellow disciples, “Let us also go, so that we may die with Him.”

John 7:4
For no one acts in secret while seeking to be known publicly. If Thou doest these things, reveal Thyself to the world.”
  John 7:10 — internal contrast: The brothers argue against acting secretly while seeking publicity; Jesus later goes to the feast not openly but as in secret. This contrasts the stated proposal and narrated manner of travel, not independent events.
    John 7:10 But when His brothers had gone up to the feast, then He also went up, not openly, but as in secret.

John 7:5
For not even His brothers believed in Him.
  Acts 1:14 — family-response comparison: John reports His brothers' unbelief at this stage; Acts later includes His brothers in the praying company after the ascent. The passages do not identify each brother or date a change of response.
    Acts 1:14 All these devoted themselves with one accord to prayer, together with women, Mary the mother of Jesus, and His brothers.

John 7:6
So Jesus said to them, “My time is not yet here, but your time is always ready.
  John 2:4 — same-book timing comparison: Both sayings describe Jesus' unfulfilled timing. John 7 says time; John 2 says hour. The words, hearers and occasions are preserved rather than assigned one date.
    John 2:4 And Jesus said to her, “What is this to Me and to you, woman? My hour has not yet come.”

John 7:7
The world cannot hate you, but it hates Me, because I testify concerning it that its works are evil.
  John 3:19–3:20 — same-book teaching parallel: Both associate opposition with disclosure of evil deeds. John 7 says the world hates Jesus' testimony; John 3 describes evildoers avoiding and hating the Light. The subjects and imagery are not identical formulations.
    John 3:19 And this is the judgment: the Light has come into the world, and people loved the darkness more than the Light, for their deeds were evil.
    John 3:20 For everyone who practices base things hates the light and does not come to the light, so that his deeds may not be exposed.

John 7:8
You go up to the feast. I am not going up to this feast, because My time has not yet been fulfilled.”
  John 7:10 — internal statement-and-action comparison: This unit says I am not going up because the time is unfulfilled; the later narrative records His going after His brothers, secretly. No not-yet word or unrecorded explanation is inserted into the earlier statement.
    John 7:10 But when His brothers had gone up to the feast, then He also went up, not openly, but as in secret.

John 7:10
But when His brothers had gone up to the feast, then He also went up, not openly, but as in secret.
  John 7:4 — internal contrast: Jesus' secret arrival contrasts with His brothers' argument for public display. The earlier proposal is not treated as the narrator's assessment of His motives or as an independently narrated trip.
    John 7:4 For no one acts in secret while seeking to be known publicly. If Thou doest these things, reveal Thyself to the world.”

John 7:11
So the Jews sought Him at the feast and said, “Where is He?”
  John 11:56 — same-book search comparison: Both report people seeking Jesus at a feast and questioning His presence. John 7 concerns Booths; John 11 concerns Passover and asks whether He will come. The occasions and possible motives are not merged.
    John 11:56 So they sought Jesus and said to one another as they stood in the temple, “What do you think? Surely He will not come to the feast?”

John 7:12
And there was much grumbling about Him among the crowds. Some said, “He is good,” but others said, “No, He leads the crowd astray.”
  John 10:19–10:21 — same-book response comparison: Both describe conflicting evaluations of Jesus. Here good versus misleading; later demon-possession versus words and healing inconsistent with that charge. Different speakers' claims are reported rather than endorsed.
    John 10:19 Division arose again among the Jews because of these words.
    John 10:20 Many of them said, “He has a demon and is mad. Why do you listen to Him?”
    John 10:21 Others said, “These are not the words of a demon-possessed person. Can a demon open the eyes of the blind?”

John 7:13
Yet no one spoke openly about Him because of fear of the Jews.
  John 9:22 — same-book fear comparison: Both describe fear restricting favorable public speech about Jesus. John 9 specifies parents and synagogue expulsion for confessing Christ; John 7 does not independently state that particular sanction.
    John 9:22 His parents said these things because they feared the Jews, for the Jews had already agreed that if anyone confessed Him as Christ, he should be put out of the synagogue.

John 7:14
When the feast was already halfway through, Jesus went up into the temple and taught.
  Luke 19:47 — teaching-setting comparison: Both place Jesus teaching in the temple. John specifies midway through this feast; Luke describes daily teaching in the later Jerusalem setting. No shared visit or complete timetable is inferred.
    Luke 19:47 And He was teaching daily in the temple. But the chief priests and the scribes, and the leading men of the people, were seeking to destroy Him.

John 7:15
So the Jews marveled, saying, “How does this man know the writings without having studied?”
  Luke 2:46–2:47 — knowledge-response comparison: Both report amazement at Jesus' understanding in temple settings. Luke concerns a twelve-year-old listening and questioning; John concerns an adult whose study background is questioned. Ages, roles and occasions differ.
    Luke 2:46 After three days they found Him in the temple, sitting among the teachers, listening to them and questioning them.
    Luke 2:47 All who heard Him were astonished at His understanding and answers.

John 7:16
So Jesus answered them, “My teaching is not Mine, but belongs to the One who sent Me.
  John 12:49–12:50 — same-book teaching parallel: Both attribute Jesus' teaching or speech to His Sender rather than Himself. John 12 explicitly names the Father's speech-command; John 7 speaks of the teaching's ownership.
    John 12:49 Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.
    John 12:50 And I know that His command is eternal life. So the things I speak, just as the Father has told Me, thus I speak.”

John 7:17
If anyone wills to do His will, he will know concerning the teaching whether it is from God or whether I speak from Myself.
  John 8:31–8:32 — same-book teaching comparison: Both connect committed response with knowing truth. John 7 concerns willingness to do God's will and discerning teaching's source; John 8 concerns remaining in Jesus' word, discipleship and liberating truth.
    John 8:31 So Jesus said to the Jews who had believed Him, “If you remain in My word, you are truly My disciples,
    John 8:32 and you will know the truth, and the truth will set you free.”

John 7:18
The one who speaks from himself seeks his own glory. But the one who seeks the glory of the one who sent him is true, and there is no unrighteousness in him.
  John 5:41–5:44 — same-book glory comparison: Both contrast self-directed human glory with God's. John 7 tests a speaker by seeking the Sender's glory; John 5 discusses receiving human glory and coming in the Father's name. Their immediate arguments differ.
    John 5:41 I do not receive glory from people,
    John 5:42 but I know you, that you do not have the love of God within yourselves.
    John 5:43 I have come in My Father’s name, and you do not receive Me. If another comes in his own name, you will receive him.
    John 5:44 How can you believe, receiving glory from one another, and not seeking the glory that comes from the only God?

John 7:19
Has not Moses given you the Law? Yet none of you keeps the Law. Why do you seek to kill Me?”
  Exodus 20:15 — law background: Jesus invokes Moses' Law while challenging the intent to kill Him. The command against murder supplies concrete legal background; it does not independently narrate this plot or every alleged law violation.
    Exodus 20:15 You shall not murder.

John 7:20
The crowd answered, “Thou hast a demon! Who seeks to kill Thee?”
  John 8:48–8:49 — same-book accusation comparison: Both contain a demon accusation against Jesus. John 7 combines it with denial of a killing plot; John 8 adds Samaritan and Jesus' denial while honoring His Father. The speeches and settings differ.
    John 8:48 The Jews answered Him, “Do we not rightly say that Thou art a Samaritan and hast a demon?”
    John 8:49 Jesus answered, “I do not have a demon, but I honor My Father, and you dishonor Me.

John 7:21
Jesus answered them, “I did one work, and you all marvel.
  John 5:8–5:9 — same-book healing callback: Read with7:23's Sabbath-healing explanation, the one-work reference corresponds to the earlier man's restoration on a Sabbath. The earlier scene does not independently record the present audience's reaction.
    John 5:8 Jesus said to him, “Rise, take up your mat, and walk.”
    John 5:9 And at once the man became well, took up his mat, and walked. Now it was a Sabbath on that day.

John 7:22
For this reason Moses has given you circumcision—not that it is from Moses, but from the fathers—and on a Sabbath you circumcise a person.
  Genesis 17:10–17:12 — law background: Genesis gives Abraham the circumcision covenant and eighth-day instruction before Moses. John expressly distinguishes the fathers' origin from Mosaic regulation and discusses Sabbath practice, which Genesis does not explicitly describe here.
    Genesis 17:10 And this is the covenant that you shall keep, between Me and you, and your seed after you throughout their generations: every male among you shall be circumcised.
    Genesis 17:11 And you shall be circumcised in the flesh of your foreskin; and it shall be a sign of the covenant between Me and you.
    Genesis 17:12 And every male child among you, eight days old, shall be circumcised throughout your generations, the slave born in your house, and the slave bought with silver from any foreigner who is not of your seed.

John 7:23
If a person receives circumcision on a Sabbath so that the Law of Moses may not be broken, are you angry with Me because I made a whole person well on a Sabbath?
  Leviticus 12:3 — law and application: Leviticus prescribes eighth-day circumcision; Jesus invokes keeping circumcision law while defending Sabbath healing. The legal verse does not itself narrate this healing or explicitly adjudicate that controversy.
    Leviticus 12:3 And on the eighth day he shall circumcise the flesh of his foreskin.

John 7:24
Do not judge according to appearance, but judge with righteous judgment.”
  Leviticus 19:15 — teaching parallel: Both require righteous judgment instead of partial assessment. Leviticus names favoritism toward poor or powerful people; John forbids judging by appearance in the Sabbath controversy.
    Leviticus 19:15 You shall do no injustice in judgment; you shall not favour the person of the poor, neither shall you admire the person of the mighty; you shall judge your neighbour in righteousness.

John 7:25
So some of the Jerusalem residents said, “Is this not the man they are seeking to kill?
  John 5:18 — same-book plot reference: The residents' question about the intended victim corresponds to the earlier narrated intent to kill Jesus. The connection does not make every resident share that intent or independently establish their private knowledge.
    John 5:18 For this reason, therefore, the Jews sought all the more to kill Him, because He not only broke the Sabbath, but also called God His own Father, making Himself equal to God.

John 7:26
And behold, He speaks openly, and they say nothing to Him. Could the rulers truly have recognized that this is the Christ?
  John 18:20 — same-book public-teaching comparison: Both emphasize Jesus speaking openly. The later trial statement describes public teaching in synagogues and temple; here residents wonder about rulers' response. Public teaching is distinguished from the earlier secret journey.
    John 18:20 Jesus answered him, “I have spoken openly to the world. I always taught in a synagogue and in the temple, where all the Jews gather, and I spoke nothing in secret.

John 7:27
But we know where this man is from; when the Christ comes, no one knows where He is from.”
  John 6:42 — same-book origin-objection comparison: Both objections rely on perceived knowledge of Jesus' earthly origin. John 6 invokes known parents against heavenly descent; John 7 invokes known origin against a claimed Messiah expectation. The expectations remain speakers' claims.
    John 6:42 And they said, “Is this not Jesus, the son of Joseph, whose father and mother we know? How does He now say, ‘I have come down from heaven’?”

John 7:28
So Jesus cried out as He taught in the temple, saying, “You both know Me and know where I am from. Yet I have not come from Myself, but the One who sent Me is true, whom you do not know.
  John 8:42 — same-book mission parallel: Both distinguish Jesus' divine sending from coming on His own initiative. John 8 frames love and God's fatherhood conditionally; John 7 challenges the hearers' knowledge of the true Sender.
    John 8:42 Jesus said to them, “If God were your Father, you would love Me, for I came out from God and have come here. For I have not come from Myself, but He sent Me.

John 7:29
I know Him, because I am from Him and He sent Me.”
  Matthew 11:27 — teaching parallel: Both give Jesus distinctive knowledge of the Father or Sender. John relates it to being from Him and sent; Matthew also describes mutual Father-Son knowledge and revelation to others.
    Matthew 11:27 All things have been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.

John 7:30
So they sought to seize Him, but no one laid a hand on Him, because His hour had not yet come.
  John 8:20 — same-book timing recurrence: Both explain failure to seize Jesus by His hour not yet coming. John 8 locates its saying at the treasury; John 7 follows temple origin-and-sending teaching. They are recurring notices, not one event.
    John 8:20 He spoke these words at the treasury as He taught in the temple, and no one seized Him, because His hour had not yet come.

John 7:31
But many from the crowd believed in Him and said, “When the Christ comes, will He do more signs than this man has done?”
  John 2:23 — same-book sign-response comparison: Both describe many believing in Jesus in relation to signs at a feast. John 7 includes the crowd's question about greater future signs; John 2 names belief in His name. Their groups and maturity are not presumed identical.
    John 2:23 While He was in Jerusalem at the Passover, during the feast, many believed in His name, seeing the signs He was doing.

John 7:32
The Pharisees heard the crowd grumbling these things about Him, and the chief priests and the Pharisees sent officers to seize Him.
  John 11:57 — same-book arrest-plan comparison: Both name chief priests and Pharisees arranging Jesus' seizure. John 7 sends officers; John 11 seeks information about His whereabouts. The methods and occasions differ.
    John 11:57 The chief priests and the Pharisees had given orders that if anyone knew where He was, he should disclose it, so that they might seize Him.

John 7:33
So Jesus said, “I am with you for a little time longer, and I go to the One who sent Me.
  John 16:5 — same-book departure teaching: Both speak of Jesus going to the One who sent Him. John 7 adds a little time longer; John 16 addresses disciples and their failure to ask His destination.
    John 16:5 But now I am going to the One who sent Me, and none of you asks Me, ‘Where art Thou going?’

John 7:34
You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come.”
  John 13:33 — internal teaching callback: Jesus later explicitly recalls telling the Jews that where He goes they cannot come and applies the saying to disciples. The changing audience is preserved, without imposing identical or permanent exclusion on both groups.
    John 13:33 Little children, I am with you a little longer. You will seek Me, and just as I told the Jews, ‘Where I go, you cannot come,’ I now tell you also.

John 7:35
So the Jews said among themselves, “Where is this man about to go that we will not find Him? Is He about to go to the Dispersion among the Greeks and teach the Greeks?
  John 12:20–12:22 — narrative parallel: The hearers speculate that Jesus might go to the Greek Dispersion and teach Greeks; later Greek visitors approach through Philip and Andrew to seek Jesus. This contrasts imagined outward travel with visitors approaching Him. It does not narrate the speculated journey, identify the two groups or claim the later requested audience is explicitly reported as granted.
    John 12:20 Now there were some Greeks among those going up to worship at the feast.
    John 12:21 These approached Philip, who was from Bethsaida of Galilee, and asked him, saying, “Sir, we want to see Jesus.”
    John 12:22 Philip came and told Andrew; Andrew and Philip came and told Jesus.

John 7:36
What is this saying He spoke: ‘You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come’?”
  John 7:34 — internal quotation reference: The question repeats Jesus' immediately preceding seek/not-find and cannot-come saying. This identifies the quoted statement, not an independent verification of the hearers' proposed destination or future events.
    John 7:34 You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come.”

John 7:37
On the last day, the great day of the feast, Jesus stood and cried out, saying, “If anyone thirsts, let him come to Me and drink.
  Isaiah 55:1 — possible allusion: Both invite thirsty people to receive provision. Isaiah summons them to water and freely obtained food and drink; Jesus invites coming to Him and drinking. John does not name Isaiah or repeat the full invitation.
    Isaiah 55:1 You who thirst, go to the water; and you who have no silver, go and buy wine and rich food, and drink without silver or price.

John 7:38
The one who believes in Me—as the Scripture said, rivers of living water will flow from his belly.”
  Zechariah 14:8 — possible imagery background: Both describe flowing living water. Zechariah locates it in Jerusalem; John relates rivers from his belly to believing in Jesus. The comparison does not identify the sources or prove this is John's sole scriptural referent.
    Zechariah 14:8 On that day living water will go out from Jerusalem, half into the first sea and half into the last sea; it will be so in summer and spring.

John 7:39
He said this concerning the Spirit, whom those who believed in Him were about to receive; for the Spirit was not yet, because Jesus had not yet been glorified.
  Acts 2:33 — Spirit-reception comparison: Both relate Spirit reception or outpouring to Jesus' glorification or exaltation. Acts describes the exalted Jesus receiving the Father's promise and pouring it out; John's Spirit-was-not-yet wording is retained rather than expanded.
    Acts 2:33 Therefore, exalted to God’s right hand and having received from the Father the promised Holy Spirit, He has poured out what you both see and hear.

John 7:40
So some from the crowd, hearing these words, said, “This is truly the Prophet.”
  John 6:14 — same-book title recurrence: Both crowds identify Jesus as truly the Prophet. John 6 follows the bread sign; John 7 follows the water discourse. The speakers and occasions are not assumed identical.
    John 6:14 When the people saw the sign He did, they said, “This is truly the Prophet who is coming into the world.”

John 7:41
Others said, “This is the Christ.” But some said, “Surely the Christ does not come from Galilee?
  John 1:45–1:46 — same-book origin-objection comparison: Both scenes question Jesus through perceived geographic origin. Nathanael questions Nazareth; the feast crowd questions Galilee and divides over Christ identification. The characters' premises are not endorsed as the narrator's geography.
    John 1:45 Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”
    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

John 7:42
Has not the Scripture said that the Christ comes from the offspring of David and from Bethlehem, the village where David was?”
  Micah 5:1 — scriptural expectation background: The crowd invokes Bethlehem as the ruler's expected origin; Micah addresses Bethlehem and announces Israel's ruler from it. The target supplies background for the claim, not John's independent narration of Jesus' birthplace.
    Micah 5:1 And you, Bethlehem, house of Ephrathah, are small to be among Judah’s thousands; from you one will come out for Me to be a ruler in Israel, and his goings out are from the beginning, from days of old.
  2 Samuel 7:12–7:13 — scriptural expectation background: The crowd's Davidic-seed expectation corresponds to God's promise of David's seed and an enduring throne. Samuel does not name Bethlehem in these units or state this crowd's knowledge of Jesus' genealogy.
    2 Samuel 7:12 When your days are fulfilled and you sleep with your fathers, I will raise up your seed after you, who will be from your own body, and prepare his kingdom.
    2 Samuel 7:13 He will build Me a house for My name, and I will set his throne upright for ever.

John 7:43
So a division arose in the crowd because of Him.
  John 9:16 — same-book division recurrence: Both explicitly report division over Jesus. John 7 concerns Messiah and origin claims; John 9 concerns Sabbath observance and healing signs. The divided groups are not automatically identical.
    John 9:16 Then some of the Pharisees said, “This man is not from God, because He does not keep the Sabbath.” But others said, “How can a sinful man do such signs?” And there was division among them.

John 7:44
Some of them wanted to seize Him, but no one laid hands on Him.
  John 10:39 — same-book arrest comparison: Both describe efforts to seize Jesus without completed custody. John 7 says no one laid hands on Him; John 10 says He went out from their hand. The exact circumstances are not made identical.
    John 10:39 So they sought again to seize Him, and He went out from their hand.

John 7:45
So the officers came to the chief priests and Pharisees, and these said to them, “Why did you not bring Him?”
  John 7:32 — internal mission callback: The officers' return and question about not bringing Jesus refer back to their earlier seizure commission from priests and Pharisees. The later notice reports failure to complete it, not another independently attested arrest mission.
    John 7:32 The Pharisees heard the crowd grumbling these things about Him, and the chief priests and the Pharisees sent officers to seize Him.

John 7:46
The officers answered, “Never has a person spoken in this way.”
  Mark 1:22 — teaching-response comparison: Both describe exceptional response to Jesus' speech. Officers say no person spoke this way; Mark's hearers marvel at authoritative teaching unlike scribes. The listeners and settings differ.
    Mark 1:22 They were astonished at His teaching, for He taught them as one having authority, and not as the scribes.

John 7:47
So the Pharisees answered them, “Have you also been led astray?
  John 7:12 — internal accusation recurrence: The Pharisees' led-astray question repeats the misleading accusation voiced in the crowd's earlier debate. Different speakers use the charge; neither occurrence makes it the narrator's judgment about Jesus.
    John 7:12 And there was much grumbling about Him among the crowds. Some said, “He is good,” but others said, “No, He leads the crowd astray.”

John 7:48
Has any of the rulers believed in Him, or any of the Pharisees?
  John 12:42 — same-book response contrast: The rhetorical question about rulers' belief is compared with the later report of many rulers believing but fearing public confession. Different times and public/private responses do not establish when each ruler first believed.
    John 12:42 Nevertheless, many even from the rulers believed in Him, but because of the Pharisees they did not confess Him, so that they might not be put out of the synagogue,

John 7:49
But this crowd that does not know the Law is accursed.”
  John 9:34 — same-book hostile-response comparison: Both record disparagement of people favorable to Jesus. Here the crowd is called accursed and law-ignorant; later a healed man is called sin-born and unfit to teach. The reported accusations are not endorsed.
    John 9:34 They answered him, “You were born entirely in sins, and do you teach us?” And they cast him out.

John 7:50
Nicodemus, who had come to Him earlier and was one of them, said to them,
  John 3:1–3:2 — internal person reference: The earlier Nicodemus visit identifies this returning participant as a Pharisee and ruler who came at night. This does not make his later legal question a record of every earlier belief.
    John 3:1 Now there was a man from the Pharisees named Nicodemus, a ruler of the Jews.
    John 3:2 He came to Him at night and said to Him, “Rabbi, we know that Thou hast come from God as a teacher, for no one can do these signs that Thou doest unless God is with him.”

John 7:51
“Does our Law judge a person unless it first hears from him and knows what he does?”
  Deuteronomy 1:16–1:17 — legal background: Moses directs judges to hear disputes and judge righteously without partiality. Nicodemus invokes prior hearing and knowledge before judgment. The rule supplies background, not a proved verbatim citation of a particular statute.
    Deuteronomy 1:16 And I commanded your judges at that time, saying: Hear the matters between your brethren, and judge justly between a man and his brother and the sojourner with him.
    Deuteronomy 1:17 You shall not show partiality in judgment; you shall judge the small and the great alike. You shall not shrink before the face of man, for judgment belongs unto God. And you shall bring unto me the matter which is too hard for you, and I will hear it.

John 7:52
They answered him, “Are you also from Galilee? Search and see that a prophet does not arise from Galilee.”
  John 1:45–1:46 — thematic parallel: The Galilee-prophet objection to Nicodemus is compared with Nathanael's earlier objection about good coming from Nazareth and Philip's invitation to come and see. Both challenge a claim through regional origin. The places and addressees are not conflated, and the comparison does not independently disprove the Galilee claim by assigning a prophet modern geographic coordinates.
    John 1:45 Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”
    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

John 8:1
But Jesus went to the Mount of Olives.
  Luke 21:37 — narrative parallel: The double-bracketed John sequence sends Jesus to the Mount of Olives. Luke describes His lodging there at night during a Temple-teaching period. The shared movement does not establish the same night or the same narrative setting.
    Luke 21:37 By day He was teaching in the temple, but at night He went out and lodged on the mount called Olivet.

John 8:2
At dawn He came again into the temple, and all the people were coming to Him; and He sat down and taught them.
  Luke 21:37–21:38 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus teaching in the Temple and people coming early to hear Him. John gives a particular dawn arrival and seated teaching within its double-bracketed sequence; Luke summarizes a recurring daily pattern.
    Luke 21:37 By day He was teaching in the temple, but at night He went out and lodged on the mount called Olivet.
    Luke 21:38 And all the people would come early in the morning to Him in the temple to hear Him.

John 8:3
And the scribes and the Pharisees brought a woman caught in adultery, and set her in the midst.
  Exodus 20:13 — thematic parallel: John identifies the woman's alleged offense as adultery; the saved Exodus command prohibits adultery. This supplies the law's moral background, not this woman's identity, the evidence against her, or the prescribed penalty.
    Exodus 20:13 You shall not commit adultery.

John 8:4
They said to Him, “Teacher, this woman was caught in the very act of committing adultery.
  Deuteronomy 22:22 — thematic parallel: The reported caught-in-adultery charge is compared with the legal case of a man found lying with another man's wife. Deuteronomy explicitly concerns both parties and death; this unit does not specify stoning. The comparison supplies legal offense background without inventing the woman's marital details, the absent man's fate or an additional verdict on her case.
    Deuteronomy 22:22 And if a man is found lying with a woman married unto a husband, you shall put both of them to death, the man who lay with the woman, and the woman; and you shall remove the evil one from Israel.

John 8:5
Now in the Law Moses commanded us that such women be stoned. What, then, dost Thou say?”
  Deuteronomy 22:22–22:24 — allusion: The accusers appeal to Moses concerning stoning. Deuteronomy orders death for both adulterous parties and, in its betrothed-city case, stoning of both. John does not supply all those case conditions or authorize restricting the law to women alone.
    Deuteronomy 22:22 And if a man is found lying with a woman married unto a husband, you shall put both of them to death, the man who lay with the woman, and the woman; and you shall remove the evil one from Israel.
    Deuteronomy 22:23 And if a young woman, a virgin, is betrothed unto a man, and a man finds her in the city and lies with her,
    Deuteronomy 22:24 you shall bring both of them forth unto the gate of their city, and they shall be stoned with stones and shall die: the young woman, because she did not cry out in the city, and the man, because he violated his neighbour’s wife. And you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.

John 8:6
They said this to test Him, so that they might have something with which to accuse Him. But Jesus bent down and wrote with His finger on the ground.
  Matthew 22:15–22:18 — thematic parallel: Both narratives explicitly describe a question meant to trap or test Jesus. Matthew concerns tax and a verbal snare; John's question concerns adultery and accusation, with ground-writing absent from Matthew. These are different confrontations.
    Matthew 22:15 Then the Pharisees went and took counsel how to trap Him in a statement.
    Matthew 22:16 They sent Him their disciples with the Herodians, saying, “Teacher, we know that Thou art truthful and dost teach God’s way in truth. Thou art not swayed by anyone, for Thou dost not look at people’s outward status.
    Matthew 22:17 Tell us, then, what Thou thinkest: is it lawful to give tribute to Caesar, or not?”
    Matthew 22:18 Knowing their evil intent, Jesus said, “Why do you test Me, hypocrites?

John 8:7
As they persisted in questioning Him, He straightened up and said to them, “Let the one among you who is without sin be first to cast a stone at her.”
  Deuteronomy 17:6–17:7 — allusion: Deuteronomy requires multiple witnesses and the witnesses' hands first in an execution. Jesus calls for the sinless person among these accusers to cast the first stone. The shared first-action imagery does not make Deuteronomy itself require sinlessness.
    Deuteronomy 17:6 Upon the testimony of two witnesses or three witnesses shall he who is condemned to death be put to death; he shall not die upon the testimony of one witness.
    Deuteronomy 17:7 And the hand of the witnesses shall be first upon him to put him to death, and afterward the hand of all the people; and you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.

John 8:8
And again He bent down and wrote on the ground.
  Exodus 31:18 — allusion: John repeats Jesus' writing on the ground, previously described as with His finger. Exodus describes stone tablets written with God's finger. This is a possible writing-and-law imagery comparison; John never identifies what Jesus wrote or equates it with the tablets.
    Exodus 31:18 When He finished speaking with Moses on Mount Sinai, He gave him the two tablets of the testimony, stone tablets written with the finger of God.

John 8:9
But when they heard, they went out one by one, beginning with the older ones. And Jesus was left alone, and the woman standing in the midst.
  John 8:7 — narrative parallel: The earlier preserved instruction tells the sinless person to throw first; the later narration marks the departure as occurring when they heard. This supplies the actual spoken antecedent without adding a conscience clause, stating each accuser's inner guilt or explaining why the older ones left first.
    John 8:7 As they persisted in questioning Him, He straightened up and said to them, “Let the one among you who is without sin be first to cast a stone at her.”

John 8:11
She said, “No one, Lord.” Jesus said, “Neither do I condemn you. Go, and from now on sin no more.”⟧
  John 5:14 — thematic parallel: Jesus tells both addressed people to sin no more. John 8 follows the accusers' departure and His refusal to condemn the woman; John 5 follows healing and includes a warning of something worse. The people and forms of deliverance differ.
    John 5:14 After these things Jesus found him in the temple and said to him, “Behold, you have become well. Sin no more, so that something worse may not happen to you.”

John 8:12
Then Jesus spoke to them again, saying, “I am the Light of the world. The one who follows Me will certainly not walk in the darkness, but will have the light of life.”
  John 1:3–1:5 — thematic parallel: Both join life and light against darkness in the presentation of Jesus. The prologue speaks through creation and Word imagery; John 8 speaks of following Jesus as the world's Light. The prologue's continuation is retained rather than detached from its preceding clause.
    John 1:3 All things came into being through Him, and without Him not one thing came into being. What has come into being
    John 1:4 in Him was life, and the life was the light of humankind.
    John 1:5 And the light shines in the darkness, and the darkness did not overcome it.

John 8:13
So the Pharisees said to Him, “Thou bearest witness concerning Thyself; Thy testimony is not true.”
  John 5:31 — thematic parallel: The opponents' self-witness objection repeats the earlier self-witness/not-true formulation. In John 5 Jesus states a conditional before discussing other witnesses; in John 8 opponents make the charge and Jesus answers it. The speakers and argumentative settings differ.
    John 5:31 If I bear witness concerning Myself, My testimony is not true.

John 8:14
Jesus answered them, “Even if I bear witness concerning Myself, My testimony is true, because I know where I came from and where I am going. But you do not know where I come from or where I am going.
  John 13:3–13:4 — thematic parallel: Both describe Jesus knowing where He comes from and where He goes. John 8 makes that knowledge a ground for His testimony; John 13 places coming from God and returning to God before the foot-washing. These are different scenes.
    John 13:3 knowing that the Father had given all things into His hands, and that He came out from God and was going to God,
    John 13:4 He rose from supper and laid aside His garments, and took a towel and tied it around Himself.

John 8:15
You judge according to the flesh; I judge no one.
  John 12:47–12:48 — thematic parallel: Both contain Jesus' statement that He does not judge in the described situation. John 8 contrasts His hearers' judgment according to flesh; John 12 explains His saving mission and the word's last-day judgment. This does not erase the Gospel's other judgment sayings.
    John 12:47 And if anyone hears My words and does not keep them, I do not judge him; for I did not come to judge the world, but to save the world.
    John 12:48 The one who rejects Me and does not receive My words has one who judges him: the word that I spoke will judge him on the last day.

John 8:16
But even if I judge, My judgment is true, because I am not alone, but I and the Father who sent Me.
  John 5:30 — thematic parallel: Both ground Jesus' judgment in His relationship to the Father who sends Him. John 8 says He is not alone and His judgment is true; John 5 speaks of hearing, righteous judgment, and seeking the Sender's will. The explanations are related but differently worded.
    John 5:30 I can do nothing from Myself. As I hear, I judge, and My judgment is righteous, because I do not seek My own will, but the will of the One who sent Me.

John 8:17
And in your own Law it is written that the testimony of two people is true.
  Deuteronomy 19:15 — quotation: Jesus invokes the law's two-person testimony rule. Deuteronomy says one witness is insufficient and two or three establish a matter. John's formulation is not a claim that any two agreeing people automatically speak factual truth.
    Deuteronomy 19:15 One witness shall not suffice to testify against a man concerning any injustice, or any trespass, or any sin which he sins. At the mouth of two witnesses, and at the mouth of three witnesses, shall every matter be established.

John 8:18
I am the One who bears witness concerning Myself, and the Father who sent Me bears witness concerning Me.”
  John 5:37 — thematic parallel: Both identify the Father who sent Jesus as bearing witness about Him. John 8 also names Jesus' own testimony; John 5 adds that its hearers have not heard the Father's voice or seen His form. The witness lists are not identical.
    John 5:37 And the Father who sent Me has Himself testified concerning Me. You have never heard His voice or seen His form,

John 8:19
So they said to Him, “Where is Thy Father?” Jesus answered, “You know neither Me nor My Father. If you knew Me, you would also know My Father.”
  John 14:7–14:9 — thematic parallel: Both link knowing Jesus with knowing the Father. John 8 answers a question about where His Father is; John 14 answers Philip's request to be shown the Father and uses seeing language as well. Knowing and seeing are not silently made identical expressions.
    John 14:7 If you have known Me, you will also know My Father. From now on you know Him and have seen Him.”
    John 14:8 Philip says to Him, “Lord, show us the Father, and it is enough for us.”
    John 14:9 Jesus says to him, “Have I been with you for so long a time, and you have not known Me, Philip? The one who has seen Me has seen the Father. How can you say, ‘Show us the Father’?

John 8:20
He spoke these words at the treasury as He taught in the temple, and no one seized Him, because His hour had not yet come.
  John 7:30 — thematic parallel: Both explain a failed attempt to seize Jesus by saying His hour had not yet come. John 8 locates teaching at the treasury; John 7 belongs to an earlier confrontation. This is an internal repeated explanation, not independent attestation of one arrest attempt.
    John 7:30 So they sought to seize Him, but no one laid a hand on Him, because His hour had not yet come.

John 8:21
So He said to them again, “I am going away, and you will seek Me, and you will die in your sin. Where I go, you cannot come.”
  John 7:33–7:34 — thematic parallel: Jesus again speaks of going away, being sought, and hearers being unable to come where He goes. John 8 adds dying in their sin; John 7 speaks of not finding Him and going to the Sender. The warnings retain their different wording.
    John 7:33 So Jesus said, “I am with you for a little time longer, and I go to the One who sent Me.
    John 7:34 You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come.”

John 8:22
So the Jews said, “Will He kill Himself, since He says, ‘Where I go, you cannot come’?”
  John 7:35–7:36 — thematic parallel: Both report hearers proposing an explanation for Jesus' inaccessible destination. John 7 asks about the Greek Dispersion; John 8 asks about suicide. These reported guesses are not endorsed as the narrator's account of Jesus' destination.
    John 7:35 So the Jews said among themselves, “Where is this man about to go that we will not find Him? Is He about to go to the Dispersion among the Greeks and teach the Greeks?
    John 7:36 What is this saying He spoke: ‘You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come’?”

John 8:23
And He said to them, “You are from below; I am from above. You are from this world; I am not from this world.
  John 3:31 — thematic parallel: Both contrast an origin from above or heaven with an earthly origin. John 8 applies above/below and world language directly to Jesus and His hearers; John 3 contrasts the heavenly One's testimony with speech from the earth.
    John 3:31 The One coming from above is above all. The one who is from the earth is from the earth and speaks from the earth. The One coming from heaven is above all.

John 8:24
Therefore I told you that you will die in your sins; for unless you believe that I am, you will die in your sins.”
