  John 3:18 — thematic parallel: Both connect unbelief with an adverse outcome. John 8 speaks of dying in sins unless the hearers believe that Jesus is 'I am'; John 3 speaks of judgment and belief in the only-begotten Son's name. Their distinct identity formulas are preserved.
    John 3:18 The one who believes in Him is not judged; but the one who does not believe has already been judged, because he has not believed in the name of the only-begotten Son of God.

John 8:25
So they said to Him, “Who art Thou?” Jesus said to them, “Why do I even speak to you at all?
  John 10:24–10:25 — narrative parallel: Both identity demands receive a reply challenging the hearers' reception of Jesus' speech. John8 preserves Why do I even speak to you at all; John10 says He told them and they do not believe, adding the witness of works. The first reply is not replaced by a familiar from-the-beginning rendering.
    John 10:24 So the Jews surrounded Him and said to Him, “How long wilt Thou keep our soul in suspense? If Thou art the Christ, tell us openly.”
    John 10:25 Jesus answered them, “I told you, and you do not believe. The works I do in My Father’s name bear witness concerning Me.

John 8:26
I have many things to speak and judge concerning you. But the One who sent Me is true, and what I heard from Him, these things I speak to the world.”
  John 3:33–3:34 — thematic parallel: Both connect God's truth and sending with the words Jesus speaks. John 3 describes accepting testimony and the sent One speaking God's words; John 8 describes speaking to the world what He heard from the true Sender. Their surrounding judgment and Spirit statements differ.
    John 3:33 The one who has received His testimony has set his seal that God is true.
    John 3:34 For the One whom God sent speaks the words of God, for He gives the Spirit without measure.

John 8:27
They did not understand that He was speaking to them about the Father.
  John 10:6 — thematic parallel: Both narrator comments explicitly state that Jesus' hearers did not understand. John 8 concerns His reference to the Father; John 10 concerns a sheep-related figure of speech. This is an internal narrative pattern with different subjects and occasions.
    John 10:6 Jesus spoke this figure of speech to them, but they did not understand what He was saying to them.

John 8:28
So Jesus said to them, “When you lift up the Son of Man, then you will know that I am, and that I do nothing from Myself, but speak these things just as the Father taught Me.
  John 12:32–12:33 — thematic parallel: Both use Jesus' being lifted up in relation to His mission. John 12 explicitly explains the expression as indicating the manner of His death; John 8 links lifting up the Son of Man with recognition and His Father-taught speech. The explanations are not word-for-word duplicates.
    John 12:32 And I, if I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all people to Myself.”
    John 12:33 He said this to signify by what kind of death He was about to die.

John 8:29
And the One who sent Me is with Me. He has not left Me alone, because I always do the things pleasing to Him.”
  John 16:32 — thematic parallel: Both affirm that Jesus is not alone because the Father is with Him. John 8 adds that He always does what pleases the Father; John 16 contrasts the Father's presence with the disciples' coming abandonment. The human circumstances differ.
    John 16:32 Behold, an hour is coming, and has come, when you will be scattered, each to his own, and will leave Me alone. Yet I am not alone, because the Father is with Me.

John 8:30
As He spoke these things, many believed in Him.
  John 2:23 — thematic parallel: Both narrator reports describe many believing in Jesus. John 8 follows His speech; John 2 concerns signs at Passover. The comparison does not identify the groups or assign every person's subsequent history or quality of faith.
    John 2:23 While He was in Jerusalem at the Passover, during the feast, many believed in His name, seeing the signs He was doing.

John 8:31
So Jesus said to the Jews who had believed Him, “If you remain in My word, you are truly My disciples,
  John 15:7–15:8 — thematic parallel: Both link remaining in Jesus or His words with discipleship. John 8 says remaining in His word marks true disciples; John 15 describes His words remaining in the disciples, prayer, and fruit glorifying the Father. The conditional formulations differ.
    John 15:7 If you remain in Me and My words remain in you, ask whatever you wish, and it will be done for you.
    John 15:8 In this My Father has been glorified: that you bear much fruit and become My disciples.

John 8:32
and you will know the truth, and the truth will set you free.”
  Romans 6:17–6:18 — thematic parallel: Both connect the reception of teaching with freedom in a discussion of sin's slavery. John speaks of knowing the truth and being freed; Romans speaks of heartfelt obedience, freedom from sin, and service to righteousness. Neither passage is reduced to a claim about civil status alone.
    Romans 6:17 But thanks be to God that, though you were slaves of sin, you obeyed from the heart the pattern of teaching to which you were handed over;
    Romans 6:18 and, having been set free from sin, you were enslaved to righteousness.

John 8:33
They answered Him, “We are Abraham’s offspring and have never been slaves to anyone. How dost Thou say, ‘You will become free’?”
  Genesis 15:13 — thematic parallel: Both concern Abraham's offspring and servitude. Genesis predicts descendants' oppression and enslavement in a foreign land; John's hearers assert that they have never been slaves. The comparison does not settle whether their claim concerns personal status or ancestral history.
    Genesis 15:13 And it was said to Abram, “Know for certain that your seed shall live as strangers in a land not their own; and they shall enslave them, and afflict them, and humble them for four hundred years.

John 8:34
Jesus answered them, “Amen, amen, I tell you: everyone who commits sin is a slave of sin.
  Romans 6:16–6:18 — thematic parallel: Both depict sin as a form of slavery. Jesus speaks of the person committing sin as sin's slave; Paul discusses obedience, sin leading to death, and transfer into service of righteousness. Their arguments are related without being identical accounts of human bondage.
    Romans 6:16 Do you not know that when you present yourselves to someone as slaves to obey, you are slaves of the one whom you obey, either of sin leading to death, or of obedience leading to righteousness?
    Romans 6:17 But thanks be to God that, though you were slaves of sin, you obeyed from the heart the pattern of teaching to which you were handed over;
    Romans 6:18 and, having been set free from sin, you were enslaved to righteousness.

John 8:35
But the slave does not remain in the house forever; the son remains forever.
  Hebrews 3:5–3:6 — thematic parallel: Both use household imagery to distinguish service from the Son's position. John contrasts the slave's nonpermanence with the Son remaining; Hebrews contrasts Moses' faithful service with Christ as Son over the house. John does not explicitly identify its slave with Moses.
    Hebrews 3:5 And Moses indeed was faithful in all His house as an attendant, for a testimony of the things to be spoken,
    Hebrews 3:6 but Christ as a Son over His house. We are that house if indeed we hold fast our confidence and the boast of our hope.

John 8:36
So if the Son sets you free, you will truly be free.
  Galatians 5:1 — thematic parallel: Both attribute freedom to the Son or Christ. John speaks of being truly free after describing sin's slavery; Galatians urges standing in Christ's freedom rather than a yoke of slavery in its own argument. John's saying is not rewritten as the whole Galatian controversy.
    Galatians 5:1 For freedom Christ set us free. Stand firm, therefore, and do not be held again under a yoke of slavery.

John 8:37
I know that you are Abraham’s offspring. Yet you seek to kill Me, because My word has no room in you.
  John 8:39–8:40 — thematic parallel: Jesus acknowledges Abrahamic descent here while confronting an intention to kill Him. The later exchange qualifies Abrahamic child-language by Abraham's conduct and contrasts it with killing a truth-speaker. This internal argument distinguishes acknowledged descent from the conduct being disputed.
    John 8:39 They answered Him, “Our father is Abraham.” Jesus said to them, “If you are Abraham’s children, you would be doing Abraham’s works.
    John 8:40 But now you seek to kill Me, a man who has spoken to you the truth that I heard from God. Abraham did not do this.

John 8:38
I speak what I have seen with the Father, and you therefore do what you have heard from the father.”
  John 3:31–3:32 — thematic parallel: Both ground testimony in what the heavenly speaker has seen or heard. John 8 specifically names what Jesus saw with the Father and then contrasts His hearers' action and their father. John 3 does not supply all the father-language of this confrontation.
    John 3:31 The One coming from above is above all. The one who is from the earth is from the earth and speaks from the earth. The One coming from heaven is above all.
    John 3:32 What He has seen and heard, this He testifies, and no one receives His testimony.

John 8:39
They answered Him, “Our father is Abraham.” Jesus said to them, “If you are Abraham’s children, you would be doing Abraham’s works.
  Galatians 3:7–3:9 — thematic parallel: Both qualify Abrahamic child-language beyond a bare descent claim. Jesus appeals to doing Abraham's works; Galatians speaks of faith and the blessing of the nations. The criteria's different wording is retained rather than substituting Paul's formulation for Jesus' words.
    Galatians 3:7 Know, then, that those who are of faith are the sons of Abraham.
    Galatians 3:8 And Scripture, foreseeing that God justifies the nations by faith, proclaimed the gospel beforehand to Abraham: “In you all the nations will be blessed.”
    Galatians 3:9 So then, those who are of faith are blessed with believing Abraham.

John 8:40
But now you seek to kill Me, a man who has spoken to you the truth that I heard from God. Abraham did not do this.
  John 7:19 — thematic parallel: Both challenge an intention to kill Jesus in relation to an ancestral authority. John 7 invokes Moses and the law; John 8 invokes Abraham and Jesus' God-heard truth. These are different appeals within different confrontations, not a denial of every hearer's ancestry.
    John 7:19 Has not Moses given you the Law? Yet none of you keeps the Law. Why do you seek to kill Me?”

John 8:41
You do the works of your father.” Then they said to Him, “We were not born from sexual immorality. We have one Father, God.”
  Malachi 2:10 — thematic parallel: Both use one-Father and God language. Malachi appeals to shared divine creation and covenant faithfulness; John's hearers claim God as their one Father, a claim Jesus then tests by their response to Him. The comparison does not endorse their every assertion or invent an accusation about Jesus' birth.
    Malachi 2:10 Did not one God create you? Is there not one Father of you all? Why have you each abandoned your brother, so as to profane the covenant of your fathers?

John 8:42
Jesus said to them, “If God were your Father, you would love Me, for I came out from God and have come here. For I have not come from Myself, but He sent Me.
  John 16:27–16:28 — thematic parallel: Both connect love for Jesus with His coming from God or the Father. John 8 tests the hearers' claim of divine fatherhood by whether they love the sent Jesus; John 16 speaks to disciples who have loved Him and believed His origin. The audiences and conditional directions differ.
    John 16:27 for the Father Himself loves you, because you have loved Me and have believed that I came forth from God.
    John 16:28 I came forth from the Father and have come into the world. Again, I am leaving the world and going to the Father.”

John 8:43
Why do you not understand My speech? Because you cannot hear My word.
  John 6:60–6:61 — thematic parallel: Both connect difficulty receiving Jesus' speech with hearing or stumbling. John 6 reports disciples asking who can hear the hard saying; John 8 has Jesus challenge opponents' inability to hear His word. The groups and stated responses are different.
    John 6:60 So many of His disciples, when they heard, said, “This saying is hard. Who can hear it?”
    John 6:61 But Jesus, knowing within Himself that His disciples were grumbling about this, said to them, “Does this cause you to stumble?

John 8:44
You are from your father, the devil, and you want to carry out your father’s desires. He was a murderer from the beginning and has not stood in the truth, because there is no truth in him. When he speaks the lie, he speaks from what is his own, because he is a liar and its father.
  1 John 3:8–3:12 — thematic parallel: Both describe the devil in relation to the beginning and moral parentage; the Epistle also describes Cain's murderous conduct as from the evil one. John 8 explicitly adds lying and a murderer from the beginning. This is a moral-character comparison, not a hereditary judgment on an ethnicity.
    1 John 3:8 The one who practices sin is from the devil, because the devil sins from the beginning. For this purpose the Son of God was manifested: to undo the works of the devil.
    1 John 3:9 Everyone who has been born of God does not practice sin, because His seed abides in him; and he cannot sin, because he has been born of God.
    1 John 3:10 By this the children of God and the children of the devil are evident: everyone who does not practice righteousness is not from God, nor is the one who does not love his brother.
    1 John 3:11 For this is the message you heard from the beginning: that we should love one another,
    1 John 3:12 not as Cain, who was from the evil one and slaughtered his brother. And for what reason did he slaughter him? Because his own works were evil, but his brother’s were righteous.

John 8:45
But because I speak the truth, you do not believe Me.
  Galatians 4:16 — thematic parallel: Both connect speaking truth with an adverse response. Jesus says His hearers do not believe Him because He tells truth; Paul asks whether telling truth has made him their enemy. The speakers, communities, and described reactions differ.
    Galatians 4:16 So have I become your enemy by telling you the truth?

John 8:46
Which of you convicts Me of sin? If I speak truth, why do you not believe Me?
  1 Peter 2:21–2:23 — thematic parallel: Jesus challenges His hearers to convict Him of sin. First Peter explicitly describes Christ committing no sin and having no deceit, within an account of suffering without retaliation. A rhetorical challenge and the Epistle's positive testimony are distinct forms of speech.
    1 Peter 2:21 For to this you were called, because Christ also suffered for you, leaving you an example, so that you might follow in His footsteps.
    1 Peter 2:22 He committed no sin, nor was deceit found in His mouth.
    1 Peter 2:23 When He was reviled, He did not revile in return; when He suffered, He did not threaten, but entrusted His cause to the One who judges justly.

John 8:47
The one who is from God hears the words of God. For this reason you do not hear, because you are not from God.”
  1 John 4:6 — thematic parallel: Both link being from God with hearing testimony and not being from God with not hearing. In the Gospel Jesus speaks of God's words; the Epistle speaks of hearing its 'us' and distinguishing truth from error. The speakers and immediate objects of hearing are not collapsed.
    1 John 4:6 We are from God. The one who knows God listens to us; whoever is not from God does not listen to us. By this we know the spirit of truth and the spirit of error.

John 8:48
The Jews answered Him, “Do we not rightly say that Thou art a Samaritan and hast a demon?”
  John 7:20 — thematic parallel: Both report a demon accusation against Jesus. John 8 also includes the Samaritan insult; John 7 asks who seeks to kill Him. These are reported hostile claims in different exchanges, not narrator endorsements.
    John 7:20 The crowd answered, “Thou hast a demon! Who seeks to kill Thee?”

John 8:49
Jesus answered, “I do not have a demon, but I honor My Father, and you dishonor Me.
  John 5:23 — thematic parallel: Both join the honor of the Son and Father. John 8 has Jesus deny a demon accusation and contrast His honoring the Father with His hearers' dishonoring Him; John 5 says all should honor the Son as the Father. The forms of the claims differ.
    John 5:23 so that all may honor the Son just as they honor the Father. The one who does not honor the Son does not honor the Father who sent Him.

John 8:50
But I do not seek My own glory. There is One who seeks it and judges.
  John 5:41–5:44 — thematic parallel: Both distinguish glory connected with God from human honor. John 8 says Jesus does not seek His own glory and another seeks and judges; John 5 says He does not receive human glory and challenges mutual human praise. Seeking and receiving are preserved as different expressions.
    John 5:41 I do not receive glory from people,
    John 5:42 but I know you, that you do not have the love of God within yourselves.
    John 5:43 I have come in My Father’s name, and you do not receive Me. If another comes in his own name, you will receive him.
    John 5:44 How can you believe, receiving glory from one another, and not seeking the glory that comes from the only God?

John 8:51
Amen, amen, I tell you: if anyone keeps My word, he will certainly never behold death.”
  John 5:24 — thematic parallel: Both connect receiving Jesus' word with life beyond death. John 8 says keeping His word means never beholding death; John 5 joins hearing and believing the Sender with eternal life and passage from death to life. The expressions are not reduced to bodily immortality during ordinary earthly life.
    John 5:24 Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who hears My word and believes the One who sent Me has eternal life, and does not come into judgment, but has passed from death into life.

John 8:52
So the Jews said to Him, “Now we know that Thou hast a demon. Abraham died, and the prophets also, and Thou sayest, ‘If anyone keeps My word, he will certainly never taste death.’
  Genesis 25:7–25:8 — thematic parallel: Genesis narrates Abraham's death after a life of 175 years, the ancestral fact invoked by these hearers. It does not endorse their demon accusation. Their 'taste death' formulation also differs from Jesus' preceding 'behold death' wording.
    Genesis 25:7 And these were the years of the days of Abraham’s life which he lived: a hundred and seventy-five years.
    Genesis 25:8 And Abraham’s strength failed, and he died in a good old age, an old man and full of days; and he was added to his people.

John 8:53
Art Thou greater than our father Abraham, who died? The prophets also died. Who dost Thou make Thyself out to be?”
  John 4:12 — thematic parallel: Both pose a greater-than-our-father question about Jesus and a patriarch. John 8 invokes Abraham and dead prophets; the Samaritan woman invokes Jacob and his well. The different speakers, patriarchs, and life-giving images remain distinct.
    John 4:12 Art Thou greater than our father Jacob, who gave us the well and drank from it himself, and his sons and his livestock?”

John 8:54
Jesus answered, “If I glorify Myself, My glory is nothing. It is My Father who glorifies Me, of whom you say, ‘He is our God.’
  John 17:1 — thematic parallel: Both speak of the Father glorifying Jesus. John 8 contrasts the Father's glory with worthless self-glorification; John 17 asks the Father to glorify the Son so the Son may glorify Him. The later prayer is not presented as this controversy's immediate event.
    John 17:1 Jesus spoke these things, and, lifting His eyes to heaven, said, “Father, the hour has come. Glorify Thy Son, so that the Son may glorify Thee,

John 8:55
Yet you have not known Him, but I know Him. And if I say that I do not know Him, I will be a liar like you. But I know Him and keep His word.
  John 7:28–7:29 — thematic parallel: Both contrast Jesus' knowledge of His Sender with His hearers' lack of knowledge. John 8 adds keeping the Father's word and the falsehood of denying that knowledge; John 7 adds being from the Sender and being sent. These are internal related claims, not independent witnesses.
    John 7:28 So Jesus cried out as He taught in the temple, saying, “You both know Me and know where I am from. Yet I have not come from Myself, but the One who sent Me is true, whom you do not know.
    John 7:29 I know Him, because I am from Him and He sent Me.”

John 8:56
Abraham your father rejoiced to see My day, and he saw it and was glad.”
  Hebrews 11:13 — thematic parallel: John singles out Abraham's joy at seeing Jesus' day. In its patriarchal discussion Hebrews describes faithful people seeing and welcoming unreceived promises from afar. This is a forward-looking faith comparison, not identification of a particular Genesis vision or a claim that Hebrews explicitly names Jesus' day here.
    Hebrews 11:13 All these died in faith, not having received the promises, but having seen and greeted them from afar and confessed that they were strangers and sojourners upon the earth.

John 8:57
So the Jews said to Him, “Thou art not yet fifty years old, and hast Thou seen Abraham?”
  Luke 3:23 — thematic parallel: Both report human age markers concerning Jesus: these hearers say He is not yet fifty, while Luke says about thirty at the beginning. They describe different moments and neither makes fifty His age or fixes the year of this exchange.
    Luke 3:23 Jesus Himself was about thirty years old when He began His ministry. He was the son, as was supposed, of Joseph, of Heli,

John 8:58
Jesus said to them, “Amen, amen, I tell you: before Abraham came into being, I am.”
  John 1:1–1:4 — thematic parallel: Jesus contrasts Abraham's coming into being with His own 'I am'; the prologue describes the Word already being in the beginning and creation coming into being through Him. This is an internal preexistence comparison, not a substitution of the prologue's wording for Jesus' exact saying.
    John 1:1 In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God.
    John 1:2 He was in the beginning with God.
    John 1:3 All things came into being through Him, and without Him not one thing came into being. What has come into being
    John 1:4 in Him was life, and the life was the light of humankind.
  Exodus 3:14 — allusion: Jesus' absolute 'I am' can be compared with God's self-designation to Moses. Exodus gives a fuller designation and Moses' commission. This is a possible divine-name allusion, not an assertion that the two passages contain an identical complete English formula.
    Exodus 3:14 And God said to Moses, “I am the One Who Is.” And He said, “This is what you shall say to the sons of Israel: ‘The One Who Is has sent me to you.’”

John 8:59
So they took up stones to cast at Him, but Jesus hid Himself and went out of the temple.
  John 10:31–10:33 — thematic parallel: Both describe opponents taking up stones against Jesus. John 10 explicitly supplies a blasphemy accusation; John 8 follows the Abraham exchange and describes Jesus hiding and leaving. John 10's spoken accusation is not imported as a quotation into John 8.
    John 10:31 Then the Jews took up stones again to stone Him.
    John 10:32 Jesus answered them, “I showed you many good works from the Father. For which work among them do you stone Me?”
    John 10:33 The Jews answered Him, “We do not stone Thee for a good work, but for blasphemy, and because Thou, being a man, makest Thyself God.”

John 9:1
And as He passed by, He saw a man blind from birth.
  Mark 8:22 — narrative parallel: Both begin an encounter between Jesus and a blind person. John specifies blindness from birth and Jesus noticing the man; Mark locates its encounter at Bethsaida and has others bring a blind man. Mark does not identify its man as congenitally blind.
    Mark 8:22 They came to Bethsaida. People brought Him a blind man and begged Him to touch him.

John 9:2
His disciples asked Him, saying, “Rabbi, who sinned, this man or his parents, so that he was born blind?”
  Luke 13:1–13:5 — thematic parallel: Both raise a proposed connection between suffering and exceptional guilt. John's disciples ask about the man or his parents; Luke reports Jesus challenging conclusions about killed Galileans and tower victims. The particular conditions and subsequent warnings differ.
    Luke 13:1 At that very time some people were present who told Him about the Galileans whose blood Pilate had mixed with their sacrifices.
    Luke 13:2 And He answered them, “Do you think these Galileans were greater sinners than all the other Galileans because they suffered these things?
    Luke 13:3 No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all likewise perish.
    Luke 13:4 Or those eighteen on whom the tower in Siloam fell and killed them—do you think they were greater debtors than all the people living in Jerusalem?
    Luke 13:5 No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all perish in the same way.”

John 9:3
Jesus answered, “Neither this man nor his parents sinned, but this was so that the works of God might be revealed in him.
  Ezekiel 18:20 — thematic parallel: Both resist transferring guilt simply through parent-child relationship. Ezekiel distinguishes each person's responsibility; Jesus rejects the offered explanations of this blindness and points to God's works. John does not say the man and parents never committed any sin or explain every disability.
    Ezekiel 18:20 The soul that sins will die. The son will not receive his father’s injustice, nor the father his son’s injustice. The righteous person’s righteousness will be upon him, and the lawless person’s lawlessness upon him.

John 9:4
We must work the works of the One who sent Me while it is day. Night is coming, when no one can work.
  John 12:35–12:36 — thematic parallel: Both urge action while day or light remains available against approaching darkness. John 9 speaks of doing the Sender's works and night when no one can work; John 12 speaks of walking and believing in the light. The images are not identical timetables.
    John 12:35 Then Jesus said to them, “For a little time longer the Light is among you. Walk while you have the Light, so that darkness may not overtake you. The one walking in the darkness does not know where he is going.
    John 12:36 While you have the Light, believe in the Light, so that you may become sons of light.” Jesus spoke these things, and went away and hid Himself from them.

John 9:5
While I am in the world, I am the Light of the world.”
  John 8:12 — thematic parallel: Jesus calls Himself the world's Light in both. John 9 adds 'while I am in the world'; John 8 describes following Him, avoiding darkness, and having life's light. The temporal clause and the following-language remain distinct.
    John 8:12 Then Jesus spoke to them again, saying, “I am the Light of the world. The one who follows Me will certainly not walk in the darkness, but will have the light of life.”

John 9:6
Having said these things, He spat on the ground and made mud from the spittle, and spread the mud upon his eyes.
  Mark 8:23–8:25 — narrative parallel: Both use Jesus' saliva during restoration of sight. John describes mud placed on eyes before washing; Mark describes spitting on eyes and a two-stage laying on of hands. They are different people and procedures, not duplicate details of one cure.
    Mark 8:23 Taking the blind man by the hand, He led him out of the village. He spat into his eyes, laid His hands on him, and asked him, “Do you see anything?”
    Mark 8:24 He looked up and said, “I see people, for I see them like trees, walking.”
    Mark 8:25 Then He laid His hands on his eyes again. The man saw clearly and was restored, and could see everything distinctly.

John 9:7
And He said to him, “Go, wash in the pool of Siloam”—which is translated Sent. So he went away and washed, and came back seeing.
  2 Kings 5:10–5:14 — narrative parallel: Both healing narratives involve obedience to a directive to wash. John names Siloam, translated Sent, and restoration of sight; Elisha directs Naaman to wash seven times in the Jordan for his skin condition. John's man is not Naaman and its procedure is not sevenfold.
    2 Kings 5:10 Elisha sent a messenger to him, saying, ‘Go and bathe seven times in the Jordan. Your flesh will be restored to you, and you will be cleansed.’
    2 Kings 5:11 Naaman became angry and went away, saying, ‘Behold, I thought that he would come out to me, stand, call on the name of his God, place his hand on the place, and remove the leprous condition.’
    2 Kings 5:12 ‘Are not Abanah and Pharpar, the rivers of Damascus, better than the Jordan and all the waters of Israel? Could I not go and bathe in them and be cleansed?’ He turned aside and went away in anger.
    2 Kings 5:13 His attendants approached and spoke to him: ‘The prophet has told you a great thing; will you not do it? And he has said to you, Bathe and be cleansed.’
    2 Kings 5:14 And Naaman went down, and dipped himself in the Jordan seven times, according to the word of Elisha; and his flesh was restored as the flesh of a little child, and he was cleansed.

John 9:8
Then the neighbors and those who had seen him before as a beggar said, “Is this not the one who sat and begged?”
  Acts 3:9–3:10 — narrative parallel: Both describe public recognition of a formerly disabled beggar after healing. John concerns a man formerly blind; Acts concerns the man who had sat at the Beautiful Gate and now walks praising God. The healers and disabilities differ.
    Acts 3:9 All the people saw him walking and praising God.
    Acts 3:10 They recognized him as the one who used to sit for alms at the temple’s Beautiful Gate, and they were filled with wonder and astonishment at what had happened to him.

John 9:9
Some said, “This is he.” Others said, “No, but he is like him.” He said, “I am he.”
  Acts 3:9–3:10 — narrative parallel: Both healed beggars become objects of public identity recognition. John reports disagreement and the man's own identification; Acts says the people recognized the former alms-seeker now walking and praising God. The disabilities, responses and individuals differ, and the ordinary I am he is not equated with Jesus' distinct identity sayings.
    Acts 3:9 All the people saw him walking and praising God.
    Acts 3:10 They recognized him as the one who used to sit for alms at the temple’s Beautiful Gate, and they were filled with wonder and astonishment at what had happened to him.

John 9:10
So they said to him, “How, then, were your eyes opened?”
  John 9:15 — thematic parallel: The neighbors' question about how the eyes opened recurs when the Pharisees ask how the man received sight. The narrator explicitly says they asked again. This is an internal recurrence within one investigation, not a second healing or independent attestation.
    John 9:15 So the Pharisees also asked him again how he received his sight. He said to them, “He put mud on my eyes, and I washed, and I see.”

John 9:11
He answered, “The man called Jesus made mud and spread it on my eyes and said to me, ‘Go to Siloam and wash.’ So I went away and washed, and received my sight.”
  John 9:6–9:7 — thematic parallel: The man's account of Jesus making mud, applying it, directing him to Siloam, and his washing and seeing recalls the narrated actions. It is the character's report within this same account, not an independently preserved second record.
    John 9:6 Having said these things, He spat on the ground and made mud from the spittle, and spread the mud upon his eyes.
    John 9:7 And He said to him, “Go, wash in the pool of Siloam”—which is translated Sent. So he went away and washed, and came back seeing.

John 9:12
And they said to him, “Where is He?” He said, “I do not know.”
  John 5:12–5:13 — narrative parallel: Both healed men are questioned about Jesus, with different limits of knowledge. John5 says the healed man did not know who it was; John9 has already named Jesus but does not know where He is. This sharpens the reported who/where difference without inventing broader ignorance, lack of faith or an escape method.
    John 5:12 So they asked him, “Who is the man who told you, ‘Take it up and walk’?”
    John 5:13 But the man who had been healed did not know who He was, for Jesus had slipped away, since there was a crowd in the place.

John 9:13
They brought the man who had formerly been blind to the Pharisees.
  John 5:10–5:12 — thematic parallel: Both Sabbath-healing accounts place a healed man under questioning by religious opponents. John 9 says he is brought to Pharisees; John 5 concerns carrying a mat and who commanded it. The people, condition, and immediate disputed action differ.
    John 5:10 So the Jews said to the man who had been healed, “It is a Sabbath, and it is not lawful for you to take up the mat.”
    John 5:11 But he answered them, “The One who made me well said to me, ‘Take up your mat and walk.’”
    John 5:12 So they asked him, “Who is the man who told you, ‘Take it up and walk’?”

John 9:14
Now it was a Sabbath on the day when Jesus made the mud and opened his eyes.
  John 5:9–5:10 — thematic parallel: Both explicitly identify the healing day as a Sabbath. John 9 mentions making mud and opening eyes; John 5 mentions becoming well, walking, and criticism of carrying the mat. The accounts do not give identical procedures or accusations.
    John 5:9 And at once the man became well, took up his mat, and walked. Now it was a Sabbath on that day.
    John 5:10 So the Jews said to the man who had been healed, “It is a Sabbath, and it is not lawful for you to take up the mat.”

John 9:15
So the Pharisees also asked him again how he received his sight. He said to them, “He put mud on my eyes, and I washed, and I see.”
  John 9:10–9:11 — thematic parallel: The Pharisees' renewed question and the man's mud/washing/sight answer repeat the earlier neighbors' question and his fuller account. The later answer is shorter and does not repeat every earlier detail. This is internal narrative repetition.
    John 9:10 So they said to him, “How, then, were your eyes opened?”
    John 9:11 He answered, “The man called Jesus made mud and spread it on my eyes and said to me, ‘Go to Siloam and wash.’ So I went away and washed, and received my sight.”

John 9:16
Then some of the Pharisees said, “This man is not from God, because He does not keep the Sabbath.” But others said, “How can a sinful man do such signs?” And there was division among them.
  John 7:23–7:24 — thematic parallel: Both connect a Sabbath healing with dispute about proper judgment of Jesus. John 7 has Jesus compare circumcision and making a whole man well; John 9 reports divided Pharisaic judgments about Sabbath observance and signs. These are different encounters and arguments.
    John 7:23 If a person receives circumcision on a Sabbath so that the Law of Moses may not be broken, are you angry with Me because I made a whole person well on a Sabbath?
    John 7:24 Do not judge according to appearance, but judge with righteous judgment.”

John 9:17
So they said to the blind man again, “What do you say about Him, since He opened your eyes?” He said, “He is a prophet.”
  Luke 7:16 — thematic parallel: Both report recognition of Jesus as a prophet after a miracle. John's healed man says He is a prophet because his eyes were opened; Luke's crowd acclaims a great prophet after a dead son is raised. Neither the witnesses nor the miracles are the same.
    Luke 7:16 Fear seized everyone, and they glorified God, saying, “A great prophet has arisen among us,” and, “God has visited His people.”

John 9:18
So the Jews did not believe concerning him that he had been blind and received his sight, until they called the parents of the man who had received his sight
  Acts 4:14–4:16 — thematic parallel: Both portray authorities confronting evidence of a healing. John's investigators first doubt prior blindness and summon parents; Acts' authorities see the healed man and admit the notable sign cannot be denied. The ailments and initial responses differ.
    Acts 4:14 Seeing the man who had been healed standing with them, they had nothing to say against it.
    Acts 4:15 They ordered them to leave the council and conferred with one another,
    Acts 4:16 saying, “What should we do with these men? That a notable sign has taken place through them is evident to everyone living in Jerusalem, and we cannot deny it.

John 9:19
and asked them, saying, “Is this your son, whom you say was born blind? How, then, does he now see?”
  John 9:20–9:21 — narrative parallel: The parents affirm that this is their son, born blind, but say they do not know how he sees or who opened his eyes and refer the questioners to him. This answer distinguishes their certified identity and former blindness from their stated limits concerning the healing, without deciding an unstated private degree of knowledge.
    John 9:20 His parents answered, “We know that this is our son and that he was born blind.
    John 9:21 But how he now sees we do not know, or who opened his eyes we do not know. Ask him; he is of age. He will speak for himself.”

John 9:20
His parents answered, “We know that this is our son and that he was born blind.
  John 9:1 — thematic parallel: The parents confirm that their son was born blind, the condition already stated by the narrator. This agreement concerns his earlier condition, not their witnessing the cure. Both statements belong to one literary account and are not independent historical attestations.
    John 9:1 And as He passed by, He saw a man blind from birth.

John 9:21
But how he now sees we do not know, or who opened his eyes we do not know. Ask him; he is of age. He will speak for himself.”
  John 9:22–9:23 — narrative parallel: The narrator explicitly relates the parents' response to fear of synagogue exclusion, then repeats their referral to their adult son. This stated explanation contextualizes their limited-knowledge answer without declaring that they lied, changing their reported knowledge or assigning the same response to every parent.
    John 9:22 His parents said these things because they feared the Jews, for the Jews had already agreed that if anyone confessed Him as Christ, he should be put out of the synagogue.
    John 9:23 For this reason his parents said, “He is of age; ask him.”

John 9:22
His parents said these things because they feared the Jews, for the Jews had already agreed that if anyone confessed Him as Christ, he should be put out of the synagogue.
  John 12:42–12:43 — thematic parallel: Both connect inhibited confession of Jesus with fear of synagogue exclusion. John 9 concerns the healed man's parents and confession as Christ; John 12 concerns believing rulers and fear of Pharisees, with an added glory contrast. The people and stated motives are not identical.
    John 12:42 Nevertheless, many even from the rulers believed in Him, but because of the Pharisees they did not confess Him, so that they might not be put out of the synagogue,
    John 12:43 for they loved the glory of people more than the glory of God.

John 9:24
So for the second time they called the man who had been blind and said to him, “Give glory to God. We know that this man is a sinner.”
  Joshua 7:19 — allusion: Both use the solemn appeal to give glory to God during questioning. Joshua addresses Achar about his own conduct; John's authorities press the healed man while declaring Jesus a sinner. The verbal correspondence does not endorse that accusation or equate the healed man with Achar.
    Joshua 7:19 Joshua said to Achar, “Give glory today to the Lord, the God of Israel, and make confession. Tell me what you have done, and do not hide it from me.”

John 9:25
He answered, “Whether He is a sinner I do not know. One thing I know: though I was blind, now I see.”
  Acts 4:19–4:20 — thematic parallel: Both emphasize testimony grounded in personal experience under official pressure. The healed man distinguishes uncertainty about the sinner accusation from knowing he now sees; Acts' apostles insist on speaking what they saw and heard. Their knowledge claims and speakers differ.
    Acts 4:19 Peter and John answered them, “Judge whether it is right before God to listen to you rather than to God;
    Acts 4:20 for we cannot keep from speaking about what we have seen and heard.”

John 9:26
So they said to him, “What did He do to you? How did He open your eyes?”
  John 9:15 — thematic parallel: The authorities repeat their request for the healing method, already answered in the earlier Pharisaic questioning. This internal recurrence explains why the man next says he has already told them; it does not describe another opening of his eyes.
    John 9:15 So the Pharisees also asked him again how he received his sight. He said to them, “He put mud on my eyes, and I washed, and I see.”

John 9:27
He answered them, “I told you already, and you did not hear. Why do you want to hear again? Do you also want to become His disciples?”
  John 9:15 — thematic parallel: The earlier answer supplies the explicit report to which 'I told you already' refers. John's later question about wanting discipleship is not evidence that the investigators actually desired to become disciples. The earlier answer and later challenge remain distinct.
    John 9:15 So the Pharisees also asked him again how he received his sight. He said to them, “He put mud on my eyes, and I washed, and I see.”

John 9:28
They reviled him and said, “You are that man’s disciple, but we are Moses’ disciples.
  John 5:45–5:47 — thematic parallel: The authorities oppose their claimed Mosaic discipleship to the man's association with Jesus. Earlier Jesus argues that believing Moses would lead to believing Him. The contrasting claims are preserved rather than endorsing the reviling or treating Moses himself as a false teacher.
    John 5:45 Do not think that I will accuse you before the Father. There is one who accuses you: Moses, in whom you have set your hope.
    John 5:46 For if you believed Moses, you would believe Me, for he wrote concerning Me.
    John 5:47 But if you do not believe his writings, how will you believe My words?”

John 9:29
We know that God has spoken to Moses, but this man—we do not know where He is from.”
  Exodus 33:11 — thematic parallel: Exodus narrates the Lord speaking with Moses, providing background for the authorities' first claim about God speaking to him. It does not attest their dismissal of Jesus or settle the claimed ignorance of His origin. Moses' conversation remains its own scene.
    Exodus 33:11 The Lord spoke to Moses face to face, as a man speaks to his friend. Then Moses returned to the camp, but his young attendant Joshua son of Nun did not leave the tent.

John 9:30
The man answered them, “Why, this is the marvelous thing: you do not know where He is from, and He opened my eyes!
  John 3:2 — thematic parallel: Both connect Jesus' extraordinary works with the question of His origin from God. The healed man marvels that the authorities do not know His origin despite his opened eyes; Nicodemus explicitly reasons that Jesus' signs require God's presence. The speakers' formulations differ.
    John 3:2 He came to Him at night and said to Him, “Rabbi, we know that Thou hast come from God as a teacher, for no one can do these signs that Thou doest unless God is with him.”

John 9:31
We know that God does not hear sinners, but if anyone worships God and does His will, He hears that person.
  Proverbs 15:29 — thematic parallel: The healed man's prayer argument resembles Proverbs' contrast between the ungodly and the righteous person's prayer. John attributes the argument to this speaker; the correspondence does not by itself make it a complete rule excluding every repentant sinner's plea.
    Proverbs 15:29 God is far distant from the impious, but He listens to the prayers of the righteous.
  Luke 18:13–18:14 — thematic parallel: Luke's parable supplies a complementary case: a self-confessed sinner asks God for mercy and goes home justified. This qualifies any indiscriminate reading of the healed man's prayer maxim. The tax collector's repentant plea is not the same situation as the accusation against Jesus.
    Luke 18:13 But the tax collector, standing far away, would not even lift his eyes to heaven, but beat his breast, saying, ‘God, be merciful to me, the sinner.’
    Luke 18:14 I tell you, this man went down to his house justified rather than the other. For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, but the one who humbles himself will be exalted.”

John 9:32
Since the age began, no one has heard of anyone opening the eyes of a person born blind.
  Isaiah 35:5 — thematic parallel: Isaiah promises opened blind eyes and hearing for deaf ears. The healed man emphasizes the unheard-of opening of eyes blind from birth. This is restoration-promise background; Isaiah does not itself specify congenital blindness or assert the exact historical claim made in John.
    Isaiah 35:5 Then blind people’s eyes will open, and deaf people’s ears will hear.

John 9:33
If this man were not from God, He could do nothing.”
  John 3:2 — thematic parallel: Both infer God's involvement from Jesus' works. The healed man says someone not from God could do nothing; Nicodemus says no one could do these signs unless God were with him. The from-God and God-with-him formulations are related but not identical.
    John 3:2 He came to Him at night and said to Him, “Rabbi, we know that Thou hast come from God as a teacher, for no one can do these signs that Thou doest unless God is with him.”

John 9:34
They answered him, “You were born entirely in sins, and do you teach us?” And they cast him out.
  John 16:2 — thematic parallel: John 9 describes the man being cast out after his testimony; the later discourse warns followers of synagogue exclusion and further persecution. Chapter9's earlier exclusion policy gives context, but this verse is not expanded into a stated legal procedure or the man's death.
    John 16:2 They will put you out of the synagogues; indeed, an hour is coming when everyone who kills you will think he is offering worship to God.

John 9:35
Jesus heard that they had cast him out, and when He found him, He said, “Do you believe in the Son of Man?”
  John 3:14–3:15 — thematic parallel: Both connect the Son of Man with belief. John 9 asks the found man whether he believes in the Son of Man; John 3 speaks of that figure's lifting up and eternal life in relation to belief. The saved Son-of-Man title is not replaced with another textual variant.
    John 3:14 And just as Moses lifted up the snake in the wilderness, so the Son of Man must be lifted up,
    John 3:15 so that everyone who believes may have eternal life in Him.

John 9:37
Jesus said to him, “You have both seen Him, and the One speaking with you is He.”
  John 4:25–4:26 — thematic parallel: Both have Jesus identify the expected figure with the One speaking to the hearer. John 9 answers about the Son of Man and adds having seen Him; John 4 answers the woman's Messiah expectation. The exact self-identification pattern does not erase the different titles or scenes.
    John 4:25 The woman said to Him, “I know that Messiah is coming, the One called Christ. When He comes, He will announce all things to us.”
    John 4:26 Jesus said to her, “I am He, the One speaking to you.”

John 9:38
He said, “I believe, Lord,” and worshiped Him.
  Matthew 14:33 — narrative parallel: Both describe worship directed to Jesus after a miraculous encounter. The healed man says he believes; Matthew's boat occupants acclaim Him as God's Son after the water episode. The people, prior miracle, and spoken confession differ.
    Matthew 14:33 Those in the boat worshiped Him, saying, “Truly Thou art God’s Son.”

John 9:39
And Jesus said, “For judgment I came into this world, so that those who do not see may see, and those who see may become blind.”
  Luke 10:21 — thematic parallel: Both present a reversal in spiritual understanding. John uses seeing and blindness in a judgment saying; Luke records Jesus acknowledging the Father for hiding things from the wise and revealing them to infants. The metaphors differ and neither comparison treats physical disability itself as moral guilt.
    Luke 10:21 At that very hour He rejoiced in the Holy Spirit and said, “I acknowledge Thee, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because Thou didst hide these things from the wise and understanding and reveal them to infants. Yes, Father, for such was Thy good pleasure.

John 9:40
Some of the Pharisees who were with Him heard these things and said to Him, “Are we also blind?”
  Matthew 15:12–15:14 — thematic parallel: Both exchanges associate Pharisaic criticism with blindness imagery. In John some nearby Pharisees ask whether they too are blind; in Matthew offended Pharisees are followed by Jesus' blind-guide saying. The episodes and the extent of each reported group remain distinct.
    Matthew 15:12 Then the disciples came and said to Him, “Dost Thou know that the Pharisees stumbled when they heard this saying?”
    Matthew 15:13 He answered, “Every plant that My heavenly Father did not plant will be uprooted.
    Matthew 15:14 Leave them; they are blind guides of the blind. If a blind person guides a blind person, both will fall into a pit.”

John 9:41
Jesus said to them, “If you were blind, you would not have sin. But now you say, ‘We see’; your sin remains.”
  Luke 12:47–12:48 — thematic parallel: Both connect knowledge with responsibility. John challenges a claim to see and says sin remains; Luke distinguishes greater and lesser punishment according to a servant's knowledge. Luke still assigns some punishment to ignorance, so the sayings are not made identical exemptions from accountability.
    Luke 12:47 That slave who knew his master’s will and did not prepare or act according to his will shall receive many blows.
    Luke 12:48 But the one who did not know and did things deserving blows shall receive few. From everyone to whom much was given, much will be required; and from the one to whom they entrusted much, they will ask all the more.

John 10:1
“Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who does not enter the sheepfold through the door, but climbs in from elsewhere, is a thief and a robber.
  John 10:10 — thematic parallel: The later explanation gives the thief's destructive purpose, clarifying the earlier unauthorized entry image. Jesus contrasts that purpose with His own life-giving mission. This is one discourse's developing figure, not a second record of a historical burglar.
    John 10:10 The thief comes only to steal and slaughter and destroy. I came so that they may have life and have abundance.

John 10:2
But the one who enters through the door is the shepherd of the sheep.
  John 10:11 — thematic parallel: The introductory figure distinguishes the legitimate shepherd; Jesus later identifies Himself as the Good Shepherd. This internal identification does not require assigning every door, entrant, and doorkeeper detail a single fixed personal equivalent.
    John 10:11 I am the good Shepherd; the good Shepherd lays down His life for the sheep.

John 10:3
To him the doorkeeper opens, and the sheep hear his voice. He calls his own sheep by name and leads them out.
  John 10:16 — thematic parallel: Both describe sheep hearing the shepherd's voice. The introduction concerns his own sheep being named and led out; the later saying concerns other sheep being brought into one flock. The doorkeeper is not independently identified with a named biblical person.
    John 10:16 And I have other sheep that are not from this fold. I must bring them also, and they will hear My voice, and they will become one flock, one shepherd.

John 10:4
When he has brought all his own out, he goes before them, and the sheep follow him, because they know his voice.
  John 10:27 — thematic parallel: Both join the shepherd's voice with the sheep following. Here the sheep know his voice; later Jesus says He knows His sheep. The direction of each knowledge statement is preserved within the recurring figure.
    John 10:27 My sheep hear My voice, and I know them, and they follow Me.

John 10:5
But they will certainly not follow a stranger; rather, they will flee from him, because they do not know the voice of strangers.”
  John 10:8 — thematic parallel: Refusal to follow an unfamiliar voice is related to the later statement that the sheep did not hear the thieves and robbers. This internal explanation does not supply a list of historical strangers or claim every earlier prophet was a thief.
    John 10:8 All who came before Me are thieves and robbers, but the sheep did not hear them.

John 10:6
Jesus spoke this figure of speech to them, but they did not understand what He was saying to them.
  John 16:25 — thematic parallel: Both explicitly discuss figurative speech. The narrator says the hearers did not understand the sheep figure; the later Jesus contrasts His figures with coming plain speech about the Father. These are different teaching settings, not a rule that every image is unintelligible.
    John 16:25 “These things I have spoken to you in figures of speech. An hour is coming when I will no longer speak to you in figures of speech, but will tell you plainly about the Father.

John 10:7
So Jesus spoke to them again: “Amen, amen, I tell you that I am the door of the sheep.
  John 14:6 — thematic parallel: Both make Jesus the means of access through a personal image. John 10 calls Him the sheep's Door; John 14 calls Him the Way and says no one comes to the Father except through Him. Door and Way remain distinct metaphors.
    John 14:6 Jesus says to him, “I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through Me.

John 10:8
All who came before Me are thieves and robbers, but the sheep did not hear them.
  Jeremiah 23:1–23:2 — thematic parallel: Jeremiah condemns shepherds who destroy and scatter God's sheep; John speaks of thieves and robbers whom the sheep did not hear. This is related counterfeit-leadership imagery, not an identification of every earlier God-sent messenger with the thieves.
    Jeremiah 23:1 O shepherds who scatter and destroy the sheep of My pasture!
    Jeremiah 23:2 Therefore thus says the Lord to those shepherding My people: You scattered My sheep and drove them out without visiting them. Behold, I am exacting justice from you according to your evil practices.

John 10:9
I am the door. If anyone enters through Me, he will be saved, and will go in and go out and find pasture.
  Psalms 22:1–22:2 — thematic parallel: Both use flock-provision imagery. John's sheep find pasture through Jesus as the Door; the Psalm describes the Lord's shepherding, nourishment, and restful water. The pastoral background is compared without treating Door and shepherd as one unchanging allegorical role.
    Psalms 22:1 A psalm of David. The Lord shepherds me, and nothing shall fail me.
    Psalms 22:2 He made me dwell in a place of green growth; He nourished me beside water of rest.

John 10:10
The thief comes only to steal and slaughter and destroy. I came so that they may have life and have abundance.
  Ezekiel 34:2–34:4 — thematic parallel: Both contrast the flock's good with exploitative leadership. Ezekiel's shepherds consume milk, use wool, and slaughter the fattened animal while failing to feed and care for the sheep; John's thief steals, slaughters, and destroys, against Jesus' abundant life. These are related destructive figures, not a fixed roster of identities.
    Ezekiel 34:2 “Son of man, prophesy against Israel’s shepherds. Prophesy and say to the shepherds, ‘Thus says the Lord, the Lord: O shepherds of Israel, surely shepherds do not feed themselves? Do shepherds not feed the sheep?
    Ezekiel 34:3 Behold, you consume the milk, clothe yourselves with the wool and slaughter the fattened animal, but you do not feed My sheep.
    Ezekiel 34:4 You have not strengthened the weak, restored the sick to health, bound up the broken, brought back the wandering or sought the lost. You have worn down the strong with toil.

John 10:11
I am the good Shepherd; the good Shepherd lays down His life for the sheep.
  Mark 10:45 — thematic parallel: Both describe Jesus giving His life for others. John uses the Good Shepherd and His sheep; Mark uses the Son of Man's service and a ransom for many. The images and beneficiary expressions are not silently interchanged.
    Mark 10:45 For even the Son of Man did not come to be served but to serve, and to give His life as a ransom in exchange for many.”

John 10:12
The hired worker, who is not a shepherd and does not own the sheep, sees the wolf coming and leaves the sheep and flees—and the wolf seizes them and scatters them—
  Ezekiel 34:5–34:6 — thematic parallel: Both depict vulnerable sheep scattered and exposed to predators when care fails. Ezekiel describes wandering sheep without a shepherd; John describes a hired worker fleeing a wolf. The absence of care and the particular form of failure differ.
    Ezekiel 34:5 My sheep scattered because there were no shepherds and became food for all the field’s beasts.
    Ezekiel 34:6 My sheep scattered on every mountain and upon every high hill, and scattered over the whole face of the earth. There was no one seeking them out or turning them back.

John 10:13
because he is a hired worker and does not care for the sheep.
  1 Peter 5:2–5:3 — thematic parallel: John explains the hired worker's flight by lack of care for the sheep. First Peter urges willing, eager shepherding rather than base gain or domination. This is a contrast of pastoral motives, not a blanket declaration that every paid worker is uncaring.
    1 Peter 5:2 Shepherd the flock of God among you, exercising oversight, not under compulsion but willingly, according to God; not for shameful gain but eagerly;
    1 Peter 5:3 not as lording it over those allotted to you, but becoming examples to the flock.

John 10:14
I am the good Shepherd, and I know My own, and My own know Me,
  2 Timothy 2:19 — thematic parallel: Both describe the Lord's knowledge of those belonging to Him. John says Jesus knows His own and they know Him; Second Timothy's seal says 'The Lord knew those who are His' and adds departure from unrighteousness. The present and past formulations and reciprocal clause remain distinct.
    2 Timothy 2:19 Nevertheless, the firm foundation of God stands, having this seal: “The Lord knew those who are His,” and, “Let everyone who names the name of the Lord depart from unrighteousness.”

John 10:15
just as the Father knows Me and I know the Father; and I lay down My life for the sheep.
  Matthew 11:27 — thematic parallel: Both affirm mutual Father-Son knowledge. John uses it in relation to shepherd-and-sheep knowledge and Jesus' self-giving; Matthew also speaks of the Son revealing the Father. Neither is a verbatim repetition of the other's full argument.
    Matthew 11:27 All things have been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.

John 10:16
And I have other sheep that are not from this fold. I must bring them also, and they will hear My voice, and they will become one flock, one shepherd.
  Ezekiel 37:24 — thematic parallel: Both use one-shepherd language. Ezekiel names David as ruler and speaks of one shepherd over all in Israel's restoration setting; John promises other sheep hearing Jesus and one flock, one shepherd. John says flock rather than fold here, and this comparison does not itself identify every group of sheep.
    Ezekiel 37:24 My servant David will be ruler among them, and one shepherd will be over all, because they will walk in My ordinances, keep My judgments and do them.

John 10:17
For this reason the Father loves Me, because I lay down My life, so that I may take it again.
  John 2:19–2:22 — thematic parallel: Both relate Jesus to the raising of His own body or the taking of His life again. John 2 interprets the sanctuary saying as about His body and later resurrection; John 10 speaks of laying down life, taking it again, and the Father's love. Their images differ.
    John 2:19 Jesus answered them, “Destroy this sanctuary, and in three days I will raise it.”
    John 2:20 So the Jews said, “This sanctuary was built in forty-six years, and wilt Thou raise it in three days?”
    John 2:21 But He was speaking about the sanctuary of His body.
    John 2:22 When, therefore, He was raised from the dead, His disciples remembered that He had said this, and they believed the Scripture and the word Jesus had spoken.

John 10:18
No one takes it from Me, but I lay it down of My own accord. I have authority to lay it down, and I have authority to take it again. This command I received from My Father.”
  John 19:10–19:11 — thematic parallel: Both discuss authority concerning Jesus' death. John 10 states His own authority to lay down and take His life under the Father's command; John 19 makes Pilate's claimed authority dependent on a grant from above. Voluntary self-giving does not erase the human agency reported in the Passion.
    John 19:10 Then Pilate says to Him, “Dost Thou not speak to me? Dost Thou not know that I have authority to release Thee, and I have authority to crucify Thee?”
    John 19:11 Jesus answered him, “You would have no authority against Me unless it had been given you from above. For this reason the one who handed Me over to you has greater sin.”

John 10:19
Division arose again among the Jews because of these words.
  John 9:16 — thematic parallel: The renewed division recalls the earlier division over the Sabbath healing and whether Jesus could be from God. John 10 concerns His present words and ensuing demon dispute. This is the Gospel's recurring divided response, not two independent accounts of one conversation.
    John 9:16 Then some of the Pharisees said, “This man is not from God, because He does not keep the Sabbath.” But others said, “How can a sinful man do such signs?” And there was division among them.

John 10:20
Many of them said, “He has a demon and is mad. Why do you listen to Him?”
  John 7:20 — thematic parallel: Both report an accusation that Jesus has a demon. John 10 adds madness and a question about listening; John 7's crowd asks who seeks to kill Him. These are hostile speakers' claims, not narrator affirmations.
    John 7:20 The crowd answered, “Thou hast a demon! Who seeks to kill Thee?”

John 10:21
Others said, “These are not the words of a demon-possessed person. Can a demon open the eyes of the blind?”
  John 9:6–9:7 — thematic parallel: The question about opening blind eyes recalls the earlier narrated healing with mud and washing. The later speakers use such a work against the demon accusation. Their rhetorical question is not an independently preserved account of a second healing.
    John 9:6 Having said these things, He spat on the ground and made mud from the spittle, and spread the mud upon his eyes.
    John 9:7 And He said to him, “Go, wash in the pool of Siloam”—which is translated Sent. So he went away and washed, and came back seeing.

John 10:22
Then the feast of Dedication took place in Jerusalem. It was winter,
  1 Maccabees 4:52–4:59 — thematic parallel: First Maccabees narrates the altar's rededication and the annual eight-day observance beginning on the twenty-fifth of the ninth month. This supplies background for John's feast of Dedication. It does not narrate Jesus' visit or fix its year from the Maccabean date.
    1 Maccabees 4:52 They rose early in the morning on the twenty-fifth day of the ninth month, which is the month of Chislev, in the one hundred and forty-eighth year.
    1 Maccabees 4:53 They offered sacrifice according to the law upon the new altar of whole burnt offerings that they had made.
    1 Maccabees 4:54 At the season and on the day on which the nations had profaned it, it was dedicated with songs, harps, lyres, and cymbals.
    1 Maccabees 4:55 All the people fell face down, worshiped, and blessed toward heaven the One who had prospered them.
    1 Maccabees 4:56 They celebrated the dedication of the altar for eight days, offered whole burnt offerings with gladness, and sacrificed a sacrifice of well-being and praise.
    1 Maccabees 4:57 They adorned the front of the temple with golden crowns and small shields, renewed the gateways and chambers, and fitted them with doors.
    1 Maccabees 4:58 There was exceedingly great gladness among the people, and the reproach of the nations was turned away.
    1 Maccabees 4:59 Judas, his brothers, and the whole assembly of Israel established that the days of the altar’s dedication should be celebrated at their proper season, year by year, for eight days beginning on the twenty-fifth of the month of Chislev, with gladness and joy.

John 10:23
and Jesus was walking in the temple, in Solomon’s Portico.
  Acts 3:11 — narrative parallel: Both locate a scene at the portico named Solomon's. John has Jesus walking in the Temple; Acts has Peter, John, the healed man, and an astonished gathering. The named setting is shared, while the participants and occasions differ.
    Acts 3:11 As he held on to Peter and John, all the people ran together to them at the portico called Solomon’s, utterly astonished.

John 10:24
So the Jews surrounded Him and said to Him, “How long wilt Thou keep our soul in suspense? If Thou art the Christ, tell us openly.”
  Luke 22:67–22:68 — thematic parallel: Both report a demand that Jesus say whether He is the Christ. John places it at the feast of Dedication; Luke places it in the Passion interrogation and records His reply about unbelief and unanswered questions. These are different confrontations.
    Luke 22:67 saying, “If Thou art the Christ, tell us.” But He said to them, “If I tell you, you will certainly not believe.
    Luke 22:68 And if I ask, you will certainly not answer.

John 10:25
Jesus answered them, “I told you, and you do not believe. The works I do in My Father’s name bear witness concerning Me.
  John 5:36 — thematic parallel: Both describe Jesus' works as testimony about Him in relation to the Father. John 10 speaks of works done in the Father's name; John 5 speaks of works given to complete and testifying that the Father sent Him. The giving and name-language are preserved separately.
    John 5:36 But I have testimony greater than John’s: for the works the Father has given Me to complete, the very works I do, bear witness concerning Me that the Father has sent Me.

John 10:26
But you do not believe, because you are not from My sheep.
  John 8:47 — thematic parallel: Both explain lack of reception through relationship language. John 8 speaks of not hearing God's words because one is not from God; John 10 speaks of unbelief because the hearers are not His sheep. The comparison does not supply a separate account of how one becomes a sheep.
    John 8:47 The one who is from God hears the words of God. For this reason you do not hear, because you are not from God.”

John 10:27
My sheep hear My voice, and I know them, and they follow Me.
  John 10:3–10:4 — thematic parallel: The personal sheep-and-voice statement recalls the opening figure's hearing and following. The earlier sheep know the shepherd's voice; here Jesus says He knows them and they follow Him. Their different directions of knowing are retained.
    John 10:3 To him the doorkeeper opens, and the sheep hear his voice. He calls his own sheep by name and leads them out.
    John 10:4 When he has brought all his own out, he goes before them, and the sheep follow him, because they know his voice.

John 10:28
And I give them eternal life, and they will certainly never perish, and no one will snatch them from My hand.
  John 6:39–6:40 — thematic parallel: Both connect eternal life and the preservation of those given by the Father. John 6 speaks of losing none and last-day raising; John 10 speaks of never perishing and no snatching from Jesus' hand. The preservation images are related without replacing their particular words.
    John 6:39 And this is the will of the One who sent Me: that of everything He has given Me I should lose nothing, but raise it up on the last day.
    John 6:40 For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”

John 10:29
What My Father has given Me is greater than all, and no one can snatch from the Father’s hand.
  John 17:6 — thematic parallel: John 10 says 'What My Father has given Me is greater than all' and speaks of protection in the Father's hand. John 17 explicitly describes people given to Jesus and belonging to the Father. This compares giving and belonging without changing John 10's grammatical subject to the Father.
    John 17:6 “I revealed Thy name to the people whom Thou gavest Me out of the world. They were Thine, and Thou gavest them to Me, and they have kept Thy word.

John 10:30
I and the Father are one.”
  John 17:21–17:23 — thematic parallel: Both use Father-Son oneness language. John 10 briefly says 'I and the Father are one'; John 17 describes reciprocal indwelling and prays for believers' unity. The related language is not made an exhaustive definition of every sense of 'one' in the earlier saying.
    John 17:21 so that they may all be one, just as Thou, Father, art in Me and I in Thee, so that they also may be in Us, so that the world may believe that Thou didst send Me.
    John 17:22 And the glory that Thou hast given Me I have given them, so that they may be one just as We are one:
    John 17:23 I in them and Thou in Me, so that they may be made completely one, so that the world may know that Thou didst send Me and didst love them just as Thou didst love Me.

John 10:31
Then the Jews took up stones again to stone Him.
  John 8:59 — thematic parallel: Taking up stones again recalls the earlier attempt after the Abraham exchange. John 8 describes Jesus hiding and leaving the Temple; John 10 pauses for His response about works. They are recurring hostile actions within one Gospel, not independent accounts of the same attempt.
    John 8:59 So they took up stones to cast at Him, but Jesus hid Himself and went out of the temple.

John 10:32
Jesus answered them, “I showed you many good works from the Father. For which work among them do you stone Me?”
  John 5:17–5:18 — thematic parallel: Both controversies connect Jesus' relationship to the Father's work with an intention to kill Him. John 5 concerns Sabbath work and equality with God; John 10 asks which good work warrants stoning. The next reply's charge and the earlier narrator's explanation retain their different settings.
    John 5:17 But Jesus answered them, “My Father is working even now, and I am working.”
    John 5:18 For this reason, therefore, the Jews sought all the more to kill Him, because He not only broke the Sabbath, but also called God His own Father, making Himself equal to God.

John 10:33
The Jews answered Him, “We do not stone Thee for a good work, but for blasphemy, and because Thou, being a man, makest Thyself God.”
  John 5:18 — thematic parallel: Both report God-related grounds for hostility toward Jesus. John 5 describes His calling God His own Father and making Himself equal to God; John 10 quotes the accusation of a man making himself God. The Sabbath issue occurs explicitly in John 5 rather than this reply.
    John 5:18 For this reason, therefore, the Jews sought all the more to kill Him, because He not only broke the Sabbath, but also called God His own Father, making Himself equal to God.

John 10:34
Jesus answered them, “Is it not written in your Law, ‘I said, you are gods’?
  Psalms 81:6 — quotation: Jesus quotes the Psalm's 'you are gods' wording. The Psalm also calls its addressees sons of the Most High. John calls this written text Law in the argument; the comparison does not supply a definitive identity for every addressee called a god.
    Psalms 81:6 “I said, ‘You are gods, and all sons of the Most High.’

John 10:35
If He called them gods, to whom the word of God came—and the Scripture cannot be broken—
  Matthew 5:17–5:18 — thematic parallel: Both affirm Scripture's enduring force in Jesus' teaching. John says Scripture cannot be broken while discussing its god-language; Matthew speaks of fulfilling the Law and Prophets and no iota or stroke passing before accomplishment. Their claims and formulations are related, not identical quotations.
    Matthew 5:17 Do not think that I came to abolish the Law or the Prophets. I did not come to abolish, but to fulfil.
    Matthew 5:18 For truly I tell you: until heaven and earth pass away, not one iota nor one stroke shall pass from the Law, until all things come to pass.

John 10:36
do you say to the One whom the Father sanctified and sent into the world, ‘Thou blasphemest,’ because I said, ‘I am the Son of God’?
  John 17:17–17:19 — thematic parallel: Both connect consecration with sending into the world. John 10 names the Father consecrating and sending Jesus; John 17 prays for the disciples, compares their sending with His own, and describes Jesus consecrating Himself for them. The agents and recipients of consecration remain distinct.
    John 17:17 Sanctify them in the truth; Thy word is truth.
    John 17:18 Just as Thou didst send Me into the world, I also sent them into the world;
    John 17:19 and for their sake I sanctify Myself, so that they also may be sanctified in truth.

John 10:37
If I do not do My Father’s works, do not believe Me.
  John 5:36 — thematic parallel: Both present works associated with the Father as grounds for testimony concerning Jesus. John 10 states the negative condition: if He does not do the Father's works, do not believe Him. John 5 positively describes given works testifying to His sending.
    John 5:36 But I have testimony greater than John’s: for the works the Father has given Me to complete, the very works I do, bear witness concerning Me that the Father has sent Me.

John 10:38
But if I do them, even if you do not believe Me, believe the works, so that you may know and continue to know that the Father is in Me and I in the Father.”
  John 14:10–14:11 — thematic parallel: Both connect the works with Father-Son indwelling, but John 10 urges belief in the works so the hearers may know and continue to know that indwelling; John 14 urges belief in the indwelling or because of the works. John 14 also distinguishes Jesus' speech from the Father's indwelling works. This is a close internal recurrence of reciprocal language, not independent historical attestation.
    John 14:10 Do you not believe that I am in the Father and the Father is in Me? The words that I say to you I do not speak from Myself; but the Father, who remains in Me, does His works.
    John 14:11 Believe Me that I am in the Father and the Father is in Me. But if not, believe because of the works themselves.

John 10:39
So they sought again to seize Him, and He went out from their hand.
  Luke 4:29–4:30 — narrative parallel: Both describe a hostile attempt against Jesus ending in His departure. Luke has a crowd lead Him toward a precipice before He passes through them; John says they seek to seize Him and He leaves their hand. John's unstated escape method is not supplied from Luke.
    Luke 4:29 They rose, threw Him out of the city, and led Him to the brow of the hill on which their city was built, intending to hurl Him down.
    Luke 4:30 But He passed through their midst and went on His way.

John 10:40
And He went away again beyond the Jordan, to the place where John was first baptizing, and remained there.
  John 1:28 — thematic parallel: The return to John's earlier baptism site recalls the named Bethany beyond the Jordan. Both concern the Baptist's location; this Bethany is not automatically identified with the later Bethany associated with Martha and Lazarus. The later return is internal narrative retrospection.
    John 1:28 These things happened in Bethany beyond the Jordan, where John was baptizing.

John 10:41
Many came to Him and said, “John indeed did no sign, but everything John said concerning this man was true.”
  John 1:29–1:34 — thematic parallel: The crowd's assessment recalls the Baptist's earlier testimony about Jesus, including the Lamb and Spirit witness. John 10 explicitly says John performed no sign; the Spirit's descent witnessed in John 1 is not a miracle performed by John. Both reports belong to this Gospel.
    John 1:29 The next day he saw Jesus coming toward him and said, “Behold, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!
    John 1:30 This is He of whom I said, ‘After me comes a man who has come before me, because He was before me.’
    John 1:31 And I did not know Him, but I came baptizing in water for this reason: so that He might be revealed to Israel.”
    John 1:32 And John testified, saying, “I have beheld the Spirit descending like a dove from heaven, and He remained upon Him.
    John 1:33 And I did not know Him, but the One who sent me to baptize in water said to me, ‘The One upon whom you see the Spirit descending and remaining—this is the One who baptizes in the Holy Spirit.’
    John 1:34 And I have seen and have testified that this is the Son of God.”

John 10:42
And many believed in Him there.
  John 4:39–4:42 — thematic parallel: Both settings join testimony about Jesus, His presence among the people, and many believing. Samaria has the woman's witness followed by hearing Jesus; beyond the Jordan the preceding verse recalls John's witness. The witnesses and communities are not identified with one another.
    John 4:39 Many of the Samaritans from that city believed in Him because of the word of the woman, who testified, “He told me everything I did.”
    John 4:40 So when the Samaritans came to Him, they asked Him to stay with them, and He stayed there two days.
    John 4:41 And many more believed because of His word.
    John 4:42 They said to the woman, “We no longer believe because of your speech, for we ourselves have heard and know that this is truly the Savior of the world.”

John 11:1
Now a certain man was ill, Lazarus of Bethany, from the village of Mary and her sister Martha.
  John 12:1 — internal character-and-place callback: Both identify Lazarus at Bethany in Jesus' narrative. John11 introduces his illness and his sisters; John12 explicitly recalls his raising.
