This links the same named household without identifying him with Luke's parable Lazarus or independently establishing every biographical detail.
    John 12:1 So six days before the Passover, Jesus came to Bethany, where Lazarus was, whom Jesus had raised from the dead.

John 11:2
It was Mary who anointed the Lord with perfumed ointment and wiped His feet with her hair, whose brother Lazarus was ill.
  John 12:3 — internal anticipation-and-event link: The narrator identifies Mary through the foot anointing and hair wiping that the later chapter narrates. The action and person are explicit in both; the later event is not imported as already occurring during Lazarus's illness.
    John 12:3 Then Mary took a litra of costly perfume of genuine nard, anointed Jesus’ feet, and wiped His feet with her hair. And the house was filled with the fragrance of the perfume.

John 11:3
So the sisters sent to Him, saying, “Lord, behold, the one Thou lovest is ill.”
  John 11:36 — internal love-language recurrence: The sisters describe Lazarus as the one Jesus loves; later onlookers respond to Jesus' tears by saying how He loved him. The speakers and moments differ, and their statements are not independent proof of every illness or grief detail.
    John 11:36 So the Jews said, “Behold, how He loved him!”

John 11:4
When Jesus heard, He said, “This illness is not for death, but for the glory of God, so that the Son of God may be glorified through it.”
  John 11:40 — internal glory-purpose link: Both connect this raising account with seeing or displaying God's glory. The earlier statement names the illness's purpose and the Son's glorification; the later answer to Martha names belief and seeing glory. Their audience and exact conditions are not equated.
    John 11:40 Jesus said to her, “Did I not tell you that if you believe, you will see the glory of God?”

John 11:5
Now Jesus loved Martha and her sister and Lazarus.
  John 11:3 — internal narrator-and-message link: The narrator's statement that Jesus loved Lazarus corresponds to the sisters' earlier description of him as the one Jesus loves. The narrator additionally names His love for Martha and Mary, which the earlier message does not separately repeat.
    John 11:3 So the sisters sent to Him, saying, “Lord, behold, the one Thou lovest is ill.”

John 11:6
So when He heard that he was ill, He then remained two days in the place where He was.
  John 11:14–11:15 — narrative parallel: Jesus later connects His absence at Lazarus's death with the disciples' believing. That explicit comment illuminates the absence within the earlier delay narrative. It does not state the arithmetic of the two-day stay, prove when Lazarus died relative to the message, or supply an unstated lack of love.
    John 11:14 So then Jesus told them openly, “Lazarus died,
    John 11:15 and for your sake I rejoice that I was not there, so that you may believe. But let us go to him.”

John 11:7
After this He said to the disciples, “Let us go into Judea again.”
  John 11:8 — internal proposal-and-objection link: Jesus' proposed return to Judea is immediately taken up in the disciples' objection to going there again after threatened stoning. This makes the stated destination and its narrated risk explicit, without treating the reply as another journey or independent attestation.
    John 11:8 The disciples said to Him, “Rabbi, the Jews were just now seeking to stone Thee, and art Thou going there again?”

John 11:8
The disciples said to Him, “Rabbi, the Jews were just now seeking to stone Thee, and art Thou going there again?”
  John 10:31–10:33 — internal danger callback: The disciples recall attempted stoning; the earlier controversy explicitly narrates stones taken up against Jesus and the ensuing exchange. The earlier units supply that danger, not the disciples' later objection or an independent date for it.
    John 10:31 Then the Jews took up stones again to stone Him.
    John 10:32 Jesus answered them, “I showed you many good works from the Father. For which work among them do you stone Me?”
    John 10:33 The Jews answered Him, “We do not stone Thee for a good work, but for blasphemy, and because Thou, being a man, makest Thyself God.”

John 11:9
Jesus answered, “Are there not twelve hours of the day? If anyone walks in the day, he does not stumble, because he sees the light of this world.
  John 12:35 — same-book light-and-walking comparison: Both connect walking in available light with avoiding darkness's danger. John11 uses daytime and twelve hours; John12 describes the Light's remaining presence. The different settings and temporal images are preserved.
    John 12:35 Then Jesus said to them, “For a little time longer the Light is among you. Walk while you have the Light, so that darkness may not overtake you. The one walking in the darkness does not know where he is going.

John 11:10
But if anyone walks in the night, he stumbles, because the light is not in him.”
  John 12:35 — same-book light-and-walking comparison: Both portray walking in darkness as impaired movement. John11 says stumbling because light is not in the person; John12 says not knowing where one goes. Neither sentence is substituted for the other.
    John 12:35 Then Jesus said to them, “For a little time longer the Light is among you. Walk while you have the Light, so that darkness may not overtake you. The one walking in the darkness does not know where he is going.

John 11:11
He said these things, and after this He said to them, “Lazarus our friend has fallen asleep, but I am going to awaken him.”
  Mark 5:39 — raising-language comparison: Both raising narratives use sleep language for someone whose death is at issue. John later explicitly explains Lazarus's sleep as death; Mark has Jesus say the girl is sleeping rather than dead. The persons, wording and events remain distinct.
    Mark 5:39 He went in and said to them, “Why are you making a commotion and weeping? The child has not died but is sleeping.”

John 11:12
So the disciples said to Him, “Lord, if he has fallen asleep, he will be saved.”
  John 11:13 — internal misunderstanding-and-explanation link: The disciples infer recovery from ordinary sleep; the narrator explicitly contrasts their resting-in-sleep understanding with Jesus' reference to death. This is the story's own explanation, not an independent medical account.
    John 11:13 But Jesus had spoken of his death, while they thought He was speaking of resting in sleep.

John 11:13
But Jesus had spoken of his death, while they thought He was speaking of resting in sleep.
  John 11:14 — internal explanation-and-clarification link: The narrator says Jesus meant death, and the following direct statement openly says Lazarus died. The link clarifies the sleep wording without inventing a death occurring between Jesus' two statements.
    John 11:14 So then Jesus told them openly, “Lazarus died,

John 11:14
So then Jesus told them openly, “Lazarus died,
  John 11:11 — internal figurative-and-direct contrast: Jesus' direct Lazarus-died statement explains His earlier description of the friend falling asleep and His intention to awaken him. The earlier sleep wording is retained rather than changed into a different condition or timeline.
    John 11:11 He said these things, and after this He said to them, “Lazarus our friend has fallen asleep, but I am going to awaken him.”

John 11:15
and for your sake I rejoice that I was not there, so that you may believe. But let us go to him.”
  John 11:42 — internal belief-purpose comparison: Both give the raising episode a stated belief-directed purpose. Jesus addresses disciples about His absence for their sake; later He explains His spoken prayer for the crowd's belief in His sending. The groups and means differ.
    John 11:42 I knew that Thou always hearest Me, but I said this because of the crowd standing around, so that they may believe that Thou didst send Me.”

John 11:16
Then Thomas, who was called Didymus, said to his fellow disciples, “Let us also go, so that we may die with Him.”
  Mark 14:31 — discipleship-response comparison: Both express willingness to die with Jesus. Thomas urges fellow disciples to go and die with Him; Mark records Peter's insistence and others agreeing before the arrest. Speakers, moments and the added denial language remain distinct.
    Mark 14:31 He spoke all the more insistently, “Even if I must die with Thee, I will certainly not deny Thee.” They all said the same.

John 11:17
So when Jesus came, He found that he had already been in the tomb four days.
  John 11:39 — internal duration recurrence: The four-day tomb notice is repeated in Martha's warning that there is already a smell after four days. This connects the narrator's duration with her stated concern without adding a medical explanation or a new burial chronology.
    John 11:39 Jesus said, “Take away the stone.” Martha, the dead man’s sister, said to Him, “Lord, there is already a smell, for he has been dead four days.”

John 11:18
Now Bethany was near Jerusalem, about fifteen stadia away.
  Luke 19:28–19:29 — thematic parallel: Both locate Bethany in relation to Jerusalem: John explicitly calls it near Jerusalem and gives about fifteen stadia; Luke names it with Bethphage by the Mount of Olives as Jesus goes toward Jerusalem. This geographical comparison does not independently verify the distance, fix coordinates or equate it with a Sabbath-day journey.
    Luke 19:28 And after saying these things, He went on ahead, going up to Jerusalem.
    Luke 19:29 And it happened that as He drew near Bethphage and Bethany, at the mount called Olivet, He sent two of the disciples,

John 11:19
Many of the Jews had come to Martha and Mary to console them concerning their brother.
  John 11:31 — internal consolation-group continuation: Both describe the people consoling Mary and Martha over their brother. The later verse follows those with Mary as she leaves; it does not independently attest every earlier arrival or number.
    John 11:31 So the Jews who were with her in the house consoling her, seeing that Mary rose quickly and went out, followed her, thinking that she was going to the tomb to weep there.

John 11:20
When Martha heard that Jesus was coming, she went to meet Him, but Mary remained seated in the house.
  John 11:29 — internal sister-response comparison: Martha hears Jesus is coming and goes to meet Him; Mary later hears the summons and rises quickly to come. Their responses occur at different moments, and Mary's initial staying home is not erased.
    John 11:29 When she heard, she rose quickly and came to Him.

John 11:21
So Martha said to Jesus, “Lord, if Thou hadst been here, my brother would not have died.
  John 11:32 — internal repeated-saying link: Martha and Mary each tell Jesus that their brother would not have died had He been there. The repeated saying is an explicit literary counterpart; Mary's falling at His feet belongs to her later scene.
    John 11:32 When Mary came where Jesus was and saw Him, she fell at His feet and said to Him, “Lord, if Thou hadst been here, my brother would not have died.”

John 11:22
But even now I know that whatever Thou askest of God, God will give Thee.”
  John 11:41–11:42 — internal prayer-confidence link: Martha expresses confidence that God answers Jesus; Jesus later thanks the Father for hearing Him and says He always hears. Martha's confidence and Jesus' thanksgiving have different speakers and forms, and neither is turned into a newly supplied divine promise or a record of an unreported petition.
    John 11:41 So they took away the stone. Jesus lifted His eyes upward and said, “Father, I thank Thee that Thou didst hear Me.
    John 11:42 I knew that Thou always hearest Me, but I said this because of the crowd standing around, so that they may believe that Thou didst send Me.”

John 11:23
Jesus said to her, “Your brother will rise.”
  John 11:43–11:44 — promise and narrated event: Jesus' promise that the brother will rise is followed by His call to Lazarus and the dead man's emergence. The later account supplies the concrete raising, not Martha's initial interpretation of its timing.
    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”
    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

John 11:24
Martha said to Him, “I know that he will rise in the resurrection on the last day.”
  John 6:39–6:40 — resurrection-teaching comparison: Both explicitly speak of raising on the last day. Martha applies that expectation to her brother; Jesus' earlier teaching speaks of those given to Him and those beholding and believing the Son. The individual expectation and broader promises remain distinct.
    John 6:39 And this is the will of the One who sent Me: that of everything He has given Me I should lose nothing, but raise it up on the last day.
    John 6:40 For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”

John 11:25
Jesus said to her, “I am the resurrection and the life; the one who believes in Me will live, even if he dies,
  John 6:40 — life-and-belief teaching comparison: Both connect believing in Jesus/the Son with life and resurrection. John11 calls Jesus resurrection and life even across death; John6 promises eternal life and last-day raising. Their exact descriptions and time language are preserved.
    John 6:40 For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”

John 11:26
and everyone who lives and believes in Me will certainly never die. Do you believe this?”
  John 8:51 — life-promise comparison: Both promise not beholding or undergoing death in relation to Jesus. John11 names living and believing; John8 names keeping His word. The conditions and expressions are not made verbatim or turned into an unsupported claim that every believer avoids bodily death.
    John 8:51 Amen, amen, I tell you: if anyone keeps My word, he will certainly never behold death.”

John 11:27
She said to Him, “Yes, Lord. I have believed that Thou art the Christ, the Son of God, the One coming into the world.”
  Matthew 16:16 — confession parallel: Both confess Jesus as the Christ and God's Son. Martha additionally names the One coming into the world; Peter says Son of the living God. The speakers and occasions are not identified.
    Matthew 16:16 Simon Peter answered, “Thou art the Christ, the Son of the living God.”

John 11:28
Having said this, she went away and called her sister Mary secretly, saying, “The Teacher is here and calls you.”
  Mark 10:49 — summons-message comparison: Both convey to another person that Jesus calls them. Martha secretly tells Mary the Teacher is here and calls; the crowd encourages the blind man that Jesus is calling. Recipients, messengers and circumstances differ.
    Mark 10:49 Jesus stopped and said, “Call him.” They called the blind man, saying to him, “Take courage! Get up; He is calling you.”

John 11:29
When she heard, she rose quickly and came to Him.
  John 11:20 — internal sister-response comparison: Mary quickly rises and goes after hearing the summons, corresponding to Martha's earlier going to meet Jesus upon hearing His arrival. The two sisters and the different messages remain distinct.
    John 11:20 When Martha heard that Jesus was coming, she went to meet Him, but Mary remained seated in the house.

John 11:30
Now Jesus had not yet entered the village, but was still at the place where Martha had met Him.
  John 11:20 — narrative parallel: The reference to where Martha had met Jesus looks back to her earlier going to meet Him while Mary remained in the house. This distinguishes the two sisters' meetings within the narrative. The earlier unit does not independently measure Jesus' position or repeat the later statement that He had not entered the village.
    John 11:20 When Martha heard that Jesus was coming, she went to meet Him, but Mary remained seated in the house.

John 11:31
So the Jews who were with her in the house consoling her, seeing that Mary rose quickly and went out, followed her, thinking that she was going to the tomb to weep there.
  John 11:19 — internal group callback: The people consoling Mary are the same narrative's previously introduced consolers of the sisters. The earlier notice does not independently report their following her or their mistaken assumption about a tomb visit.
    John 11:19 Many of the Jews had come to Martha and Mary to console them concerning their brother.

John 11:32
When Mary came where Jesus was and saw Him, she fell at His feet and said to Him, “Lord, if Thou hadst been here, my brother would not have died.”
  John 11:21 — internal repeated-saying link: Mary's conditional statement about Jesus' presence and her brother's death repeats Martha's earlier words. Mary also falls at His feet; that gesture is not assigned to Martha's statement.
    John 11:21 So Martha said to Jesus, “Lord, if Thou hadst been here, my brother would not have died.

John 11:33
So when Jesus saw her weeping, and the Jews who had come with her weeping, He was indignant in spirit and troubled Himself.
  John 13:21 — emotional-description comparison: Both describe Jesus being troubled in spirit. The raising scene also names indignation while people weep; the supper scene introduces His betrayal announcement. The causes, occasions and additional emotions are different.
    John 13:21 Having said these things, Jesus was troubled in spirit and testified, saying, “Amen, amen, I tell you that one of you will hand Me over.”

John 11:34
And He said, “Where have you laid him?” They said to Him, “Lord, come and see.”
  John 20:13–20:15 — narrative parallel: Both scenes combine weeping with a question about where a body has been laid. Jesus asks about Lazarus and receives a come-and-see answer; Magdalene seeks where Jesus has been laid while not recognizing Him. The speakers and bodies differ, and neither Mary is identified with the other or either question made proof of an unstated limit of divine knowledge.
    John 20:13 And they say to her, “Woman, why do you weep?” She says to them, “They took my Lord, and I do not know where they laid Him.”
    John 20:14 Having said these things, she turned backward and beholds Jesus standing there, and did not know that it was Jesus.
    John 20:15 Jesus says to her, “Woman, why do you weep? Whom do you seek?” She, supposing that He is the gardener, says to Him, “Sir, if Thou didst carry Him away, tell me where Thou didst lay Him, and I will take Him away.”

John 11:35
Jesus wept.
  Luke 19:41 — narrative emotion comparison: Both explicitly narrate Jesus weeping. John places the tears in Lazarus's mourning scene; Luke places them at the sight of Jerusalem. The objects and circumstances of grief are not equated.
    Luke 19:41 As He drew near and saw the city, He wept over it,

John 11:36
So the Jews said, “Behold, how He loved him!”
  John 11:3 — internal love-language recurrence: The onlookers' statement about Jesus loving Lazarus recalls the sisters' description of the ill man as the one He loves. Different speakers interpret different moments; the earlier message does not independently narrate these tears.
    John 11:3 So the sisters sent to Him, saying, “Lord, behold, the one Thou lovest is ill.”

John 11:37
But some of them said, “Could not this man, who opened the blind man’s eyes, have kept this man from dying too?”
  John 9:6–9:7 — internal healing-reference comparison: The onlookers recall opening a blind man's eyes; the earlier Johannine account narrates Jesus' mud-and-washing healing followed by sight. That account supplies a concrete counterpart to the recalled deed, not the later claim about preventing Lazarus's death or a list of every witness.
    John 9:6 Having said these things, He spat on the ground and made mud from the spittle, and spread the mud upon his eyes.
    John 9:7 And He said to him, “Go, wash in the pool of Siloam”—which is translated Sent. So he went away and washed, and came back seeing.

John 11:38
Then Jesus, again indignant within Himself, came to the tomb. It was a cave, and a stone lay against it.
  John 20:1 — tomb-stone comparison: Both raising accounts describe a stone at a tomb. Lazarus's cave still has its stone before the removal command; Jesus' tomb is found with its stone already removed. The tombs, deceased persons and narrative stages are different.
    John 20:1 Now on the first day of the week Mary Magdalene comes early to the tomb, while it is still dark, and sees the stone taken away from the tomb.

John 11:39
Jesus said, “Take away the stone.” Martha, the dead man’s sister, said to Him, “Lord, there is already a smell, for he has been dead four days.”
  John 11:17 — internal duration recurrence: Martha's four-day warning recalls the narrator's four-day tomb notice at Jesus' arrival. The earlier unit supports the stated duration, not an independent sensory or medical report of the smell.
    John 11:17 So when Jesus came, He found that he had already been in the tomb four days.

John 11:40
Jesus said to her, “Did I not tell you that if you believe, you will see the glory of God?”
  John 11:4 — internal glory-purpose comparison: The promise of seeing God's glory returns to the earlier statement that the illness serves God's glory and the Son's glorification. John11:4 does not name Martha as the original hearer or reproduce the later conditional belief wording.
    John 11:4 When Jesus heard, He said, “This illness is not for death, but for the glory of God, so that the Son of God may be glorified through it.”

John 11:41
So they took away the stone. Jesus lifted His eyes upward and said, “Father, I thank Thee that Thou didst hear Me.
  John 17:1 — prayer-introduction comparison: Both describe Jesus lifting His eyes heavenward and addressing Father. Here He thanks for being heard before the raising; John17 opens the hour-and-glory prayer. Their requests, responses and settings remain distinct.
    John 17:1 Jesus spoke these things, and, lifting His eyes to heaven, said, “Father, the hour has come. Glorify Thy Son, so that the Son may glorify Thee,

John 11:42
I knew that Thou always hearest Me, but I said this because of the crowd standing around, so that they may believe that Thou didst send Me.”
  John 17:8 — mission-and-belief prayer comparison: Both prayers connect hearers' belief with the Father's sending Jesus. Here Jesus explains His public words for the crowd; John17 describes disciples receiving His words and believing the sending. The crowd, disciples and reported stages of belief differ.
    John 17:8 because the words that Thou gavest Me I have given them; and they received them and truly knew that I came forth from Thee, and believed that Thou didst send Me.

John 11:43
Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”
  John 5:28–5:29 — voice-and-life sign comparison: Jesus' call into Lazarus's tomb provides a specific example to compare with His teaching that those in tombs will hear His voice and come out. One raising does not complete John5's universal future resurrection or its contrasting outcomes.
    John 5:28 Do not marvel at this, because an hour is coming in which all who are in the tombs will hear His voice
    John 5:29 and come out: those who did good things to a resurrection of life, but those who practiced base things to a resurrection of judgment.

John 11:44
The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”
  John 20:6–20:7 — grave-clothing comparison: Both accounts describe burial cloths including a face cloth. Lazarus emerges still bound and Jesus tells those present to unbind and release him; Peter finds Jesus' linen lying and face cloth separately wrapped. The addressees, states of the cloths and raising narratives are not made identical.
    John 20:6 Then Simon Peter also comes, following him, and entered the tomb; and he beholds the linen cloths lying there,
    John 20:7 and the face cloth that had been on His head, not lying with the linen cloths, but wrapped up separately in one place.

John 11:45
So many of the Jews who had come to Mary and beheld what He did believed in Him.
  John 2:23 — sign-and-belief narrative comparison: Both narrate many people believing after seeing Jesus' signs or deed. John11 concerns witnesses with Mary after Lazarus's raising; John2 concerns Jerusalem's feast signs. Neither account independently counts the other's converts or establishes identical quality of belief.
    John 2:23 While He was in Jerusalem at the Passover, during the feast, many believed in His name, seeing the signs He was doing.

John 11:46
But some of them went away to the Pharisees and told them what Jesus did.
  John 5:15–5:16 — report-and-opposition comparison: Both narrate people reporting a healing deed to religious interlocutors before opposition intensifies. John11 has some witnesses inform Pharisees; John5 has the restored man identify Jesus to the Jews. The reporters, methods and unspoken motives are not equated.
    John 5:15 The man went away and reported to the Jews that it was Jesus who made him well.
    John 5:16 And for this reason the Jews persecuted Jesus, because He did these things on a Sabbath.

John 11:47
Then the chief priests and the Pharisees gathered a council and said, “What are we doing? For this man does many signs.
  Matthew 26:3–26:4 — council-scene comparison: Both describe religious leaders gathering before a death plot against Jesus. Matthew names the high priest Caiaphas and chief priests/elders; John names chief priests/Pharisees and their acknowledgment of many signs. Their meetings, speeches and stages are not declared identical.
    Matthew 26:3 Then the chief priests and elders of the people gathered in the courtyard of the high priest called Caiaphas.
    Matthew 26:4 They took counsel to seize Jesus by deceit and kill Him.

John 11:48
If we leave Him like this, everyone will believe in Him, and the Romans will come and take away both our place and our nation.”
  John 19:12–19:15 — thematic parallel: The council invokes the Romans as a threat to their place and nation if belief spreads; the Passion scene invokes Caesar while pressuring Pilate, and the chief priests profess Caesar as king. This compares explicit Roman political arguments around Jesus without verifying the council's prediction, defining place as one compulsory referent or equating every participant.
    John 19:12 From this Pilate sought to release Him; but the Jews cried out, saying, “If you release this Man, you are not Caesar’s friend. Everyone who makes himself a king speaks against Caesar.”
    John 19:13 So Pilate, hearing these words, brought Jesus outside and sat on the judgment seat at a place called Stone Pavement, and in Hebrew, Gabbatha.
    John 19:14 Now it was the Preparation of the Passover; it was about the sixth hour. And he says to the Jews, “Behold your King!”
    John 19:15 Then they cried out, “Take Him away! Take Him away! Crucify Him!” Pilate says to them, “Shall I crucify your King?” The chief priests answered, “We have no king except Caesar.”

John 11:49
But a certain one of them, Caiaphas, who was high priest that year, said to them, “You know nothing,
  John 18:13–18:14 — internal identity-and-advice callback: The later trial narration identifies Caiaphas as high priest that year and recalls his advice about one person dying for the people. This is the same named counselor's explicit narrative return, not independent evidence for every institutional detail.
    John 18:13 and led Him first to Annas, for he was father-in-law to Caiaphas, who was high priest that year.
    John 18:14 Now Caiaphas was the one who advised the Jews that it was better for them for one person to die for the people.

John 11:50
nor do you consider that it is better for you for one man to die for the people than for the whole nation to perish.”
  John 18:14 — internal repeated-advice link: John18 explicitly recalls Caiaphas's better-for-one-person-to-die advice given here. The later summary does not reproduce the whole nation-perishing contrast or create a separate independent witness.
    John 18:14 Now Caiaphas was the one who advised the Jews that it was better for them for one person to die for the people.

John 11:51
He did not say this from himself, but being high priest that year, he prophesied that Jesus was about to die for the nation,
  John 11:50 — internal speech-and-interpretation link: The narrator interprets Caiaphas's preceding one-for-the-people advice as prophecy of Jesus' death for the nation. The hostile counselor's words and the narrator's explanation are distinguished; no unreported understanding of the prophecy is assigned to Caiaphas.
    John 11:50 nor do you consider that it is better for you for one man to die for the people than for the whole nation to perish.”

John 11:52
and not for the nation alone, but also to gather into one the children of God who had been scattered.
  John 10:16 — gathering-into-one comparison: Both describe Jesus' people brought into one collective. John11 names scattered children of God beyond the nation; John10 names other sheep and one flock/shepherd. The metaphors are related without identifying every group with an unstated nation or assigning individuals by analogy.
    John 10:16 And I have other sheep that are not from this fold. I must bring them also, and they will hear My voice, and they will become one flock, one shepherd.

John 11:53
So from that day they planned to kill Him.
  Mark 14:1 — death-plot comparison: Both narrate leaders planning Jesus' death. John dates the decision from the Lazarus council; Mark introduces the plot when the feasts are two days away. No single meeting date or identical sequence is imposed.
    Mark 14:1 The Passover and the Feast of Unleavened Bread were two days away. The chief priests and the scribes sought how to seize Him by deceit and kill Him.

John 11:54
Therefore Jesus no longer walked openly among the Jews, but went away from there into the region near the wilderness, to a city called Ephraim, and stayed there with the disciples.
  John 10:39–10:40 — internal retreat-pattern comparison: Both describe Jesus withdrawing after danger of seizure. John11 names Ephraim near wilderness and the disciples; John10 names escape and retreat beyond the Jordan. The places and journeys are not equated.
    John 10:39 So they sought again to seize Him, and He went out from their hand.
    John 10:40 And He went away again beyond the Jordan, to the place where John was first baptizing, and remained there.

John 11:55
Now the Passover of the Jews was near, and many went up to Jerusalem from the countryside before the Passover to purify themselves.
  John 2:13 — festival-journey comparison: Both connect approaching Passover with going up to Jerusalem. John11 concerns pilgrims coming beforehand to purify themselves; John2 narrates Jesus going. Travelers, purification details and visits remain distinct.
    John 2:13 Now the Passover of the Jews was near, and Jesus went up to Jerusalem.

John 11:56
So they sought Jesus and said to one another as they stood in the temple, “What do you think? Surely He will not come to the feast?”
  John 7:10–7:11 — festival-search comparison: Both describe uncertainty and searching for Jesus at a feast. John11 concerns the approaching Passover and temple speculation; John7 concerns His initially secret arrival at Booths. Feasts, locations of speech and journeys are not merged.
    John 7:10 But when His brothers had gone up to the feast, then He also went up, not openly, but as in secret.
    John 7:11 So the Jews sought Him at the feast and said, “Where is He?”

John 11:57
The chief priests and the Pharisees had given orders that if anyone knew where He was, he should disclose it, so that they might seize Him.
  John 7:32 — seizure-plan comparison: Both name chief priests and Pharisees seeking to seize Jesus. John11 gives a location-reporting order; John7 describes sending officers after hearing crowd talk. The orders and moments differ.
    John 7:32 The Pharisees heard the crowd grumbling these things about Him, and the chief priests and the Pharisees sent officers to seize Him.

John 12:1
So six days before the Passover, Jesus came to Bethany, where Lazarus was, whom Jesus had raised from the dead.
  John 11:43–11:44 — internal raising callback: The introduction explicitly recalls the raising of Lazarus that the earlier chapter narrates through the call and emergence. The six-days-before-Passover arrival notice belongs here rather than to the earlier raising account.
    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”
    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

John 12:2
They made a supper for Him there, and Martha served, while Lazarus was one of those reclining at table with Him.
  Luke 10:40 — hospitality-service comparison: Both describe Martha serving in Jesus' presence. Luke adds distraction and a complaint about her sister; John names Lazarus reclining at this supper without that complaint. The visits and Luke's unnamed village are not identified with one dated meal.
    Luke 10:40 But Martha was distracted by much serving. She came up and said, “Lord, dost Thou not care that my sister has left me to serve alone? Tell her, then, to help me.”

John 12:3
Then Mary took a litra of costly perfume of genuine nard, anointed Jesus’ feet, and wiped His feet with her hair. And the house was filled with the fragrance of the perfume.
  Mark 14:3 — anointing-account comparison: Both describe costly genuine/pure nard used to anoint Jesus in a Bethany meal setting. John names Mary, a litra, feet and hair; Mark names an unnamed woman, a broken alabaster jar, head and Simon the leper's house. Different details and chronology are preserved without supplying an identity or date.
    Mark 14:3 While He was in Bethany, reclining in the house of Simon the leper, a woman came with an alabaster jar of very costly ointment of pure nard. She broke the alabaster jar and poured it over His head.

John 12:4
But Judas Iscariot, one of His disciples, who was about to hand Him over, said,
  Mark 14:10 — identity-and-betrayal comparison: Both name Judas Iscariot among Jesus' disciples/Twelve and connect him with handing Jesus over. John makes him the perfume objection's speaker; Mark14:10 does not independently identify the unnamed critics of its earlier meal scene.
    Mark 14:10 Judas Iscariot, one of the Twelve, went away to the chief priests to hand Him over to them.

John 12:5
“Why was this perfume not sold for three hundred denarii and given to the poor?”
  Mark 14:4–14:5 — anointing-objection comparison: Both object that the perfume could have been sold and its price given to the poor. John names Judas and three hundred denarii; Mark names some objectors and more than three hundred. The speakers and amounts remain distinct.
    Mark 14:4 Some were indignant among themselves: “Why has this ointment been wasted?
    Mark 14:5 For this ointment could have been sold for more than three hundred denarii and given to the poor.” They scolded her harshly.

John 12:6
He said this not because he cared about the poor, but because he was a thief, and having the money box, he used to carry off what was put into it.
  John 13:29 — internal fiscal-role recurrence: Both identify Judas with the money box. John12's narrator calls him a thief and rejects a poor-care motive; John13 reports disciples' guesses about purchases or charity. The repeated role does not turn their guesses into the narrator's judgment or independently prove the theft.
    John 13:29 For some thought, since Judas had the money box, that Jesus was telling him, “Buy what we need for the feast,” or that he should give something to the poor.

John 12:7
So Jesus said, “Leave her, so that she may keep it for the day of My burial.
  Mark 14:6–14:8 — burial-anointing teaching comparison: Both defend the woman and connect her act with Jesus' burial. John says leave her so she may keep it for His burial day; Mark says she already anointed His body beforehand. Those formulations and Mark's additional good-work praise are not harmonized.
    Mark 14:6 Jesus said, “Leave her alone. Why are you troubling her? She has done a good work for Me.
    Mark 14:7 For you always have the poor with you, and whenever you want, you can do good to them; but you do not always have Me.
    Mark 14:8 She did what she could. She has anointed My body beforehand for burial.

John 12:8
For you always have the poor with you, but you do not always have Me.”
  Mark 14:7 — teaching parallel: Both contrast the poor always being with the hearers with Jesus not always being present. Mark additionally permits doing them good whenever desired.
    Mark 14:7 For you always have the poor with you, and whenever you want, you can do good to them; but you do not always have Me.

John 12:9
A great crowd of the Jews learned that He was there, and they came not because of Jesus alone, but also to see Lazarus, whom He raised from the dead.
  John 11:43–11:45 — sign-and-crowd sequel link: The later crowd comes also to see the raised Lazarus; the earlier scene narrates his emergence and witnesses believing. These are responses to the same named raising, without identifying the arriving crowd with every earlier eyewitness or adding a second raising.
    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”
    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”
    John 11:45 So many of the Jews who had come to Mary and beheld what He did believed in Him.

John 12:10
But the chief priests planned to kill Lazarus also,
  John 11:53 — internal extension-of-plot contrast: The earlier decision targets Jesus; the later chief-priest plot targets Lazarus also. The explicit extension of danger is compared without identifying the two victims or narrating the second plan as carried out.
    John 11:53 So from that day they planned to kill Him.

John 12:11
because on his account many of the Jews were going away and believing in Jesus.
  John 11:45 — sign-and-belief recurrence: Both relate belief in Jesus to Lazarus's raising. The earlier notice names people who saw it with Mary; the later notice explains the plot through people going away and believing on Lazarus's account. The groups and stages are not automatically identical.
    John 11:45 So many of the Jews who had come to Mary and beheld what He did believed in Him.

John 12:12
The next day, the great crowd that had come to the feast, hearing that Jesus was coming to Jerusalem,
  Mark 11:8–11:10 — entry-scene comparison: Both place a public crowd reception at Jesus' Jerusalem approach. John introduces a feast crowd hearing of His arrival before the next unit's acclamation; Mark describes garments, branches and those ahead/behind acclaiming Him. The next-day and feast-participant details are not supplied from Mark.
    Mark 11:8 Many spread their garments on the road; others spread leafy branches they had cut from the fields.
    Mark 11:9 Those going ahead and those following cried out, “Hosanna! Blessed is the One who comes in the name of the Lord!
    Mark 11:10 Blessed is the coming kingdom of our father David! Hosanna in the highest!”

John 12:13
took branches of the palm trees and went out to meet Him, and cried out, “Hosanna! Blessed is the One who comes in the name of the Lord, even the King of Israel!”
  Psalms 117:26 — quotation: The crowd's blessing of the One coming in the Lord's name corresponds to the psalm's blessing. John adds Hosanna, King of Israel and palm branches in the entry narrative; the psalm adds blessing from the Lord's house.
    Psalms 117:26 Blessed is the one who comes in the name of the Lord. We have blessed you from the house of the Lord.

John 12:14
Jesus found a young donkey and sat upon it, just as it is written,
  Mark 11:7 — entry-scene parallel: Both describe Jesus seated on a colt. John says He found a young donkey and introduces Scripture; Mark says disciples brought the colt and put garments on it. The means of obtaining it are not merged.
    Mark 11:7 They brought the colt to Jesus, threw their garments on it, and He sat on it.

John 12:15
“Do not fear, daughter Zion. Behold, your King comes, sitting upon a donkey’s colt.”
  Zechariah 9:9 — quotation comparison: Both announce Zion's coming king mounted on the animal. John says Do not fear and a donkey's colt; Zechariah says Rejoice/proclaim and a beast of burden and young colt, adding righteous, saving and gentle. The actual supplied animal and introductory wording remain distinct.
    Zechariah 9:9 Rejoice greatly, daughter Zion; proclaim, daughter Jerusalem. Behold, your king comes to you, righteous and saving, gentle, and mounted upon a beast of burden and a young colt.

John 12:16
His disciples did not understand these things at first. But when Jesus was glorified, then they remembered that these things were written about Him and that they had done these things to Him.
  John 2:22 — later-remembrance comparison: Both recount disciples later remembering Scripture or Jesus' words. John12 says after His glorification concerning entry actions; John2 says after His resurrection concerning the temple saying. The remembered content and time formulations are not made identical.
    John 2:22 When, therefore, He was raised from the dead, His disciples remembered that He had said this, and they believed the Scripture and the word Jesus had spoken.

John 12:17
So the crowd that was with Him when He called Lazarus from the tomb and raised him from the dead bore witness.
  John 11:43–11:44 — internal eyewitness-and-event link: The entry narrative explicitly recalls the crowd present when Jesus called Lazarus from the tomb. The earlier passage narrates that call and emergence; it does not independently narrate the later testimony or identify every witness.
    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”
    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

John 12:18
For this reason the crowd also met Him, because they heard that He had done this sign.
  John 12:17 — internal testimony-and-reception link: The crowd's meeting Jesus because it heard of the sign follows the earlier eyewitness crowd's testimony about Lazarus. The narrative connects the same sign with testimony and reception; eyewitnesses and hearers are not automatically one group.
    John 12:17 So the crowd that was with Him when He called Lazarus from the tomb and raised him from the dead bore witness.

John 12:19
So the Pharisees said among themselves, “You see that you accomplish nothing. Behold, the world has gone after Him.”
  John 11:48 — internal opponents-response comparison: Both report authorities treating widespread belief in Jesus as their problem. John12's Pharisees say the world went after Him; John11's speakers fear everyone believing and Roman action. Their rhetoric is not an independently verified worldwide conversion count.
    John 11:48 If we leave Him like this, everyone will believe in Him, and the Romans will come and take away both our place and our nation.”

John 12:20
Now there were some Greeks among those going up to worship at the feast.
  Isaiah 56:6–56:7 — scriptural worship-background comparison: Greeks going up to worship can be compared with Isaiah's foreigners attached to the Lord and welcome in His prayer house for all nations. John does not specify these individuals' circumcision, Sabbath or covenant status, or declare this group complete fulfillment of the promise.
    Isaiah 56:6 And the foreigners attached to the Lord, to serve Him and love the Lord’s name, to become His male and female slaves, and all those keeping My Sabbaths from profanation and holding fast to My covenant—
    Isaiah 56:7 I will bring them into My holy mountain and make them rejoice in My house of prayer. Their whole burnt offerings and sacrifices will be acceptable on My altar, for My house will be called a house of prayer for all nations,”

John 12:21
These approached Philip, who was from Bethsaida of Galilee, and asked him, saying, “Sir, we want to see Jesus.”
  John 1:43–1:44 — internal identity-and-origin link: Both identify Philip with Bethsaida in Galilee's ministry context. John12 additionally records Greeks' request to see Jesus; John1 describes Philip's call and the town shared with Andrew and Peter, without independently narrating that request.
    John 1:43 The next day He wanted to go out into Galilee. And He found Philip, and Jesus said to him, “Follow Me.”
    John 1:44 Now Philip was from Bethsaida, the city of Andrew and Peter.

John 12:22
Philip came and told Andrew; Andrew and Philip came and told Jesus.
  John 1:40–1:46 — narrative parallel: Andrew and Philip mediate the request to Jesus here; earlier Andrew brings Simon and Philip tells Nathanael about Jesus and invites him to see. The named disciples' mediating roles recur, but the persons and actions differ. The comparison does not assert that the Greeks' requested audience occurs or supply the motive for Philip consulting Andrew.
    John 1:40 Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, was one of the two who heard from John and followed Him.
    John 1:41 He first found his own brother Simon and said to him, “We have found the Messiah”—which, translated, is Christ.
    John 1:42 He brought him to Jesus. Jesus looked at him and said, “You are Simon, the son of John; you shall be called Cephas”—which is translated Peter.
    John 1:43 The next day He wanted to go out into Galilee. And He found Philip, and Jesus said to him, “Follow Me.”
    John 1:44 Now Philip was from Bethsaida, the city of Andrew and Peter.
    John 1:45 Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”
    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

John 12:23
Jesus answered them, saying, “The hour has come for the Son of Man to be glorified.
  John 13:31–13:32 — glorification-announcement comparison: Both announce the Son of Man's glorification. John12 says the hour has come; John13 says now after Judas leaves and names God's reciprocal glorification. Their temporal markers and explanatory clauses are preserved.
    John 13:31 When he had gone out, Jesus said, “Now the Son of Man was glorified, and God was glorified in Him.
    John 13:32 If God was glorified in Him, God will also glorify Him in Himself, and will glorify Him at once.

John 12:24
Amen, amen, I tell you: unless the grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it bears much fruit.
  1 Corinthians 15:36–15:38 — grain-and-life imagery comparison: Both use a sown grain's death before subsequent life or fruit. Jesus speaks of wheat no longer remaining alone but bearing much fruit; Paul discusses the resurrection body's relation to bare seed and God's giving a body. The images serve different expositions, not one literal botanical or resurrection timetable.
    1 Corinthians 15:36 Foolish one! What you sow is not made alive unless it dies.
    1 Corinthians 15:37 And what you sow is not the body that will come to be, but a bare grain, perhaps of wheat or of one of the other kinds.
    1 Corinthians 15:38 But God gives it a body just as He wished, and to each of the seeds its own body.

John 12:25
The one who loves his life loses it, and the one who hates his life in this world will keep it for eternal life.
  Mark 8:35 — life-loss teaching comparison: Both contrast preserving life by surrender with losing it through attachment to present life. John says loving/hating one's life in this world and keeping it for eternal life; Mark names losing it for Jesus and the gospel. The supplied verbs and qualifications remain distinct.
    Mark 8:35 For whoever wants to save his life will lose it; but whoever loses his life for My sake and for the gospel will save it.

John 12:26
If anyone serves Me, let him follow Me; and where I am, there My servant will be also. If anyone serves Me, the Father will honor him.
  John 14:3 — presence-with-Jesus promise comparison: Both promise Jesus' followers a place with Him where He is. John12 speaks of the servant following Him and the Father's honor; John14 speaks of preparing and receiving them. Those additional promises and their formulations differ.
    John 14:3 And if I go and prepare a place for you, I am coming again and will take you to Myself, so that where I am, you also may be.

John 12:27
Now My soul is troubled, and what shall I say? Father, save Me from this hour? Yet for this reason I came to this hour.
  Mark 14:34–14:36 — passion-prayer comparison: Both portray Jesus deeply troubled while facing His hour. John poses whether to ask rescue and affirms His purpose in reaching the hour; Mark quotes the cup-removal request and submission to the Father's will. The public discourse and Gethsemane scene are not relocated or made verbally identical.
    Mark 14:34 He said to them, “My soul is deeply grieved, to the point of death. Remain here and keep watch.”
    Mark 14:35 Going forward a little, He fell on the ground and prayed that, if possible, the hour might pass from Him.
    Mark 14:36 He said, “Abba, Father, all things are possible for Thee. Take this cup away from Me. Yet not what I will, but what Thou wilt.”

John 12:28
Father, glorify Thy name.” Then a voice came from heaven: “I both glorified it and will glorify it again.”
  John 17:1 — glory-prayer comparison: Both address Father and request glorification in the hour's context. John12 asks glory for the Father's name and receives a heavenly reply; John17 asks the Son's glorification so He may glorify the Father. The request objects and response narration remain distinct.
    John 17:1 Jesus spoke these things, and, lifting His eyes to heaven, said, “Father, the hour has come. Glorify Thy Son, so that the Son may glorify Thee,

John 12:29
So the crowd standing there and hearing it said that it had thundered. Others said, “An angel has spoken to Him.”
  Psalms 28:3 — thematic parallel: The Psalm explicitly joins the Lord's voice with the God of glory thundering. John's crowd hears the heavenly voice but divides between thunder and an angel speaking. This compares concrete divine-voice/thunder imagery without endorsing both crowd explanations as fact, inventing a storm or making the Psalm a report of this event.
    Psalms 28:3 The Lord’s voice is over the waters; the God of glory thundered; the Lord is over many waters.

John 12:30
Jesus answered, “This voice has come not for My sake, but for yours.
  John 11:42 — public-communication purpose comparison: Both have Jesus explain spoken revelation as benefiting the surrounding hearers. Here the heavenly voice is for them; earlier Jesus speaks His own prayer so the crowd may believe His sending. The vocal agents and events are not equated.
    John 11:42 I knew that Thou always hearest Me, but I said this because of the crowd standing around, so that they may believe that Thou didst send Me.”

John 12:31
Now is the judgment of this world; now the ruler of this world will be cast out.
  John 16:11 — world-ruler judgment comparison: Both connect judgment with the ruler of this world. John12 says now judgment and the ruler will be cast out; John16 says the ruler has been judged. No unnamed earthly ruler or single reconciled tense is substituted.
    John 16:11 and concerning judgment, because the ruler of this world has been judged.

John 12:32
And I, if I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all people to Myself.”
  John 3:14–3:15 — lifted-up teaching comparison: Both speak of the Son of Man/Jesus being lifted up with a life-giving result. John3 adds Moses' lifted snake and faith unto life; John12 says drawing all to Himself, with the narrator next explaining death. Those images and effects remain distinct.
    John 3:14 And just as Moses lifted up the snake in the wilderness, so the Son of Man must be lifted up,
    John 3:15 so that everyone who believes may have eternal life in Him.

John 12:33
He said this to signify by what kind of death He was about to die.
  John 18:31–18:32 — internal death-indication callback: Both narrator comments describe Jesus' words as indicating the kind of death He would die. John18 adds the handover dialogue and fulfillment language. It recalls the earlier death indication without independently verifying every legal claim made by the speakers.
    John 18:31 Then Pilate said to them, “Take Him yourselves, and judge Him according to your law.” The Jews said to him, “It is not lawful for us to put anyone to death,”
    John 18:32 so that the word of Jesus might be fulfilled, which He spoke to indicate by what kind of death He was going to die.

John 12:34
So the crowd answered Him, “We have heard from the Law that the Christ remains forever. How dost Thou say that the Son of Man must be lifted up? Who is this Son of Man?”
  Daniel 7:13–7:14 — scriptural expectation comparison: The crowd's Son-of-Man question and expectation of lasting messianic rule can be compared with Daniel's son-of-man figure and everlasting authority/kingdom. Daniel does not verbatim say Christ cannot die, and its actual Greek-based Ancient-of-Days formulation is not replaced with another edition's wording.
    Daniel 7:13 I watched in the night vision, and there, upon the clouds of heaven, one like a son of man was coming. He was present as an Ancient of Days, and those standing by were present with him.
    Daniel 7:14 Authority was given him, and all the nations of the earth according to their families, and all glory, worshipped him. His authority is everlasting authority that will never be taken away, and his kingdom will never be destroyed.

John 12:35
Then Jesus said to them, “For a little time longer the Light is among you. Walk while you have the Light, so that darkness may not overtake you. The one walking in the darkness does not know where he is going.
  John 11:9–11:10 — same-book light-and-walking comparison: Both connect available light with safe walking and darkness with impaired movement. John12 names the Light's short remaining presence and not knowing one's direction; John11 names day, night and stumbling. Their images and time descriptions are not identical.
    John 11:9 Jesus answered, “Are there not twelve hours of the day? If anyone walks in the day, he does not stumble, because he sees the light of this world.
    John 11:10 But if anyone walks in the night, he stumbles, because the light is not in him.”

John 12:36
While you have the Light, believe in the Light, so that you may become sons of light.” Jesus spoke these things, and went away and hid Himself from them.
  1 Thessalonians 5:5–5:6 — sons-of-light teaching comparison: Both associate the hearers with sons of light. John commands belief in the Light toward that status; Paul affirms it and urges watchfulness and sobriety. The withdrawal of Jesus belongs only to John's narrative.
    1 Thessalonians 5:5 For you are all sons of light and sons of day. We are not of night or darkness.
    1 Thessalonians 5:6 So then, let us not sleep as the rest do, but let us keep watch and be sober.

John 12:37
Though He had done so many signs before them, they did not believe in Him,
  John 6:36 — sight-and-unbelief comparison: Both explicitly deny that seeing Jesus or His signs necessarily produced belief among the stated hearers. The narrator here names many signs; John6 quotes Jesus about having seen Him. This does not erase the same Gospel's other accounts of people believing.
    John 6:36 But I told you that you have both seen Me and do not believe.

John 12:38
so that the word of Isaiah the prophet might be fulfilled, which he spoke: “Lord, who believed our report? And to whom was the arm of the Lord revealed?”
  Isaiah 53:1 — quotation: John quotes the report-unbelieved and Lord's-arm-revealed questions from Isaiah. The application to this unbelief is John's narrative explanation; Isaiah's unit does not name the particular Gospel hearers.
    Isaiah 53:1 O Lord, who has believed our report? And to whom has the arm of the Lord been revealed?

John 12:39
For this reason they could not believe, because Isaiah said again,
  Isaiah 6:10 — quotation introduction: John introduces Isaiah's blindness/heart saying as the reason they could not believe, then quotes it next. The target supplies the relevant imagery while retaining its own heart-grown-dull and self-closed-eyes formulation rather than John's active He wording.
    Isaiah 6:10 For the heart of this people has grown dull, and with their ears they have heard heavily, and they have closed their eyes; lest they see with their eyes, and hear with their ears, and understand with their heart, and turn, and I will heal them.

John 12:40
“He has blinded their eyes and hardened their heart, so that they may not see with the eyes and understand with the heart and turn—and I will heal them.”
  Isaiah 6:10 — quotation comparison: Both concern eyes, heart, turning and healing. John says He blinded and hardened; the supplied Isaiah says the people's heart grew dull, ears heard heavily and they closed their eyes. The agency, hearing clause and sentence forms are preserved rather than harmonized.
    Isaiah 6:10 For the heart of this people has grown dull, and with their ears they have heard heavily, and they have closed their eyes; lest they see with their eyes, and hear with their ears, and understand with their heart, and turn, and I will heal them.

John 12:41
Isaiah said these things because he saw His glory and spoke concerning Him.
  Isaiah 6:1–6:3 — vision-and-application comparison: Isaiah narrates seeing the enthroned Lord and speaks of His glory filling house and earth. John applies Isaiah's seeing and speaking to Jesus' glory. The identification is John's stated application; Isaiah's vision is not rewritten to contain Jesus' name or a new independent identification.
    Isaiah 6:1 In the year that King Oziah died, I saw the Lord seated on a throne, high and lifted up; and the house was full of His glory.
    Isaiah 6:2 And seraphim stood around Him: each one had six wings, and each one had six wings; with two they covered their faces, and with two they covered their feet, and with two they flew.
    Isaiah 6:3 And one cried to another, saying: Holy, holy, holy is the Lord Sabaoth; all the earth is full of His glory.

John 12:42
Nevertheless, many even from the rulers believed in Him, but because of the Pharisees they did not confess Him, so that they might not be put out of the synagogue,
  John 9:22 — confession-and-exclusion comparison: Both describe fear of synagogue expulsion related to confessing Jesus as Christ. John12 concerns rulers who believed without confessing; John9 concerns the healed man's parents. Their knowledge, statements and groups remain distinct.
    John 9:22 His parents said these things because they feared the Jews, for the Jews had already agreed that if anyone confessed Him as Christ, he should be put out of the synagogue.

John 12:43
for they loved the glory of people more than the glory of God.
  John 5:44 — glory-and-belief comparison: Both oppose human glory to God's glory in the reception of Jesus. John12 explains rulers' hidden belief; John5 asks how people can believe while seeking each other's glory. Belief-without-confession and the earlier question are not silently made identical.
    John 5:44 How can you believe, receiving glory from one another, and not seeking the glory that comes from the only God?

John 12:44
And Jesus cried out and said, “The one who believes in Me believes not in Me, but in the One who sent Me.
  John 14:1 — Father-and-Son belief comparison: Both connect belief in Jesus with belief in God or His Sender. John12 says not in Me but in the One who sent Me; John14 commands belief in God and also in Me. The distinct formulations are retained rather than removing either Jesus or the Father from the statements.
    John 14:1 “Do not let your heart be troubled. Believe in God, and believe also in Me.

John 12:45
And the one who beholds Me beholds the One who sent Me.
  John 14:9 — revelation comparison: Both connect beholding Jesus with beholding the Father or Sender. John14 addresses Philip's request and explicitly names the Father; John12 states the shorter Sender formulation. No collapse of the speakers or roles is inferred.
    John 14:9 Jesus says to him, “Have I been with you for so long a time, and you have not known Me, Philip? The one who has seen Me has seen the Father. How can you say, ‘Show us the Father’?

John 12:46
I have come as Light into the world, so that everyone who believes in Me may not remain in the darkness.
  John 8:12 — Light teaching comparison: Both call Jesus Light in the world and connect response to Him with escape from darkness. John12 names believing and not remaining in darkness; John8 names following and having life's light. The conditions remain their supplied words.
    John 8:12 Then Jesus spoke to them again, saying, “I am the Light of the world. The one who follows Me will certainly not walk in the darkness, but will have the light of life.”

John 12:47
And if anyone hears My words and does not keep them, I do not judge him; for I did not come to judge the world, but to save the world.
  John 3:17 — saving-mission comparison: Both distinguish Jesus' saving mission from judging the world. John12 has Jesus describe His coming while addressing hearers who do not keep His words; John3 describes God's sending the Son. Their agents and additional clauses remain distinct.
    John 3:17 For God did not send the Son into the world to judge the world, but so that the world might be saved through Him.

John 12:48
The one who rejects Me and does not receive My words has one who judges him: the word that I spoke will judge him on the last day.
  John 5:24 — internal reception-and-judgment contrast: Both relate response to Jesus' word to judgment. John12 warns of rejection and the word judging on the last day; John5 promises the hearer who believes the Sender does not come into judgment. The opposing responses and exact time language are not erased.
    John 5:24 Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who hears My word and believes the One who sent Me has eternal life, and does not come into judgment, but has passed from death into life.

John 12:49
Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.
  John 14:10 — Father-origin speech comparison: Both deny speaking from Jesus Himself and refer His words to the Father. John12 describes a command concerning what to say; John14 describes the indwelling Father's works. The related speech and work claims are not made identical.
    John 14:10 Do you not believe that I am in the Father and the Father is in Me? The words that I say to you I do not speak from Myself; but the Father, who remains in Me, does His works.

John 12:50
And I know that His command is eternal life. So the things I speak, just as the Father has told Me, thus I speak.”
  John 6:68 — eternal-life words comparison: Jesus calls the Father's command eternal life and speaks as told; Peter says Jesus has words of eternal life. Their speaker roles and Father-command/Jesus-word formulations differ without losing the explicit life-giving speech connection.
    John 6:68 Simon Peter answered Him, “Lord, to whom shall we go? Thou hast words of eternal life.

John 13:1
Before the feast of the Passover, Jesus, knowing that His hour had come to pass from this world to the Father, having loved His own who were in the world, loved them to the end.
  John 16:28 — origin-and-return comparison: Both connect Jesus' departure from the world with going to the Father. John13's narrator adds His hour and love to the end; John16 gives Jesus' own coming-from-Father and returning statement. Neither narration is silently substituted for the other's wording.
    John 16:28 I came forth from the Father and have come into the world. Again, I am leaving the world and going to the Father.”

John 13:2
And while supper was taking place, the devil having already put it into the heart that Judas, son of Simon Iscariot, should hand Him over,
  Luke 22:3–22:4 — betrayal-agency comparison: Both connect Judas's betrayal with evil agency. John13:2 says the devil already put the intention in his heart during supper's introduction, with a later Satan-entry at13:27; Luke places Satan entering before Judas's bargain. The actions and chronology are not collapsed.
    Luke 22:3 Then Satan entered Judas, who was called Iscariot and was one of the number of the Twelve.
    Luke 22:4 He went away and spoke with the chief priests and the captains about how he might hand Him over to them.

John 13:3
knowing that the Father had given all things into His hands, and that He came out from God and was going to God,
  John 3:35 — Father-to-Son entrusting comparison: Both say the Father gave all things into the Son's hands. John13 additionally describes Jesus' knowledge of divine origin and return; John3 adds the Father's love.
    John 3:35 The Father loves the Son and has given all things into His hand.

John 13:4
He rose from supper and laid aside His garments, and took a towel and tied it around Himself.
  Luke 12:37 — service-role comparison: Both picture the superior girding himself to serve others. John narrates Jesus taking a towel after rising from supper; Luke's parable promises a master girding and serving watchful slaves. The towel, task, people and genre differ.
    Luke 12:37 Blessed are those slaves whom the master finds keeping watch when he comes. Amen, I tell you: he will gird himself, have them recline at table, and come and serve them.

John 13:5
Then He poured water into the basin and began to wash the disciples’ feet and wipe them with the towel He had tied around Himself.
  1 Timothy 5:10 — foot-washing service comparison: Both explicitly describe washing the feet of God's people. Jesus washes and towels the disciples' feet; Timothy lists washing saints' feet among a widow's good works. The actors and settings are not identified.
    1 Timothy 5:10 attested for good works: if she brought up children, if she received strangers, if she washed saints’ feet, if she assisted the afflicted, if she followed every good work.

John 13:6
So He came to Simon Peter, who said to Him, “Lord, dost Thou wash my feet?”
  John 13:14 — internal role-question and explanation: Peter's question about the Lord washing his feet can be compared with Jesus' later Lord-and-Teacher explanation that He washed their feet as a pattern for mutual service. The later teaching explains the role reversal, not independent detail of Peter's words.
    John 13:14 So if I, the Lord and the Teacher, washed your feet, you also ought to wash one another’s feet.

John 13:7
Jesus answered him, “What I do you do not understand now, but you will understand after these things.”
  John 12:16 — later-understanding comparison: Jesus contrasts Peter's present nonunderstanding with promised later understanding; John12 contrasts initial nonunderstanding with later remembering of Scripture and entry actions after glorification. The parallel concerns delayed reception, while understanding and remembering, and promised versus narrated stages, remain distinct.
    John 12:16 His disciples did not understand these things at first. But when Jesus was glorified, then they remembered that these things were written about Him and that they had done these things to Him.

John 13:8
Peter said to Him, “Thou wilt certainly never wash my feet.” Jesus answered him, “Unless I wash you, you have no share with Me.”
  1 Corinthians 6:11 — washing-and-participation comparison: Both connect washing with a changed holy relationship. John makes Jesus' washing necessary for Peter's share with Him; Corinthians says you washed yourselves clean, then were sanctified and justified in Jesus' name and God's Spirit. Different agency and language are preserved without equating foot washing and a particular rite.
    1 Corinthians 6:11 And some of you were these things. But you washed yourselves clean, but you were sanctified, but you were justified in the name of the Lord Jesus Christ and in the Spirit of our God.

John 13:9
Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, not my feet only, but also my hands and my head!”
  Exodus 30:18–30:21 — thematic parallel: Peter requests hands and head as well as feet after Jesus' no-share warning. Exodus specifies priestly hand-and-foot washing before service. Both specify bodily washing in relation to participation, but Exodus does not add head washing here, and John does not make Peter an Aaronite or impose the priestly death warning on this conversation.
    Exodus 30:18 “Make a bronze basin with a bronze base for washing. Put it between the Tent of Testimony and the altar, and pour water into it.
    Exodus 30:19 Aaron and his sons shall wash their hands and feet with water from it.
    Exodus 30:20 When they enter the Tent of Testimony, they shall wash with water so that they do not die. When they approach the altar to minister and offer whole burnt offerings to the Lord,
    Exodus 30:21 they shall wash their hands and feet with water. When they enter the Tent of Testimony, they shall wash with water so that they do not die. This shall be a perpetual statute for him and his generations after him.”

John 13:10
Jesus said to him, “The one who has bathed has no need to wash except the feet, but is wholly clean. And you are clean, but not all.”
  John 15:3 — disciples-cleanness comparison: Both call Jesus' disciples clean. John13 uses bathing/feet language and an exception explained through the betrayer; John15 attributes cleanness to the spoken word without repeating that exception. The grounds and qualifications remain distinct.
    John 15:3 You are already clean because of the word that I have spoken to you.

John 13:11
For He knew the one who was handing Him over; for this reason He said, “Not all of you are clean.”
  John 6:64 — betrayal-foreknowledge comparison: Both explicitly describe Jesus knowing the one who would hand Him over. John13 uses that knowledge to explain not-all-clean; John6 describes knowledge from the beginning alongside unbelief. No new account of compulsion or unidentified persons is supplied.
    John 6:64 But there are some among you who do not believe.” For Jesus knew from the beginning who those were who did not believe and who it was who would hand Him over.

John 13:12
So when He had washed their feet and taken His garments and reclined again, He said to them, “Do you understand what I have done to you?
  John 13:15 — internal act-and-example explanation: Jesus' question about understanding what He did receives its stated example-purpose in13:15. The link interprets the narrated foot washing through His own teaching, not another independent washing account.
    John 13:15 For I have given you an example, so that you also may do just as I did to you.

John 13:13
You call Me the Teacher and the Lord, and you speak well, for I am.
  Luke 6:46 — Lord-title and obedience comparison: Both relate calling Jesus Lord to the response He expects. John affirms Teacher and Lord before His service command; Luke challenges Lord, Lord speech without doing His sayings. The title affirmation and rebuke have different settings and wording.
    Luke 6:46 Why do you call Me ‘Lord, Lord,’ and not do what I say?

John 13:14
So if I, the Lord and the Teacher, washed your feet, you also ought to wash one another’s feet.
  1 Timothy 5:10 — command-and-practice comparison: Jesus commands mutual foot washing after His example; Timothy names washing saints' feet as a good work. The later description offers a concrete service counterpart, not proof that every recipient reenacted this supper or that the directive had one unstated institutional form.
    1 Timothy 5:10 attested for good works: if she brought up children, if she received strangers, if she washed saints’ feet, if she assisted the afflicted, if she followed every good work.

John 13:15
For I have given you an example, so that you also may do just as I did to you.
  1 Peter 2:21 — example-and-imitation comparison: Both describe Christ's action as an example for followers. John concerns mutual service following foot washing; Peter concerns suffering and following His footsteps. Different acts and contexts are not conflated.
    1 Peter 2:21 For to this you were called, because Christ also suffered for you, leaving you an example, so that you might follow in His footsteps.

John 13:16
Amen, amen, I tell you: a slave is not greater than his master, nor a messenger greater than the one who sent him.
  Matthew 10:24–10:25 — teaching parallel: Both deny a slave being greater than the master. John also names messenger and sender in the service setting; Matthew names disciple/teacher and applies the pattern to hostile treatment. The contexts and additional analogies differ.
    Matthew 10:24 A disciple is not above the teacher, nor a slave above his master.
    Matthew 10:25 It is enough for the disciple to become like his teacher, and the slave like his master. If they called the master of the house Beelzebul, how much more his household members!

John 13:17
If you know these things, blessed are you if you do them.
  Luke 11:28 — knowing-and-doing blessing comparison: Both connect blessing with acting on received teaching rather than reception alone. John names knowing and doing these things; Luke names hearing God's word and keeping it. The instruction contexts and formulations remain distinct.
    Luke 11:28 But He said, “Rather, blessed are those who hear the word of God and keep it!”

John 13:18
I do not speak about all of you. I know whom I chose. But so that the Scripture may be fulfilled: ‘The one eating My bread lifted his heel against Me.’
  Psalms 40:10 — quotation comparison: Both describe betrayal by a trusted table companion. John says eating My bread and lifting a heel; the actual psalm says eating my loaves and magnifying treachery. The heel image is not inserted into this supplied psalm, and Judas is not named there.
    Psalms 40:10 For even the man of my peace, in whom I hoped, who ate my loaves, magnified treachery against me.

John 13:19
From now on I tell you before it happens, so that when it happens you may believe that I am.
  John 14:29 — forewarning-and-belief recurrence: Both say Jesus tells the disciples before an event so that they believe when it occurs. John13 additionally says that I am; John14 does not repeat that object in the cited unit.
    John 14:29 And now I have told you before it happens, so that when it happens you may believe.

John 13:20
Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who receives anyone I send receives Me, and the one who receives Me receives the One who sent Me.”
  Matthew 10:40 — messenger-reception parallel: Both link receiving Jesus' messengers with receiving Jesus and the One who sent Him. John says anyone I send; Matthew addresses you. The chain is explicit without identifying every later messenger.
    Matthew 10:40 Whoever receives you receives Me, and whoever receives Me receives the One who sent Me.

John 13:21
Having said these things, Jesus was troubled in spirit and testified, saying, “Amen, amen, I tell you that one of you will hand Me over.”
  Mark 14:18 — betrayal-announcement parallel: Both announce that one of the supper group will hand Jesus over. John describes His troubled spirit; Mark names someone eating with Him. The emotions, wording and feast chronology are not merged.
    Mark 14:18 As they reclined and ate, Jesus said, “Truly I tell you, one of you will hand Me over—one who is eating with Me.”

John 13:22
The disciples looked at one another, perplexed about whom He was speaking.
  Mark 14:19 — betrayal-response comparison: Both describe the group's disturbed uncertainty about the betrayer. John narrates exchanged looks and perplexity; Mark narrates grief and individual Surely-not-I questions. The responses are not made verbally identical.
    Mark 14:19 They began to be grieved and said to Him one by one, “Surely not I?”

John 13:23
One of His disciples, whom Jesus loved, was reclining in Jesus’ bosom.
  John 21:20 — internal disciple-description callback: John21 explicitly recalls the loved disciple reclining against Jesus at supper and asking about betrayal. This identifies the narrative description repeated here without assigning the unnamed disciple a name not given in these units.
    John 21:20 Turning, Peter sees the disciple whom Jesus loved following, the one who also leaned back at the supper upon His chest and said, “Lord, who is the one handing Thee over?”

John 13:24
So Simon Peter signaled to this one to ask whom He was speaking about.
  John 21:20 — internal disciple-and-question callback: The later retrospect identifies the loved supper disciple who asked about betrayal, providing a concrete counterpart for the unnamed one Peter prompts here. It does not independently narrate Peter's signal or supply a personal name for the narrative's unnamed description.
    John 21:20 Turning, Peter sees the disciple whom Jesus loved following, the one who also leaned back at the supper upon His chest and said, “Lord, who is the one handing Thee over?”

John 13:25
Leaning back thus upon Jesus’ chest, he said to Him, “Lord, who is it?”
  John 21:20 — internal supper-question callback: John21 explicitly recalls the chest-reclining disciple's Lord-directed betrayal question. John13 says Lord, who is it; John21 expands the question to who hands Thee over. The retrospect's wording and unidentified person remain as supplied.
    John 21:20 Turning, Peter sees the disciple whom Jesus loved following, the one who also leaned back at the supper upon His chest and said, “Lord, who is the one handing Thee over?”

John 13:26
Jesus answered, “It is the one for whom I will dip the morsel and give it to him.” So He dipped the morsel, took it, and gave it to Judas, son of Simon Iscariot.
