  Mark 14:20 — food-and-betrayal comparison: Both identify the betrayer through shared food. John has Jesus dip and give a morsel to named Judas; Mark names one dipping with Him into the dish. The actions are not made identical or the recipient named within Mark's cited saying.
    Mark 14:20 He said to them, “One of the Twelve, the one dipping with Me into the dish.

John 13:27
And after the morsel, then Satan entered him. So Jesus said to him, “What you do, do more quickly.”
  Luke 22:3 — betrayal-agency comparison: Both explicitly say Satan entered Judas. John places it after the morsel and adds Jesus' do-more-quickly command; Luke places it before the authorities' bargain. The different timing is not harmonized.
    Luke 22:3 Then Satan entered Judas, who was called Iscariot and was one of the number of the Twelve.

John 13:28
But none of those reclining knew for what purpose He said this to him.
  John 13:29 — internal ignorance-and-guess explanation: The group's not knowing why Jesus spoke to Judas is followed by their purchase-or-charity guesses. The next unit explains possible interpretations without establishing that either was Jesus' real purpose.
    John 13:29 For some thought, since Judas had the money box, that Jesus was telling him, “Buy what we need for the feast,” or that he should give something to the poor.

John 13:29
For some thought, since Judas had the money box, that Jesus was telling him, “Buy what we need for the feast,” or that he should give something to the poor.
  John 12:6 — internal fiscal-role callback: The disciples' guesses depend on Judas having the money box, the same role the narrator earlier describes. John12's theft and motive judgment is not transferred into the disciples' later thoughts or made evidence that their charity guess was true.
    John 12:6 He said this not because he cared about the poor, but because he was a thief, and having the money box, he used to carry off what was put into it.

John 13:30
So, having received the morsel, he went out at once. And it was night.
  John 13:26–13:27 — internal action-sequence link: Judas's receiving the morsel and immediate exit follow the narrated gift and do-more-quickly command. The link identifies the sequence's stated antecedents, not independent evidence for the night notice or an unquoted mission.
    John 13:26 Jesus answered, “It is the one for whom I will dip the morsel and give it to him.” So He dipped the morsel, took it, and gave it to Judas, son of Simon Iscariot.
    John 13:27 And after the morsel, then Satan entered him. So Jesus said to him, “What you do, do more quickly.”

John 13:31
When he had gone out, Jesus said, “Now the Son of Man was glorified, and God was glorified in Him.
  John 12:23 — glorification-announcement recurrence: Both announce the Son of Man's glorification. John13 says now after Judas leaves and names God glorified in Him; John12 names the arriving hour. Their stage and wording are not collapsed.
    John 12:23 Jesus answered them, saying, “The hour has come for the Son of Man to be glorified.

John 13:32
If God was glorified in Him, God will also glorify Him in Himself, and will glorify Him at once.
  John 17:1 — reciprocal-glory comparison: Both connect the Son's and Father's glorification. John13 speaks of God glorifying Him in Himself at once; John17 requests the Son's glory so that He may glorify the Father. The condition, request and temporal forms are preserved.
    John 17:1 Jesus spoke these things, and, lifting His eyes to heaven, said, “Father, the hour has come. Glorify Thy Son, so that the Son may glorify Thee,

John 13:33
Little children, I am with you a little longer. You will seek Me, and just as I told the Jews, ‘Where I go, you cannot come,’ I now tell you also.
  John 7:33–7:34 — explicit earlier-saying callback: Jesus expressly recalls the where-I-go-you-cannot-come saying previously addressed to the Jews. John7 gives the earlier little-time and seek/not-find language; John13 now addresses disciples. Audience and where-I-am/go wording are not silently made identical.
    John 7:33 So Jesus said, “I am with you for a little time longer, and I go to the One who sent Me.
    John 7:34 You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come.”

John 13:34
I give you a new commandment: that you love one another. Just as I have loved you, you also are to love one another.
  John 15:12 — commandment recurrence: Both command loving one another after Jesus' love. John13 calls it new; John15 states This is My commandment. No additional historical theory of the word new is imposed.
    John 15:12 “This is My commandment: that you love one another just as I loved you.

John 13:35
By this everyone will know that you are My disciples, if you have love among one another.”
  1 John 3:14 — love-as-evidence comparison: Both present mutual love as evidence of relationship to Jesus or spiritual life. John13 concerns everyone recognizing His disciples; the letter concerns knowing passage from death into life through brotherly love. Observers, claims and warnings differ.
    1 John 3:14 We know that we have passed from death into life, because we love the brothers. The one who does not love abides in death.

John 13:36
Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, where art Thou going?” Jesus answered him, “Where I go, you cannot follow Me now, but you will follow later.”
  John 21:18–21:19 — later-following comparison: Jesus' promise that Peter will follow later can be compared with His later Follow Me command after a prediction of Peter's death. John21's narrator interprets that prediction as death glorifying God; it does not settle every meaning or exact execution method of the earlier promise.
    John 21:18 Amen, amen, I say to you: when you were younger, you tied your belt around yourself and walked where you wished; but when you grow old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will tie your belt around you and carry you where you do not wish.”
    John 21:19 Now He said this to indicate by what kind of death he would glorify God. And having said this, He says to him, “Follow Me.”

John 13:37
Peter said to Him, “Lord, why can I not follow Thee now? I will lay down my life for Thee.”
  Mark 14:31 — Peter-pledge comparison: Both give Peter's expressed readiness for death, but John says he will lay down his life for Jesus, while Mark says even if he must die with Him he will not deny Him. In John, Peter also asks why he cannot follow now; Mark places the insistence after the denial prediction. The relation words and speech sequence remain distinct.
    Mark 14:31 He spoke all the more insistently, “Even if I must die with Thee, I will certainly not deny Thee.” They all said the same.

John 13:38
Jesus answered, “Will you lay down your life for Me? Amen, amen, I tell you: a rooster will certainly not crow until you deny Me three times.”
  Mark 14:30 — denial-prediction parallel: Both predict three denials before cockcrow. John first questions Peter's life-laying pledge and does not say twice; Mark specifies this night and two crows. No crow-count or timing reconciliation is invented.
    Mark 14:30 Jesus said to him, “Truly I tell you, today, this very night, before a rooster crows twice, you will deny Me three times.”

John 14:1
“Do not let your heart be troubled. Believe in God, and believe also in Me.
  John 14:27 — internal reassurance recurrence: Both tell the disciples not to let their hearts be troubled. The opening joins belief in God and Jesus; the later assurance gives Jesus' peace and forbids fear. The repeated comfort does not make every clause identical.
    John 14:27 Peace I leave with you; My peace I give you. I do not give to you as the world gives. Do not let your heart be troubled or afraid.

John 14:2
In My Father’s house are many dwelling places. If it were not so, would I have told you that I am going to prepare a place for you?
  Hebrews 11:16 — prepared-home comparison: Both describe a divinely prepared future home. Jesus speaks of His Father's house and preparing dwelling places; Hebrews names God preparing a city for those desiring a heavenly country. The metaphors, recipients and descriptions of preparation are not converted into an architectural map.
    Hebrews 11:16 But now they desire a better one, that is, a heavenly one. Therefore God is not ashamed of them, to be called their God, for He has prepared a city for them.

John 14:3
And if I go and prepare a place for you, I am coming again and will take you to Myself, so that where I am, you also may be.
  1 Thessalonians 4:16–4:17 — presence-with-the-Lord promise comparison: Both anticipate followers being with the coming Lord. John names prepared place and Jesus receiving them to Himself; Paul names resurrection, clouds and meeting Him. The passages are compared without assigning one meaning or chronology to every coming saying in John14.
    1 Thessalonians 4:16 For the Lord Himself will descend from heaven with a commanding cry, with an archangel’s voice and with God’s trumpet, and the dead in Christ will rise first.
    1 Thessalonians 4:17 Then we who are alive, who remain, will be caught up together with them in clouds to meet the Lord in the air; and so we will always be with the Lord.

John 14:4
And you know the way to where I am going.”
  John 14:6 — internal way-saying explanation: The statement that they know the way is clarified by Jesus' I-am-the-way answer following Thomas's objection. The answer concerns coming to the Father through Jesus, not a supplied geographical route to dwelling places.
    John 14:6 Jesus says to him, “I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through Me.

John 14:5
Thomas says to Him, “Lord, we do not know where Thou art going. How can we know the way?”
  John 13:36 — destination-question comparison: Both disciples ask about Jesus' destination. Thomas links not knowing where He goes with not knowing the way; Peter asks where and receives a cannot-follow-now promise. The disciples, questions and answers remain distinct.
    John 13:36 Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, where art Thou going?” Jesus answered him, “Where I go, you cannot follow Me now, but you will follow later.”

John 14:6
Jesus says to him, “I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through Me.
  Ephesians 2:18 — access-to-the-Father comparison: Both present access to the Father through Jesus. John names Jesus way, truth and life and says no one comes except through Him; Ephesians names both groups' access through Him in one Spirit. The Spirit and group descriptions are not inserted into John's sentence.
    Ephesians 2:18 because through Him we both have access in one Spirit to the Father.

John 14:7
If you have known Me, you will also know My Father. From now on you know Him and have seen Him.”
  John 8:19 — Father-and-Son knowledge comparison: Both connect knowing Jesus with knowing His Father. John14 adds now knowing and seeing; John8 rebukes questioners who know neither. Their positive promise and negative rebuke remain distinct.
    John 8:19 So they said to Him, “Where is Thy Father?” Jesus answered, “You know neither Me nor My Father. If you knew Me, you would also know My Father.”

John 14:8
Philip says to Him, “Lord, show us the Father, and it is enough for us.”
  Exodus 33:18–33:20 — divine-vision request comparison: Both request a divine disclosure. Philip asks Jesus to show the Father; Moses asks the Lord to show His glory and receives a response about glory and the impossibility of seeing His face and living. Revealer, request object and resulting words are not equated.
    Exodus 33:18 Moses said, “Show me Thy glory.”
    Exodus 33:19 He said, “I will pass before you in My glory and proclaim My name, Lord, before you. I will have mercy on whom I will have mercy, and compassion on whom I will have compassion.”
    Exodus 33:20 He said, “You cannot see My face, for no man shall see My face and live.”

John 14:9
Jesus says to him, “Have I been with you for so long a time, and you have not known Me, Philip? The one who has seen Me has seen the Father. How can you say, ‘Show us the Father’?
  John 12:45 — Father-revelation recurrence: Both connect seeing Jesus with seeing His Father or Sender. John14 answers named Philip and his request; John12 gives the shorter beholding-the-Sender saying. The statements do not erase the Father/Son distinction.
    John 12:45 And the one who beholds Me beholds the One who sent Me.

John 14:10
Do you not believe that I am in the Father and the Father is in Me? The words that I say to you I do not speak from Myself; but the Father, who remains in Me, does His works.
  John 12:49 — Father-origin speech comparison: Both deny Jesus speaking from Himself and connect His speech with the Father. John14 adds reciprocal indwelling and the Father's works; John12 describes the Father's command about what to say. Speech, work and command formulations remain distinct.
    John 12:49 Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.

John 14:11
Believe Me that I am in the Father and the Father is in Me. But if not, believe because of the works themselves.
  John 10:37–10:38 — works-and-indwelling comparison: Both appeal to the works as grounds for recognizing Father/Son indwelling even when the speaker is not believed. John10 adds the conditional failure-to-do-Father's-works clause; John14 directly commands belief.
    John 10:37 If I do not do My Father’s works, do not believe Me.
    John 10:38 But if I do them, even if you do not believe Me, believe the works, so that you may know and continue to know that the Father is in Me and I in the Father.”

John 14:12
Amen, amen, I say to you: the one who believes in Me will also do the works that I do, and will do greater works than these, because I am going to the Father.
  Acts 5:12–5:16 — promise-and-reported-work comparison: Acts narrates many signs and healings through apostles after Jesus' ministry, providing concrete followers' works to compare. It does not quantify greater, identify every believer's work, or turn the crowd's hoped-for shadow contact into the stated cause of every healing.
    Acts 5:12 Many signs and wonders were taking place among the people through the apostles’ hands. They were all together with one accord in Solomon’s Portico.
    Acts 5:13 None of the rest dared to join them, but the people held them in high esteem.
    Acts 5:14 More and more believers were added to the Lord, multitudes of both men and women,
    Acts 5:15 so that they even carried the sick into the streets and laid them on small beds and mats, so that as Peter came by, at least his shadow might fall upon one of them.
    Acts 5:16 The crowd also came together from the towns around Jerusalem, bringing the sick and those troubled by unclean spirits, and they were all healed.

John 14:13
And whatever you ask in My name, this I will do, so that the Father may be glorified in the Son.
  John 16:23 — name-and-request promise comparison: Both promise response to asking in Jesus' name. John14 says Jesus will do it for the Father's glorification in the Son; John16 explicitly names the Father as giver. Their stated agents and purpose clauses are preserved.
    John 16:23 And on that day you will ask Me nothing. Amen, amen, I say to you: if you ask the Father anything, He will give it to you in My name.

John 14:14
If you ask Me anything in My name, I will do it.
  John 16:23 — request-address comparison: Both relate requests in Jesus' name to promised response. John14 explicitly says ask Me and I will do it; John16 names asking the Father and His giving. Neither address or agent is silently rewritten to match the other.
    John 16:23 And on that day you will ask Me nothing. Amen, amen, I say to you: if you ask the Father anything, He will give it to you in My name.

John 14:15
“If you love Me, you will keep My commandments.
  1 John 5:3 — love-and-commandment comparison: Both connect love with keeping commandments. Jesus says loving Me leads to keeping My commands; the letter describes love of God as keeping His commands and adds they are not burdensome. The pronouns, addressed persons and added claim remain distinct.
    1 John 5:3 For this is the love of God: that we keep His commandments; and His commandments are not burdensome,

John 14:16
And I will ask the Father, and He will give you another Advocate, so that He may be with you forever:
  John 16:7 — Advocate-promise comparison: Both promise the Advocate for the disciples. John14 has Jesus ask and the Father give another Advocate forever; John16 has Jesus send Him upon departing. The sending/giving roles and departure condition are not collapsed.
    John 16:7 Yet I tell you the truth: it is better for you that I go away. For if I do not go away, the Advocate certainly will not come to you; but if I go, I will send Him to you.

John 14:17
the Spirit of truth, whom the world cannot receive, because it neither beholds Him nor knows Him. You know Him, because He remains with you and will be in you.
  John 15:26 — Spirit-of-truth comparison: Both name the Spirit of truth. John14 contrasts the world's inability to receive Him with disciples' knowledge and indwelling promise; John15 names proceeding from the Father, Jesus' sending and witness to Him. The distinct relations and tasks are preserved.
    John 15:26 “When the Advocate comes, whom I will send you from the Father, the Spirit of truth who proceeds from the Father, He will bear witness about Me.

John 14:18
I will not leave you orphans; I am coming to you.
  Matthew 28:20 — continued-presence promise comparison: Both assure disciples of Jesus' continuing presence. John says He will not leave them orphans and is coming to them; Matthew says He is with them through the age's consummation. The formulations do not determine an unmentioned date for every Johannine coming promise.
    Matthew 28:20 teaching them to keep all that I commanded you; and behold, I am with you all the days, until the consummation of the age.”

John 14:19
Yet a little while, and the world no longer beholds Me, but you behold Me. Because I live, you also will live.
  John 6:57 — dependent-life comparison: Both connect followers' life with Jesus' life. John14 says because I live you will live and contrasts world/disciple sight; John6 connects the living Sender, Jesus and the eater who lives because of Him. The eating image and visibility clauses remain distinct.
    John 6:57 Just as the living Father sent Me and I live because of the Father, so the one who eats Me will also live because of Me.

John 14:20
On that day you will know that I am in My Father, and you in Me, and I in you.
  John 17:21–17:23 — indwelling-and-unity comparison: Both passages describe the Father-Son-followers indwelling relation. John14 says Jesus is in the Father and the disciples in Jesus and Jesus in them; John17 additionally says the Father is in Jesus and prays for unity and the world's belief and knowledge. The directions, petitions and predicted recognition remain distinct.
    John 17:21 so that they may all be one, just as Thou, Father, art in Me and I in Thee, so that they also may be in Us, so that the world may believe that Thou didst send Me.
    John 17:22 And the glory that Thou hast given Me I have given them, so that they may be one just as We are one:
    John 17:23 I in them and Thou in Me, so that they may be made completely one, so that the world may know that Thou didst send Me and didst love them just as Thou didst love Me.

John 14:21
The one who has My commandments and keeps them is the one who loves Me. And the one who loves Me will be loved by My Father, and I will love him and reveal Myself to him.”
  John 14:23 — internal love-and-obedience recurrence: Both connect love, keeping Jesus' teaching and the Father's love. The first promises Jesus' self-revelation; the later answer promises Father and Son making their dwelling with the person. The related promises remain their stated forms.
    John 14:23 Jesus answered and said to him, “If anyone loves Me, he will keep My word, and My Father will love him, and We will come to him and make Our dwelling with him.

John 14:22
Judas—not the Iscariot—says to Him, “Lord, what then has happened that Thou art going to reveal Thyself to us and not to the world?”
  John 7:3–7:4 — public-revelation question comparison: Both question Jesus' manifestation to the world. Judas explicitly not Iscariot asks why revelation to the disciples rather than the world; His brothers earlier urged a public display. Their persons, motives and stated requests are not equated.
    John 7:3 So His brothers said to Him, “Leave here and go into Judea, so that Thy disciples also may see the works Thou doest.
    John 7:4 For no one acts in secret while seeking to be known publicly. If Thou doest these things, reveal Thyself to the world.”

John 14:23
Jesus answered and said to him, “If anyone loves Me, he will keep My word, and My Father will love him, and We will come to him and make Our dwelling with him.
  1 John 3:23–3:24 — obedience-and-indwelling comparison: Both connect keeping divine teaching with indwelling. Jesus promises Father and Son dwelling with the one loving and keeping His word; the letter describes commandment keeping, mutual abiding and the given Spirit as assurance. The explicit persons and conditions remain distinct.
    1 John 3:23 And this is His commandment: that we believe in the name of His Son Jesus Christ and love one another, just as He gave us a commandment.
    1 John 3:24 And the one who keeps His commandments abides in Him, and He in that person. And by this we know that He abides in us: by the Spirit whom He gave us.

John 14:24
The one who does not love Me does not keep My words. And the word that you hear is not Mine, but the Father’s who sent Me.
  John 12:49 — Father-origin word comparison: Both refer Jesus' speech to the Father who sent Him rather than autonomous speech. John14 adds lack of love and failure to keep His words; John12 describes the Father's command. The moral contrast is not supplied as though present in the target.
    John 12:49 Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.

John 14:25
“These things I have spoken to you while remaining with you.
  John 14:26 — internal teaching-and-remembrance link: Jesus' words spoken while with the disciples are the stated subject the coming Advocate will remind them of. The following promise supplies a future remembrance relation, not independent evidence for every present speech or an identity of the two teachers.
    John 14:26 But the Advocate, the Holy Spirit, whom the Father will send in My name, He will teach you all things and remind you of all that I said to you.

John 14:26
But the Advocate, the Holy Spirit, whom the Father will send in My name, He will teach you all things and remind you of all that I said to you.
  John 16:13 — Spirit-teaching comparison: Both promise the Spirit's instruction. John14 names the Father sending the Holy Spirit in Jesus' name and remembering His words; John16 names guidance into truth, heard speech and announced coming things. Those agents, relations and tasks are not made verbally identical.
    John 16:13 But when He comes, the Spirit of truth, He will guide you in all the truth; for He will not speak from Himself, but will speak whatever He hears, and will announce to you the things to come.

John 14:27
Peace I leave with you; My peace I give you. I do not give to you as the world gives. Do not let your heart be troubled or afraid.
  John 16:33 — peace-and-world reassurance comparison: Both promise peace in Jesus amid the world's contrasting condition. John14 names His gift and fearless hearts; John16 names affliction and His victory. The sayings do not deny the stated affliction or repeat identical promises.
    John 16:33 These things I have spoken to you so that in Me you may have peace. In the world you have affliction; but take courage: I have overcome the world.”

John 14:28
You heard that I said to you, ‘I am going away, and I am coming to you.’ If you loved Me, you would have rejoiced that I am going to the Father, because the Father is greater than I.
  John 16:28 — departure-to-the-Father comparison: Both describe Jesus going to the Father. John14 also recalls coming again and says the Father is greater than I; John16 states coming from the Father into the world and leaving it. The greater-than wording is retained, not inserted into the target or explained away.
    John 16:28 I came forth from the Father and have come into the world. Again, I am leaving the world and going to the Father.”

John 14:29
And now I have told you before it happens, so that when it happens you may believe.
  John 13:19 — forewarning-and-belief recurrence: Both explain advance telling so that later events produce belief. John13 additionally says that I am and concerns the betrayal context; John14 does not repeat that same object or identify all events.
    John 13:19 From now on I tell you before it happens, so that when it happens you may believe that I am.

John 14:30
I will no longer speak much with you, for the ruler of the world is coming, and he has nothing in Me.
  John 12:31 — world-ruler comparison: Both name the ruler of the world in Jesus' approaching passion discourse. John14 says he is coming and has nothing in Jesus; John12 says judgment is now and he will be cast out. The temporal and relational claims remain distinct.
    John 12:31 Now is the judgment of this world; now the ruler of this world will be cast out.

John 14:31
But so that the world may know that I love the Father, I do just as the Father commanded Me. Rise, let us go from here.”
  John 6:38 — Father-will obedience comparison: Both present Jesus as acting according to His Father's will rather than His own initiative. John14 explicitly names love and the Father's command; John6 names heavenly descent and the Sender's will. The departure instruction is not independently narrated in the target.
    John 6:38 because I have come down from heaven not to do My own will, but the will of the One who sent Me.

John 15:1
“I am the true vine, and My Father is the vinegrower.
  Jeremiah 2:21 — vine-imagery comparison: Both connect a vine's genuine quality with divine cultivation. Jesus calls Himself the true vine and His Father vinegrower; Jeremiah has the Lord address a planted fruitful, wholly genuine vine that became bitter and foreign. The vine's identity, cultivation roles and outcomes are not equated.
    Jeremiah 2:21 But I planted you as a fruitful vine, wholly genuine. How have you changed into bitterness, you foreign vine?

John 15:2
Every branch in Me that does not bear fruit He takes away; and every one that bears fruit He prunes, so that it may bear more fruit.
  Luke 13:6–13:9 — fruit-and-cultivation comparison: Both describe fruit expected from cultivation and removal threatened for failure. John distinguishes fruitless branches removed and fruitful ones pruned; Luke's fig-tree parable gives extra care and time before cutting. Plants, treatment and outcomes remain distinct.
    Luke 13:6 And He told this parable: “A certain man had a fig tree planted in his vineyard. He came looking for fruit on it and found none.
    Luke 13:7 So he said to the vineyard worker, ‘Behold, for three years I have been coming to look for fruit on this fig tree and have found none. Cut it down, then! Why should it also make the ground unproductive?’
    Luke 13:8 But he answered him, ‘Master, leave it this year also, until I dig around it and put on manure.
    Luke 13:9 And if it produces fruit in the coming year—but if not, you shall cut it down.’”

John 15:3
You are already clean because of the word that I have spoken to you.
  John 13:10–13:11 — disciples-cleanness recurrence: Both call the disciples clean. John15 explains it through Jesus' word; John13 uses bath/feet language and an exception explained by the betrayer. Neither explanation is substituted for the other's actual wording.
    John 13:10 Jesus said to him, “The one who has bathed has no need to wash except the feet, but is wholly clean. And you are clean, but not all.”
    John 13:11 For He knew the one who was handing Him over; for this reason He said, “Not all of you are clean.”

John 15:4
Remain in Me, and I in you. Just as the branch cannot bear fruit from itself unless it remains in the vine, neither can you unless you remain in Me.
  1 John 2:24 — remaining-and-message comparison: Both connect remaining in the Son with what is received from Him. John15 gives the vine-and-branch dependence image; the letter urges the original message remaining so hearers abide in Son and Father. Message, persons and fruit imagery remain distinct.
    1 John 2:24 As for you, let what you heard from the beginning abide in you. If what you heard from the beginning abides in you, you too will abide in the Son and in the Father.

John 15:5
I am the vine; you are the branches. The one who remains in Me, and I in him, bears much fruit, because apart from Me you can do nothing.
  Philippians 4:11–4:13 — dependence-and-empowerment comparison: Jesus says fruit comes by remaining in Him and apart from Him nothing can be done; Paul describes strength in the One empowering him amid plenty or need. Vine fruit and circumstantial endurance differ, and the target is not rewritten into Jesus' exact first-person formulation.
    Philippians 4:11 Not that I speak because of need, for I have learned to be content whatever my circumstances.
    Philippians 4:12 I know how to be brought low, and I know how to abound. In everything and in all things I have learned the secret, both to be filled and to hunger, both to abound and to be in need.
    Philippians 4:13 I have strength for all things in the One who empowers me.

John 15:6
If anyone does not remain in Me, he is thrown outside like the branch and withers; and they gather them and throw them into the fire, and they are burned.
  Ezekiel 15:2–15:6 — vinewood-and-fire comparison: Both picture vine material being consumed by fire. John concerns a nonremaining branch that withers; Ezekiel compares unusable vinewood and burned prunings with Jerusalem's inhabitants. The people, conditions and images are bounded, not one new identity or judgment date.
    Ezekiel 15:2 “And you, son of man, what could vinewood be among all the branch wood found among the forest trees?
    Ezekiel 15:3 Will they take wood from it to make anything useful? Will they take a peg from it to hang any vessel upon it?
    Ezekiel 15:4 It has only been given to the fire to be consumed. Fire consumes its yearly prunings, and it is utterly spent. Surely it will not be useful for work?
    Ezekiel 15:5 Even when it is whole, it will not serve for work. How much less if fire consumes it completely—will it still serve for work?
    Ezekiel 15:6 Therefore say, ‘Thus says the Lord: As with the vinewood among the forest trees, which I have given to the fire to be consumed, so I have given Jerusalem’s inhabitants.

John 15:7
If you remain in Me and My words remain in you, ask whatever you wish, and it will be done for you.
  John 16:23 — remaining-and-request comparison: Both promise requests answered. John15 conditions asking on remaining in Jesus and His words; John16 names the Father giving in Jesus' name on that day. The conditions, named recipient and timing remain the supplied formulations.
    John 16:23 And on that day you will ask Me nothing. Amen, amen, I say to you: if you ask the Father anything, He will give it to you in My name.

John 15:8
In this My Father has been glorified: that you bear much fruit and become My disciples.
  Matthew 5:16 — Father-glorified-through-followers comparison: Both connect disciples' conduct with the Father's glory. John uses much fruit and discipleship; Matthew uses visible good works and a shining light. The images and observing audience differ without making the moral purpose identical wording.
    Matthew 5:16 So let your light shine before people, that they may see your good works and glorify your Father who is in the heavens.

John 15:9
Just as the Father loved Me, I also loved you. Remain in My love.
  John 17:23 — Father-Son-follower love comparison: Both relate love for followers to the Father's love for Jesus. John15 has Jesus love them as the Father loved Him; John17 says the Father loved them as He loved Jesus. The directions of the love statements are explicit rather than collapsed.
    John 17:23 I in them and Thou in Me, so that they may be made completely one, so that the world may know that Thou didst send Me and didst love them just as Thou didst love Me.

John 15:10
If you keep My commandments, you will remain in My love, just as I have kept My Father’s commandments and remain in His love.
  John 14:15 — love-and-commandment recurrence: Both connect love and keeping Jesus' commands. John15 says obedience remains in love and gives Jesus' obedience to His Father as model; John14 says love leads to commandment keeping. The conditional directions and added model remain distinct.
    John 14:15 “If you love Me, you will keep My commandments.

John 15:11
These things I have spoken to you so that My joy may be in you and your joy may be made full.
  John 16:24 — full-joy purpose recurrence: Both use the purpose of joy being made full. John15 relates it to Jesus' spoken teaching and His joy in them; John16 relates it to asking and receiving in His name. The stated means are not equated.
    John 16:24 Until now you have asked nothing in My name. Ask, and you will receive, so that your joy may be made full.

John 15:12
“This is My commandment: that you love one another just as I loved you.
  John 13:34 — commandment recurrence: Both command mutual love just as Jesus loved the disciples. John13 additionally calls the command new; John15 gives it as the definition of My commandment.
    John 13:34 I give you a new commandment: that you love one another. Just as I have loved you, you also are to love one another.

John 15:13
No one has greater love than this: that someone should lay down his life for his friends.
  1 John 3:16 — life-giving love comparison: Both describe laying down life as love and a pattern for followers. John15 names the greatest love for friends; the letter names His life given for us and the duty toward brothers. The recipients and rhetorical forms remain distinct.
    1 John 3:16 By this we have come to know love: He laid down His life for us; and we ought to lay down our lives for the brothers.

John 15:14
You are My friends if you do what I command you.
  John 15:15 — internal friendship explanation: The friends designation here is linked to obedience, and the following verse explains friendship through disclosed knowledge instead of an uninformed slave's position. These are the discourse's stated relational explanations, not independent accounts or identical exclusive formulas.
    John 15:15 I no longer call you slaves, because the slave does not know what his master is doing; but I have called you friends, because I have made known to you everything that I heard from My Father.

John 15:15
I no longer call you slaves, because the slave does not know what his master is doing; but I have called you friends, because I have made known to you everything that I heard from My Father.
  Amos 3:7 — divine-disclosure comparison: Both portray privileged recipients informed of divine matters. Jesus calls disciples friends because He disclosed what He heard from the Father; Amos says the Lord reveals His discipline to servant-prophets. Servant/friend imagery, discipline/all-heard content and speakers are preserved rather than made identical.
    Amos 3:7 For the Lord God will certainly do no deed unless He reveals His discipline to His servants the prophets.

John 15:16
You did not choose Me, but I chose you, and appointed you so that you should go and bear fruit and your fruit should remain, so that whatever you ask the Father in My name He may give you.
  John 6:70–6:71 — disciples-choice comparison: Both explicitly state that Jesus chose disciples. John15 adds appointment for lasting fruit and Father-addressed requests; John6 names the Twelve including the future betrayer. The later discourse follows Judas's departure, and choosing is not made proof of identical outcomes for every member.
    John 6:70 Jesus answered them, “Did I not choose you, the Twelve? Yet one of you is a devil.”
    John 6:71 He was speaking of Judas, son of Simon Iscariot, for this man was about to hand Him over, one of the Twelve.

John 15:17
These things I command you, so that you love one another.
  John 15:12 — internal command refrain: The repeated love-one-another instruction recalls this chapter's earlier definition of Jesus' command. The earlier unit also names His own love as model; the refrain is not additional independent testimony.
    John 15:12 “This is My commandment: that you love one another just as I loved you.

John 15:18
“If the world hates you, know that it has hated Me before you.
  1 John 3:13 — world-hatred comparison: Both address followers hated by the world. John15 grounds it in earlier hatred of Jesus; the letter tells brothers not to be surprised. The target does not independently narrate the prior hatred or identify each persecutor.
    1 John 3:13 And do not be surprised, brothers, if the world hates you.

John 15:19
If you were from the world, the world would love its own. But because you are not from the world, but I chose you out of the world, for this reason the world hates you.
  John 17:14 — world-and-belonging comparison: Both say the world hates Jesus' followers because they are not from it. John15 names Jesus' choosing them out of it; John17 names the given word and Jesus also not being from it. Their explanations are related without turning world into one ethnic group.
    John 17:14 I have given them Thy word, and the world hated them, because they are not from the world, just as I am not from the world.

John 15:20
Remember the word that I said to you: ‘A slave is not greater than his master.’ If they persecuted Me, they will also persecute you; if they kept My word, they will also keep yours.
  John 13:16 — explicit earlier-saying callback: Jesus expressly recalls the slave-not-greater-than-master saying. John13 applies it to service and also names messenger/sender; John15 applies it to persecution and reception of words. The new applications do not change the earlier text.
    John 13:16 Amen, amen, I tell you: a slave is not greater than his master, nor a messenger greater than the one who sent him.

John 15:21
But they will do all these things to you because of My name, because they do not know the One who sent Me.
  John 16:3 — opposition-and-knowledge comparison: Both explain hostile actions through not knowing the Father or Sender. John15 names Jesus' name and the sending One; John16 adds neither the Father nor Jesus known. The stated persons and grounds remain distinct.
    John 16:3 And they will do these things because they have known neither the Father nor Me.

John 15:22
If I had not come and spoken to them, they would have no sin; but now they have no excuse for their sin.
  John 9:40–9:41 — revelation-and-responsibility comparison: Both use a counterfactual no-sin statement before assigning present responsibility. John15 names Jesus' coming speech and no excuse; John9 contrasts blindness with claiming sight and sin remaining. Neither is rewritten into a different universal definition of sin or the same condition.
    John 9:40 Some of the Pharisees who were with Him heard these things and said to Him, “Are we also blind?”
    John 9:41 Jesus said to them, “If you were blind, you would not have sin. But now you say, ‘We see’; your sin remains.”

John 15:23
The one who hates Me hates My Father also.
  John 5:23 — Father-Son response comparison: Both connect response to Jesus with response to His Father. John15 speaks of hatred; John5 speaks of withholding honor from the Son and Sender. The different moral terms and formulations are preserved.
    John 5:23 so that all may honor the Son just as they honor the Father. The one who does not honor the Son does not honor the Father who sent Him.

John 15:24
If I had not done among them the works that no one else did, they would have no sin; but now they have both seen and hated both Me and My Father.
  John 5:36 — works-as-disclosure comparison: Both present Jesus' exceptional works as disclosing His Father-related mission. John15 emphasizes culpable hatred despite seeing works; John5 names works given by the Father as testimony to His sending. The no-sin counterfactual and hatred are not imported into the target.
    John 5:36 But I have testimony greater than John’s: for the works the Father has given Me to complete, the very works I do, bear witness concerning Me that the Father has sent Me.

John 15:25
But this is so that the word written in their law might be fulfilled: ‘They hated Me without cause.’
  Psalms 68:5 — quotation allusion: Both describe hatred without cause. The psalm also names numerous unjust enemies and repayment of what was not plundered; John applies the written saying to hostility toward Jesus. The target is not rewritten to name the Gospel's people or to omit its other clauses.
    Psalms 68:5 More numerous than the hairs on my head became those hating me without cause; my enemies pursuing me unjustly became strong. What I did not plunder, I was then repaying.

John 15:26
“When the Advocate comes, whom I will send you from the Father, the Spirit of truth who proceeds from the Father, He will bear witness about Me.
  John 14:26 — Advocate-promise comparison: Both name the Advocate's future work for disciples. John15 says Jesus sends from the Father, the Spirit proceeds from the Father and testifies to Jesus; John14 says the Father sends the Holy Spirit in Jesus' name to teach/remind. All stated relations remain explicit without adding another procession clause.
    John 14:26 But the Advocate, the Holy Spirit, whom the Father will send in My name, He will teach you all things and remind you of all that I said to you.

John 15:27
And you also bear witness, because you have been with Me from the beginning.
  Acts 1:21–1:22 — witness-and-companionship comparison: Both connect witnessing to prior companionship with Jesus from the beginning. Acts specifies the replacement-apostle criterion from John's baptism to the ascent and resurrection witness; John speaks to the existing disciples alongside the Spirit's witness. Groups and exact commissions are not equated.
    Acts 1:21 Therefore, of the men who accompanied us throughout the time when the Lord Jesus went in and out among us,
    Acts 1:22 beginning with John’s baptism until the day when He was taken up from us, one of these must become with us a witness of His resurrection.”

John 16:1
“These things I have spoken to you so that you may not stumble.
  Matthew 24:9–24:10 — forewarning-and-stumbling comparison: Both connect persecution warnings with danger of stumbling. John states His words aim to prevent it; Matthew predicts many stumbling, handing others over and hatred after affliction. A preventive purpose and predicted failures are not identical outcomes for every hearer.
    Matthew 24:9 Then they will hand you over to affliction and kill you, and you will be hated by all nations because of My name.
    Matthew 24:10 Then many will stumble, hand one another over, and hate one another.

John 16:2
They will put you out of the synagogues; indeed, an hour is coming when everyone who kills you will think he is offering worship to God.
  Acts 26:9–26:11 — religious-persecution comparison: Paul recounts thinking he must oppose Jesus' name, prison, death votes and synagogue punishment. This is a concrete comparison for the foretold hostility and persecutors' conviction of duty. Acts does not repeat the exact offering-worship claim or identify every future killer with Paul.
    Acts 26:9 I myself thought that I must do many things against the name of Jesus the Nazarene.
    Acts 26:10 This I did in Jerusalem: I shut up many of the saints in prisons, having received authority from the chief priests, and when they were being killed, I cast my vote against them.
    Acts 26:11 I punished them often in all the synagogues and tried to compel them to blaspheme. In my intense rage against them, I pursued them even to foreign cities.

John 16:3
And they will do these things because they have known neither the Father nor Me.
  John 15:21 — opposition-and-knowledge recurrence: Both explain persecution through ignorance of the Father or Sender. John16 explicitly says neither Father nor Jesus known; John15 names acting because of Jesus' name and not knowing His Sender. Their stated objects remain distinct.
    John 15:21 But they will do all these things to you because of My name, because they do not know the One who sent Me.

John 16:4
But these things I have spoken to you so that, when their hour comes, you may remember that I told you about them. I did not tell you these things from the beginning, because I was with you.
  John 13:19 — forewarning-and-remembrance comparison: Both explain advance teaching before events. John16 aims at remembering the warning in persecution's hour and contrasts earlier presence; John13 aims at belief that I am when the betrayal occurs. Purposes, events and temporal scope remain distinct.
    John 13:19 From now on I tell you before it happens, so that when it happens you may believe that I am.

John 16:5
But now I am going to the One who sent Me, and none of you asks Me, ‘Where art Thou going?’
  John 13:36 — internal destination-question contrast: Both contain the Where-art-Thou-going question. Peter actually asks it in13:36;16:5 now says none asks while Jesus goes to His Sender. The earlier question and present statement are preserved without changing none to never or inventing an unrecorded chronology.
    John 13:36 Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, where art Thou going?” Jesus answered him, “Where I go, you cannot follow Me now, but you will follow later.”

John 16:6
But because I have spoken these things to you, sorrow has filled your heart.
  John 16:22 — internal sorrow-and-joy contrast: The disciples' sorrow-filled heart is the present state later contrasted with a rejoicing heart when Jesus sees them again. The later promise interprets the stated sorrow's future reversal, not an independent report of every feeling.
    John 16:22 So you too now have sorrow; but I will see you again, and your heart will rejoice, and no one takes your joy away from you.

John 16:7
Yet I tell you the truth: it is better for you that I go away. For if I do not go away, the Advocate certainly will not come to you; but if I go, I will send Him to you.
  John 14:16–14:17 — Advocate-promise comparison: Both promise the Advocate's presence with disciples. John16 names Jesus' departure as condition and His sending; John14 names His asking and the Father's giving, with the Spirit's remaining and indwelling. The Father/Son/Spirit roles and exact conditions remain distinct.
    John 14:16 And I will ask the Father, and He will give you another Advocate, so that He may be with you forever:
    John 14:17 the Spirit of truth, whom the world cannot receive, because it neither beholds Him nor knows Him. You know Him, because He remains with you and will be in you.

John 16:8
And when He comes, He will convict the world concerning sin and concerning righteousness and concerning judgment:
  John 16:9–16:11 — internal threefold explanation: The following units repeat and explain each stated heading: sin through unbelief, righteousness with Jesus' going to the Father, and judgment through the world's ruler judged. The link identifies Jesus' actual explanation without supplying an additional causal theory.
    John 16:9 concerning sin, because they do not believe in Me;
    John 16:10 concerning righteousness, because I am going to the Father and you no longer behold Me;
    John 16:11 and concerning judgment, because the ruler of this world has been judged.

John 16:9
concerning sin, because they do not believe in Me;
  John 3:18 — unbelief-and-judgment comparison: Both connect not believing Jesus/the Son with moral or judicial consequences. John16 explains the Spirit's sin-conviction heading; John3 contrasts believers unjudged with unbelief already judged. Sin and judgment are not silently substituted or turned into an exhaustive sin list.
    John 3:18 The one who believes in Him is not judged; but the one who does not believe has already been judged, because he has not believed in the name of the only-begotten Son of God.

John 16:10
concerning righteousness, because I am going to the Father and you no longer behold Me;
  John 16:5 — internal departure-premise recurrence: The going-to-the-Father premise recalls Jesus' earlier statement of going to His Sender in this discourse. The target does not independently explain righteousness or the no-longer-beholding clause; those remain the present verse's stated explanation.
    John 16:5 But now I am going to the One who sent Me, and none of you asks Me, ‘Where art Thou going?’

John 16:11
and concerning judgment, because the ruler of this world has been judged.
  John 12:31 — world-ruler judgment recurrence: Both relate judgment to the ruler of this world. John16 says he has been judged; John12 says now judgment and future casting out. No new personal identity, date or uniform tense is supplied.
    John 12:31 Now is the judgment of this world; now the ruler of this world will be cast out.

John 16:12
“I still have many things to say to you, but you cannot bear them now.
  Mark 4:33–4:34 — teaching-capacity comparison: Both describe Jesus' teaching in relation to what hearers can receive. John says disciples cannot bear further matters now; Mark speaks to the crowd as able to hear and privately explains to disciples. Audiences, parables and the additional future-teaching context remain distinct.
    Mark 4:33 With many such parables He spoke the word to them, as they were able to hear.
    Mark 4:34 He did not speak to them without a parable, but privately He explained everything to His own disciples.

John 16:13
But when He comes, the Spirit of truth, He will guide you in all the truth; for He will not speak from Himself, but will speak whatever He hears, and will announce to you the things to come.
  John 14:26 — Spirit-teaching comparison: Both promise instruction by the coming Spirit. John16 names the Spirit of truth, heard speech and future announcements; John14 names the Father sending in Jesus' name and remembrance of His words. No Sender or speech relation is silently changed.
    John 14:26 But the Advocate, the Holy Spirit, whom the Father will send in My name, He will teach you all things and remind you of all that I said to you.

John 16:14
He will glorify Me, because He will receive from what is Mine and announce it to you.
  John 16:15 — internal reception-and-announcement explanation: The next verse explicitly repeats the Spirit's taking from what is Jesus' and announcing it, explaining it through the Father's possessions being His. This links the stated grounds for the Spirit's glorifying role without making the persons identical.
    John 16:15 Everything that the Father has is Mine. For this reason I said that He receives from what is Mine and will announce it to you.

John 16:15
Everything that the Father has is Mine. For this reason I said that He receives from what is Mine and will announce it to you.
  John 17:10 — Father-Son possession comparison: Both say what belongs to the Father belongs to Jesus. John16 grounds the Spirit's communication in that statement; John17 uses Mine/Thine language while praying concerning disciples. The different referential contexts and roles remain visible.
    John 17:10 and all that is Mine is Thine, and what is Thine is Mine, and I have been glorified in them.

John 16:16
“A little while, and you no longer behold Me; and again a little while, and you will see Me.”
  John 14:19 — visibility-promise comparison: Both describe a little while and changed beholding of Jesus. John16 contrasts not seeing and seeing again for the addressed disciples; John14 contrasts the world no longer beholding with disciples beholding. The groups and temporal formulations are not merged.
    John 14:19 Yet a little while, and the world no longer beholds Me, but you behold Me. Because I live, you also will live.

John 16:17
Then some of His disciples said to one another, “What is this that He says to us: ‘A little while, and you do not behold Me; and again a little while, and you will see Me,’ and, ‘Because I am going to the Father’?”
  John 16:16 — internal quoted-saying link: The disciples repeat the two little-while clauses actually spoken just before their discussion. This identifies the quotation's antecedent, not an independent vision or understanding of the coming events.
    John 16:16 “A little while, and you no longer behold Me; and again a little while, and you will see Me.”
  John 16:10 — internal quoted-premise link: The going-to-the-Father clause in the disciples' composite question recalls Jesus' same because-I-am-going wording in16:10. That target also names righteousness and no longer beholding; those are not all repeated in their question.
    John 16:10 concerning righteousness, because I am going to the Father and you no longer behold Me;

John 16:18
So they were saying, “What is this ‘little while’ that He speaks of? We do not know what He is saying.”
  John 16:16 — internal question-and-saying link: Their question about little while points back to Jesus' repeated little-while saying. The earlier statement supplies the words being questioned, not independent evidence of the disciples' later incomprehension.
    John 16:16 “A little while, and you no longer behold Me; and again a little while, and you will see Me.”

John 16:19
Jesus knew that they wanted to ask Him, and said to them, “Are you seeking an answer among yourselves about this, because I said, ‘A little while, and you do not behold Me; and again a little while, and you will see Me’?
  John 16:17–16:18 — internal discussion-and-response link: Jesus' response explicitly takes up the disciples' recorded discussion and its repeated little-while question. The earlier narration documents their questions, not another independent report of Jesus' knowledge or answer.
    John 16:17 Then some of His disciples said to one another, “What is this that He says to us: ‘A little while, and you do not behold Me; and again a little while, and you will see Me,’ and, ‘Because I am going to the Father’?”
    John 16:18 So they were saying, “What is this ‘little while’ that He speaks of? We do not know what He is saying.”

John 16:20
Amen, amen, I say to you that you will weep and lament, but the world will rejoice. You will be sorrowful, but your sorrow will become joy.
  John 20:20 — sorrow-to-joy promise and narrated example: The later disciples' joy at seeing the risen Lord is a concrete counterpart to the promised reversal from sorrow to joy. The target does not independently narrate the world's rejoicing or exhaust every meaning and time of the promise.
    John 20:20 And having said this, He showed them His hands and His side. Then the disciples rejoiced when they saw the Lord.

John 16:21
When a woman is giving birth, she has sorrow because her hour has come; but when she has borne the child, she no longer remembers the anguish because of the joy that a human being has been born into the world.
  Isaiah 26:17 — labor-imagery comparison: Both compare distress with a woman nearing childbirth and experiencing pangs. John adds birth followed by joy that displaces remembered anguish; Isaiah uses the image for a fear-related address to the Lord. The target does not repeat the happy-birth conclusion or identify the woman.
    Isaiah 26:17 Like a woman in labor nearing childbirth and crying out in her pang, so we became toward Thy beloved because of fear of Thee, Lord.

John 16:22
So you too now have sorrow; but I will see you again, and your heart will rejoice, and no one takes your joy away from you.
  John 20:20 — seeing-and-joy promise comparison: Jesus' promise of renewed sight and rejoicing has a concrete narrative counterpart when disciples see the risen Lord and rejoice. The target does not repeat the cannot-be-taken-away qualification or define every future application of the promise.
    John 20:20 And having said this, He showed them His hands and His side. Then the disciples rejoiced when they saw the Lord.

John 16:23
And on that day you will ask Me nothing. Amen, amen, I say to you: if you ask the Father anything, He will give it to you in My name.
  John 14:13–14:14 — request-promise comparison: Both promise response to asking in Jesus' name. John16 names asking the Father and His giving, alongside asking Jesus nothing; John14 says Jesus will do and explicitly ask Me. The different address, agency and day language are retained rather than harmonized.
    John 14:13 And whatever you ask in My name, this I will do, so that the Father may be glorified in the Son.
    John 14:14 If you ask Me anything in My name, I will do it.

John 16:24
Until now you have asked nothing in My name. Ask, and you will receive, so that your joy may be made full.
  John 15:11 — full-joy purpose recurrence: Both speak of the hearers' joy being made full. John16 joins it to asking and receiving in His name; John15 joins it to Jesus' spoken teaching and His joy in them. The means and prior-history statement remain distinct.
    John 15:11 These things I have spoken to you so that My joy may be in you and your joy may be made full.

John 16:25
“These things I have spoken to you in figures of speech. An hour is coming when I will no longer speak to you in figures of speech, but will tell you plainly about the Father.
  John 16:29 — internal speech-form comparison: Jesus promises a future move from figures of speech to plain disclosure of the Father; disciples subsequently claim He now speaks plainly without figures. Future promise and their present claim are compared without declaring their understanding complete.
    John 16:29 His disciples say, “See, now Thou speakest plainly and usest no figure of speech.

John 16:26
On that day you will ask in My name; and I do not say to you that I will ask the Father concerning you,
  John 14:16 — Father-request formulation comparison: Both mention Jesus asking the Father regarding disciples. John16 says I do not say I will ask concerning you in this name-request context; John14 promises asking for another Advocate. The differing formulations and objects are preserved without converting either into a universal denial or requirement of intercession.
    John 14:16 And I will ask the Father, and He will give you another Advocate, so that He may be with you forever:

John 16:27
for the Father Himself loves you, because you have loved Me and have believed that I came forth from God.
  John 14:21 — Father-love comparison: Both connect the Father's love for followers with their love for Jesus. John16 also names belief that He came from God; John14 connects love with commandment keeping and self-revelation. These supplied grounds and promises are not made one identical formula.
    John 14:21 The one who has My commandments and keeps them is the one who loves Me. And the one who loves Me will be loved by My Father, and I will love him and reveal Myself to him.”

John 16:28
I came forth from the Father and have come into the world. Again, I am leaving the world and going to the Father.”
  John 13:3 — origin-and-return recurrence: Both describe Jesus coming from God/the Father and going back. John16 explicitly names entering and leaving the world; John13's narrator adds His knowledge and the Father's entrusting all things. The different clauses remain intact.
    John 13:3 knowing that the Father had given all things into His hands, and that He came out from God and was going to God,

John 16:29
His disciples say, “See, now Thou speakest plainly and usest no figure of speech.
  John 16:25 — internal speech-form comparison: The disciples' now-plain/no-figure claim explicitly corresponds to Jesus' earlier promise of plain speech after figures. Their assertion is not made independent proof that every earlier future promise has already been exhausted.
    John 16:25 “These things I have spoken to you in figures of speech. An hour is coming when I will no longer speak to you in figures of speech, but will tell you plainly about the Father.

John 16:30
Now we know that Thou knowest all things, and hast no need for anyone to question Thee. By this we believe that Thou didst come forth from God.”
  John 21:17 — knowledge-confession comparison: The group in John16 and Peter in John21 each affirm that Jesus knows all things. The group adds no need for questions and belief in His divine origin; Peter later applies the affirmation to Jesus knowing his love. Collective and individual speakers, applications and dialogue settings remain distinct.
    John 21:17 He says to him the third time, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me?” Peter was grieved because He said to him the third time, “Do you love Me?” And he said to Him, “Lord, Thou knowest all things; Thou knowest that I love Thee.” Jesus says to him, “Feed My sheep.

John 16:31
Jesus answered them, “Do you now believe?
  John 13:37–13:38 — confidence-and-challenge comparison: Both have Jesus answer confident disciples with a question before predicting failure. Here He asks Do you now believe before scattering; earlier He questions Peter's life-laying pledge before denial. The kinds of profession, people and predicted failures remain distinct.
    John 13:37 Peter said to Him, “Lord, why can I not follow Thee now? I will lay down my life for Thee.”
    John 13:38 Jesus answered, “Will you lay down your life for Me? Amen, amen, I tell you: a rooster will certainly not crow until you deny Me three times.”

John 16:32
Behold, an hour is coming, and has come, when you will be scattered, each to his own, and will leave Me alone. Yet I am not alone, because the Father is with Me.
  Mark 14:50 — prediction and narrated abandonment comparison: The predicted leaving Jesus has a concrete counterpart when the arrest account says all left Him and fled. Mark does not repeat John's each-to-his-own language or Father's presence; the lack of human companions is not made absence of the Father.
    Mark 14:50 They all left Him and fled.

John 16:33
These things I have spoken to you so that in Me you may have peace. In the world you have affliction; but take courage: I have overcome the world.”
  John 14:27 — peace-and-world reassurance recurrence: Both promise peace connected with Jesus rather than the world's giving or circumstances. John16 explicitly preserves worldly affliction and victory; John14 gives His peace and forbids troubled fearful hearts. The differing claims remain intact.
    John 14:27 Peace I leave with you; My peace I give you. I do not give to you as the world gives. Do not let your heart be troubled or afraid.

John 17:1
Jesus spoke these things, and, lifting His eyes to heaven, said, “Father, the hour has come. Glorify Thy Son, so that the Son may glorify Thee,
  John 12:23 — teaching parallel: Both sayings announce that the hour has come and connect it with the Son's glorification. John 12 names the Son of Man's glorification; the prayer asks the Father to glorify the Son so the Son may glorify the Father. The earlier announcement and later petition remain distinct speeches.
    John 12:23 Jesus answered them, saying, “The hour has come for the Son of Man to be glorified.

John 17:2
just as Thou gavest Him authority over all flesh, so that He may give eternal life to them—to all that Thou hast given Him.
  John 5:21–5:27 — teaching parallel: Both passages connect the Father's grant to the Son with authority and the giving of life. John 5 also specifies judgment and life in Himself, while the prayer gives authority over all flesh and life to those the Father gives. The different scopes and purposes are retained rather than making every authority statement an identical promise.
    John 5:21 For just as the Father raises the dead and gives life, so also the Son gives life to whom He wills.
    John 5:22 For the Father judges no one, but has given all judgment to the Son,
    John 5:23 so that all may honor the Son just as they honor the Father. The one who does not honor the Son does not honor the Father who sent Him.
    John 5:24 Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who hears My word and believes the One who sent Me has eternal life, and does not come into judgment, but has passed from death into life.
    John 5:25 Amen, amen, I tell you: an hour is coming, and now is, when the dead will hear the voice of the Son of God, and those who hear will live.
    John 5:26 For just as the Father has life in Himself, so He also gave the Son to have life in Himself.
    John 5:27 And He gave Him authority to execute judgment, because He is the Son of Man.

John 17:3
And this is eternal life: that they know Thee, the only true God, and Jesus Christ, whom Thou didst send.
  1 John 5:20 — teaching parallel: Both passages join knowing the true God with Jesus Christ and eternal life. The prayer expressly addresses the Father and identifies Jesus Christ as His sent One; the epistle speaks of knowing the True One and being in His Son. The comparison preserves the clauses rather than newly deciding an ambiguous pronoun's antecedent.
    1 John 5:20 And we know that the Son of God has come and has given us understanding so that we may know the True One. And we are in the True One, in His Son Jesus Christ. This is the true God and eternal life.

John 17:4
I glorified Thee on the earth, having completed the work that Thou hast given Me to do.
  John 4:34 — teaching parallel: Jesus earlier describes His food as doing His Sender's will and completing His work; the prayer says He glorified the Father by completing the work given Him. This is the same Father-given-work motif expressed as mission and completion, without deleting the still-forthcoming passion or the later It is finished saying.
    John 4:34 Jesus said to them, “My food is to do the will of the One who sent Me and to complete His work.

John 17:5
And now, Father, glorify Me with Thyself, with the glory that I had with Thee before the world existed.
  John 1:1–1:3 — teaching parallel: The prayer speaks of the glory Jesus had with the Father before the world's existence; the prologue places the Word with God in the beginning and creation through Him. Both locate His relation to God before the created world. The prayer's glory petition is additional rather than a word-for-word repetition of the prologue.
    John 1:1 In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God.
    John 1:2 He was in the beginning with God.
    John 1:3 All things came into being through Him, and without Him not one thing came into being. What has come into being

John 17:6
“I revealed Thy name to the people whom Thou gavest Me out of the world. They were Thine, and Thou gavest them to Me, and they have kept Thy word.
  John 17:26 — teaching parallel: The prayer later returns explicitly to making the Father's name known to the disciples and its continuing purpose in love and indwelling. That recalls this earlier revealed-name statement within the same prayer. It is an internal recurrence, not a claim about a newly reconstructed pronunciation of the divine name or another independent revelation.
    John 17:26 and I made Thy name known to them, and will make it known, so that the love with which Thou didst love Me may be in them, and I in them.”

John 17:7
Now they have known that everything Thou hast given Me is from Thee,
  John 16:29–16:30 — narrative parallel: The disciples have just said now we know and affirmed Jesus' coming from God; Jesus now presents their recognition that His gifts come from the Father. The connection is their acknowledged understanding in the immediate farewell setting. It does not turn their knowledge into perfect comprehension of everything, especially given the following warning about scattering.
    John 16:29 His disciples say, “See, now Thou speakest plainly and usest no figure of speech.
    John 16:30 Now we know that Thou knowest all things, and hast no need for anyone to question Thee. By this we believe that Thou didst come forth from God.”

John 17:8
because the words that Thou gavest Me I have given them; and they received them and truly knew that I came forth from Thee, and believed that Thou didst send Me.
  John 16:27–16:28 — teaching parallel: The prayer says the disciples received the given words and believed Jesus came forth from and was sent by the Father. The earlier discourse says they believed He came forth from God and explicitly describes His coming from the Father into the world. The comparison preserves received testimony and the Sender-Son relationship.
    John 16:27 for the Father Himself loves you, because you have loved Me and have believed that I came forth from God.
    John 16:28 I came forth from the Father and have come into the world. Again, I am leaving the world and going to the Father.”

John 17:9
I ask concerning them. I do not ask concerning the world, but concerning those whom Thou hast given Me, because they are Thine;
  Luke 22:31–22:32 — thematic parallel: Jesus prays specifically for the disciples given Him; Luke records His prayer that Peter's faith not fail amid Satan's demand to sift the group. Both portray intercession for threatened followers. The group versus Peter focus differs, and this particular prayer's not-for-the-world clause is not made a statement that Jesus never seeks the world's salvation.
    Luke 22:31 Simon, Simon, behold, Satan demanded to have you all, to sift you like wheat.
    Luke 22:32 But I prayed for you, that your faith might not fail; and you, once you have turned back, strengthen your brothers.”

John 17:10
and all that is Mine is Thine, and what is Thine is Mine, and I have been glorified in them.
  John 16:14–16:15 — teaching parallel: The prayer declares the mutual belonging of the Father's and Son's things and Jesus' glorification in the disciples; the preceding discourse says everything the Father has is His and the Spirit will glorify Him by declaring what is His. The repeated possession-and-glory language retains the different roles of Spirit and disciples.
    John 16:14 He will glorify Me, because He will receive from what is Mine and announce it to you.
    John 16:15 Everything that the Father has is Mine. For this reason I said that He receives from what is Mine and will announce it to you.

John 17:11
And I am no longer in the world, but they are in the world, and I am coming to Thee. Holy Father, keep them in Thy name, which Thou hast given Me, so that they may be one just as We are.
  John 10:28–10:30 — teaching parallel: The prayer asks the Father to preserve the disciples and seeks their oneness according to Father and Son; John 10 speaks of preservation from snatching in the Son's and Father's hands and says they are one. The shared preservation-and-unity language does not change the prayer's name-given-to-the-Son wording or equate all kinds of unity without qualification.
    John 10:28 And I give them eternal life, and they will certainly never perish, and no one will snatch them from My hand.
    John 10:29 What My Father has given Me is greater than all, and no one can snatch from the Father’s hand.
    John 10:30 I and the Father are one.”

John 17:12
When I was with them, I kept them in Thy name, which Thou hast given Me, and guarded them; and not one of them perished except the son of destruction, so that the Scripture might be fulfilled.
  John 6:70–6:71 — narrative parallel: The prayer's exception among the guarded disciples is compared with Jesus' earlier warning that one of the chosen Twelve is a devil, explicitly identified as Judas who would hand Him over. This illuminates the betrayal exception in its narrative context without asserting Judas was already physically dead when the prayer was spoken.
    John 6:70 Jesus answered them, “Did I not choose you, the Twelve? Yet one of you is a devil.”
    John 6:71 He was speaking of Judas, son of Simon Iscariot, for this man was about to hand Him over, one of the Twelve.

John 17:13
But now I am coming to Thee, and I speak these things in the world so that they may have My joy made full within themselves.
  John 15:11 — teaching parallel: Both statements explain Jesus' speaking as directed toward His joy being within the disciples and their joy being made full. The earlier farewell instruction and later prayer repeat this specific purpose, with the prayer additionally expressing His coming to the Father.
    John 15:11 These things I have spoken to you so that My joy may be in you and your joy may be made full.

John 17:14
I have given them Thy word, and the world hated them, because they are not from the world, just as I am not from the world.
  John 15:18–15:20 — teaching parallel: The prayer joins the given word with the world's hatred and the disciples' not being from the world; the farewell teaching explicitly says the world hates them because Jesus chose them out of it and had hated Him first. The comparison retains the disciples' relation to their Master rather than inventing a separate origin story.
    John 15:18 “If the world hates you, know that it has hated Me before you.
    John 15:19 If you were from the world, the world would love its own. But because you are not from the world, but I chose you out of the world, for this reason the world hates you.
    John 15:20 Remember the word that I said to you: ‘A slave is not greater than his master.’ If they persecuted Me, they will also persecute you; if they kept My word, they will also keep yours.

John 17:15
I do not ask that Thou take them out of the world, but that Thou keep them from the evil one.
  Matthew 6:13 — thematic parallel: Both prayers ask God for protection or deliverance from evil. The Lord's Prayer says deliver us from evil, while John says keep them from the evil one and expressly distinguishes preservation from removal out of the world. The supplied evil and evil-one forms are not silently made identical.
    Matthew 6:13 and bring us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil.’

John 17:16
They are not from the world, just as I am not from the world.
  John 15:19 — teaching parallel: Both passages expressly describe the disciples as not from the world. John 15 explains that Jesus chose them out of the world; the prayer relates their position to His own. This is a repeated belonging-and-allegiance statement, not an assertion that the disciples share every feature of Jesus' heavenly preexistence.
    John 15:19 If you were from the world, the world would love its own. But because you are not from the world, but I chose you out of the world, for this reason the world hates you.

John 17:17
Sanctify them in the truth; Thy word is truth.
  Psalms 118:160 — thematic parallel: The prayer calls the Father's word truth; the actual Greek-OT psalm says the beginning of His words is truth and His righteous judgments endure. This is a specific divine-word-and-truth comparison, with sanctifying the disciples as the prayer's additional petition and the exact Psalm numbering retained.
    Psalms 118:160 The beginning of Thy words is truth, and all Thy righteous judgments endure for ever.

John 17:18
Just as Thou didst send Me into the world, I also sent them into the world;
  John 20:21 — teaching parallel: The Father-sends-Jesus and Jesus-sends-disciples pattern is explicitly repeated after the resurrection. The prayer speaks of sending them into the world, while the later appearance directly commissions the hearers. The earlier prayer and later risen-Jesus commission retain their order and wording.
    John 20:21 Then Jesus said to them again, “Peace to you. Just as the Father has sent Me, I also send you.”

John 17:19
and for their sake I sanctify Myself, so that they also may be sanctified in truth.
  Hebrews 10:10 — thematic parallel: Jesus dedicates Himself for the disciples' sanctification; Hebrews explains sanctification through the offering of Jesus Christ's body according to God's will. The shared relation is His self-giving for others' sanctification. John frames it in truth, while Hebrews expressly describes the once-for-all bodily offering rather than using identical wording.
    Hebrews 10:10 By this will we have been sanctified through the offering of the body of Jesus Christ once for all.

John 17:20
“And I ask not only concerning these, but also concerning those who believe in Me through their word,
  Romans 10:14–10:17 — thematic parallel: Jesus extends His prayer to believers reached through the disciples' word; Romans connects believing with hearing and hearing with sent proclamation and Christ's word. Both describe faith mediated through communicated testimony. Romans also says not all obey, so the connection does not turn every hearer into a believer.
    Romans 10:14 How, then, are they to call upon One in whom they have not believed? And how are they to believe in One whom they have not heard? And how are they to hear without someone proclaiming?
    Romans 10:15 And how are they to proclaim unless they are sent? Just as it is written, “How beautiful are the feet of those announcing good things!”
    Romans 10:16 But not all obeyed the gospel. For Isaiah says, “Lord, who believed our report?”
    Romans 10:17 So faith is from hearing, and hearing through the word of Christ.

John 17:21
so that they may all be one, just as Thou, Father, art in Me and I in Thee, so that they also may be in Us, so that the world may believe that Thou didst send Me.
  Ephesians 4:3–4:6 — thematic parallel: The prayer seeks believers' oneness in relation to Father and Son, with witness to the world; Ephesians urges unity of the Spirit and names one body, Spirit, hope, Lord, faith, baptism and God and Father. Both ground communal unity in divine relation, with the prayer's mutual-indwelling and mission clauses remaining distinct.
    Ephesians 4:3 making every effort to keep the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace:
    Ephesians 4:4 one body and one Spirit, just as you were also called in one hope of your calling;
    Ephesians 4:5 one Lord, one faith, one baptism;
    Ephesians 4:6 one God and Father of all, who is over all and through all and in all.

John 17:22
