And the glory that Thou hast given Me I have given them, so that they may be one just as We are one:
  2 Corinthians 3:18 — thematic parallel: Jesus describes giving the disciples received glory for their unity; Paul describes believers beholding the Lord's glory and being transformed from glory to glory through the Spirit. Both concern believers' participation in glory. Giving for unity and transformation into an image are distinct expressions, not interchangeable accounts of one event.
    2 Corinthians 3:18 And we all, with unveiled face, beholding as in a mirror the glory of the Lord, are being transformed into the same image from glory to glory, as from the Lord, the Spirit.

John 17:23
I in them and Thou in Me, so that they may be made completely one, so that the world may know that Thou didst send Me and didst love them just as Thou didst love Me.
  John 14:20–14:21 — teaching parallel: The prayer's I in them and Thou in Me corresponds to the earlier I in My Father, you in Me and I in you, joined with the Father's love for Jesus' followers. The mutual-relation and love motifs recur, while the later prayer additionally gives unity and the world's recognition as purposes.
    John 14:20 On that day you will know that I am in My Father, and you in Me, and I in you.
    John 14:21 The one who has My commandments and keeps them is the one who loves Me. And the one who loves Me will be loved by My Father, and I will love him and reveal Myself to him.”

John 17:24
Father, as for what Thou hast given Me, I desire that they too may be with Me where I am, so that they may behold My glory, which Thou hast given Me, because Thou didst love Me before the foundation of the world.
  John 14:2–14:3 — teaching parallel: The prayer desires that those given Him be with Him where He is and behold His glory; the earlier promise says He prepares a place and will take the disciples to Himself so they may be where He is. The shared with-Me-where-I-am promise does not supply a new timetable or replace the prayer's before-the-world love clause.
    John 14:2 In My Father’s house are many dwelling places. If it were not so, would I have told you that I am going to prepare a place for you?
    John 14:3 And if I go and prepare a place for you, I am coming again and will take you to Myself, so that where I am, you also may be.

John 17:25
Righteous Father, although the world did not know Thee, I knew Thee, and these knew that Thou didst send Me;
  John 8:54–8:55 — teaching parallel: Both passages contrast others' failure to know the Father with Jesus' knowledge of Him. John 8 addresses particular opponents and also speaks of the Father's glorifying Jesus, while the prayer speaks of the world and the disciples' recognition of His sending. Those different audiences and scopes remain visible.
    John 8:54 Jesus answered, “If I glorify Myself, My glory is nothing. It is My Father who glorifies Me, of whom you say, ‘He is our God.’
    John 8:55 Yet you have not known Him, but I know Him. And if I say that I do not know Him, I will be a liar like you. But I know Him and keep His word.

John 17:26
and I made Thy name known to them, and will make it known, so that the love with which Thou didst love Me may be in them, and I in them.”
  John 15:9–15:10 — teaching parallel: The prayer seeks the Father's love for the Son within the disciples and Jesus in them; the earlier discourse says the Father's love for Jesus is extended through Jesus' love to them and calls them to remain in it. This is the recurring Father-Son-disciples love relation, not another narrated visit or a new set of speakers.
    John 15:9 Just as the Father loved Me, I also loved you. Remain in My love.
    John 15:10 If you keep My commandments, you will remain in My love, just as I have kept My Father’s commandments and remain in His love.

John 18:1
Having said these things, Jesus went out with His disciples beyond the winter-stream Kidron, where there was a garden, into which He and His disciples entered.
  Mark 14:32 — narrative parallel: Both accounts move Jesus and the disciples to the place of the ensuing arrest. John specifies a garden beyond the winter-stream Kidron; Mark names Gethsemane and introduces prayer there. The descriptions are compared without replacing John's geographical wording or inserting Mark's place-name into John's actual text.
    Mark 14:32 They came to a place whose name was Gethsemane. He said to His disciples, “Sit here while I pray.”

John 18:2
Now Judas, who was handing Him over, also knew the place, because Jesus had often gathered there with His disciples.
  Luke 22:39 — narrative parallel: Judas knows the place because Jesus often gathered there with the disciples; Luke describes Jesus' customary journey to the Mount of Olives with disciples following. The recurring practice illuminates the familiarity of the setting, without making Luke independently state Judas's knowledge or the garden's precise location.
    Luke 22:39 And He went out and traveled, as was His custom, to the Mount of Olives; and the disciples also followed Him.

John 18:3
So Judas, taking the cohort and officers from the chief priests and from the Pharisees, comes there with lanterns and torches and weapons.
  Mark 14:43 — narrative parallel: Judas arrives with an armed group in both accounts. John specifies a cohort and officers associated with chief priests and Pharisees, plus lights and weapons; Mark names a sword-and-club crowd from chief priests, scribes and elders. The different group descriptions are retained without estimating troop numbers or adding a narrated kiss to John.
    Mark 14:43 Immediately, while He was still speaking, Judas, one of the Twelve, arrived, and with him a crowd with swords and clubs from the chief priests, the scribes, and the elders.

John 18:4
Then Jesus, knowing everything that was coming upon Him, went out and says to them, “Whom do you seek?”
  John 10:17–10:18 — thematic parallel: Jesus knowingly steps forward to meet the arrest party; His earlier saying explains that He lays down His life of His own accord under the Father's command. The action is compared with the teaching about voluntary self-giving, without making the encounter a denial that others arrest Him or adding a mechanism for His foreknowledge.
    John 10:17 For this reason the Father loves Me, because I lay down My life, so that I may take it again.
    John 10:18 No one takes it from Me, but I lay it down of My own accord. I have authority to lay it down, and I have authority to take it again. This command I received from My Father.”

John 18:5
They answered Him, “Jesus the Nazarene.” He says to them, “I am.” Now Judas, who was handing Him over, was also standing with them.
  John 18:8 — narrative parallel: Jesus' I am answer to the requested identity is explicitly recalled by I told you that I am when He asks that the disciples be allowed to leave. This is a repeated self-identification within the arrest encounter, not a claim that the ordinary identity question alone proves a separately reconstructed divine-name quotation.
    John 18:8 Jesus answered, “I told you that I am. So if you seek Me, let these go away,”

John 18:6
So when He said to them, “I am,” they went backward and fell to the ground.
  Psalms 26:2 — thematic parallel: The arrest party goes backward and falls when Jesus speaks; the psalm describes enemies approaching to consume the righteous speaker, growing weak and falling. The concrete approach-and-fall pattern is compared without asserting an explicit Psalm quotation, identical enemies, or a physical mechanism beyond what John narrates.
    Psalms 26:2 When those doing evil approached me to eat my flesh, those who afflict me and my enemies, they grew weak and fell.

John 18:7
Then He asked them again, “Whom do you seek?” And they said, “Jesus the Nazarene.”
  John 18:4–18:5 — narrative parallel: John explicitly repeats Jesus' question Whom do you seek and the party's Jesus-the-Nazarene answer after the first exchange and the fall. The matched question-and-answer sequence is an internal narrative repetition. It does not exchange the speakers or collapse the repeated stages into a single utterance.
    John 18:4 Then Jesus, knowing everything that was coming upon Him, went out and says to them, “Whom do you seek?”
    John 18:5 They answered Him, “Jesus the Nazarene.” He says to them, “I am.” Now Judas, who was handing Him over, was also standing with them.

John 18:8
Jesus answered, “I told you that I am. So if you seek Me, let these go away,”
  John 10:11–10:12 — thematic parallel: Jesus offers Himself as the sought person while asking that His followers go; His shepherd teaching contrasts laying down His life for the sheep with abandoning them to danger. The arrest action illustrates concern for followers within His self-giving. It does not turn the armed party into the literal wolf of the figure.
    John 10:11 I am the good Shepherd; the good Shepherd lays down His life for the sheep.
    John 10:12 The hired worker, who is not a shepherd and does not own the sheep, sees the wolf coming and leaves the sheep and flees—and the wolf seizes them and scatters them—

John 18:9
so that the word that He spoke might be fulfilled: “Of those whom Thou hast given Me, I lost not one.”
  John 6:39 — teaching parallel: The narrator relates the disciples' preservation to Jesus' lost-not-one word about those given by the Father. John 6 similarly promises losing nothing given Him and raising it on the last day. The immediate protection at arrest and the broader life-and-resurrection promise remain distinct scopes rather than a new verbatim harmonized quotation.
    John 6:39 And this is the will of the One who sent Me: that of everything He has given Me I should lose nothing, but raise it up on the last day.

John 18:10
Then Simon Peter, having a sword, drew it and struck the high priest’s slave and cut off his right ear. The slave’s name was Malchus.
  Luke 22:50–22:51 — narrative parallel: Both accounts identify the high priest's slave's right ear as cut off. John names Peter as the swordsman and Malchus as the slave; Luke leaves them unnamed and expressly narrates Jesus' healing the ear. The shared injury does not add Luke's healing sentence to John or remove John's names.
    Luke 22:50 And one of them struck the high priest’s slave and took off his right ear.
    Luke 22:51 But Jesus answered, “Allow this much.” And He touched the ear and healed him.

John 18:11
So Jesus said to Peter, “Put the sword into the sheath. The cup that the Father has given Me—shall I not drink it?”
  Matthew 26:52–26:54 — narrative parallel: Jesus commands the sword returned in both arrest accounts. John addresses Peter and speaks of the Father's cup; Matthew addresses the unnamed swordsman and adds the sword warning, available angelic aid and Scripture's fulfillment. The close rebuke parallel preserves those different explanations.
    Matthew 26:52 Then Jesus said to him, “Return your sword to its place, for all who take a sword will perish by a sword.
    Matthew 26:53 Or do you think I cannot plead with My Father, and He will provide Me at once more than twelve legions of angels?
    Matthew 26:54 How, then, would the Scriptures be fulfilled, that it must happen this way?”
  Mark 14:36 — thematic parallel: John's Jesus accepts the cup the Father gives Him; Mark's earlier prayer asks the Father that the cup be removed while submitting to His will. These are complementary cup-and-Father sayings at different stages, not the same speech or a claim that John records the earlier prayer.
    Mark 14:36 He said, “Abba, Father, all things are possible for Thee. Take this cup away from Me. Yet not what I will, but what Thou wilt.”

John 18:12
Then the cohort and the commander and the officers of the Jews arrested Jesus and bound Him,
  Mark 14:46 — narrative parallel: Both accounts narrate Jesus being seized. John additionally identifies the cohort, commander and officers and explicitly says He is bound; Mark's immediate sentence simply says they lay hands on and seize Him. The link does not import John's military titles or immediate binding into Mark's sentence.
    Mark 14:46 They laid their hands on Him and seized Him.

John 18:13
and led Him first to Annas, for he was father-in-law to Caiaphas, who was high priest that year.
  Luke 3:2 — thematic parallel: John names Annas and Caiaphas in explaining the first destination and their family relation; Luke names both in its high-priesthood dating notice. The comparison supplies a specific shared priestly setting, not independent confirmation of the father-in-law relation, the first Annas hearing or an identical legal-office arrangement at every date.
    Luke 3:2 during the high priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas, God’s word came to John, Zechariah’s son, in the wilderness.

John 18:14
Now Caiaphas was the one who advised the Jews that it was better for them for one person to die for the people.
  John 11:49–11:52 — narrative parallel: John explicitly recalls Caiaphas's earlier advice that one person should die for the people. The earlier scene gives the statement and the narrator's further interpretation about gathering God's scattered children. This is a direct retrospective speech reference rather than an independent repetition of the original council meeting.
    John 11:49 But a certain one of them, Caiaphas, who was high priest that year, said to them, “You know nothing,
    John 11:50 nor do you consider that it is better for you for one man to die for the people than for the whole nation to perish.”
    John 11:51 He did not say this from himself, but being high priest that year, he prophesied that Jesus was about to die for the nation,
    John 11:52 and not for the nation alone, but also to gather into one the children of God who had been scattered.

John 18:15
And Simon Peter and another disciple were following Jesus. That disciple was known to the high priest, and entered with Jesus into the high priest’s courtyard;
  Matthew 26:58 — narrative parallel: Peter follows Jesus into the high-priestly courtyard setting in both accounts. John distinguishes another disciple who enters first because he is known there, with Peter initially outside; Matthew says Peter follows at a distance and enters to sit with the officers. John's unnamed other disciple is not assigned a personal name by this parallel.
    Matthew 26:58 Peter followed Him at a distance as far as the high priest’s courtyard. Going inside, he sat with the officers to see the outcome.

John 18:16
but Peter was standing outside at the door. So the other disciple, who was known to the high priest, went out and spoke to the woman keeping the door, and brought Peter in.
  Acts 12:13–12:16 — narrative parallel: Both scenes place Peter at a doorway with a female servant involved in admission. John has another disciple speak to the doorkeeper and bring him in; Acts has Rhoda recognize his voice but leave the gate shut in joy before it is opened. The women, houses and circumstances differ, and the unnamed disciple remains unnamed.
    Acts 12:13 When he knocked at the outer gate’s door, a slave girl named Rhoda came to answer.
    Acts 12:14 Recognizing Peter’s voice, in her joy she did not open the gate, but ran inside and announced that Peter was standing before it.
    Acts 12:15 They said to her, “You are out of your mind.” But she insisted that it was so. They said, “It is his angel.”
    Acts 12:16 Peter kept knocking. When they opened the door, they saw him and were astonished.

John 18:17
Then the slave girl keeping the door says to Peter, “You are not also one of this Man’s disciples, are you?” He says, “I am not.”
  Matthew 26:69–26:70 — narrative parallel: A servant girl associates Peter with Jesus and Peter denies it in both accounts. John identifies her as the doorkeeper and records I am not; Matthew places the challenge in the courtyard and gives I do not know what you are saying. Location, question form and denial wording remain distinct.
    Matthew 26:69 Peter was sitting outside in the courtyard. One servant girl approached him, saying, “You too were with Jesus the Galilean.”
    Matthew 26:70 But he denied it before everyone, saying, “I do not know what you are saying.”

John 18:18
Now the slaves and the officers were standing there, having made a charcoal fire because it was cold, and they were warming themselves. Peter too was with them, standing and warming himself.
  Mark 14:54 — narrative parallel: Both passages place Peter with the attendants or officers warming at a fire in the high priest's courtyard. John specifies charcoal, cold weather and standing; Mark says Peter sits and warms at the firelight. The comparison preserves the recorded posture and descriptions without inventing a shared moment or change of position.
    Mark 14:54 Peter followed Him from a distance, as far as inside the high priest’s courtyard. He sat with the attendants and warmed himself at the firelight.

John 18:19
Then the high priest questioned Jesus about His disciples and about His teaching.
  Matthew 26:62–26:64 — narrative parallel: Both passages give high-priestly questioning of Jesus, but John asks about His disciples and teaching, whereas Matthew asks about testimony and His messianic sonship. Their different questions and responses are compared as religious-hearing scenes, without making a single transcript or moving John's Annas-to-Caiaphas sequence.
    Matthew 26:62 The high priest rose and said to Him, “Dost Thou answer nothing? What is this that they testify against Thee?”
    Matthew 26:63 But Jesus remained silent. The high priest said to Him, “I adjure Thee by the living God to tell us whether Thou art the Christ, the Son of God.”
    Matthew 26:64 Jesus said to him, “You have said it. Nevertheless I tell you, from now on you will see the Son of Man seated at the right of the Power and coming upon the clouds of heaven.”

John 18:20
Jesus answered him, “I have spoken openly to the world. I always taught in a synagogue and in the temple, where all the Jews gather, and I spoke nothing in secret.
  Mark 14:49 — narrative parallel: Jesus appeals to His publicly accessible temple teaching in both scenes. Mark places the statement at arrest, emphasizing daily teaching without seizure; John places it in the hearing, also naming synagogues and denying secret teaching. The close public-teaching defense keeps its different occasions and additional clauses.
    Mark 14:49 Day after day I was with you in the temple, teaching, and you did not seize Me. But this is so that the Scriptures may be fulfilled.”

John 18:21
Why do you question Me? Question those who have heard what I spoke to them. See, these know what I said.”
  John 7:51 — thematic parallel: Jesus asks that those who heard His teaching be questioned; Nicodemus earlier challenges judgment without hearing a person and knowing what he does. Both demand informed hearing rather than unsupported judgment, but questioning hearers and hearing the accused are distinct applications. No additional historical trial-law ruling is inferred.
    John 7:51 “Does our Law judge a person unless it first hears from him and knows what he does?”

John 18:22
When He said these things, one of the officers standing nearby gave Jesus a slap, saying, “Is this how Thou answerest the high priest?”
  Mark 14:65 — narrative parallel: John narrates an officer slapping Jesus after His answer to the high priest; Mark records attendants receiving Him with slaps after other abuse in the hearing. The shared physical mistreatment does not merge the stated triggers or add Mark's blindfolding, spitting or fists to John's specific slap.
    Mark 14:65 Some began spitting on Him, covering His face, striking Him with their fists, and saying to Him, “Prophesy!” The attendants received Him with slaps.

John 18:23
Jesus answered him, “If I spoke wrongly, bear witness about the wrong; but if rightly, why do you beat Me?”
  Acts 23:2–23:5 — thematic parallel: Jesus challenges being struck if His speech is right; Paul protests an ordered mouth-blow while being judged according to law. Both hearing scenes join a blow with a challenge about its justification. Jesus' conditional appeal and Paul's sharp reply, subsequent explanation and named high priest are different and are not treated as one response.
    Acts 23:2 The high priest Ananias ordered those standing beside him to strike his mouth.
    Acts 23:3 Then Paul said to him, “God is going to strike you, whitewashed wall! You sit judging me according to the law, yet in violation of the law you order me struck?”
    Acts 23:4 Those standing nearby said, “Are you insulting God’s high priest?”
    Acts 23:5 Paul said, “Brothers, I did not know that he was high priest, for it is written, ‘You shall not speak evil of a ruler of your people.’”

John 18:24
So Annas sent Him bound to Caiaphas the high priest.
  Matthew 26:57 — narrative parallel: John says Annas sends Jesus bound to Caiaphas; Matthew says those who seize Jesus lead Him to Caiaphas where scribes and elders gather. The shared Caiaphas destination is retained, while Matthew does not narrate John's prior Annas stage and the texts are not reordered to erase that difference.
    Matthew 26:57 Those who seized Jesus led Him away to Caiaphas the high priest, where the scribes and elders had gathered.

John 18:25
Now Simon Peter was standing and warming himself. So they said to him, “You are not also one of His disciples, are you?” He denied it and said, “I am not.”
  Matthew 26:71–26:72 — narrative parallel: Both accounts narrate a further accusation and denial by Peter. John keeps him warming and records a plural challenge with I am not; Matthew places a second girl's statement at the gateway and adds an oath denying knowledge of the Man. The accusers, position and denial wording remain as given.
    Matthew 26:71 When he went out to the gateway, another girl saw him and said to those there, “This man was with Jesus the Nazarene.”
    Matthew 26:72 Again he denied it with an oath: “I do not know the Man.”

John 18:26
One of the high priest’s slaves, a relative of the man whose ear Peter had cut off, says, “Did I not see you in the garden with Him?”
  John 18:10 — narrative parallel: The accuser is expressly described as a relative of the slave whose ear Peter cut off, pointing back to the named Malchus episode. This direct internal reference clarifies why the garden encounter matters. It does not identify the relative as Malchus himself or supply his personal name.
    John 18:10 Then Simon Peter, having a sword, drew it and struck the high priest’s slave and cut off his right ear. The slave’s name was Malchus.

John 18:27
Then Peter denied it again, and immediately a rooster crowed.
  John 13:38 — narrative parallel: Peter's further denial is followed immediately by the rooster, fulfilling the earlier prediction of three denials before it crows. This is an explicit forecast-and-event relationship within John. It does not import Mark's two-crow wording or another Gospel's look and tears into John's account.
    John 13:38 Jesus answered, “Will you lay down your life for Me? Amen, amen, I tell you: a rooster will certainly not crow until you deny Me three times.”

John 18:28
Then they lead Jesus from Caiaphas into the praetorium. It was early morning, and they themselves did not enter the praetorium, so that they might not be defiled, but might eat the Passover.
  Mark 15:1 — narrative parallel: Both accounts narrate the early-morning movement from the religious hearing to Pilate. John names the praetorium and the accusers' purification concern; Mark names the consultation and binding before delivery. These compare the transfer without resolving Passover-meal chronology or moving John's earlier binding to Mark's narrated point.
    Mark 15:1 Immediately in the morning, the chief priests held a consultation with the elders, the scribes, and the whole council. They bound Jesus, led Him away, and handed Him over to Pilate.

John 18:29
So Pilate went outside to them and says, “What accusation do you bring against this Man?”
  Luke 23:1–23:2 — narrative parallel: John's Pilate asks the charge after the accusers bring Jesus; Luke narrates their bringing Him and states their accusations. Luke supplies a related recorded charge scene rather than a verbatim answer to John's particular question, and the allegations are not thereby endorsed as facts.
    Luke 23:1 Then their whole assembly rose and led Him before Pilate.
    Luke 23:2 And they began to accuse Him, saying, “We found this man turning our nation astray and forbidding tribute to be given to Caesar, and saying that He Himself is Christ, a king.”

John 18:30
They answered and said to him, “If this Man were not doing evil, we would not have handed Him over to you.”
  Luke 23:2 — narrative parallel: John's accusers justify delivery by alleging wrongdoing; Luke specifies allegations about the nation, tribute and a royal claim. The shared prosecutorial setting is compared while preserving John's nonspecific answer. In particular, the tribute allegation remains the accusers' statement, not a narrator's finding that Jesus forbade payment.
    Luke 23:2 And they began to accuse Him, saying, “We found this man turning our nation astray and forbidding tribute to be given to Caesar, and saying that He Himself is Christ, a king.”

John 18:31
Then Pilate said to them, “Take Him yourselves, and judge Him according to your law.” The Jews said to him, “It is not lawful for us to put anyone to death,”
  John 19:6–19:7 — narrative parallel: The exchange about judging Jesus under the accusers' law and their stated lack of death authority recurs in a changed form when Pilate tells them to take and crucify Him and they invoke their law. This is an internal legal-claim contrast, not a newly reconstructed uniform rule about historical execution powers.
    John 19:6 So when the chief priests and the officers saw Him, they cried out, saying, “Crucify! Crucify!” Pilate says to them, “Take Him yourselves and crucify Him, for I find no charge against Him.”
    John 19:7 The Jews answered him, “We have a law, and according to the law He ought to die, because He made Himself Son of God.”

John 18:32
so that the word of Jesus might be fulfilled, which He spoke to indicate by what kind of death He was going to die.
  John 12:32–12:33 — narrative parallel: John earlier explicitly explains Jesus' lifted-up saying as indicating the kind of death He would die; the trial narrative now explicitly invokes fulfillment of His saying about that kind of death. The matching narrative explanation links the prediction and passion setting without adding a new chronology or execution method.
    John 12:32 And I, if I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all people to Myself.”
    John 12:33 He said this to signify by what kind of death He was about to die.

John 18:33
Then Pilate entered the praetorium again, called Jesus, and said to Him, “Art Thou the King of the Jews?”
  Mark 15:2 — narrative parallel: Pilate asks Jesus whether He is King of the Jews in both passages. Mark immediately gives You say so, while John develops a longer exchange before its royal response. The matching interrogation does not flatten John's intervening questions into Mark's shorter form.
    Mark 15:2 Pilate questioned Him, “Art Thou the King of the Jews?” He answered him, “You say so.”

John 18:35
Pilate answered, “I am not a Jew, am I? Thy own nation and the chief priests handed Thee over to me. What hast Thou done?”
  Mark 15:1–15:3 — narrative parallel: Pilate refers to Jesus' own nation and chief priests delivering Him and asks His deed; Mark narrates delivery by the assembled leaders and ensuing accusations by chief priests. The common delivery-and-charge setting does not assign every member of the nation an identical role or add Mark's wording to Pilate's question.
    Mark 15:1 Immediately in the morning, the chief priests held a consultation with the elders, the scribes, and the whole council. They bound Jesus, led Him away, and handed Him over to Pilate.
    Mark 15:2 Pilate questioned Him, “Art Thou the King of the Jews?” He answered him, “You say so.”
    Mark 15:3 The chief priests accused Him of many things.

John 18:36
Jesus answered, “My kingdom is not from this world. If My kingdom were from this world, My attendants would be fighting so that I would not be handed over to the Jews. But as it is, My kingdom is not from here.”
  John 6:15 — thematic parallel: Jesus says His kingdom does not originate from this world or depend on fighting attendants; earlier He withdraws when people intend to seize Him and make Him king. Both passages distinguish His kingship from a humanly imposed or force-secured rule. They do not deny His kingship or assert that His mission has no earthly consequences.
    John 6:15 So Jesus, knowing that they were about to come and seize Him to make Him king, withdrew again to the mountain, Himself alone.

John 18:37
Then Pilate said to Him, “So then, Thou art a king?” Jesus answered, “You say that I am a king. For this I have been born, and for this I have come into the world: to bear witness to the truth. Everyone who is from the truth hears My voice.”
  1 Timothy 6:13 — thematic parallel: Jesus explains before Pilate that He came to bear witness to the truth; the epistle expressly recalls Christ Jesus testifying the good confession before Pontius Pilate. This is a concrete canonical reference to the same witness setting, without claiming the epistle reproduces every royal or truth clause of John's dialogue.
    1 Timothy 6:13 I command you before God, who preserves all things alive, and Christ Jesus, who testified the good confession before Pontius Pilate,

John 18:38
Pilate says to Him, “What is truth?” And having said this, he went out again to the Jews, and says to them, “I find no charge against Him.
  Luke 23:4 — narrative parallel: Both passages record Pilate declaring that he finds no charge or ground for a charge against Jesus. John places the verdict after the distinctive What is truth question; Luke addresses the chief priests and crowds without that question. The matching finding does not insert John's preceding dialogue into Luke.
    Luke 23:4 And Pilate said to the chief priests and the crowds, “I find no ground for a charge against this man.”

John 18:39
But you have a custom that I release one person to you at the Passover. So do you wish me to release to you the King of the Jews?”
  Mark 15:6–15:9 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe a festival prisoner-release custom and Pilate's proposal to release the King of the Jews. Mark adds the crowd's request and a description of Barabbas's company. This is a comparison of the supplied narrative notices, not external confirmation of the custom's historical scope.
    Mark 15:6 At the feast he used to release for them one prisoner whom they requested.
    Mark 15:7 The man called Barabbas was imprisoned with the rebels who had committed murder in the uprising.
    Mark 15:8 The crowd came up and began asking him to do as he usually did for them.
    Mark 15:9 Pilate answered them, “Do you want me to release for you the King of the Jews?”

John 18:40
Then they cried out again, saying, “Not this Man, but Barabbas!” Now Barabbas was a bandit.
  Luke 23:18–23:19 — narrative parallel: Both accounts record the demand for Barabbas rather than Jesus. John calls him a bandit; Luke describes his imprisonment for an uprising and murder. The different narrator descriptions remain explicit, while the crowd's demand is not confused with Pilate's proposed release of Jesus.
    Luke 23:18 But they cried out all together, saying, “Take this man away, and release Barabbas to us!”
    Luke 23:19 He had been thrown into prison for a certain uprising that took place in the city and for murder.

John 19:1
Then Pilate took Jesus and had Him scourged.
  Mark 15:15 — narrative parallel: Both accounts attribute Jesus' scourging to Pilate's action. Mark includes it with the prisoner release and delivery for crucifixion; John narrates it before the further public exchanges and final delivery. The comparison preserves those narrative placements rather than positing an unrecorded second scourging to reconcile them.
    Mark 15:15 Pilate, wanting to satisfy the crowd, released Barabbas for them. After having Jesus scourged, he handed Him over to be crucified.

John 19:2
And the soldiers, weaving a crown from thorns, put it on His head and clothed Him in a purple garment;
  Mark 15:17 — narrative parallel: Both passages describe the soldiers' purple clothing of Jesus and a woven crown of thorns. John specifies placement on His head and Mark gives the order in its own sentence. These close mocking-costume details are retained without adding other accounts' garments or actions.
    Mark 15:17 They clothed Him in purple and, after weaving a crown of thorns, put it on Him.

John 19:3
and they kept coming to Him and saying, “Hail, King of the Jews!” And they gave Him slaps.
  Mark 15:18–15:19 — narrative parallel: The soldiers salute Jesus as King of the Jews and mistreat Him in both accounts. John records slaps and their repeated approach; Mark records a reed striking His head, spitting and kneeling. The matching mock-royal address does not make the different physical acts identical.
    Mark 15:18 They began saluting Him, “Hail, King of the Jews!”
    Mark 15:19 They kept striking His head with a reed and spitting on Him, and knelt down to bow before Him.

John 19:4
And Pilate went outside again and says to them, “See, I am bringing Him outside to you so that you may know that I find no charge against Him.”
  Luke 23:14 — narrative parallel: Pilate again declares no charge against Jesus in John and says his examination found no ground for the accusers' charges in Luke. John adds the plan to bring Jesus outside. The repeated innocence finding is compared without importing every detail of one public speech into the other.
    Luke 23:14 and said to them, “You brought this man to me as one turning the people astray. And behold, I examined Him before you and found no ground in this man for the accusations you bring against Him.

John 19:5
Then Jesus came outside, wearing the thorny crown and the purple garment. And he says to them, “Behold the Man!”
  Matthew 27:28–27:31 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe Jesus in a thorn crown and a mocking garment. John presents Him outside in purple with Behold the Man; Matthew describes a scarlet cloak, the soldiers' mockery, then its removal before departure. Color descriptions, speakers and the public-presentation versus removal stages are preserved without a textile or timeline harmonization.
    Matthew 27:28 They stripped Him and put a scarlet cloak around Him.
    Matthew 27:29 They wove a crown from thorns, placed it upon His head, and put a reed in His right hand. Kneeling before Him, they mocked Him, saying, “Greetings, King of the Jews!”
    Matthew 27:30 They spat on Him, took the reed, and kept striking Him upon the head.
    Matthew 27:31 After mocking Him, they stripped off the cloak, dressed Him in His own garments, and led Him away to crucify Him.

John 19:6
So when the chief priests and the officers saw Him, they cried out, saying, “Crucify! Crucify!” Pilate says to them, “Take Him yourselves and crucify Him, for I find no charge against Him.”
  Luke 23:21–23:22 — narrative parallel: Both passages join repeated cries for crucifixion with Pilate's claim that he finds no ground for a capital charge. John identifies chief priests and officers at this point and adds Take Him yourselves; Luke gives the third protest and a proposed punishment-and-release. The speaker groups and proposals remain as narrated.
    Luke 23:21 But they kept shouting, saying, “Crucify, crucify Him!”
    Luke 23:22 And for the third time he said to them, “Why, what evil has this man done? I found no ground for death in Him. Therefore I will punish Him and release Him.”

John 19:7
The Jews answered him, “We have a law, and according to the law He ought to die, because He made Himself Son of God.”
  Matthew 26:63–26:66 — narrative parallel: John's accusers invoke their law and a death penalty because of Jesus' claimed sonship; Matthew's religious hearing proceeds from the Christ-and-Son-of-God question to a blasphemy charge and liability to death. The connection concerns the stated accusations and judgments, not an endorsement that Jesus' sonship is false or a new legal ruling.
    Matthew 26:63 But Jesus remained silent. The high priest said to Him, “I adjure Thee by the living God to tell us whether Thou art the Christ, the Son of God.”
    Matthew 26:64 Jesus said to him, “You have said it. Nevertheless I tell you, from now on you will see the Son of Man seated at the right of the Power and coming upon the clouds of heaven.”
    Matthew 26:65 Then the high priest tore his garments, saying, “He has blasphemed! What further need do we have of witnesses? Look, now you heard the blasphemy.
    Matthew 26:66 What do you think?” They answered, “He is liable to death.”

John 19:9
and entered the praetorium again and says to Jesus, “Where art Thou from?” But Jesus gave him no answer.
  Mark 15:4–15:5 — narrative parallel: Both accounts narrate Jesus giving Pilate no answer at a particular stage. John's question is Where art Thou from; Mark's concerns the many accusations and adds Pilate's amazement. The bounded silences are compared without claiming Jesus never speaks to Pilate or combining their different questions.
    Mark 15:4 Pilate questioned Him again, saying, “Dost Thou answer nothing? Look how many accusations they bring against Thee.”
    Mark 15:5 But Jesus gave no further answer, so that Pilate marveled.

John 19:10
Then Pilate says to Him, “Dost Thou not speak to me? Dost Thou not know that I have authority to release Thee, and I have authority to crucify Thee?”
  John 10:17–10:18 — thematic parallel: Pilate claims authority to release or crucify Jesus; Jesus earlier claims Father-commanded authority over laying down and taking up His own life. The same life-and-authority subject exposes different kinds of claims: a governor's judicial choice and Jesus' self-giving and resurrection. Neither claim is silently substituted for the other.
    John 10:17 For this reason the Father loves Me, because I lay down My life, so that I may take it again.
    John 10:18 No one takes it from Me, but I lay it down of My own accord. I have authority to lay it down, and I have authority to take it again. This command I received from My Father.”

John 19:11
Jesus answered him, “You would have no authority against Me unless it had been given you from above. For this reason the one who handed Me over to you has greater sin.”
  Romans 13:1 — thematic parallel: Jesus denies that Pilate has authority independent of a grant from above; Romans says existing authorities derive from God. The specific comparison is the divine derivation of authority, not approval of every exercise of it. John's greater-sin comparison remains, and this unit does not name the other hander-over.
    Romans 13:1 Let every soul submit to the higher authorities, for there is no authority except from God, and those that exist have been appointed by God.

John 19:12
From this Pilate sought to release Him; but the Jews cried out, saying, “If you release this Man, you are not Caesar’s friend. Everyone who makes himself a king speaks against Caesar.”
  Luke 23:2 — narrative parallel: Both accusation scenes frame Jesus' royal identity as a matter involving Caesar. John gives the pressure that releasing such a king opposes Caesar; Luke records charges about tribute and being Christ, a king. These are the accusers' political claims, not narrator findings that Jesus forbade tribute or unlawfully seized rule.
    Luke 23:2 And they began to accuse Him, saying, “We found this man turning our nation astray and forbidding tribute to be given to Caesar, and saying that He Himself is Christ, a king.”

John 19:13
So Pilate, hearing these words, brought Jesus outside and sat on the judgment seat at a place called Stone Pavement, and in Hebrew, Gabbatha.
  Matthew 27:19 — narrative parallel: Both narratives place Pilate on the judgment seat during Jesus' case. John names the Stone Pavement and Gabbatha; Matthew gives his wife's message at that point in its account. The shared judicial setting does not add the wife's dream to John or independently locate the named pavement.
    Matthew 27:19 While he sat on the judgment seat, his wife sent to him, saying, “Have nothing to do with that righteous Man, for I suffered many things today in a dream because of Him.”

John 19:14
Now it was the Preparation of the Passover; it was about the sixth hour. And he says to the Jews, “Behold your King!”
  Mark 15:25 — narrative parallel: Both supply explicit passion-hour notices, but they differ: John gives about the sixth hour for Pilate's presentation on the Preparation of Passover, while Mark gives the third hour for crucifixion. This compares the notices with their distinct events and expressions; it does not invent a clock conversion or identify them as the same moment.
    Mark 15:25 It was the third hour when they crucified Him.

John 19:15
Then they cried out, “Take Him away! Take Him away! Crucify Him!” Pilate says to them, “Shall I crucify your King?” The chief priests answered, “We have no king except Caesar.”
  Mark 15:13–15:14 — narrative parallel: The cries for crucifixion and Pilate's continued questioning recur in both accounts. John additionally records his royal question and the chief priests' no-king-except-Caesar reply; Mark asks what evil Jesus has done. The shared demand does not erase the different dialogue or transfer John's explicit chief-priest answer to an undifferentiated whole people.
    Mark 15:13 They cried out again, “Crucify Him!”
    Mark 15:14 Pilate said to them, “Why, what evil has He done?” They cried out all the more, “Crucify Him!”

John 19:16
Then he handed Him over to them to be crucified. So they took Jesus;
  Luke 23:24–23:25 — narrative parallel: Both passages conclude the proceeding with Jesus handed over to the demanded outcome. Luke explicitly pairs this with release of the uprising-and-murder prisoner; John moves directly into Jesus being taken for crucifixion. The matching delivery is retained without assigning every subsequent action to the same unnamed group.
    Luke 23:24 And Pilate decided that their demand should be granted.
    Luke 23:25 He released the man who had been thrown into prison for uprising and murder, whom they were demanding; but he handed Jesus over to their will.

John 19:17
and, bearing the cross for Himself, He went out to what is called the Place of a Skull, which in Hebrew is called Golgotha,
  Mark 15:21–15:22 — narrative parallel: Both accounts reach Golgotha, the Place of a Skull, but identify cross-carrying differently: John says Jesus bears it for Himself, while Mark compels Simon of Cyrene. The shared destination and carrying motif are compared without inventing the point of a handoff or replacing either named carrier.
    Mark 15:21 They compelled a passerby, Simon of Cyrene, coming from the country, the father of Alexander and Rufus, to carry His cross.
    Mark 15:22 They brought Him to the place Golgotha, which translated means Place of a Skull.

John 19:18
where they crucified Him, and with Him two others, one on either side, and Jesus in the middle.
  Luke 23:32–23:33 — narrative parallel: Both accounts place two others alongside Jesus in the crucifixion, one on each side. Luke explicitly calls them criminals and names the Skull location; John calls them two others and places Jesus in the middle. Those narrator descriptions remain distinct rather than supplying every Gospel with the same label.
    Luke 23:32 Two others also, criminals, were being led with Him to be put to death.
    Luke 23:33 When they came to the place called Skull, there they crucified Him and the criminals, one on the right and the other on the left.

John 19:19
And Pilate also wrote an inscription and put it on the cross. It was written: “Jesus the Nazarene, the King of the Jews.”
  Matthew 27:37 — narrative parallel: Both record a royal charge over Jesus' cross, but John gives Jesus the Nazarene, the King of the Jews and attributes the writing to Pilate; Matthew gives This is Jesus, the King of the Jews. The close inscription parallel preserves the actual title forms and each account's additional placement or authorship detail.
    Matthew 27:37 Above His head they placed the written charge against Him: “This is Jesus, the King of the Jews.”

John 19:20
Then many of the Jews read this inscription, because the place where Jesus was crucified was near the city; and it was written in Hebrew, in Latin, in Greek.
  Luke 23:38 — narrative parallel: Both passages concern the inscription above the crucified Jesus. John additionally gives its many readers, nearness to the city and Hebrew, Latin and Greek wording; this supplied Luke unit simply gives the royal inscription. It does not independently attest the three languages or allow their restoration from another edition.
    Luke 23:38 There was also an inscription above Him: “This is the King of the Jews.”

John 19:21
So the chief priests of the Jews were saying to Pilate, “Do not write, ‘The King of the Jews,’ but that He said, ‘I am King of the Jews.’”
  John 18:37 — thematic parallel: The chief priests want the placard recast as Jesus' self-claim to kingship; the recorded exchange with Pilate gives Jesus' You say that I am a king and His truth-witness explanation. Comparing the actual speech with the proposed wording distinguishes the accusers' formulation from a verbatim transcript of Jesus' answer.
    John 18:37 Then Pilate said to Him, “So then, Thou art a king?” Jesus answered, “You say that I am a king. For this I have been born, and for this I have come into the world: to bear witness to the truth. Everyone who is from the truth hears My voice.”

John 19:23
Then the soldiers, when they crucified Jesus, took His garments and made four parts, a part for each soldier, and also took the tunic. Now the tunic was seamless, woven from the top throughout.
  Mark 15:24 — narrative parallel: Both passages narrate the soldiers' division of Jesus' clothing. John distinguishes four parts and a separate seamless tunic, with the lot decision following; Mark summarizes division by lots. The comparison preserves garments and tunic as John distinguishes them, rather than treating every item as one undivided robe.
    Mark 15:24 They crucified Him and divided His garments, casting a lot over them to determine who would take what.

John 19:24
So they said to one another, “Let us not tear it, but cast lots for it, to decide whose it will be,” so that the Scripture might be fulfilled which says, “They divided My garments among themselves, and for My clothing they cast a lot.” So the soldiers did these things.
  Psalms 21:19 — quotation: John explicitly quotes Scripture about divided garments and a lot for clothing; the actual Greek-OT Psalm unit gives both actions in closely matching words. The fulfillment citation is anchored to its supplied Psalm ID, while John's specific tunic decision remains the narrative application rather than an added detail in the psalm.
    Psalms 21:19 They divided my garments among themselves, and cast a lot for my clothing.

John 19:25
But standing beside Jesus’ cross were His mother and His mother’s sister, Mary of Clopas, and Mary Magdalene.
  Mark 15:40–15:41 — narrative parallel: Mary Magdalene appears among the women in both crucifixion accounts. John places the named women beside the cross and includes Jesus' mother and her sister; Mark describes women watching from a distance with a different list. Their positions and names are preserved without identifying the unnamed sister with Salome or equating different Mary descriptions.
    Mark 15:40 There were also women watching from a distance, among them Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James the Little and Joses, and Salome.
    Mark 15:41 When He was in Galilee, they had followed Him and served Him; there were also many other women who had come up with Him to Jerusalem.

John 19:26
Then Jesus, seeing His mother and the disciple whom He loved standing nearby, says to His mother, “Woman, behold your son.”
  John 13:23 — narrative parallel: The disciple whom Jesus loved appears at the cross; the supper narrative earlier introduces a disciple with that same identifying description reclining near Jesus. This links the recurring unnamed figure without assigning a personal name or deriving his identity merely from later tradition. The mother's new-son charge remains additional here.
    John 13:23 One of His disciples, whom Jesus loved, was reclining in Jesus’ bosom.

John 19:27
Then He says to the disciple, “Behold your mother.” And from that hour the disciple took her into his own home.
  John 19:26 — narrative parallel: The two sayings form explicit complementary directions: Jesus tells His mother behold your son, then tells the disciple behold your mother. This is an internal reciprocal charge followed here by home care, not independent testimony or a separately established legal adoption ceremony.
    John 19:26 Then Jesus, seeing His mother and the disciple whom He loved standing nearby, says to His mother, “Woman, behold your son.”

John 19:28
After this, Jesus, knowing that all things had already been completed, says, so that the Scripture might be brought to completion, “I thirst.”
  Psalms 68:22 — allusion: The psalm describes thirst met with sour wine; John links Jesus' I thirst with Scripture's completion and then narrates the sour wine. The concrete thirst-and-drink relation is compared using the actual Greek-OT ID. The psalm's additional gall for food is not inserted into John's scene.
    Psalms 68:22 They gave gall for my food, and for my thirst made me drink sour wine.

John 19:29
A vessel full of sour wine was standing there. So, putting a sponge full of the sour wine on hyssop, they brought it to His mouth.
  Mark 15:36 — narrative parallel: Both passages describe a sour-wine-filled sponge brought to Jesus. John gives a vessel and hyssop; Mark places it on a reed and adds the Elijah remark. Implements and accompanying speech remain distinct, and this drink is not confused with an earlier offered mixture that another scene says He refused.
    Mark 15:36 Someone ran, filled a sponge with sour wine, placed it on a reed, and gave Him a drink, saying, “Leave Him; let us see whether Elijah comes to take Him down.”

John 19:30
When therefore Jesus had received the sour wine, He said, “It is finished.” And bowing His head, He gave up His spirit.
  Luke 23:46 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus' death in connection with His final speech and spirit. John gives It is finished, the bowed head and giving up His spirit; Luke gives the loud Father-into-Thy-hands prayer and breathing His last. The different recorded sayings and actions are not assembled into an unprovided sequence of last words.
    Luke 23:46 And Jesus cried out with a loud voice and said, “Father, into Thy hands I entrust My spirit.” Having said this, He breathed His last.

John 19:31
Then, because it was Preparation, the Jews asked Pilate to have their legs broken and their bodies taken away, so that the bodies would not remain on the cross on the Sabbath—for that Sabbath was a great day.
  Deuteronomy 21:22–21:23 — thematic parallel: John explains the request that bodies not remain on the crosses over the Sabbath; Deuteronomy forbids leaving a hanged body overnight and commands burial that day. The body-removal concern has concrete legal background, while John adds the great Sabbath and the leg-breaking request, neither of which the law itself prescribes here.
    Deuteronomy 21:22 And if there is in a man a sin deserving judgment of death, and he dies, and you hang him upon a tree,
    Deuteronomy 21:23 his body shall not remain upon the tree overnight, but you shall surely bury him that day; for everyone who hangs upon a tree is cursed by God. And you shall not defile the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession.

John 19:32
So the soldiers came and broke the legs of the first man and of the other who was crucified with Him;
  John 19:33 — narrative parallel: The narrative explicitly contrasts the soldiers' breaking the two others' legs with not breaking Jesus' legs because He is already dead. This is a deliberate adjacent difference in treatment and its stated reason, not a claim about an unreported exact death time for the other men.
    John 19:33 but when they came to Jesus, as they saw that He was already dead, they did not break His legs.

John 19:33
but when they came to Jesus, as they saw that He was already dead, they did not break His legs.
  John 19:36 — narrative parallel: The later narrator explicitly interprets Jesus' unbroken bones as Scripture's fulfillment. It points back to the soldiers' decision not to break His legs after finding Him dead. This is an action-and-scriptural-explanation link within the same account, with no added leg-breaking or additional injury.
    John 19:36 For these things happened so that the Scripture might be fulfilled: “A bone of His will not be broken.”

John 19:34
But one of the soldiers pierced His side with a spear, and immediately blood and water came out.
  1 John 5:6–5:8 — thematic parallel: John narrates blood and water from Jesus' pierced side and immediately appeals to witness; the epistle joins Jesus Christ with water, blood and the Spirit's testimony. The distinctive water-blood-witness cluster is compared without asserting that the epistle explicitly describes this wound or determines a medical or sacramental explanation of its fluids.
    1 John 5:6 This is He who came through water and blood, Jesus Christ—not in water only, but in water and in blood. And the Spirit is the one who bears witness, because the Spirit is the truth.
    1 John 5:7 For there are three who bear witness:
    1 John 5:8 the Spirit and the water and the blood, and the three agree as one.

John 19:35
And the one who has seen has borne witness, and his testimony is true; and he knows that he speaks the truth, so that you also may believe.
  John 21:24 — narrative parallel: Both narrator statements affirm true testimony: one concerns the person who saw and testifies so others may believe, the other the disciple who testifies and wrote these things, with we know. The recurring witness formula does not by itself establish an additional personal name or a new theory about the plural voice.
    John 21:24 This is the disciple who bears witness about these things and who wrote these things; and we know that his testimony is true.

John 19:36
For these things happened so that the Scripture might be fulfilled: “A bone of His will not be broken.”
  Exodus 12:46 — allusion: John presents the unbroken bone as Scripture fulfilled; the Passover law specifically forbids breaking a bone of the animal eaten. This is concrete possible Passover-scripture background, with the command's you-shall-not-break wording and John's passive fulfillment wording preserved rather than claimed as a verbatim or uniquely identified citation.
    Exodus 12:46 It shall be eaten in one house. You shall not take any of the meat outside the house, and you shall not break a bone of it.
  Psalms 33:21 — allusion: The actual psalm says the Lord guards all their bones and not one will be shattered; John cites the unbroken bone of His as fulfillment. The shared protection-from-breaking form supplies another possible scriptural connection, while the psalm's plural righteous and John's singular application remain distinct.
    Psalms 33:21 The Lord guards all their bones; not one of them will be shattered.

John 19:37
And again another Scripture says, “They will look on the One whom they pierced.”
  Zechariah 12:10 — allusion: John's Scripture wording is look on the One whom they pierced. The supplied Zechariah says look toward Me because they danced in mockery and adds mourning over a beloved one and firstborn. The prophetic looking-and-mistreatment context is compared as possible citation background with an explicit wording difference, not a verbatim match or a replacement of mockery with pierced.
    Zechariah 12:10 I will pour upon David’s house and Jerusalem’s residents a spirit of grace and compassion. They will look toward Me because they danced in mockery, and mourn over him as over a beloved one; they will suffer grief as over a firstborn.

John 19:38
And after these things Joseph from Arimathea, being a disciple of Jesus but secretly because of fear of the Jews, asked Pilate that he might take away the body of Jesus; and Pilate permitted it. So he came and took away His body.
  Mark 15:43–15:45 — narrative parallel: Both accounts name Joseph of Arimathea asking Pilate for Jesus' body and receiving permission. John calls him a secret disciple because of fear; Mark describes his daring approach, council standing and Pilate's inquiry. Those different descriptions are retained without inventing a new prior biography or making the public request erase the narrated fear.
    Mark 15:43 Joseph of Arimathea, a respected council member who himself was also awaiting the kingdom of God, came and dared to enter before Pilate and ask for Jesus’ body.
    Mark 15:44 Pilate marveled that He was already dead. Calling the centurion to him, he asked whether He had died some time ago.
    Mark 15:45 When he learned from the centurion, he granted the corpse to Joseph.

John 19:39
And Nicodemus also came, the one who had first come to Him by night, bringing a mixture of myrrh and aloes, about a hundred litrai.
  John 3:1–3:2 — narrative parallel: John expressly identifies Nicodemus here as the man who first came to Jesus by night, matching the earlier named visit. This is a direct retrospective person-and-visit reference. The myrrh, aloes and hundred-litrai quantity remain particular to the burial scene rather than being added to his earlier conversation.
    John 3:1 Now there was a man from the Pharisees named Nicodemus, a ruler of the Jews.
    John 3:2 He came to Him at night and said to Him, “Rabbi, we know that Thou hast come from God as a teacher, for no one can do these signs that Thou doest unless God is with him.”

John 19:40
So they took the body of Jesus and bound it in linen cloths with the spices, as is the Jews’ custom for burial.
  Luke 23:53 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe Jesus' body wrapped in linen for burial. John uses a plural subject after Joseph and Nicodemus and adds cloths with spices and the burial custom; Luke narrates Joseph's wrapping in linen before placement. The agents and the spice detail remain as given rather than being supplied to every account.
    Luke 23:53 Taking it down, he wrapped it in linen and laid Him in a rock-cut tomb where no one had yet been laid.

John 19:41
Now in the place where He was crucified there was a garden, and in the garden a new tomb, in which no one had yet been laid.
  Luke 23:53 — narrative parallel: Both passages specify a tomb in which no one had yet been laid. John additionally locates the new tomb in a garden near the crucifixion; Luke describes a rock-cut tomb. The unused-tomb correspondence does not independently confirm John's garden layout or erase the different architectural descriptions.
    Luke 23:53 Taking it down, he wrapped it in linen and laid Him in a rock-cut tomb where no one had yet been laid.

John 19:42
So there, because of the Jews’ Preparation, since the tomb was nearby, they laid Jesus.
  Luke 23:53–23:54 — narrative parallel: Both accounts connect Jesus' burial with Preparation and the approaching Sabbath. John expressly gives the tomb's nearness as the reason for using it; Luke states the day's designation and Sabbath's beginning after describing the burial. The shared scheduling context does not invent an extra travel route or harmonized clock.
    Luke 23:53 Taking it down, he wrapped it in linen and laid Him in a rock-cut tomb where no one had yet been laid.
    Luke 23:54 It was the day of Preparation, and the Sabbath was beginning.

John 20:1
Now on the first day of the week Mary Magdalene comes early to the tomb, while it is still dark, and sees the stone taken away from the tomb.
  Mark 16:2–16:4 — narrative parallel: Both accounts place a tomb visit early on the first day of the week and describe the stone already moved. John focuses on Mary Magdalene while it is still dark; Mark's preceding list gives several women and says the sun has risen. Group and light descriptions are retained without inventing separate departure and arrival times to reconcile them.
    Mark 16:2 Very early on the first day of the week, they came to the tomb when the sun had risen.
    Mark 16:3 They said to one another, “Who will roll the stone away from the entrance of the tomb for us?”
    Mark 16:4 Looking up, they saw that the stone had been rolled away, for it was very large.

John 20:2
So she runs and comes to Simon Peter and to the other disciple whom Jesus loved, and says to them, “They took the Lord out of the tomb, and we do not know where they laid Him.”
  Luke 24:9–24:12 — narrative parallel: Both accounts connect a women's tomb report with Peter's movement to inspect it. John's initial report to Peter and the beloved disciple says the body was taken and its location unknown; Luke's group reports the preceding tomb message to the Eleven and others. The first report is not silently turned into Mary's later I-have-seen-the-Lord announcement.
    Luke 24:9 and returned from the tomb and reported all these things to the Eleven and to all the rest.
    Luke 24:10 It was Mary Magdalene and Joanna and Mary of James and the other women with them who told these things to the apostles.
    Luke 24:11 But these words seemed to them like nonsense, and they did not believe the women.
    Luke 24:12 Yet Peter rose and ran to the tomb. Stooping to look in, he saw the linen cloths alone, and went away, wondering to himself at what had happened.

John 20:3
Then Peter and the other disciple went out, and they were coming to the tomb.
  Luke 24:12 — narrative parallel: Both passages narrate Peter going to the tomb after the report. John explicitly includes the other disciple and subsequently distinguishes their movements; Luke names Peter running and inspecting the linen. The shared Peter journey does not add or deny an unnamed companion in Luke's shorter sentence.
    Luke 24:12 Yet Peter rose and ran to the tomb. Stooping to look in, he saw the linen cloths alone, and went away, wondering to himself at what had happened.

John 20:4
The two were running together, and the other disciple ran ahead faster than Peter and came to the tomb first;
  John 20:8 — narrative parallel: The later entry statement explicitly recalls the other disciple as the one who came to the tomb first, pointing back to his outrunning Peter. This is a direct internal arrival reference. John still gives Peter the first narrated entry, so arriving first and entering first are not assigned to the same person.
    John 20:8 Then the other disciple who had come to the tomb first also entered, and he saw and believed;

John 20:5
and, stooping to look in, he sees the linen cloths lying there; yet he did not enter.
  Luke 24:12 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe stooping to inspect the tomb and seeing linen cloths, but John initially assigns that look without entry to the other disciple, while Luke assigns the stooping to Peter. The matching action is compared with its different named or unnamed agent rather than exchanging their roles.
    Luke 24:12 Yet Peter rose and ran to the tomb. Stooping to look in, he saw the linen cloths alone, and went away, wondering to himself at what had happened.

John 20:6
Then Simon Peter also comes, following him, and entered the tomb; and he beholds the linen cloths lying there,
  Luke 24:12 — narrative parallel: Peter sees the linen cloths at the tomb in both accounts. John expressly narrates his entering after the other disciple arrives; Luke says he stoops to look and then departs in wonder. The comparison preserves John's explicit entry and does not manufacture an entry sentence or shared race order in Luke.
    Luke 24:12 Yet Peter rose and ran to the tomb. Stooping to look in, he saw the linen cloths alone, and went away, wondering to himself at what had happened.

John 20:7
and the face cloth that had been on His head, not lying with the linen cloths, but wrapped up separately in one place.
  John 11:44 — narrative parallel: Both resurrection scenes identify a face cloth along with bodily linen wrappings. Lazarus comes out still bound and must be unbound; Jesus' face cloth is separate from the lying linen cloths in the empty tomb. The different persons and arrangements remain, with no invented folded-napkin custom or physical mechanism of resurrection.
    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

John 20:8
Then the other disciple who had come to the tomb first also entered, and he saw and believed;
  John 2:22 — narrative parallel: Both narrator statements connect the resurrection setting with belief. The earlier forward-looking notice says the disciples remember and believe the Scripture and Jesus' word after His rising; here the other disciple sees and believes before the following not-yet-Scripture-knowledge statement. The unstated object and extent of this first belief are not filled in as complete scriptural understanding.
    John 2:22 When, therefore, He was raised from the dead, His disciples remembered that He had said this, and they believed the Scripture and the word Jesus had spoken.

John 20:9
for they did not yet know the Scripture, that He must rise from the dead.
  Luke 24:44–24:46 — narrative parallel: John states that the disciples did not yet know the Scripture about His necessary rising; Luke later narrates Jesus opening the disciples' minds to Scripture and explaining the Messiah's suffering and third-day rising. The knowledge-and-Scripture connection preserves the differing stages and does not identify an Old Testament verse John himself leaves unnamed.
    Luke 24:44 And He said to them, “These are My words that I spoke to you while I was still with you: everything written about Me in the Law of Moses and the Prophets and Psalms must be fulfilled.”
    Luke 24:45 Then He opened their mind to understand the Scriptures
    Luke 24:46 and said to them, “Thus it is written that the Christ should suffer and rise from the dead on the third day,

John 20:10
Then the disciples went back to their own place.
  Luke 24:12 — narrative parallel: Both accounts end the tomb inspection with departure. John specifies the disciples returning to their own place without adding an emotional description here; Luke narrates Peter going away while wondering. The shared departure does not add John's stated destination to Luke or transfer Luke's reaction to both of John's disciples.
    Luke 24:12 Yet Peter rose and ran to the tomb. Stooping to look in, he saw the linen cloths alone, and went away, wondering to himself at what had happened.

John 20:11
But Mary was standing outside beside the tomb, weeping. So, as she wept, she stooped to look into the tomb,
  John 20:13 — narrative parallel: Mary's weeping while looking into the tomb is explicitly addressed by the angels' subsequent Why do you weep and her answer about the missing body. This is an internal explanation of the grief narrated here. Her assumption about removal remains her report rather than the narrator's explanation of the empty tomb.
    John 20:13 And they say to her, “Woman, why do you weep?” She says to them, “They took my Lord, and I do not know where they laid Him.”

John 20:12
and beholds two angels in white, sitting one at the head and one at the feet, where the body of Jesus had lain.
  Luke 24:4–24:5 — narrative parallel: Both tomb scenes introduce two white or radiant figures addressing women. John calls them angels seated at the head and feet of the former body-place; Luke introduces men standing in flashing clothing and the women bow. Their descriptions, positions, questions and audiences remain distinct rather than a combined stage direction.
    Luke 24:4 And it happened that, while they were perplexed about this, behold, two men stood beside them in flashing clothing.
    Luke 24:5 They became afraid and bowed their faces to the ground, and the men said to them, “Why do you seek the living One among the dead?

John 20:13
And they say to her, “Woman, why do you weep?” She says to them, “They took my Lord, and I do not know where they laid Him.”
  John 20:2 — narrative parallel: Mary repeats her earlier body-removal report and uncertainty about its location, now to the angels rather than to Peter and the other disciple. John changes we do not know to I do not know and the Lord to my Lord. The explicit recurrence keeps those pronouns and remains a character's mistaken assumption, not a narrator finding of theft.
    John 20:2 So she runs and comes to Simon Peter and to the other disciple whom Jesus loved, and says to them, “They took the Lord out of the tomb, and we do not know where they laid Him.”

John 20:14
Having said these things, she turned backward and beholds Jesus standing there, and did not know that it was Jesus.
  Luke 24:15–24:16 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe the risen Jesus present with followers who do not initially recognize Him. Luke expressly says the travelers' eyes were held back; John simply reports Mary's failure to know Him. Luke's cause is not imposed on John, nor are the tomb garden and Emmaus road treated as one encounter.
    Luke 24:15 And it happened that, as they talked and discussed, Jesus Himself drew near and traveled with them.
    Luke 24:16 But their eyes were held back from recognizing Him.

John 20:15
Jesus says to her, “Woman, why do you weep? Whom do you seek?” She, supposing that He is the gardener, says to Him, “Sir, if Thou didst carry Him away, tell me where Thou didst lay Him, and I will take Him away.”
  John 20:13 — narrative parallel: Jesus repeats the angels' Woman, why do you weep question and adds Whom do you seek. Mary again responds from the assumption of a removed body, now supposing a gardener. This is a deliberate question-and-misunderstanding recurrence, not an identification of the angels with Jesus or narrator approval of the gardener assumption.
    John 20:13 And they say to her, “Woman, why do you weep?” She says to them, “They took my Lord, and I do not know where they laid Him.”

John 20:16
Jesus says to her, “Mary.” Turning, she says to Him in Hebrew, “Rabbouni!”—which means Teacher.
  John 10:3–10:4 — thematic parallel: Jesus' personal Mary call leads to recognition; the shepherd figure describes calling his own sheep by name and their knowing his voice. The scene provides a concrete illustration of personal call and recognition, without claiming John explicitly quotes the earlier figure or explains the cause of Mary's previous nonrecognition.
    John 10:3 To him the doorkeeper opens, and the sheep hear his voice. He calls his own sheep by name and leads them out.
    John 10:4 When he has brought all his own out, he goes before them, and the sheep follow him, because they know his voice.

John 20:17
Jesus says to her, “Do not cling to Me, for I have not yet ascended to the Father. But go to My brothers and tell them, ‘I am ascending to My Father and your Father, and My God and your God.’”
  Matthew 28:9–28:10 — narrative parallel: Both passages give women a message for Jesus' brothers after meeting Him. Matthew narrates grasped feet, worship and a Galilee instruction; John records Do not cling and the Father-and-God ascent message. John's command is not turned into a separately narrated completed act of clinging, and the different messages and contact descriptions remain.
    Matthew 28:9 And behold, Jesus met them, saying, “Greetings!” They came, grasped His feet, and worshiped Him.
    Matthew 28:10 Then Jesus said to them, “Do not fear. Go and report to My brothers that they should go to Galilee, and there they will see Me.”

John 20:18
Mary Magdalene comes, announcing to the disciples, “I have seen the Lord,” and that He said these things to her.
  Mark 16:9–16:11 — narrative parallel: Both supplied texts describe Mary Magdalene reporting that she has seen the risen Jesus. John's report includes His message; Mark's bracketed longer ending adds the recipients' mourning and unbelief and its own first-appearance statement. These are compared as supplied without adding those details to John or giving the longer ending fresh critical-text approval.
    Mark 16:9 ⟦After He had risen early on the first day of the week, He appeared first to Mary Magdalene, from whom He had cast out seven demons.
    Mark 16:10 She went and reported to those who had been with Him, as they mourned and wept.
    Mark 16:11 When they heard that He was alive and had been seen by her, they disbelieved.

John 20:19
Then, when it was evening on that day, the first day of the week, and the doors were shut where the disciples were, because of fear of the Jews, Jesus came and stood in the midst, and says to them, “Peace to you.”
  Luke 24:36 — narrative parallel: Both passages describe Jesus standing among the disciples and offering peace. John adds the first-day evening, shut doors and fear context; Luke places the appearance amid the gathered group's discussion. Those details remain distinct, with John's later explicit Thomas absence not erased by assumptions about Luke's collective group label.
    Luke 24:36 While they were speaking these things, He Himself stood among them and said to them, “Peace be with you.”

John 20:20
And having said this, He showed them His hands and His side. Then the disciples rejoiced when they saw the Lord.
  Luke 24:39–24:41 — narrative parallel: Both risen-Jesus scenes show bodily evidence and joy among the followers. John names hands and side, with rejoicing at seeing the Lord; Luke names hands and feet and describes continuing disbelief for joy. Body parts and stated responses are preserved rather than making John's side wound into Luke's feet or all joy identical belief.
    Luke 24:39 See My hands and My feet, that it is I Myself. Feel Me and see, because a spirit does not have flesh and bones as you see that I have.”
    Luke 24:40 And having said this, He showed them His hands and feet.
    Luke 24:41 While they still did not believe for joy and were marveling, He said to them, “Do you have anything to eat here?”

John 20:21
Then Jesus said to them again, “Peace to you. Just as the Father has sent Me, I also send you.”
  John 17:18 — teaching parallel: The risen Jesus repeats the Father-sent-Me and I-send-you relation already spoken in the prayer. The prayer describes sending into the world; the appearance directly addresses and commissions the disciples with peace. The recurring mission formula keeps its earlier prayer and later appearance settings.
    John 17:18 Just as Thou didst send Me into the world, I also sent them into the world;

John 20:22
And having said this, He breathed and says to them, “Receive the Holy Spirit.
  Genesis 2:7 — allusion: Jesus breathes and commands reception of the Holy Spirit; Genesis depicts God breathing life's breath into the formed human. This is a possible creative-breath image connecting life-giving actions, not a claim that Genesis itself names the Holy Spirit here or that the bodily formation and risen-Jesus scene are one act.
    Genesis 2:7 And God formed man from the dust of the earth, and breathed into his face the breath of life; and man became a living soul.

John 20:23
If you forgive the sins of any, they have been forgiven them; if you retain those of any, they have been retained.”
  Matthew 18:18 — teaching parallel: Jesus gives a consequential forgiving-and-retaining charge; Matthew gives a binding-and-loosing charge with earthly and heavenly correspondence. The comparison concerns delegated responsibility and its stated consequences. It does not make the different verbs identical definitions or settle every later account of the scope and manner of that responsibility.
    Matthew 18:18 Truly I tell you, whatever you bind upon earth will have been bound in heaven, and whatever you loose upon earth will have been loosed in heaven.

John 20:24
But Thomas, one of the Twelve, who was called Didymus, was not with them when Jesus came.
  John 11:16 — narrative parallel: Both passages identify Thomas with the explicit name Didymus. The earlier account gives his words about going with Jesus, whereas this one records his absence from the first group appearance. The recurring-person reference does not reconstruct his reason for absence or make one statement describe his permanent attitude.
    John 11:16 Then Thomas, who was called Didymus, said to his fellow disciples, “Let us also go, so that we may die with Him.”

John 20:25
So the other disciples were saying to him, “We have seen the Lord.” But he said to them, “Unless I see in His hands the mark of the nails and put my finger into the mark of the nails and put my hand into His side, I certainly will not believe.”
  John 20:27 — narrative parallel: Jesus later explicitly answers Thomas's requested hand-and-side tests with corresponding invitations and a call from unbelief to belief. This is a stated-demand and offered-response relationship, preserving finger, hand, nail marks and side. Neither verse states that Thomas actually performs the proposed touching.
    John 20:27 Then He says to Thomas, “Bring your finger here and see My hands, and bring your hand and put it into My side; and do not become unbelieving, but believing.”

John 20:26
And after eight days His disciples were inside again, and Thomas with them. Jesus comes, the doors being shut, and stood in the midst and said, “Peace to you.”
  John 20:19 — narrative parallel: The account repeats the shut doors, Jesus' standing in the midst and His peace greeting, but now dates it after eight days and explicitly includes Thomas. The two appearances are deliberately parallel and distinct; the earlier Thomas absence and later presence are not collapsed into one evening.
    John 20:19 Then, when it was evening on that day, the first day of the week, and the doors were shut where the disciples were, because of fear of the Jews, Jesus came and stood in the midst, and says to them, “Peace to you.”

John 20:27
Then He says to Thomas, “Bring your finger here and see My hands, and bring your hand and put it into My side; and do not become unbelieving, but believing.”
