  Luke 24:39 — narrative parallel: Both passages offer bodily verification by seeing and touch. John directs Thomas's finger and hand toward hands and side; Luke addresses the group about hands, feet, flesh and bones. The invitations, body parts and audiences differ, and neither is used to invent an unreported completed touch.
    Luke 24:39 See My hands and My feet, that it is I Myself. Feel Me and see, because a spirit does not have flesh and bones as you see that I have.”

John 20:28
Thomas answered and said to Him, “My Lord and my God!”
  John 1:1 — teaching parallel: Thomas addresses Jesus as my Lord and my God; the prologue identifies the Word as with God and as God. The confession and opening declaration form a concrete deity-language connection within the Gospel, without replacing either passage's actual speaker, personal address or formulation.
    John 1:1 In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God.

John 20:29
Jesus says to him, “Because you have seen Me, have you believed? Blessed are those who have not seen and have believed.”
  1 Peter 1:8–1:9 — teaching parallel: Jesus blesses those who believe without having seen; the epistle describes readers who have not seen Him yet love, believe and rejoice, receiving faith's saving outcome. Both explicitly relate faith to the absence of physical sight, with a beatitude and a pastoral description as different forms of the teaching.
    1 Peter 1:8 Though you have not seen Him, you love Him; though you do not see Him now, yet believing in Him, you rejoice with joy inexpressible and full of glory,
    1 Peter 1:9 receiving the outcome of your faith, the salvation of your souls.

John 20:30
Now Jesus also did many other signs before His disciples, which are not written in this book;
  John 21:25 — narrative parallel: Both closing notices say Jesus did more than the written account records. John 20 specifically names other signs before disciples; John 21 gives the broader things-done statement and the world-and-books expression. The selection notices do not verify an invented list of unwritten acts or literalize the book-capacity rhetoric.
    John 21:25 And there are also many other things that Jesus did, which, if each one were written, I suppose that even the world itself would not contain the books being written.

John 20:31
but these have been written so that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and so that, believing, you may have life in His name.
  1 John 5:11–5:13 — teaching parallel: Both passages explain writing in relation to the Son, belief in His name and eternal life. The Gospel gives a belief-and-life purpose for its written signs; the epistle writes to believers so they may know they have life. Those related purposes remain distinct rather than making their audiences and exact wording identical.
    1 John 5:11 And this is the testimony: God gave us eternal life, and this life is in His Son.
    1 John 5:12 The one who has the Son has life; the one who does not have the Son of God does not have life.
    1 John 5:13 I have written these things to you who believe in the name of the Son of God, so that you may know that you have eternal life.

John 21:1
After these things Jesus revealed Himself again to the disciples at the Sea of Tiberias, and He revealed Himself in this way.
  John 6:1 — narrative parallel: The risen-Jesus scene is located at the Sea of Tiberias; the earlier narrative expressly identifies the Sea of Galilee as the Sea of Tiberias. This is a direct same-book place-name clarification, not a claim that the earlier crossing or feeding and the later fishing appearance are one event.
    John 6:1 After these things Jesus went away across the Sea of Galilee, the Sea of Tiberias.

John 21:2
Simon Peter and Thomas, who was called Didymus, and Nathanael from Cana of Galilee, and the sons of Zebedee, and two others of His disciples were together.
  Mark 1:19–1:20 — narrative parallel: John includes the sons of Zebedee among this fishing company; Mark names James and John as Zebedee's sons in the earlier boat-and-calling scene. The reference clarifies the family designation without identifying John's two unnamed companions or automatically assigning the beloved-disciple description to one named son.
    Mark 1:19 Going a little farther, He saw James son of Zebedee and John his brother, who were also in the boat, putting the nets in order.
    Mark 1:20 Immediately He called them. They left their father Zebedee in the boat with the hired workers and went after Him.

John 21:3
Simon Peter says to them, “I am going fishing.” They say to him, “We also are coming with you.” They went out and got into the boat, and during that night they caught nothing.
  Luke 5:4–5:5 — narrative parallel: Both fishing narratives begin with a fruitless night's work before a Jesus-directed abundant catch. John records Peter's decision and the group's unsuccessful night after the resurrection; Luke gives Simon's report of the night's labor before obeying the command. The different settings and stages of discipleship are not combined.
    Luke 5:4 When He finished speaking, He said to Simon, “Put out into the deep water, and let down your nets for a catch.”
    Luke 5:5 Simon answered, “Master, we labored through the whole night and caught nothing, but at Thy word I will let down the nets.”

John 21:4
But when morning had already come, Jesus stood on the shore; yet the disciples did not know that it was Jesus.
  John 20:14 — narrative parallel: Both scenes present the risen Jesus in view without immediate recognition: the disciples see Him on the shore and Mary sees Him near the tomb. John states nonrecognition without naming the same cause in either scene, so distance, light or altered appearance is not supplied as an explanation.
    John 20:14 Having said these things, she turned backward and beholds Jesus standing there, and did not know that it was Jesus.

John 21:5
Then Jesus says to them, “Children, you do not have any fish to eat, do you?” They answered Him, “No.”
  Luke 24:41–24:43 — narrative parallel: The risen Jesus asks His followers about food in both scenes. John gives the shore-to-boat fish question and their No; Luke gives a request for something to eat, broiled fish and His explicit eating. The different answers, places and food actions are retained rather than made one meal.
    Luke 24:41 While they still did not believe for joy and were marveling, He said to them, “Do you have anything to eat here?”
    Luke 24:42 They gave Him a portion of broiled fish.
    Luke 24:43 And He took it and ate before them.

John 21:6
And He said to them, “Cast the net on the right side of the boat, and you will find some.” So they cast it, and were no longer strong enough to draw it in because of the multitude of fish.
  Luke 5:4–5:7 — narrative parallel: Both accounts give Jesus' command, obedient casting and an unexpectedly great catch after failure. John specifies the boat's right side and difficulty drawing the net; Luke specifies deep water, nets beginning to tear and assistance from another boat. Their catches, equipment outcomes and before/after-resurrection settings remain distinct.
    Luke 5:4 When He finished speaking, He said to Simon, “Put out into the deep water, and let down your nets for a catch.”
    Luke 5:5 Simon answered, “Master, we labored through the whole night and caught nothing, but at Thy word I will let down the nets.”
    Luke 5:6 When they did this, they enclosed a great multitude of fish, and their nets began to tear.
    Luke 5:7 They signaled to their partners in the other boat to come and help them. They came and filled both boats, so that they began to sink.

John 21:7
Then that disciple whom Jesus loved says to Peter, “It is the Lord.” So Simon Peter, hearing that it is the Lord, tied his outer garment around himself—for he was naked—and threw himself into the sea.
  John 20:2–20:8 — narrative parallel: Both resurrection narratives distinguish Peter's actions from those of the beloved disciple. At the tomb the other disciple arrives first but Peter enters first; here the beloved disciple recognizes the Lord and Peter ties on his outer garment before entering the sea. The differing priorities and clothing sequence remain, and the unnamed disciple receives no added personal name.
    John 20:2 So she runs and comes to Simon Peter and to the other disciple whom Jesus loved, and says to them, “They took the Lord out of the tomb, and we do not know where they laid Him.”
    John 20:3 Then Peter and the other disciple went out, and they were coming to the tomb.
    John 20:4 The two were running together, and the other disciple ran ahead faster than Peter and came to the tomb first;
    John 20:5 and, stooping to look in, he sees the linen cloths lying there; yet he did not enter.
    John 20:6 Then Simon Peter also comes, following him, and entered the tomb; and he beholds the linen cloths lying there,
    John 20:7 and the face cloth that had been on His head, not lying with the linen cloths, but wrapped up separately in one place.
    John 20:8 Then the other disciple who had come to the tomb first also entered, and he saw and believed;

John 21:8
But the other disciples came in the little boat, dragging the net of fish, for they were not far from the land, but about two hundred cubits away.
  Luke 5:7 — narrative parallel: Both great-catch narratives describe fellow disciples handling the fish with boats. Luke brings partners in the other boat and describes both boats dangerously full; John has the other disciples come in the little boat dragging the net after Peter leaves it. Boat roles, distance and the absence of a sinking statement in John remain distinct.
    Luke 5:7 They signaled to their partners in the other boat to come and help them. They came and filled both boats, so that they began to sink.

John 21:9
So when they got out onto the land, they see a charcoal fire laid there, and fish lying on it, and bread.
  John 18:18 — narrative parallel: The charcoal fire recurs in Peter's story: one is in the high-priestly courtyard amid his denials, the other on the shore before the later love-and-flock charge. This is a specific repeated setting detail with contrasting company and purpose, not the same fire or a statement that John names who lit the shore fire.
    John 18:18 Now the slaves and the officers were standing there, having made a charcoal fire because it was cold, and they were warming themselves. Peter too was with them, standing and warming himself.

John 21:10
Jesus says to them, “Bring some of the fish that you have just caught.”
  Matthew 14:17–14:19 — narrative parallel: Jesus' request for newly caught fish is compared with His command to bring the available loaves and fish before feeding the crowd. The concrete food-contribution command precedes a meal in both narratives. Catch and existing supplies, disciples' breakfast and crowd feeding remain distinct; no new multiplication or identity of occasions is asserted.
    Matthew 14:17 They said to Him, “We have here only five loaves and two fish.”
    Matthew 14:18 He said, “Bring them here to Me.”
    Matthew 14:19 He ordered the crowds to recline on the grass. Taking the five loaves and the two fish, He looked up to heaven, blessed, broke the loaves, and gave them to the disciples, and the disciples to the crowds.

John 21:11
So Simon Peter went up and drew the net onto the land, full of large fish, a hundred and fifty-three; and though there were so many, the net was not torn.
  Luke 5:6–5:7 — narrative parallel: Both narratives describe a great fish catch and its strain on the equipment. Luke says the nets begin to tear and two boats become loaded; John gives 153 large fish and expressly says the net is not torn. Number, net outcome and boat details are retained without an invented symbolic fish count or an identical catch.
    Luke 5:6 When they did this, they enclosed a great multitude of fish, and their nets began to tear.
    Luke 5:7 They signaled to their partners in the other boat to come and help them. They came and filled both boats, so that they began to sink.

John 21:12
Jesus says to them, “Come, have breakfast.” But none of the disciples dared to question Him, “Who art Thou?” knowing that it was the Lord.
  Luke 24:41–24:43 — narrative parallel: Both risen-Jesus scenes involve food with the disciples, but the roles and recognition differ. John gives His breakfast invitation to disciples who know the Lord; Luke gives His request and explicit fish-eating while the group still struggles to believe for joy. Luke's eating statement is not added as an explicit action in John's invitation.
    Luke 24:41 While they still did not believe for joy and were marveling, He said to them, “Do you have anything to eat here?”
    Luke 24:42 They gave Him a portion of broiled fish.
    Luke 24:43 And He took it and ate before them.

John 21:13
Jesus comes and takes the bread and gives it to them, and the fish likewise.
  John 6:11 — narrative parallel: Jesus takes and distributes bread and fish in both scenes. The earlier sign feeds the reclining crowd with thanksgiving; the shore scene gives the same foods to the disciples after the catch. The recipients and scale differ, and the earlier thanksgiving is not inserted into a scene that does not explicitly repeat it.
    John 6:11 Then Jesus took the loaves, gave thanks, and distributed them to those reclining, and likewise from the fish, as much as they wanted.

John 21:14
This was now the third time Jesus was revealed to the disciples after being raised from the dead.
  John 20:19–20:29 — narrative parallel: The third-revelation notice follows the two narrated group appearances: the first-day evening without Thomas and the gathering after eight days with him. This relates John's count to those prior scenes without making it a universal count of every appearance or denying Mary's earlier encounter and other supplied Gospel accounts.
    John 20:19 Then, when it was evening on that day, the first day of the week, and the doors were shut where the disciples were, because of fear of the Jews, Jesus came and stood in the midst, and says to them, “Peace to you.”
    John 20:20 And having said this, He showed them His hands and His side. Then the disciples rejoiced when they saw the Lord.
    John 20:21 Then Jesus said to them again, “Peace to you. Just as the Father has sent Me, I also send you.”
    John 20:22 And having said this, He breathed and says to them, “Receive the Holy Spirit.
    John 20:23 If you forgive the sins of any, they have been forgiven them; if you retain those of any, they have been retained.”
    John 20:24 But Thomas, one of the Twelve, who was called Didymus, was not with them when Jesus came.
    John 20:25 So the other disciples were saying to him, “We have seen the Lord.” But he said to them, “Unless I see in His hands the mark of the nails and put my finger into the mark of the nails and put my hand into His side, I certainly will not believe.”
    John 20:26 And after eight days His disciples were inside again, and Thomas with them. Jesus comes, the doors being shut, and stood in the midst and said, “Peace to you.”
    John 20:27 Then He says to Thomas, “Bring your finger here and see My hands, and bring your hand and put it into My side; and do not become unbelieving, but believing.”
    John 20:28 Thomas answered and said to Him, “My Lord and my God!”
    John 20:29 Jesus says to him, “Because you have seen Me, have you believed? Blessed are those who have not seen and have believed.”

John 21:15
So when they had eaten breakfast, Jesus says to Simon Peter, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me more than these?” He says to Him, “Yes, Lord, Thou knowest that I love Thee.” He says to him, “Feed My lambs.”
  1 Peter 5:1–5:4 — thematic parallel: Jesus charges Peter to feed His lambs; the epistle's fellow-elder exhortation calls for willing care of God's flock under the Chief Shepherd. This is a concrete pastoral-care comparison, not identical wording or a new determination of the ambiguous more than these in Jesus' love question.
    1 Peter 5:1 I exhort the elders among you, as a fellow elder and a witness of the sufferings of Christ, and also a participant in the glory about to be revealed:
    1 Peter 5:2 Shepherd the flock of God among you, exercising oversight, not under compulsion but willingly, according to God; not for shameful gain but eagerly;
    1 Peter 5:3 not as lording it over those allotted to you, but becoming examples to the flock.
    1 Peter 5:4 And when the Chief Shepherd is manifested, you will receive the unfading crown of glory.

John 21:16
He says to him again a second time, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me?” He says to Him, “Yes, Lord, Thou knowest that I love Thee.” He says to him, “Shepherd My sheep.”
  Acts 20:28 — thematic parallel: Jesus tells Peter to shepherd His sheep; the elders' charge in Acts tells Spirit-appointed overseers to shepherd the Church of God. Both entrust care of God's people through the shepherd-and-flock image. Individual and plural recipients and the stated appointing roles remain distinct rather than an identical commissioning scene.
    Acts 20:28 Pay attention to yourselves and to all the flock, among whom the Holy Spirit has placed you as overseers, to shepherd the Church of God, which He acquired through His own blood.

John 21:17
He says to him the third time, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me?” Peter was grieved because He said to him the third time, “Do you love Me?” And he said to Him, “Lord, Thou knowest all things; Thou knowest that I love Thee.” Jesus says to him, “Feed My sheep.
  John 13:37–13:38 — narrative parallel: The explicitly third love question and renewed feeding charge are compared with Peter's earlier life-pledge and Jesus' prediction of three denials. The triadic faithfulness-and-failure pattern is concrete in John's narrative. It does not make the three questions a newly supplied one-for-one absolution formula or introduce love-word distinctions absent from this English text.
    John 13:37 Peter said to Him, “Lord, why can I not follow Thee now? I will lay down my life for Thee.”
    John 13:38 Jesus answered, “Will you lay down your life for Me? Amen, amen, I tell you: a rooster will certainly not crow until you deny Me three times.”

John 21:18
Amen, amen, I say to you: when you were younger, you tied your belt around yourself and walked where you wished; but when you grow old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will tie your belt around you and carry you where you do not wish.”
  2 Peter 1:13–1:14 — thematic parallel: John narrates Jesus' forecast of Peter's belt being tied by another and his being carried where he does not wish, interpreted next as death; the epistle says the Lord made clear the approaching putting-off of the speaker's tent. Both concern a Christ-disclosed death prospect, without adding a date, an inverted crucifixion, or proof that the epistle quotes this exact disclosure.
    2 Peter 1:13 And I consider it right, as long as I am in this tent, to stir you up by reminding you,
    2 Peter 1:14 knowing that I will soon put off my tent, as our Lord Jesus Christ also made clear to me.

John 21:19
Now He said this to indicate by what kind of death he would glorify God. And having said this, He says to him, “Follow Me.”
  John 12:32–12:33 — narrative parallel: Both narrator explanations interpret Jesus' preceding words as indicating the kind of death: John 12 concerns Jesus' being lifted up, and John 21 concerns Peter's forecast. The recurring explanatory formula does not exchange the persons or establish an identical execution method from the two images alone.
    John 12:32 And I, if I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all people to Myself.”
    John 12:33 He said this to signify by what kind of death He was about to die.
  John 13:36 — narrative parallel: Jesus earlier tells Peter he cannot follow now but will follow later; after the death interpretation He now directly commands Follow Me. This is a specific Petrine following promise and renewed summons, not a new calendar date or an assertion that every earlier inability has been explained in the same way.
    John 13:36 Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, where art Thou going?” Jesus answered him, “Where I go, you cannot follow Me now, but you will follow later.”

John 21:20
Turning, Peter sees the disciple whom Jesus loved following, the one who also leaned back at the supper upon His chest and said, “Lord, who is the one handing Thee over?”
  John 13:23–13:25 — narrative parallel: John expressly recalls the disciple who reclined against Jesus at supper and asked about the hander-over. The earlier supper narrative records the beloved disciple's position, Peter's signal and the question. The retrospective formulation is fuller than the earlier Lord, who is it, and neither passage supplies the disciple's personal name.
    John 13:23 One of His disciples, whom Jesus loved, was reclining in Jesus’ bosom.
    John 13:24 So Simon Peter signaled to this one to ask whom He was speaking about.
    John 13:25 Leaning back thus upon Jesus’ chest, he said to Him, “Lord, who is it?”

John 21:21
So Peter, seeing him, says to Jesus, “Lord, and what about this man?”
  Acts 1:6–1:8 — narrative parallel: In both post-resurrection exchanges, a question about the future is answered with a boundary on the disciples' concern and a task from Jesus. Peter asks about the other disciple before being told to follow; the group asks about kingdom timing before being commissioned as witnesses. Neither question is assigned an unspoken motive or its requested timetable supplied.
    Acts 1:6 So those who had come together asked Him, “Lord, is this the time when Thou art restoring the kingdom to Israel?”
    Acts 1:7 He said to them, “It is not for you to know times or seasons that the Father has set by His own authority.
    Acts 1:8 But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem and [in] all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.”

John 21:22
Jesus says to him, “If I wish him to remain until I come, what is that to you? You follow Me.”
  John 21:19 — narrative parallel: Jesus repeats the personal Follow Me charge after Peter asks about the other disciple. The earlier command follows Peter's death forecast; the later one contrasts Peter's task with a conditional statement about the other's remaining. The refrain does not turn that conditional into a promise of immortality.
    John 21:19 Now He said this to indicate by what kind of death he would glorify God. And having said this, He says to him, “Follow Me.”

John 21:23
So this word went out among the brothers, that this disciple does not die. But Jesus did not say to him that he does not die, but, “If I wish him to remain until I come, what is that to you?”
  John 21:22 — narrative parallel: The narrator explicitly corrects the undying-disciple rumor by repeating Jesus' actual conditional words from the preceding answer. This is a direct quotation-and-correction relationship. The if is retained, with no claim that Jesus predicted the disciple would never die or that the rumor establishes his later biography.
    John 21:22 Jesus says to him, “If I wish him to remain until I come, what is that to you? You follow Me.”

John 21:24
This is the disciple who bears witness about these things and who wrote these things; and we know that his testimony is true.
  John 19:35 — narrative parallel: Both narrative witness statements affirm the testimony's truth. John 19 describes the one who saw and bears witness so others may believe; John 21 adds writing and the plural we know. The recurring formula is compared without assigning an unrecorded personal name or settling a new editorial theory about the plural voice.
    John 19:35 And the one who has seen has borne witness, and his testimony is true; and he knows that he speaks the truth, so that you also may believe.

John 21:25
And there are also many other things that Jesus did, which, if each one were written, I suppose that even the world itself would not contain the books being written.
  John 20:30 — narrative parallel: Both ending statements distinguish Jesus' wider activity from what the book records. John 20 specifically mentions signs before the disciples; John 21 broadens to other things and the world-and-books expression. The correspondence does not literalize the capacity rhetoric or establish particular unwritten deeds as verified facts.
    John 20:30 Now Jesus also did many other signs before His disciples, which are not written in this book;

Reviewed units still unfinished
John 2:2: The wedding invitation was reconsidered in its Cana setting. A parable about refusing a king's wedding invitation still does not supply a particular counterpart for Jesus and His disciples being invited here; no bridegroom identity is assigned.
John 2:8: First Kings17:10–13 was freshly read: Elijah requests water in a vessel and bread from a widow. Unlike this draw-and-bring-to-the-feast-master command, it addresses him as recipient and then a different food provision. The broad request/service resemblance remains insufficiently particular for retention.
John 4:16: The husband-fetching instruction was reread with the no-husband reply. No husband is narrated as fetched, and generic spouse-summoning passages would not supply this request or its unexplained full purpose.
John 7:9: The stay in Galilee follows the exact not-going statement and precedes the later private trip. No yet is inserted into7:8, no unstated timing is calculated, and no generic stay is retained as another account of this transition.
John 7:53: The double-bracketed each-to-his-house departure is preserved. The following contrasting destination of Jesus does not provide a separate counterpart for each person's departure, and no identities or itinerary are supplied.
John 8:10: The question was reread with the actual woman's reply and Jesus' instruction. They complete this exchange, but no different absolution scene is made the same event or a further comparison for these departed accusers.
John 9:23: The stated for-this-reason referral was reread with9:21–22. Its immediate causal and repeated wording is already considered in the particular9:21 comparison; no additional cross-passage relation is assigned merely to repeat that comment.
John 9:36: The request to identify the Son of Man was reread with the exact following self-identification. That title is not changed to Son of God, and no unrelated who question is made the same particular request to believe.
John 18:34: The personal-versus-reported source question was reread with Pilate's response. Other identity demands do not reproduce this specific distinction, and neither Pilate's hidden motives nor a new reply is supplied.
John 19:8: Pilate's increased fear is explicitly linked to the preceding allegation. Other warnings or judicial anxieties do not establish that cause or justify an unstated inner belief, and the wife's different experience is not transferred to him.
John 19:22: The refusal to revise the placard was reread with the requested change. A rule about irrevocable royal decrees would not establish Pilate's legal obligation or particular motive here; no general law is imposed on this rhetorical reply.
